Summary

Agatha lives alone on the cattle ranch she inherited too young, too stubborn, and too angry to ever leave. She's built a quiet life there, fence posts, coffee at dawn, hard work that leaves her too tired to want anything more. Until Rio Vidal shows up on the edge of her property with a field kit and a research grant.

Rio is calm, focused, quietly competent and clearly out of place in the dust and heat. But she stays. And Agatha, who’s spent her whole life guarding softness like it’s weakness, finds herself watching. Through storms, silence, cattle, and coffee, they find something slow and steady between them. Something that doesn't need to be named to be real.

Notes

Hi, hello, welcome to the slowest of slow burns.

This fic is a rancher AU where Agatha is a grumpy cattle rancher with emotional walls higher than her fences, and Rio is a calm, competent field biologist who shows up asking too many questions and looking entirely too good in the heat. Tension ensues. So does caretaking and staring. And probably kissing later if agatha ever figures her shit out. Enjoy the vibes, and please feel free to yell in the comments. I live off the slow-burn-induced suffering of others.

✓♥︎

Hard Soil, Soft Hands

by LabNotesAndFiction

https://archiveofourown.org/works/70614196

    Chapter 1: Stranger on the Fence Line

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes The truck was too clean.

    Agatha clocked it the second the dust cloud kicked up along the drive, a wide, slow-moving plume rising like smoke off dry gravel. Sunlight hit chrome just wrong enough to make her squint, and even from the porch, she could already tell: the damn thing barely had a scratch on it. No rust. No busted tail light. Tires that hadn’t tasted mud more than once. “All hat and no cattle,” Agatha muttered, flicking ash off the end of her cigarette.

    She didn’t move. Just leaned back in the creaking porch rocker, one boot braced against the warped floorboard to keep from drifting. The late- morning sun was already baking the porch rail. Heat shimmered across the pasture like spilled gasoline. Somewhere out by the west paddock, a cow lowed once and then went silent again.

    Agatha took a slow drag from her cigarette. Blew the smoke out through her nose like she had all the time in the world.

    The truck came to a stop just outside the gate. Not at the wrong angle, not too close, no rookie mistakes. Just a neat little parking job, neat little turn of the key, and then the door opened.

    Not a man. That was the first surprise.

    Not the old, sun-wrinkled county inspector she’d been imagining. Not some clipboard-toting grad student with more arrogance than sense, either. No. The woman who stepped out looked like she belonged behind a desk, not out here.

    Slim. Light brown skin and dark hair braided down her back in a way that was too clean, too deliberate for this heat. Long-sleeved button-down, pale khaki still crisp despite the drive. A satchel over one shoulder, notebook tucked under her arm. And sunglasses. Designer ones, maybe. Hard to tell. Agatha didn’t keep up with brands.

    What she did know was that the woman took one long look at the land—at the pasture, the fence, the sagging barn in the distance—and then turned her face toward the porch with a quiet, unfazed expression.

    And she smiled.

    Agatha didn’t smile back. She didn’t so much as twitch.

    City folk always smiled like that when they first showed up, like they didn’t know what the heat could do to a person. Like they thought the silence out here was peaceful instead of just empty.

    She hated when people looked at the ranch like that.

    Like it was something quaint. Picturesque. A goddamn pastoral scene out of a travel brochure. It wasn’t. It was old fence posts and too-dry soil and a thousand things that could go wrong before noon. There wasn’t a single thing romantic about it, just work, dust, and sweat. The only ones who didn’t leave were the ones who didn’t have anywhere else to go. And this woman? She had somewhere else to go. That was written all over her.

    The stranger adjusted her bag on her shoulder and began walking toward the gate, slow and even-paced. Like she wasn’t in a rush. Like she belonged there and knew it. Her work boots—Agatha noted with some irritation— didn’t make that usual too-stiff, too-shiny kind of sound. They were new- ish, sure, but they didn’t squeak. They moved right. Quietly. Agatha reached the end of her cigarette and stubbed it out in the chipped tin ashtray on the porch rail. She didn’t stand. Not yet. Just leaned forward slightly, elbows on her knees, watching with the weight of a woman who didn’t offer welcome freely—and rarely offered it at all. When the stranger reached the gate, she paused.

    Didn’t yank at the latch. Didn’t shout. Didn’t trespass. Just looked up toward the porch and shaded her eyes with one hand.

    “Ms. Harkness?” she called, voice steady, clear, and warm. Agatha didn’t answer. Not immediately.

    There it was again, that same damn smile. Not sugary. Not fake. Just… open. Like she hadn’t learned to guard herself out here yet. Or maybe she had. Maybe it was a trick.

    Agatha had seen more than her fair share of polite strangers. Inspectors, developers, oil reps, land consultants. Always smiling. Always courteous. Always ready to talk about things like “opportunity” and “community improvement.”

    Always ready to tell her what she ought to do with the land like it wasn’t the only thing that’d ever stayed.

    She stood slowly, finally, letting the boards creak under her boots as she stepped down from the porch and came to a stop a few yards from the gate. Dust swirled once around her ankles before settling.

    “Who’s askin’?” she said, voice low and unimpressed.

    The woman didn’t flinch. If anything, her smile deepened—just a little. Like she’d expected the reaction and didn’t take it personal. “Rio Vidal,” she said. “I’m with the regional biodiversity initiative. You should’ve received notice—I’m here to conduct a site survey.” Agatha said nothing. Just studied her, quiet as a hawk and twice as likely to bite.

    Rio didn’t squirm under the stare, which—again—shouldn’t have bothered Agatha as much as it did. She was used to people blinking too much, laughing too loud, making nervous small talk. This woman just stood there, calm and still, like it didn’t occur to her to be uncomfortable. Agatha finally muttered, “You’re early.”

    “The letter said Monday,” Rio replied.

    “It’s Sunday.”

    A pause. Then: “Is it?”

    Agatha grunted. “Looks like you’re ridin’ two trails at once, and neither’s marked.”

    She didn’t like how that accent curled around vowels. Soft. Refined, maybe. City-born, definitely. Probably educated. Probably knew the names of every damn weed on the property but hadn’t once mucked a stall or driven a fence post.

    And still… there was something steady about her.

    Which was its own kind of threat.

    Agatha reached for the gate latch, fingers brushing the sun-heated metal, and said, without looking up, “Gate’s got a latch problem. Don’t yank it.” “I wouldn’t dream of it,” came the reply—mild, dry, and a little too self- assured.

    Agatha’s jaw twitched.

    Smartass.

    The gate creaked as it opened—loud and stubborn like everything else on the property. Rio handled it carefully, exactly the way Agatha told her to, like she’d actually listened. That, in itself, was suspicious. Agatha held the gate open without a word. Rio stepped through with a quiet nod, like she was stepping into someone’s home instead of a dry, sunbaked stretch of nowhere that hadn’t seen a good rain in weeks. Her boots hit the dirt with a low crunch. Dust curled around her ankles and clung to the hem of her pants, and still, somehow, she didn’t look bothered. They walked side by side, unspeaking. Rio adjusted the strap of her satchel once, her eyes moving across the land, watching the fences, the distant cattle, the dry grass that crunched underfoot like old bones. She didn’t rush, didn’t drag her feet. Just paced herself quietly, like someone used to silence. “This land’s bigger than I expected,” she said after a while. Agatha shrugged. “Lot of space don’t mean much when the soil’s bad and the cows are stubborn.”

    “I don’t mind stubborn.”

    That earned her a look, brief, unimpressed.

    “I mean plants,” Rio clarified, glancing over with a faint smile. “Invasive ones. The ones that fight you when you try to clear them out. They always tell a better story than the ones that go easy.”

    Agatha didn’t answer. Just shoved her hands into the back pockets of her jeans and kept walking. She was already sweating under her collar, the sun bearing down hard enough to pull it out of her even when she was standing still. The cicadas were screaming loud enough to shake the damn air. Still, she didn’t break stride.

    “How long you been out here?” Rio asked.

    “All my life.”

    “Must know the land better than anyone.”

    Agatha gave her a sharp glance. “Wouldn’t go that far.”

    “Well,” Rio said mildly, “I’m not here to interrogate you on it.” Agatha didn’t reply.

    That silence stretched long enough for Rio to take the hint or seem to. She eased ahead by a step or two, giving Agatha space. Most people did. It was the smart thing to do. Agatha didn’t invite closeness. Not in words, not in movement. She didn’t need it.

    And yet the woman didn’t move like she was afraid. Just… respectful. Unshaken.

    Agatha looked at the back of her braid, clean, precise, tucked down her spine like it’d been done first thing that morning. Even the wisps at her neck looked intentional. There wasn’t a drop of sweat showing, though she had to be cooking under those sleeves.

    “Used to this kind of heat?” Agatha asked, suspicion curling under her tongue.

    “I’ve worked in worse.”

    “Worse than this?”

    “Rainforest heat. Wet. Sticky. This is almost pleasant in comparison.” Agatha scoffed. “You ain’t been here long enough to be talkin’ like that.” “Maybe not. But I adapt quickly.”

    There it was again, that calm confidence. Not cocky, just... rooted. Like she’d already found her footing.

    Agatha hated it.

    “You here alone?” she asked.

    “Yes.”

    “No assistants?”

    “No one but me.”

    “Suppose you’re armed?”

    That made Rio blink. “No.”

    “Then you’re an idiot.”

    “I’ve been called worse.”

    Agatha bit back the smallest twitch at the corner of her mouth. She wasn’t sure if it was the answer or the tone that got her.

    “Rattlesnakes don’t care if you’re polite.”

    “I’ll watch where I step.”

    “You better.”

    Rio didn’t fill the quiet after that. No explanation. No nervous chatter. Just silence. Just her steady breath and steady walk and that same unreadable stillness that was starting to dig under Agatha’s skin.

    Most people couldn’t handle the quiet.

    They broke under it, talked too much, asked dumb questions, filled the space with noise because they were too afraid of what might rise up without it.

    But not this woman.

    Agatha didn’t like being impressed. But she was.

    And that, more than anything, pissed her off.

    They reached the pasture’s edge where the dirt faded into dry, yellowing grass. Agatha slowed, letting her boots settle into the cracked soil, arms folded loosely across her chest. Rio stopped just ahead, pulling a small notebook from her satchel and flipping it open with practiced ease. No clipboard. No flimsy carbon paper. Just a battered notebook, thick with pressed pages, edges curled from sun and sweat and use.

    Agatha watched her crouch down without hesitation. Not careful. Not clumsy either. Just efficient. Like someone used to working low to the ground, someone whose knees didn’t mind gravel, whose hands didn’t mind dirt. That irritated her. She’d been counting on clumsiness, wanted some kind of proof that this woman was out of her depth.

    Instead, Rio touched a clump of wild buckwheat like it was something sacred. Her fingertips moved gently along the stalks, separating each bloom with practiced care. Her eyes scanned the base, the leaves, the surrounding growth. She didn’t make a sound. Didn’t even mutter to herself. Just observed.

    She scribbled something down in the notebook, quick, tight handwriting and moved a few steps to the left, crouching again.

    Agatha leaned against the nearest fence post, arms still folded, and narrowed her eyes.

    That’s when the horse wandered over.

    Of course it was that horse, Finn. Big, nosy, and forever curious. Brown coat sun-faded at the edges, ears flicking with interest. He snorted and meandered toward Rio like he’d known her for years, ears perked, posture loose.

    “Don’t you dare,” Agatha muttered under her breath.

    Finn did, in fact, dare. He walked right up and sniffed Rio’s shoulder. Rio turned slightly and blinked at him, surprised but not scared. She raised a slow hand, palm open, and let him sniff it. Then, carefully, she ran her fingers along his muzzle, just once, her touch featherlight. Finn leaned into it.

    Agatha almost choked on her own spit.

    She wanted to say something. Anything. Wanted to bark at the horse, tell him to get lost, remind him that strangers didn’t get that kind of attention. But her throat stuck, and all she could do was watch as Rio gave the animal a few soft strokes along the cheek and then returned her attention to the plants like nothing had happened.

    No gushing. No squealing. No damn selfie.

    Just… calm.

    Like it hadn’t rattled her at all.

    Agatha swallowed hard, the heat thick in her lungs.

    She didn’t trust people who liked animals too easily. But she trusted animals even less when they liked people too easily. Finn wasn’t sweet. He wasn’t some therapy horse. He’d nearly bitten the last man who tried to ride him. And now he was nuzzling this woman’s shoulder like a puppy? Absolutely not.

    Agatha pushed off the fence and approached slowly, boots crunching over bone-dry weeds. She stopped a few paces behind, not wanting to crowd, not sure why she was bothering to approach at all.

    Rio didn’t turn around. She was inspecting the stem of a cluster of low, scrubby wildflowers now, face unreadable.

    Agatha cleared her throat.

    “You always touch every plant you see?” she asked.

    Rio’s voice was soft, even. “Only the ones that want to be touched.” Agatha blinked. “You talk to ‘em, too?”

    “Sometimes.”

    “You expect ‘em to talk back?”

    “I don’t need them to.”

    That was not the answer Agatha had been expecting. She frowned, watching the way Rio’s hand hovered just above a bloom, not quite touching it this time. Her fingers curled slightly, hovering in the air like she was listening for something Agatha couldn’t hear.

    She couldn’t decide if it was ridiculous or unsettling.

    Rio scribbled another note, then finally stood, brushing her palms off on the side of her pants.

    “You got any water on you?” Agatha asked, mostly to distract herself. Rio nodded. “In the truck.”

    “You didn’t bring it out here?”

    “I’ll be fine.”

    Agatha stared at her. “You’re gonna pass out before lunch.” Rio just offered a small, lopsided smile. “Not the first time I’ve worked in the sun.”

    “Don’t mean you’re immune to heatstroke.”

    “I’ll keep that in mind.”

    That infuriating calm again. That sense of being perfectly centered, like nothing could shake her.

    Agatha hated how much she noticed it. Hated how much she felt it, like it was affecting the air around her. Like every word out of this woman’s mouth was slow-dripping into her bloodstream whether she wanted it or not.

    “What exactly are you lookin’ for out here?” she asked, voice sharp. “Primarily endemic flora. Climate impact trends. Any signs of native species that have become vulnerable over the last few decades.” She paused. “There’s a possibility of finding Verbesina microptera. It’s only ever been documented in this region twice.”

    “You think it’s on my land?”

    “I think there’s a chance.”

    Agatha exhaled slowly, long and hard through her nose.

    “I don’t like surprises.”

    “I’m not here to surprise you.”

    “You’re here,” Agatha said flatly. “That’s already enough.” Rio took that in with a calm nod. Didn’t get defensive. Didn’t offer comfort. Just accepted the statement and stood there in the dirt like she had every right to.

    Finn gave a low snort and wandered off again.

    Agatha watched him go.

    Rio turned back to her notebook and flipped a page.

    “You always this welcoming?” she asked lightly.

    Agatha’s jaw twitched.

    “Only when I like people,” she muttered.

    “And you don’t like me?”

    Agatha didn’t answer. She just looked at her, long and slow and unreadable. And Rio—damn her—smiled again. Just barely. Like she already knew. Rio flipped the page in her notebook, pen tapping lightly against the spine as she scanned the horizon. The pasture stretched wide and flat, nothing but dry wind and grazing cattle and heat rising in shimmering waves off the backs of slow-moving animals.

    She didn’t seem nervous.

    That, Agatha thought, was dangerous.

    She shifted her stance and jerked her chin toward the far side of the fence line.

    “You see the black one out past the windmill?”

    Rio followed her gaze again, squinting into the distance. One of the cattle stood apart from the rest, massive, broad-chested, and still as stone. The heat warped his shape at the edges, but his horns were unmistakable. Heavy. Sharp. Curving forward like he’d been built for war.

    “Yes,” Rio said. “I see him.”

    “Stay clear of him.” Agatha’s voice was low but firm. “That’s Ruger. One of my bulls.”

    Rio didn’t blink. “I figured.”

    “He don’t like strangers. Doesn’t like noise. Doesn’t like bein’ looked at too long. He gets twitchy, and when he gets twitchy, things bleed.” Rio didn’t smile. Didn’t joke. Just nodded once, taking it in. “Should I avoid the pasture entirely?”

    “You can go where you need,” Agatha said, “but you stay on the outer edges. Keep a fence to your back if you can. If you see him standin’ still and starin’ at you, you back away slow and you don’t turn your back. He won’t charge unless he sees an opening.”

    “And if he does?”

    Agatha didn’t miss a beat. “You hit the ground and pray to whatever gods you got left. That bull’ll stomp you flatter’n a skillet pancake.” The words hung in the air between them, blunt, matter-of-fact, and not at all exaggerated. Agatha had seen what Ruger could do when he was pissed. Had stitched up enough flesh—animal and human—to know better than to sugarcoat anything.

    But Rio didn’t flinch. She didn’t laugh it off or act offended. She just tilted her head slightly, thoughtful.

    “He’s territorial.”

    “He’s an asshole,” Agatha corrected. “But yeah.”

    “I’ll stay clear.”

    Agatha nodded once, then added, quieter, “Some people think the danger makes ‘em brave. But there’s nothin’ brave about bein’ stupid near something that outweighs you ten to one.”

    “That’s fair.”

    “I ain’t riskin’ a lawsuit just because someone wanted a better look at the angry one.”

    “I wouldn’t do that.”

    Agatha looked at her then. Really looked. At the steady way Rio met her eyes. At the absence of bravado or fear. Just clarity. Calm. “You sure you ain’t scared of him?” she asked.

    “I’m not stupid,” Rio said. “I respect danger when it tells me where it is.” Agatha didn’t respond. Couldn’t. The silence that followed wasn’t awkward, but it buzzed with something too large to name.

    Then she turned back toward the pasture.

    “Ruger’s not the only bull on this land,” she muttered.

    And Rio, voice soft but steady, replied: “I know.”

    Rio didn’t ask for a tour.

    She didn’t ask for help, didn’t expect company, didn’t make conversation. After the warning about Ruger, she just gave a single nod and peeled off toward the eastern edge of the pasture like she knew exactly where to begin. She moved through the heat with deliberate patience, her shoulders square, her path careful—not slow, but not careless either. Like every footstep had been chosen a second before it landed. She didn’t look back to see if Agatha was watching.

    She didn’t have to.

    Agatha leaned against the fence again, arms crossed, fingers tapping against the inside of her elbow as she watched the outline of Rio’s body blur in the distance, softened by heat shimmer and dust. It wasn’t like she meant to stare. But she did. Every movement pulled her gaze back like a magnet, steady hands brushing soil from plant roots, knees sinking into brittle grass, pen moving in quick, tight bursts across the pages of that damn notebook. She hated how quiet it all was.

    There were no complaints. No muttering. No exasperated sighs or dramatic swipes at sweat. Just the soft rustle of movement, the occasional flick of her braid against her shoulder, the glint of her glasses as she tilted her head to study a cluster of wild thistle.

    She looked like she belonged out there.

    That bothered Agatha more than it should’ve.

    She wasn’t used to people fitting themselves into her space so easily. Not without asking. Not without fumbling. They always tripped on something, always asked if the cows bit or what breed the horses were or whether she had air conditioning in the house—which she didn’t, unless you counted the ceiling fan that made a noise like it was about to rattle itself apart. But this one? She just... slotted into the silence like she’d been part of it all along.

    It unsettled something.

    Agatha squinted into the sun and tried not to shift her stance. She didn’t need this woman’s approval. She didn’t need anyone’s. But some traitorous part of her brain kept tracking Rio’s movements like a dog at heel, watching how she crouched low, hands moving the way Agatha’s did when she worked with a newborn calf or replaced a broken fence post. Gentle. Efficient. Intentional.

    Hell, even her handwriting looked clean from this far out. Agatha let her arms drop and stepped away from the fence. She moved slowly, more to keep herself from acting irritated than anything else. Her boots sent up little puffs of dry earth with every step, but Rio didn’t turn around. Didn’t acknowledge the sound. She was kneeling now beside a patch of low-lying wildflowers, one hand brushing along the stems, the other flipping through a weathered field guide tucked into the back of her notebook.

    Agatha stopped just shy of six feet away and folded her arms again. “You plannin’ to name every weed out here?” she asked, dry as bone. Rio glanced up without flinching. “If I can.”

    Agatha raised a brow. “You gonna tell me what they’re called, or just scribble it all down in your science bible?”

    Rio tapped the edge of the page without looking away from the plant. “This one’s Croton capitatus. Hogwort.”

    “Hogwort?”

    “Yes.”

    “Sounds made up.”

    “I assure you it’s not.” Rio flipped the page. “Native to this region. Shows up in dry prairies and disturbed soil. It’s a good indicator species.” Agatha snorted. “Everything out here’s disturbed.”

    Rio’s mouth twitched at that. Not quite a smile, but something close. Agatha hated that she noticed.

    She looked down at the plant, small, scraggly, barely holding on in the heat. Nothing pretty about it. No color to speak of. Just something clinging to life out of spite.

    Familiar.

    “And that’s useful to you?” she asked.

    “It tells me about water history. Nutrient levels. Competition between species. A few of these might mean grazing pressure is forcing out native perennials.”

    Agatha frowned. “You sayin’ I’m runnin’ my land too hard?” “No.” Rio met her eyes. “I’m saying it’s surviving anyway.” That gave Agatha pause. Not because it was flattering. But because it felt… close to something else. Something more pointed. She wasn’t sure what. Rio turned back to the plant before she could think too long on it. “You always this chatty when people work?” she asked.

    Agatha narrowed her eyes. “Only when they’re doin’ somethin’ strange.” Rio’s head tilted, just a little. “You find this strange?” “I find most people strange.”

    “Fair.”

    The silence settled again, comfortable for one of them, prickling for the other. Agatha shifted her weight and looked out across the pasture. Cattle moved slow in the distance, brown, black and red backs rising and dipping against the horizon. Somewhere farther out, a bird called sharp from a fence post. Wind tugged at the grass but didn’t cool the air. Rio crouched again, moving farther along the edge of the field. She didn’t ask Agatha to follow.

    She didn’t ask her to leave, either.

    Agatha stayed.

    Watched her move from patch to patch like she was reading something written in the land itself. Not just identifying plants, interpreting them. Her fingers hovered without touching, like she could feel their stories through proximity alone.

    Agatha scowled and looked away.

    She didn’t like people who tried to find meaning in dirt. She didn’t like that she almost wanted to ask what it all meant. She didn’t like that she wanted to keep watching her work. By the time Agatha wandered back to the fence again, the sun was high enough to burn. Not just hot, hostile. The kind of heat that made the air shimmer and pressed down on your shoulders like a weight you couldn’t set down. It clung to the skin, got into your bones, made the ground crack and the cattle slow.

    But Rio didn’t slow.

    She moved through the field like she was made for it, sleeves rolled to the elbow now, braid sticking to the back of her neck. She was sweating finally but it wasn’t the kind of sweat that said I’m about to pass out. It was steady. Measured. Like her body knew how to ration everything, including discomfort.

    She crouched beside a clump of waist-high grass and tugged a specimen bag from her satchel, careful not to disturb the soil more than necessary. She used a plastic scoop to lift a thin layer of sediment, sealing it with a practiced touch and scribbling a number on the label before tucking it away. Agatha watched from the shade of a half-dead mesquite tree, arms folded, one shoulder leaned against the trunk. She didn’t need to be out there. Didn’t need to supervise. Didn’t owe this woman anything but fence-line access and a warning not to piss off the livestock.

    But still, she stayed.

    It was the way Rio moved, she told herself. That’s all. Agatha had seen enough people come through the ranch pretending to be competent. Trying to impress her. Making messes they didn’t know how to fix. Rio wasn’t trying to impress anyone. She was just… working. And that? That was dangerous.

    Because Agatha had always respected people who knew what the hell they were doing.

    And she was starting to respect Rio Vidal far more than she wanted to. She shifted her stance, the bark of the tree digging into her spine, and squinted into the light.

    Rio had taken off her sunglasses. They hung from the front of her shirt now, forgotten. Her eyes were dark and focused, narrowed slightly as she examined a plant from root to flower, rotating it slowly between her fingers. Her lips moved now and then, silent, forming names Agatha didn’t know, theories she wasn’t privy to.

    There was something about the way she held herself that made her look untouchable.

    Not cold. Not closed off. Just… solid. Like she didn’t need anyone else to validate her presence.

    Agatha hated that kind of self-possession. It made her skin itch. Made her want to test it.

    She turned away. Walked five slow paces toward the pasture fence, paused, and turned back.

    Rio was still crouched, writing again, the sun casting sharp shadows across her cheekbones.

    Agatha rubbed a hand along the back of her neck and muttered, “You’re gonna fry out here.”

    Rio didn’t look up. “I’m alright.”

    “You say that now.”

    “I brought sunscreen.”

    “That ain’t gonna help when your brain boils.”

    “I’ve had worse.”

    “You keep sayin’ that.”

    “Because it’s true.”

    Agatha scowled. “You always like this?”

    “Unbothered.”

    Rio finally looked up, shielding her eyes with the back of her hand. “I’m not unbothered,” she said. “I just don’t complain out loud.” Agatha stared at her for a long moment. “That supposed to impress me?” “No.”

    “Good.”

    A beat of silence passed.

    Then Rio added, “But I think it did.”

    Agatha’s jaw flexed.

    She turned again. Took another few steps away. Kicked a rock into the weeds.

    God, this woman was getting to her.

    And it wasn’t even noon.

    She considered going back to the house, pretending she had something better to do. Something more important than standing in the heat, watching some city-bred biologist poke around her land like she owned the place. But something stopped her.

    Maybe it was the way Rio stood next, stretching tall, arching her back to ease the pull on her spine. Her shirt clung damp to the middle of her back, the heat darkening the fabric in slow, creeping patches. She pulled a hair tie from her wrist and twisted her braid into a bun, fast and efficient. Not delicate. Not fussy. Just necessary.

    Agatha looked away.

    She needed to look away.

    But her gaze betrayed her again a second later when Rio pulled a folded cloth from her satchel and pressed it against her neck, wiping slow. There was nothing overtly seductive about it. Nothing intended. But Agatha’s mouth went dry anyway.

    She swallowed and said, with more bite than she meant to, “You keep workin’ like that, you’re gonna pass out and leave me to explain it to the sheriff.”

    Rio glanced over her shoulder. “You think I’m delicate?”

    “I think you don’t know this land yet.”

    “That’s why I’m here.”

    “And if it decides it don’t like you?”

    Rio turned fully then, arms loose at her sides, eyes calm as ever. “Then I’ll listen harder.”

    The wind picked up just enough to rustle the dry grass between them. A cicada buzzed high in a nearby tree. One of the cows groaned, deep and disinterested, somewhere behind the barn.

    Agatha shifted her weight.

    “You keep talkin’ like that,” she muttered, “you’re gonna piss it off.” “Wouldn’t be the first time,” Rio said. “I’ve pissed off a lot of things by not leaving.”

    Agatha blinked. The words hit harder than they should’ve. She didn’t answer.

    Didn’t want to answer.

    So instead, she turned back toward the fence, found Finn nosing around the far gate, and focused on him like her life depended on it. But she didn’t stop listening to the sound of Rio moving behind her. Didn’t stop noticing every time her boots hit the ground, steady and quiet, like she belonged here already.

    Like she’d been part of the silence longer than Agatha had. The house was cooler than the field, but not by much.

    Agatha left the front door open out of habit, letting the screen hang half- shut while she kicked off her boots just inside the threshold. Dust trailed behind her like a second shadow, clinging to the creases of her jeans, the rolled sleeves of her shirt, the back of her neck where sweat had dried in a salt ring.

    She didn’t bother heading for the kitchen. Just stopped in the living room, hands on her hips, and stood there, staring at nothing.

    The ceiling fan spun slow overhead, blades ticking like a lazy metronome. One of the dogs let out a sleepy huff from where it lay curled in the corner, too heat-drunk to do much else.

    Agatha didn’t move.

    Didn’t sit.

    Didn’t take off her hat.

    She just stood there in the middle of the floor like she was waiting for something to shake loose from her own skin.

    The taste of dust still sat on her tongue. She reached for the water bottle on the windowsill—lukewarm by now—and drank without thinking, long and slow.

    But it didn’t clear the feeling.

    Didn’t clear the image burned behind her eyes.

    That woman—Rio—kneeling in the dirt like she’d been born there. Hands on plants like they were old friends. Sunlight catching the curve of her neck. That loose knot of hair, the heat pressed into the shoulders of her shirt.

    The way she looked at the land.

    Like it was talking to her.

    Agatha hated that. Hated how quiet it had made her feel. How off-center. Like something had shifted half an inch to the left and she was the only one who noticed.

    She set the bottle down too hard on the table. It clacked against the wood, tipped slightly, and settled with a hollow sound.

    The dog blinked but didn’t move.

    Agatha exhaled through her nose, jaw tight.

    “She’s just a visitor,” she muttered aloud, to no one. “Flash in the pan. City folks blow in, blow out. Wind don’t change its mind once it starts blowin’.” No one answered.

    She crossed the room and opened the screen door again, letting the hot breeze roll in. The porch boards groaned under her step. She leaned against the railing and squinted out toward the pasture where the woman still moved, just a flicker of motion now between golden grass and shade. Still working.

    Still calm.

    Still out there like she belonged.

    Agatha watched for a minute too long before catching herself. She muttered something ugly under her breath and turned back inside.

    She didn’t look again.

    But she left the door open.

    Chapter End Notes Agatha: I don't like her also Agatha: has not stopped watching her for a full hour Thank you for reading!! Chapter 2 is already in the works, expect more fence line tension, quiet fieldwork, and agatha continuing to pretend she’s not wildly affected by literally everything Rio does. Let me know your thoughts if you’re into it, comments, theories, emotional damage predictions, I want it all.

    Chapter 2: Terms of the Land

    Chapter Summary Agatha doesn’t like guests, questions, or strangers on her land. Rio doesn’t offer flattery, apologies, or lies. In the silence that follows, both women find themselves pressed against the edges of what they’re willing to admit.

    Chapter Notes Welcome back to two emotionally stunted women circling each other like suspicious cats.

    This chapter is all about: Agatha asking questions like she’s conducting an interrogation, Rio answering like she’s already read the answers off the back of a calm, well-organized cereal box, and both of them pretending this isn’t the most intense conversation they’ve had in years.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes The knock was light. Polite. Just two short raps like Rio was trying not to impose.

    Agatha didn’t answer right away.

    She was at the table, boots off, mug of coffee going cold in her hands, and a knot between her shoulders that hadn’t let up since sunrise. Outside, the wind rustled the dry mesquite near the porch, and the screen door creaked once on its hinges. The late afternoon heat was still heavy, pressing against the house like it wanted in. The fan overhead spun lazy, halfhearted, the motor ticking like it needed oil.

    Rio didn’t knock again.

    She just stood in the doorway, waiting. Not impatient. Not uncomfortable. Just still. That quiet, solid stillness she carried like silence wasn’t something she ever felt the need to fill.

    Agatha’s jaw flexed.

    “Get in here before the dog barks at you,” she muttered without looking up. The screen door creaked open. The wooden one stayed wide. Rio stepped inside like she belonged, steady, calm, without a trace of hesitance. She wiped her boots on the mat just inside the door before crossing the threshold, even though Agatha hadn’t said a word about it. Dust clung to her sleeves and the collar of her shirt. Her braid looked looser now, frayed at the edges from the wind, a few strands of hair escaping and sticking to the side of her neck where the sweat hadn’t quite dried. She didn’t say anything yet.

    Just took a look around the kitchen, casual but not nosy. Noting things, probably. The cracked tile. The old cabinets. The chipped ceramic dog bowl tucked near the back door. She didn’t ask questions, didn’t compliment anything, didn’t play at small talk.

    She just stood there, waiting.

    Agatha looked up at her finally, gave her a once-over without meaning to. “You want water or not?”

    Rio nodded once. “Yes, please.”

    Agatha set her coffee aside and moved to the sink. The faucet squealed as she turned it, and the water came out warm for the first few seconds. She let it run longer than necessary, pretending it needed to cool, even though she knew damn well it wouldn’t.

    She could feel Rio’s presence at her back. Not heavy. Not invasive. Just there. Quiet and patient like she had all the time in the world. Agatha grabbed a glass from the cabinet, filled it, and set it on the table a little harder than she needed to.

    Rio crossed the room and took the seat across from her. Calm, measured. Hands loose. Shoulders relaxed. She didn’t pull out her notebook yet. She just reached for the glass and took a slow sip, like this wasn’t a test she was already sitting in the middle of.

    Agatha sat down across from her. Folded her arms. Let the silence stretch. She’d always been good at silence. Letting it settle into a room like dust, slow, patient, oppressive. Most people cracked in under a minute. They squirmed, coughed, filled the space with chatter just to hear their own voice.

    But Rio didn’t fidget.

    She just sipped her water, set the glass down, and looked at her. Not challenging. Not deferential either.

    Just… even.

    “You said you’re out here for some survey,” she said finally, breaking the silence like it owed her something. “Plants and soil and whatnot.” “I am.”

    Agatha leaned back. “You gonna tell me what that actually means, or am I supposed to just sit here and trust that you’re not planning to turn the land into some kind of wildlife amusement park?”

    Rio didn’t laugh. She didn’t even blink.

    “I told you I’m with the biodiversity initiative,” she said. “That’s still true. But I can explain in more detail, if you want.”

    “I don’t want a lecture,” Agatha said. “I want the kind of answer someone gives when they know they’re being watched.”

    Rio’s brow rose just slightly, more surprised than offended. Agatha gestured vaguely toward the window, where the pasture stretched out across the fields, shimmering gold in the heat.

    “You’re on my land,” she said. “Which means every step you take, every sample you bag, every weed you poke at—I’ve got a right to know why.” “You do.”

    “Good. Then talk.”

    A pause.

    Rio reached slowly for the notebook at her hip and placed it on the table like she was laying down cards.

    Agatha watched her every move. Watched the curve of her fingers, the way she opened the cover to a bookmarked page, how the tip of her pen was stained with green from whatever she'd been touching in the field. God, even her supplies were too neat.

    Agatha couldn’t decide if she wanted to be impressed or irritated, so she settled for both.

    Rio looked up again. “Just to be clear,” she said, soft but steady, “you don’t want the pitch. You want the version I’d give someone who could tell me to leave.”

    Agatha stared at her.

    Then, slowly, nodded once.

    Rio nodded back.

    Rio tapped the edge of her notebook once with the back of her pen. Not nervous. Just thinking.

    “The short version,” she said slowly, “is that your land sits in the middle of a zone marked as ‘ecologically unexamined’—which basically means the state knows it exists but hasn’t taken the time to look at it since the seventies. Most plots like this have either been turned into subdivisions, over-farmed, or cleared for livestock. This one hasn’t.”

    Agatha raised a brow. “You sure about that?”

    “I’ve looked at satellite images going back twenty-five years,” Rio said. “The topography hasn’t changed. And even after walking it—twice—I can see the species layers. This pasture isn’t restored. It’s been resilient. There’s a difference.”

    Agatha let that settle. Let the word hang in the air like heat. Resilient.

    She didn’t like how that sounded. Like it meant something bigger than the grass.

    Rio didn’t wait for approval.

    “I’m looking at native prairie species,” she continued, flipping the page with one slow finger. “Specifically the way they respond to stress. Drought. Heatwaves. Pressure from grazing. What’s managing to hang on—and how.”

    Agatha leaned back a little in her chair. “You trying to make a case for conservation?”

    “Not exactly.”

    “Then what?”

    “I’m building a record,” Rio said. “One that might help someone make a case. Or understand how these systems work long after most people forgot they existed.”

    Agatha narrowed her eyes. “You sound like a scientist.”

    “I am a scientist.”

    “You sound like one of the good ones.”

    “I’m trying to be.”

    Agatha stared at her. Not hostile. Just steady. Testing.

    “So what do you need me for?”

    “You’re the one letting me stay.”

    “I could change my mind.”

    “I know.”

    Silence bloomed again, warm and slow and thick as August. The fan overhead clicked with a slight hiccup in its rhythm.

    Agatha glanced down at the open notebook.

    There were notes scribbled in two colors, black and green. Sections divided with clean margins. A short list in the top corner with names she didn’t recognize. Plant names, probably. One had a little star beside it. “You find what you were lookin’ for yet?” she asked, nodding at the page. Rio’s eyes dropped to the notebook. “Not yet. But I’ve found traces. Fragments of things that shouldn’t still be growing here. Not unless something about this land kept them safe.”

    Agatha’s jaw tensed. “And what—exactly—are you gonna do if you find somethin’ rare?”

    “I’ll report it.”

    “To who?”

    “The state. The regional initiative. Possibly a few independent conservation networks.”

    “And then?”

    Rio met her eyes again. “Then nothing. Your land is private. It’s protected unless you choose otherwise. All I’ll be doing is documenting what’s here.” Agatha studied her for a long moment. Her voice had stayed steady through the whole thing. Calm. Measured. No pitch to it. No plea. Just facts. Just purpose.

    But it still rubbed something raw inside her.

    “People usually try harder to convince me,” Agatha said, low. “They offer flattery. Promise they’ll stay out of the way. Pretend like they’re doin’ me a favor.”

    “I’m not pretending,” Rio said. “And I’m not asking for a favor.” Agatha tilted her head slightly. “So what’re you asking for?” Rio held her gaze. “Time.”

    It was too quiet for a beat.

    The kind of quiet that didn’t settle. That strained.

    Agatha looked back down at the notebook.

    Then to the map. Then to Rio’s face again.

    She didn’t smile. Didn’t soften.

    But she didn’t tell her to leave, either.

    Agatha didn’t move right away. Just sat there, arms folded across her chest, her gaze sharp enough to cut through wood. She hadn’t touched her coffee since Rio walked in. It sat cold on the table, forgotten. Rio’s notebook was still open. Her fingers rested on the edge of the paper like she was expecting to be interrupted again, and didn’t mind. Her pen lay between them like a line in the sand.

    Agatha looked at it, then at her.

    “You got a permit?” she asked, flat and sudden.

    Rio didn’t even blink. “Yes.”

    “You carrying it?”

    “In my bag.”

    “Let me see it.”

    Rio nodded once, calm as you please, and reached slowly for her satchel, which she’d tucked neatly beneath her chair. She unzipped a side pocket and pulled out a folded document, sealed in plastic to keep the dust out. She handed it over without ceremony.

    Agatha took it with a grunt. Flipped it open. Scanned the signature, the letterhead, the barcode. It was legit. Boring, even. Which somehow made it more trustworthy.

    Still.

    “Easy to fake paper,” Agatha muttered.

    Rio met her eyes. “So is politeness.”

    That pulled something taut in Agatha’s chest.

    She handed the document back. “You always this smart-ass with people who can throw you off a property?”

    “I’m not being a smart-ass.”

    Agatha’s brow lifted. “Then what are you bein’?”

    “Honest.”

    Agatha sat back, arms still crossed. “That your specialty?” “Sometimes.”

    Agatha didn’t like that answer. Or maybe she didn’t like how even it sounded. No defense. No challenge. Just even. That evenness again. Like she didn’t mind being tested. Like she was used to it.

    That made Agatha want to push harder.

    She gestured toward the map still spread on the table, half-shadowed by the angle of the light.

    “You plan on pokin’ around the whole damn place?”

    “Only the areas with native growth. East pasture. Creekbed. Southern ridge.”

    “You’ll stay outta the herd field.”

    Rio nodded. “Of course.”

    “They don’t take kindly to strangers.”

    “I noticed.”

    “And Ruger—” Agatha leaned forward slightly, voice low. “If you so much as look at that bull the wrong way, I’m not dragging your ass to the ER.” “I remember.”

    “You can remember all you want. Won’t stop him goring you.” Rio’s mouth quirked slightly. “You care?”

    Agatha’s eyes narrowed. “I care about liability.”

    “Of course.”

    Another pause. Another silence. Heavier this time.

    Agatha leaned on the table, forearms flat against the wood. “You’ve walked the land,” she said slowly. “You’ve taken samples. You’ve seen the plants. So tell me something.”

    “Alright.”

    “What do you really want to find out here?”

    Rio tilted her head, just slightly. “In what sense?”

    “In the sense that people like you don’t take on field work in hundred- degree heat unless they’re looking for something specific.” Rio didn’t smile. Didn’t bristle. Just… considered.

    Then, quietly, “I want to find something that shouldn’t be here.” Agatha’s jaw ticked. “Why?”

    “Because if it is, it means something we thought was gone isn’t. And maybe that means the land knows more than we do.”

    That answer landed like a brick.

    Agatha didn’t speak for a long moment. She just stared at her, heart beating too slow and too loud in her chest.

    “You believe that?” she asked eventually.

    “Yes.”

    “You believe the land remembers.”

    “I believe it doesn’t forget as fast as we do.”

    Agatha swallowed.

    It wasn’t the answer she expected.

    And she didn’t like what it stirred in her.

    She pushed her chair back with a scrape and stood, she turned to the sink and dumped her coffee with too much force. The mug clinked against the basin as she set it down harder than necessary.

    Behind her, Rio remained seated. Still calm. Still quiet. Still not leaving.

    Agatha turned back, leaned against the counter, arms crossed again. “You’re not gonna ask me for anything, are you?”

    Rio looked up. “What do you mean?”

    “You’re not gonna try to convince me. Not gonna sweet-talk me into thinking you’re here for my benefit.”

    Rio shook her head. “No.”

    “Why not?”

    “Because I don’t want to lie to you.”

    Agatha stared at her for a long moment.

    Then—almost grudgingly—she said, “Most people would.”

    “I’m not most people.”

    “Yeah,” Agatha muttered, eyes dropping to the notebook. “I’m starting to get that.”

    Agatha didn’t speak for a while.

    Not because she didn’t have anything to say, there was plenty knocking around in her skull, sharp and irritating like grit in a boot, but because nothing that wanted to come out sounded like something she was ready to admit.

    The silence hung between them like a curtain. Not heavy. Just thick. Saturated. The kind of quiet that doesn’t just happen, you choose it. And Rio was still choosing it. Just sitting there at the kitchen table, notebook closed, pen set aside, hands folded loose in front of her like she wasn’t in any kind of hurry to get anywhere else.

    Agatha wasn’t sure what to do with that.

    Most folks filled silence like they were scared of it. They cracked jokes, asked questions, leaned too far into a performance of friendliness, or flinched when they realized she wasn’t going to make it easy on them. But Rio didn’t fidget. Didn’t blink too much. Didn’t smile like she thought it would help.

    She just sat still.

    Present.

    Unbothered.

    Worse, she looked like she belonged there.

    Agatha’s throat worked as she swallowed against nothing, dry. She set her hands flat on the countertop, too gently. The quiet made every sound seem louder than it needed to be.

    “You’re not gonna ask to stay in the house, are you?”

    “No.”

    Agatha didn’t look at her. Just stared out the kitchen window like it might offer her an escape. All it gave her was a warped reflection of herself and a sliver of sky already turning the color of cooling steel. “Good,” she muttered. “I don’t like guests.”

    “I figured.”

    “You sleepin’ in your truck?”

    “I have a cot in the back. Camp rig. It’s meant for field work.” Agatha frowned slightly. “There’s a motel down in town.”

    “It’s thirty minutes away.”

    “You drive?”

    “I walk.”

    That pulled Agatha’s gaze back to her. Slowly. Like she wasn’t sure she wanted to look but couldn’t help herself.

    “In this heat?”

    Rio just nodded.

    Agatha huffed. “You’re stubborn.”

    Rio gave a faint shrug.

    Agatha’s mouth twitched. Almost a smirk. But not quite.

    She turned again. Walked a few slow paces across the kitchen, then paused at the back door. One hand braced on the frame like she needed something to hold onto, not because she was tired, but because she was restless. Rattled in a way she didn’t want to name.

    “You’re not gonna ask me for help, either,” she said.

    “No.”

    “You don’t want me to show you around. Don’t want a guide.” “I’d rather not inconvenience you.”

    Agatha snorted. “Too late for that.”

    But it didn’t come out sharp. More like a tired exhale.

    She stood there a while, staring at the barn roof visible just over the line of brush. It was faded, rust-red, and leaned a little too far to the left. She hadn’t fixed it yet. Hadn’t wanted to. But now she wondered if Rio had noticed it. If she’d made some quiet note in her field book about the angle or the light.

    “You start diggin’ into anything that don’t belong to the land,” Agatha said, voice low but firm, “you tell me first. Glass. Wire. Bones.” Rio’s voice behind her was softer. “Bones?”

    Agatha shrugged without turning. “Not everything out there’s alive.” “Understood.”

    The air shifted again, warmer now, but less suffocating.

    Agatha turned slowly, leaned back against the doorframe with her arms crossed.

    “If you find something rare,” she said, “something important—something you think oughta be protected—”

    “I’ll tell you.”

    “Even if you think I won’t care?”

    Rio met her gaze across the room. Steady. Open. No hesitation. “Especially then.”

    Something loosened behind Agatha’s ribs. Just a little.

    She didn’t want it to. She didn’t mean for it to. But there it was, like breath caught on the edge of something unfamiliar.

    “You believe in all this enough to let it get personal?” Agatha asked quietly. “Yes.”

    “Even when it’s not your land?”

    “It’s someone’s,” Rio said. “And that has to matter.”

    Agatha studied her for a long, weighted second.

    Then—without softening her voice—“You better know how to prove it.” Rio nodded once. “I do.”

    Agatha exhaled slowly, the kind of breath that didn’t quite let go of its tension but didn’t hold it as tightly either. She looked at Rio like she was seeing her again for the first time, and this time, there was no test in her gaze. Just something still, and measuring.

    “You’ll be back out there before sundown?”

    “If there’s light.”

    Agatha looked past her to the porch, where the heat had softened into gold. “You can watch,” Rio said.

    Agatha didn’t respond.

    She didn’t say yes.

    But she didn’t say no.

    And when Rio stood and gathered her notebook, quiet and sure, Agatha didn’t stop her from going.

    She just watched.

    And let her.

    Agatha stayed in the kitchen long after Rio left.

    She could’ve gone back outside, checked the barn, swept the porch, picked one of a hundred chores that didn’t require thinking. But her body stayed still, planted firm in the doorway, like moving would confirm something had shifted and she wasn’t ready to admit that yet.

    Outside, the heat had mellowed. The light was low and stretched thin across the grass. Long shadows pooled at the fence line, and the barn threw a silhouette sharp enough to cut the horizon in two.

    She could just make out Rio’s figure in the distance, small but steady, walking along the edge of the ridge with her bag slung over one shoulder. Her stride never changed. Even now, with the day wearing down and the sun dragging itself toward the dirt, she moved like she had all the time in the world.

    Agatha watched her go.

    Watched too long.

    Then—irritated with herself—she shut the back door with a little more force than necessary. The screen slapped back into place behind it, and the dog shifted in his spot near the hallway but didn’t get up. Agatha scrubbed a hand down her face. The skin along her jaw was sun- tight, gritty with dried sweat. She hadn’t even realized she hadn’t changed yet. Her shirt stuck to her shoulder blades. Her hair was half-falling out of its band. She felt… unmoored. Too aware of her own body in her own kitchen.

    She hated that feeling.

    She went to the sink and ran cold water over her hands, then pressed her damp palms to the back of her neck. The sudden chill made her flinch, but at least it pulled her thoughts back down to earth.

    She’s just some biologist, she told herself.

    Just a scientist with a map and too much patience. Just someone passing through. Just someone who didn’t flinch when challenged. Who listened without groveling. Who noticed things and didn’t make a show of it. Agatha didn’t like people like that. Didn’t trust people like that. And yet…

    She dried her hands on the back of her jeans and moved back toward the table. Her boot scuffed the corner of Rio’s empty water glass. She caught it before it tipped and set it straight again.

    Then she stared at it.

    Too long.

    A glass was just a glass. But it felt like a line had been crossed. Not something huge. Not something dramatic. Just one of those small things that builds without warning.

    She sat down in the chair Rio had used, elbows on the table, and stared out the window.

    No one had ever asked if she cared about the land.

    No one had ever said they would tell her anyway.

    And now there was a woman walking her fenceline like it might tell her secrets, while Agatha sat inside trying to pretend she didn’t want to follow. Chapter End Notes And that’s the chapter where agatha went full “prove it,” and Rio did. Politely. With a notebook and exactly zero fear.

    Agatha: I don’t like you.

    Also Agatha: here’s some water. Here's a warning about the bull again. Here's a tiny piece of my trust, but don’t touch it.

    Thank you so much for reading!

    Chapter 3: The Weight of Shade

    Chapter Summary The sun rises hard and angry, baking the land before the day even begins. Rio endures it. Agatha watches too long. And when a hat changes hands without a word, silence becomes something heavier. Chapter Notes Welcome back to two emotionally constipated women and one cowboy hat with unbearable significance.

    This chapter is about: sunburns, silent judgments, internal screaming, and agatha doing something quietly kind and immediately regretting how much it meant. Also, Rio just keeps being competent and calm while Agatha tries to keep it together and fails spectacularly. Hope you enjoy this deep dive into the fine art of “i’m not helping you, I just noticed you were dying.”

    See the end of the chapter for more notes The sun rose angry.

    Not warm. Not gentle. Not slow.

    Just sharp and sudden, burning the dew off the grass before it could settle, turning every surface into something that bit when touched. By the time the clock on the stove blinked past 7:00, the ranch already felt baked through. Heat like this didn’t creep in, it claimed.

    “Hotter’n a two-dollar pistol,” Agatha muttered under her breath. It turned dirt to powder, fence rails to stovetops, the barn roof into a skillet fit for frying an egg if you were stupid enough to try. The kind of heat that got into your skull and made your thoughts slosh around like water in a boiling pot.

    Agatha had been up since before dawn. She’d hauled sacks of feed to the troughs, scattering cubes while the cattle pressed close, grumbling low in their throats. The bull had pawed at the dirt like he had something to prove, and she had to clap her hands loud to send him off before he crowded the others. She checked the salt blocks along the fence line, kicked one back into place when the calves had nosed it loose, and counted heads through the rising steam of their breath.

    The water troughs had been worse, flies already thick, the pump squealing with rust as she filled them again.

    By the time she stepped onto the porch with a coffee mug cradled in both hands like the weight of it might anchor her, the back of her neck felt baked raw.

    The wood beneath her boots was already hot. Not warm. Hot. She shifted her weight automatically, trying to find a cooler spot near the railing. Her dog, Bandit, barely lifted his head from under the porch swing, let out something between a sigh and a wheeze, and flopped an ear back against the deck like he’d already surrendered.

    “Smart,” she muttered to him. “You’ve got it figured. Smart dog knows to hug the shade ’fore the sun gets mean.”

    Her own clothes clung in places, under the arms, across the small of her back, behind her knees where the denim wouldn’t breathe. She’d only finished the bare minimum—feed, water, count—and already her body felt like it was at war with the weather.

    She lifted the mug, took a sip, and winced.

    Coffee, lukewarm. Burnt. Bitter.

    Still, she drank it.

    Habit dies slow.

    Her eyes drifted, just casually, just out of instinct, across the pasture. And there she was.

    Of course she was.

    Rio Vidal. Already out in the far east field. Already moving. Already working.

    No bandana.

    No goddamn sense.

    “Sun’ll skin you alive out here,” Agatha muttered. “Don’t squat with your spurs on, and don’t stand in July without a hat.”

    Agatha watched her crouch low, knees tucked beneath her, shoulders folding in like a hinge as she reached for something in the dirt. She wasn’t rushing. Wasn’t fumbling. Each movement intentional. Smooth. Measured. Efficient.

    Which pissed Agatha off for reasons she didn’t want to think about. It was too hot to be impressed.

    And too early to be this annoyed.

    Rio adjusted her glasses, brushed a thumb along the edge of a leaf no bigger than a fingernail, and reached into her bag for a sample envelope like she’d done it a thousand times. Probably had.

    She had that stillness about her. That calm. Like the heat didn’t affect her the way it did normal people. Like she wasn’t dripping sweat or lightheaded or fighting the urge to crawl into a stock tank and live there until October. But Agatha could see it. The sweat soaking into the back of her shirt. The shine along her hairline. The pink beginning to rise at the edges of her ears and the back of her neck.

    She was cooking out there, whether she felt it or not.

    And she wasn’t doing a damn thing to stop it.

    Agatha swore under her breath. “What are you tryin’ to prove?” Rio knelt again, this time with both hands pressed to the soil, like she was listening to it. Like it would whisper secrets to her if she gave it long enough. Her notebook was open in the grass beside her, pages fluttering under the occasional dry breeze. The pen she’d been using was clamped between her teeth.

    Agatha blinked. Ground her teeth. Took another sip of coffee and immediately regretted it.

    From this distance, she could make out the shape of Rio’s braid. The way it stuck to her back in one damp, heavy line. Her shirt was clinging, pulled tight across her shoulder blades, and the outline of her spine showed faint through the fabric every time she leaned forward.

    And the whole time, no protection from the sun.

    Not even a damn attempt at shade.

    Agatha didn’t realize she was gripping the mug too tight until she felt it shift in her hand.

    She loosened her grip. Set it on the porch rail with a soft thunk and stared harder, like maybe that would cool the other woman down by force of will alone.

    No luck.

    Rio kept working like the sun wasn’t trying to kill her.

    Like her skin wasn’t turning a slow, steady red.

    Like someone wasn’t watching her from a shaded porch and growing more irritated by the second.

    Agatha didn’t sigh, but it was a near thing.

    She turned back toward the door, muttering to no one, “You’re gonna pass out before noon, you stubborn jackass.”

    Bandit thumped his tail once against the floorboards in what might’ve been agreement. Or maybe he just liked the sound of her voice. Agatha glanced back once.

    Just once.

    Then she stepped inside and let the screen door slap shut behind her. But the heat didn’t leave.

    Not from her skin.

    Not from her mind.

    And definitely not from the way that damn woman had looked in the sun. Agatha stood just inside the house, one hand on the kitchen counter, the other gripping the edge of the windowsill like it might steady her. The glass was grimy. Streaked with hard water and dust that caught in the early light. But even through the mess, she could see Rio, clear as day, still out in the pasture, crouched down like she was examining the holy grail instead of what looked like a dried-out weed and a few rocks. Every few minutes, she’d shift. Move a little to the left. Adjust her footing. Swipe a thumb across her forehead only to leave the sweat smeared, not gone. She didn’t slow down. Didn’t flinch. Just kept poking at the ground like she couldn’t feel the sun chewing at the back of her neck. Agatha’s jaw ached before she realized she’d been clenching. She made herself blink. Backed away from the window a few inches. But her eyes didn’t stop tracking.

    Like watching a train head for a cliff.

    And the worst part?

    Rio wasn’t flailing. Wasn’t dramatic. Wasn’t stumbling around like she needed help.

    She was just... enduring.

    Like this wasn’t her first heatwave. Like she knew it was bad but didn’t see a reason to stop. Like she was used to discomfort. To silence. To being alone in her own head.

    Agatha felt that in a way she didn’t like.

    She didn’t move from her spot.

    But she judged the hell out of her anyway.

    “You’re gonna make yourself sick,” she muttered through clenched teeth, voice too quiet for anyone but herself. “And I’m not carrying your ass to a hospital.”

    Five minutes passed. Then twenty.

    Agatha still hadn’t moved.

    She leaned against the kitchen counter now, arms folded so tight it felt like she was holding herself together. She’d kicked her boots off earlier but hadn’t gone back to get them. The fan overhead clicked with each slow revolution, but it didn’t help.

    Outside, Rio kept working.

    Her pace had slowed. That much was obvious now. Her movements had shifted from efficient to… careful. Like her limbs had gotten heavier. Like she was measuring every step between breaths.

    Agatha caught herself holding her own.

    Then exhaled hard.

    “This is not my problem,” she muttered. “Ain’t my first rodeo with other people’s bad ideas.”

    But the words didn’t land.

    She pushed off the counter and started pacing, short laps between the sink and the hallway like she might be able to shake off the feeling that had settled into her chest.

    She didn’t care. She didn’t. She was being reasonable. Smart. There was nothing noble about letting someone collapse on her land. That was a lawsuit waiting to happen. Hell, it was a headline waiting to happen. And if something happened to Rio, Agatha would be the one stuck talking to reporters or whatever poor bastard the state sent out to replace her. That was the only reason this mattered.

    That was it.

    And yet, her steps slowed.

    She moved back to the window again, just to look. Just to confirm Rio was still on her feet.

    She was. Barely.

    One hand braced on her thigh. The other curled around a pen that hovered above her notebook, motionless. Her shoulders looked too high, like she couldn’t quite drop them. Her head tilted, face turned slightly down. Not collapsed.

    But not steady.

    Agatha felt something curl tight behind her ribs.

    And she hated it.

    Agatha didn’t stomp.

    She didn’t sigh. Didn’t curse. Didn’t even roll her eyes. She just moved, like gravity had shifted inside her and she didn’t have much choice in the matter anymore. Each step felt deliberate. Mechanical. Like her body had made the decision three heartbeats before her brain caught up.

    She stopped in the mudroom.

    The air was still, warm, tinged with leather and dust and the faint, permanent smell of saddle soap. The racks along the wall were cluttered with ropes, gloves, and half-folded tarps. Three hats hung on the hooks, two stiff, newer, shaped for hard work.

    And one older.

    Soft-brimmed. Faded brown. Frayed at the edges. The one she always reached for when the sun was too cruel for stiff felt and too honest for pretense. It was more fabric than structure now—creased just so, with the kind of bend that only came from years of hard use.

    The one that never quite fit right anymore.

    The one she didn’t wear much but never gave away.

    Agatha stared at it.

    Her arms stayed crossed. Her boots stayed planted.

    She didn’t reach for it.

    Not yet.

    She just stood there, the same way she’d stood at the window. Still. Tense. Unmoving. Watching.

    This was stupid.

    It was stupid to even think about it. Stupid to offer something she couldn’t take back. Stupid to let this mean more than it had to.

    But she knew it would mean more. Knew that offering it—giving something of hers, something used and worn and familiar—wasn’t going to read like an act of neutrality.

    It was going to read like what it was.

    Care.

    Unspoken. Inarguable.

    And Agatha didn’t know what the hell she was supposed to do with that. She rubbed a thumb along the seam of her jeans and took one step closer to the hook.

    Then stopped again.

    And stayed there.

    Undecided. Unmoving.

    But not unaware.

    Not anymore.

    The decision happened before her hands touched the brim.

    One second, Agatha was staring at the hat. The next, it was in her grip, familiar and weather-worn, still holding the faint scent of dust and old effort. She didn’t remember taking it off the hook. Didn’t remember stepping into her boots again. It was all just movement. Instinct. “You don’t hand a hat to a stranger ’less you mean it,” she muttered. Her chest felt too tight.

    The air in the house pressed in around her like a fist. No breeze. No distraction. Nowhere to point her focus except forward, through the door, down the steps, across the yard, and into the heat that hadn’t let up since sunrise.

    She crossed the porch without looking at anything. Without letting herself feel it.

    The screen door slapped shut behind her, sharp in the quiet. She didn’t stop walking.

    Not until she reached the old cedar fence that separated the yard from the field.

    She kept her eyes on the post as she approached it, not the pasture. Not the figure still bent at the edge of the ridge. She didn’t want to look. Didn’t want to give herself the chance to reconsider.

    The hat was heavy in her hand.

    Not in weight. In meaning.

    She hooked it over the post. Careful. Deliberate. Let it rest there, brim tilted slightly down, casting its own shade like it already knew what it was for. She didn’t say anything.

    Didn’t call out.

    Didn’t wave.

    Just turned around and started walking towards the barn.

    No pause. No glance over her shoulder.

    If she looked back, it would be real.

    And she couldn’t have that.

    She made it halfway to the barn before she heard the fence creak. She didn’t stop.

    But she felt it.

    That shift in the air. The tiny noise of movement. The faintest scrape of fingers brushing against sun-warmed wood.

    Rio had taken it.

    Of course she had.

    Agatha didn’t know why that tightened her throat.

    She kept walking, straight into the barn’s dark interior, where the heat was thick but shaded. The smell of hay and sweat and animal dust wrapped around her like an old coat. She paced three steps past the nearest stall before stopping short and pressing her palm flat against the wall. “Well, hell,” she muttered. “There I go, feedin’ a stray with my good hand.” She stayed like that for a while. Breathing.

    Trying not to feel what she already knew.

    Outside, Rio adjusted the chinstrap.

    The hat sat low on her brow now, wide enough to cast shade across her cheekbones, wide enough to cool the back of her neck. She didn’t touch the brim. Didn’t fuss with the fit.

    She just let it sit.

    Kept working.

    Said nothing.

    Like it hadn’t happened.

    Like Agatha hadn’t crossed the yard in silence and left a piece of herself on a fencepost.

    But when she moved to kneel again, it was slower. Her movements steadied. Her writing smoothed. The shadow moved with her, quiet, constant.

    Unspoken.

    Undeniable.

    Agatha stayed in the barn, staring at nothing.

    The muscles in her jaw ached.

    She could still feel the ghost of the hat in her hand, still see it hanging on the fence post like it might burn a hole through the air. She didn’t know what she’d been hoping for.

    She wasn’t sure she’d gotten it.

    But when she stepped outside twenty minutes later to check the troughs and Rio was still there, bent low, focused, the crown of Agatha’s hat cutting the glare from her brow—

    She didn’t say a word.

    She just exhaled.

    Long and slow.

    And told herself it didn’t mean anything.

    Even though it did.

    The first thing Agatha heard was the screen door creaking open. It was soft, almost respectful. Not the way most people entered her house, too careful, too considerate. But Rio was nothing if not quiet. Always moved like she was trying not to take up too much space.

    Agatha didn’t look up.

    She was at the sink, elbow-deep in a half-scrubbed bucket, one hand braced against the metal rim while the other scoured something stubborn out of the bottom. Her shirt stuck to her spine. Her fingers were raw from the heat of the water and the hard-bristled brush. Her jaw had been clenched so long it felt locked.

    Behind her, the kitchen shifted.

    Footsteps crossed the threshold, slow, steady, soft against the old wood floor. No hesitation. No apology. But no demand, either.

    Agatha rinsed the bucket again, the water slapping loud against the tin. The noise filled the space between them like insulation.

    She heard Rio stop moving. Just inside the room.

    Agatha stayed facing the window.

    Said nothing.

    Cast her eyes to her.

    The hat was still on her head.

    Of course it was.

    It was an old thing, that hat. Worn soft, shaped by time and sweat and effort. It didn’t sit straight anymore, tilted slightly forward, brim low enough to cast shadow over the eyes. On Agatha, it looked like muscle memory. On Rio, it looked—

    Wrong.

    But not bad.

    Just meaningful.

    And Agatha hated that she noticed.

    She rinsed her hands, shook off the water, reached for the rag on the hook by the sink. Dried her fingers with more force than necessary. Still didn’t turn around.

    Rio moved again, quiet as anything. She crossed the kitchen in a slow arc, toward the far counter where the water glasses were kept, and filled one from the pitcher. Her movements were easy, practiced, like she’d done it a dozen times, even though this was only her second time in the kitchen. The hat stayed on the whole time.

    Not touched. Not fidgeted with. Not removed out of politeness. Agatha’s pulse thudded once, hard, for no damn reason.

    She turned finally, just enough to glance over.

    Rio leaned against the edge of the counter, one arm crossed over her chest, the other holding the glass. Her braid had started to fray again, pulled loose by the wind and the sweat and the hours in the field. Her shirt clung at the collar, darker in places, damp from heat. Her cheeks were flushed, not as bad as they’d been before, but still blooming faintly red. The hat shaded most of her face.

    But not her eyes.

    They were calm. Watchful.

    And looking straight at her.

    Agatha looked away.

    “I think there’s something unusual growing along the ridge,” Rio said after a stretch of silence.

    Her voice was even. Soft. Like it didn’t want to interrupt anything. Like it wasn’t there to break the quiet, just exist inside it.

    Agatha didn’t answer right away.

    She reached for another bucket. Set it in the sink. Ran the water again. After a beat, she said, “You gonna name it, or keep me guessin’?” “I want to be sure first.”

    “You always this careful?”

    “Yes.”

    “Good. Careful keeps you vertical. Stupid gets you stacked.” That was all.

    Silence again.

    The water ran. A glass clinked gently against the counter. A fly buzzed near the back screen, tapping lightly against the frame like it didn’t realize it was already stuck inside.

    Agatha didn’t ask about the hat.

    Didn’t say anything about it. Didn’t ask if it fit, or if it helped, or if it was going to be returned.

    She didn’t even glance at it again.

    But she knew it was there.

    Could feel the shape of it in the room like a third presence. Could feel the weight of her own decision sitting on Rio’s head like a truth neither of them knew how to carry.

    It had been hers. Now it wasn’t.

    Rio hadn’t asked.

    Agatha hadn’t offered.

    But there it was.

    And now, it was real.

    Eventually, Rio drained the last of her water and set the glass in the sink beside her.

    She turned slightly, nodded once, and said, “Thanks.”

    Agatha didn’t ask what for.

    She just grunted. “Don’t thank me ’til you bring it back.” Then reached for the bucket again, as if the task in front of her mattered more than the one that had already passed between them.

    Rio didn’t wait for anything else.

    She stepped away from the counter, crossed the kitchen with the same slow, steady pace, and disappeared out the screen door like she hadn’t just set fire to Agatha’s morning with a single, quiet word.

    The door clicked shut behind her.

    And the house was too quiet again.

    But the space she’d filled still felt full.

    Chapter End Notes And that’s the chapter where agatha gave Rio her spare hat, didn’t say a word about it, and then stood in her own kitchen literally vibrating with unresolved feelings.

    Also Rio? wore it. Didn’t comment. Didn’t hand it back. Thanked her once.

    That’s it. That's the romance.

    As always, feel free to yell in the comments about the hat, the heat, or the fact that neither of them knows how to handle a single emotion. See y’all in chapter 4!

    Chapter 4: Cows and Soft Things

    Chapter Summary The morning comes in gentler than most, but peace doesn’t last. Brandy the cow takes a shine to Rio, much to Agatha’s disbelief and irritation. What starts as livestock mischief turns into something else entirely when Rio laughs for the first time, leaving Agatha unsettled, jealous, and more affected than she wants to admit.

    Chapter Notes This is the chapter where Agatha gets upstaged by a cow and feels personally victimized about it.

    The hat is still in play. Rio laughs. Agatha’s internal firewall flickers. Nothing is confessed, but everything softens.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes The morning came in soft, for once.

    Not cool, not really, but less cruel. The kind of warmth that settled into your bones instead of slicing through them. A pale breeze threaded through the grass like it had something to say, lifting bits of dust off the porch rail, nudging curls of hay across the barn floor.

    Agatha noticed it the moment she stepped outside.

    She paused on the porch, mug in hand, gaze sweeping the line of fence and field like she did every morning. Nothing urgent. Nothing unusual. The herd was where it should be, roaming slow, heads low, tails flicking with idle annoyance at the occasional fly. The air carried the faint, familiar scent of sweat and earth and something green struggling to grow. And there she was.

    Rio.

    Out in the East pasture, crouched again in her now-usual way: one knee down, the other braced, notebook balanced on her thigh, hat pulled low over her brow. She was nearly motionless except for the occasional tilt of her head or the precise movement of her hand as she wrote something down. To anyone else, she might’ve looked like a rock someone forgot to clear from the field.

    But Agatha’s eyes found her immediately.

    Not that she was looking.

    She leaned against the porch post, coffee mug warm in her grip. Let the morning breathe around her for a while. Let her eyes trace the curve of the land, the movement of the herd, the easy sprawl of cattle soaking in the sun and the one cow already far too interested in something that wasn’t hay. “Goddammit, Brandy. You’re fixin’ to make me gray before my time.” Agatha muttered under her breath.

    The red-speckled heifer had peeled off from the rest of the herd with an almost suspicious amount of intent. Wide-eyed. Curious. Quiet as a ghost. She moved slow, but deliberate, each hoofstep placed with the kind of care that usually meant mischief. Like a cowdog circlin’ chickens when she thinks no one’s watchin’.

    Straight toward Rio.

    Agatha squinted into the field.

    From where she stood, she could see the moment Brandy honed in. Not just a casual wander. Not just grazing.

    No.

    That cow was fixated.

    Rio was still kneeling, head tilted toward the ground, her hand brushing through a patch of fine grass that had no business growing in this soil. She didn’t seem to notice the giant animal making a slow, deliberate approach behind her. Didn’t shift. Didn’t turn.

    Agatha tensed.

    Not from danger. Brandy wasn’t mean. Just… overly familiar. Friendlier than a salesman at a county fair.

    And nosy.

    And far too bold with people who didn’t know how to handle 900 pounds of poorly-disguised chaos.

    Agatha swore again and took a slow sip of her coffee, just to keep from calling out.

    She couldn’t look away.

    Brandy came to a stop two paces behind Rio.
