    She sniffed the satchel slumped in the grass. Nudged it, experimentally. Snorted once, shook her head, and flicked her ear with what could only be described as interest.

    Then, with absolutely no shame, she leaned forward and placed her entire head over Rio’s shoulder like she was trying to read the notebook. Agatha choked on her coffee.

    Rio startled, but only slightly, more like a sharp inhale than a full reaction. She turned her head just enough to catch the heifer’s expression: wide-eyed, blank, curious.

    There was a long pause.

    Rio blinked at her.

    Brandy blinked back.

    Then slowly, thoughtfully, Brandy stuck out her tongue and licked Rio directly on the shoulder.

    Not a quick swipe.

    A committed one.

    From bicep to collarbone.

    Agatha smacked her palm against her forehead.

    “I raise you on good feed, I let you free range, I fix your damn fence every time you kick it down,” she muttered to no one. “And this is how you thank me. Unbelievable.”

    She watched as Rio very gently pushed the cow’s head away and turned back to her sample, like this was just… normal.

    Brandy, undeterred, took one step closer and planted herself firmly in Rio’s personal space, nose near her neck, ears twitching in contentment. Agatha narrowed her eyes.

    “This is ridiculous.”

    The cow had liked Rio. That much was clear.

    And Rio didn’t seem even slightly upset about it.

    That was what bothered her the most, maybe. The way Rio just… accepted it. Took it in stride. Let it happen. Like the cow’s attention was just one more part of the morning.

    Like she belonged here.

    And that—more than the sun, more than the sweat clinging to Agatha’s spine, more than the low thrum of jealousy curling behind her ribs—was something Agatha wasn’t ready to think about.

    Brandy stayed right where she was.

    Not looming. Not aggressive. Just close, that unsettling kind of closeness that only animals and children could get away with. Her big dumb eyes blinked once, twice. She let out a slow exhale, all breath and heat and hay- sweet air, like she was settling in.

    Rio didn’t move.

    Not away. Not forward. Not even to brush the cow off.

    She just looked at her.

    Not alarmed. Not irritated. Just curious.

    And that might’ve been the most unforgivable part.

    Agatha had started on the porch, coffee in hand, hip leaned against the railing, content to observe from a safe distance like she always did. But the second Brandy made her approach, the second that stupid red- speckled traitor peeled off from the herd with a kind of single-minded interest that no cow had any business having, Agatha had set her mug down and walked.

    Casual. Controlled. Completely normal.

    Now she stood at the fence, arms braced on the top rail, jaw tight, the brim of her hat low enough to hide her expression from anyone who might be watching.

    She hadn’t meant to come closer.

    But here she was.

    Watching a goddamn meet-cute unfold between her emotionally unstable cow and the biologist she was absolutely not thinking about at night. “You’re embarrassing yourself,” she muttered to Brandy. “You’re embarrassing me. Carryin’ on like a calf that ain’t been weaned.” Brandy turned her head slightly, as if hearing her from across the pasture. Then she lowered it slow and deliberate and butted the side of Rio’s shoulder with her nose.

    It wasn’t rough.

    It was affectionate.

    Agatha flinched like she had been hit.

    Rio shifted with the impact, let out a breath of startled laughter, and—with actual kindness—lifted a hand to gently pat the side of Brandy’s thick, freckled neck.

    She didn’t push her away.

    She petted her.

    Like they were old friends. Like Brandy wasn’t known to be a clingy, attention-starved menace who once got her head stuck in a feed bucket and dragged it around the yard like a drum for an hour.

    “She’s doing this on purpose.”

    Brandy’s tail flicked lazily.

    Rio murmured something too quiet to carry on the breeze, but her voice had that soft, indulgent lilt to it, like she wasn’t even talking to a cow. Like she was talking to someone who could actually understand her. And worse, someone she didn’t mind being close to.

    The cow pressed in closer.

    The betrayal deepened.

    The second lick came slow.

    Calculated.

    Right up Rio’s arm this time, starting at her elbow and ending with a flick of bovine tongue against her sleeve.

    Agatha watched it happen with a kind of deadpan horror usually reserved for car crashes and wedding toasts that started with “So I’ve known the groom since college.”

    She blinked. Once.

    “Unreal,” she said flatly.

    And it wasn’t even the lick itself. It was the way Rio reacted, like she expected it this time. Like this was simply how her morning was going now. Rio just looked at the cow.

    Tilted her head.

    Raised one brow—one single brow—and said, calm as anything, “That’s really not necessary.”

    Brandy made a low contented sound. Almost smug, if a cow could manage it.

    Rio, still half-knelt in the dirt, reached for her notebook again with her free hand and simply kept writing.

    Like nothing had happened.

    Like she didn’t now carry the scent of spit and treason on her skin. Agatha groaned into her palm.

    “She’s bonded. You’ve bonded. Ain’t that just the way, cow’ll love the hand that don’t feed her. I hope you’re proud of yourself.”

    The fence didn’t respond.

    Neither did Bandit, who had wandered halfway across the yard and now sat like a solemn spectator in the shade, watching this all unfold like it was part of some slow rural opera.

    Agatha exhaled slowly.

    Brandy nosed at Rio’s satchel.

    Rio didn’t stop her.

    Agatha clenched her jaw and hissed through her teeth, “Touch the notebooks and I swear I’ll turn you into jerky, hang your hide on the barn wall, and not lose a wink of sleep.”

    Rio turned then, just a little, just enough to glance toward the fence line and for a second, her gaze brushed Agatha.

    It wasn’t immediate.

    Like she hadn’t expected anyone to be watching. Like she’d only looked out of instinct and was surprised by what she found there. Agatha didn’t move.

    Didn’t speak.

    Their eyes met and held.

    Not long. No more than two, maybe three seconds. But it felt stretched across something wider than time. Like silence could expand to hold everything neither of them had said.

    The air between them shifted.

    The wind stilled. The hum of the pasture dropped out. The herd might as well have disappeared.

    There was no cow.

    No fence.

    Just Agatha, arms folded tight over the top rail, eyes narrow beneath the brim of her hat and Rio, crouching in the field, notebook forgotten at her side, the hat she’d been given throwing just enough shadow over her face to make her unreadable.

    Almost.

    Because her smile gave her away.

    Small. Barely-there. But real. Like a secret she wasn’t sure she was allowed to share, so she tucked it into the corner of her mouth and let it sit there. It wasn’t a smirk.

    It wasn’t cocky.

    It was something warmer than that.

    Softer.

    Agatha felt something go tight behind her ribs—then give. Just a little.

    Just enough to hurt.

    She looked away first.

    Of course she did.

    It wasn’t sudden. Wasn’t sharp. Just slow, like peeling off a sunburn or walking away from something that might’ve felt good if she’d let it. She dropped her gaze to the ground.

    Cleared her throat.

    Then muttered, barely audible, “Disloyal bastard of a cow.” Behind her, Bandit thumped his tail once, like he agreed. She didn’t look back.

    But she didn’t walk away, either.

    It wasn’t supposed to feel like anything.

    That’s what Agatha told herself as she adjusted her stance against the fence, fingers curling around the weatherworn rail, jaw set tight enough to ache. She watched as Rio shifted her weight, lifted her notebook, scribbled something with that same maddening calm.

    And Brandy, the insufferable cow who now seemed convinced she had made a lifelong friend, continued to hover close, like she might start reading over Rio’s shoulder at any moment.

    Agatha should’ve been annoyed.

    She was annoyed.

    But beneath the annoyance, there was a growing unease. A buzz just under her skin.

    Because Rio wasn’t reacting the way people usually did.

    Most folks flinched the second a cow got too close, waved their arms, panicked, tripped over themselves trying to retreat. They didn’t smile. They didn’t pet the damn thing. They didn’t speak to her like she was just another old friend who’d wandered over for a chat.

    But Rio had. Calm. Level. Undisturbed.

    And now she turned her head toward the fence again.

    This time, deliberately.

    Agatha tensed.

    Rio’s eyes were shaded, but Agatha could still feel the weight of them. Still read the slight lift in her brows, the gentleness in her gaze. “She seems friendly,” Rio called, voice low and amused.

    Agatha snorted. “That’s one word for it.”

    Rio reached out and touched Brandy’s shoulder again. Just a brief contact. But not hesitant. Familiar. “She’s not bothering me.”

    “She’s bothering me.”

    The cow let out a low, breathy moo, as if agreeing with Agatha just to be difficult.

    Rio smiled.

    Agatha narrowed her eyes. “You think this is funny?”

    “I think she’s chosen a side.”

    “She shouldn’t have one. She’s a cow.”

    “Well,” Rio said, and there was something in her voice now, something light and teasing and far too at ease, “she seems to disagree.” And then—God help her—Rio laughed.

    Really laughed.

    Not just a huff or a breath through the nose. No. A full, unguarded laugh, the kind that cracked open her face and made her look ten degrees brighter. Her head tipped back just slightly, her shoulders shook, and her voice lifted into something warm and unexpected and beautiful.

    It hit Agatha like a kick to the chest.

    Not because it was loud.

    Not because it was inappropriate.

    But because it was real.

    Because it was the first time she’d heard it.

    And it didn’t sound like it came from someone trying to be polite or professional or liked.

    It sounded like someone who felt safe.

    Agatha’s throat went tight.

    She didn’t know what to do with that.

    She hadn’t even realized she was holding her breath until Rio laughed again, softer now, lingering in the space between them like smoke. “She’s persistent,” Rio said, wiping at her sleeve where Brandy had licked her again.

    Agatha didn’t answer right away.

    She couldn’t.

    Her pulse had climbed to somewhere unfamiliar, her palms felt damp and her tongue had stuck to the roof of her mouth.

    She forced herself to swallow. “She’s a menace.”

    Rio gave the cow a gentle push. “A lovable one.”

    “That’s what she wants you to think.”

    “You’re very protective,” Rio said, and this time there was a note in her voice Agatha couldn’t name—soft but steady. Observing her. Testing her, maybe.

    Agatha scoffed. “She’s my cow. Born and raised right here, meanin’ she oughta know better than to go moonin’ over strangers.”

    “Of course.”

    “I feed her, water her, patch up every damn fence she busts through, she’s licked me exactly three times in her whole life. Three. You, meanwhile, waltz in and she acts like you hung the moon.”

    Rio bit back another laugh. “Maybe you’re not her type.” “Oh, she has a type now?”

    Rio met her eyes again. “Apparently, yes.”

    Agatha blinked.

    And in that pause—sharp and awful and intimate—something inside her cracked just slightly sideways.

    Because it wasn’t just the joke. It wasn’t just the way Rio kept her voice gentle, or how her face relaxed when she was being teased. It was the fact that she was still looking at her.

    Like she meant it.

    Like Agatha was the only one here.

    Like she enjoyed her.

    Agatha looked away.

    Fast.

    Back toward the barn, toward anything but that smile.

    “Cows are traitorous,” she muttered.

    Rio grinned. “Sounds like a personal problem.”

    Agatha clenched her jaw again, but the edge was gone. Replaced by something else, something softer. Something she didn’t have the tools for. So she didn’t reply.

    Didn’t push.

    Didn’t run, either.

    She stayed.

    Gripping the fence rail like it might anchor her to the dirt. White-knuckled, the way you hang on when a bronc starts buckin’ mean. Heart thudding like someone had shaken it loose from where it usually slept. She told herself it didn’t mean anything.

    Just a laugh.

    Just a cow.

    Just the sun playin’ tricks, like a mirage on a long hot trail. But deep down, she already knew better.

    The sun was higher by the time Agatha made it back to the south pasture. The breeze had started to fade, and the sweat between her shoulder blades clung thicker than she liked. She kept her hands moving, hauling feed buckets, checking water levels, pulling a piece of baling twine loose from a tangled fencepost.

    Keep busy. Keep moving.

    If her hands were working, her thoughts stayed quieter.

    That was the rule.

    And she stuck to it. Until she heard the gravel crunch behind her. Footsteps.

    Slow, steady. Not urgent. Not heavy enough to be a neighbor, not light enough to be ignored.

    She didn’t turn right away.

    Instead, she twisted the length of wire she was holding into place and tugged it hard enough to hear it bite. The metal sang low in the heat. She brushed her arm across her brow, leaving a streak of sweat and dust across her forehead.

    Then finally she turned.

    Rio stood just inside the fence line.

    Same boots. Same notebook tucked under her arm. Same posture relaxed but alert, always a little like she was seeing more than she let on. Agatha tried not to let her face say anything about it.

    She failed.

    “You lost?” she asked gruffly.

    Rio blinked once, then smiled. “No.”

    “Then what’re you doin’ out here?”

    “Following your traitorous livestock.”

    Agatha huffed. “Don’t flatter yourself. Brandy follows anything that looks like it might feed her or tell her she’s pretty.”

    “I’ve done neither.”

    “Yet she still picked you.”

    Rio stepped closer. Not all the way, just a few slow steps through the patchy grass, stopping a comfortable distance away. Not too close. Not too far. Just close enough that Agatha could make out the faint pink sunburn still blooming at the edge of her collarbone.

    “You working?” Rio asked.

    “No, I’m dancing,” Agatha deadpanned, yanking another knot free from the post. “What’s it look like?”

    Rio’s smile tilted. “Looks like you’re avoiding me.”

    That made Agatha pause.

    Just for a second.

    Just long enough for the wire to slip under her glove and bite at her palm. She swore under her breath. Turned away again. Focused on the knot. “Ain’t got time to avoid anybody. I’ve got things that need doing.” Rio didn’t press.

    Didn’t apologize.

    She just stood there calm, quiet, content to watch.

    The silence stretched.

    And the longer it did, the louder it felt.

    Not uncomfortable. Just full.

    Weighted.

    Like something had changed and neither of them knew how to name it yet. Agatha finished with the wire and stepped back, letting her gloves hang loose from her fingertips. “You need somethin’?”

    “No.”

    “You sure?”

    “Yes.”

    Another silence.

    The kind that buzzed under the skin.

    Agatha glanced sideways and caught her again—Rio, standing there with her shoulders relaxed, sunlight glinting off her glasses, the corner of her mouth still curled like she knew something Agatha didn’t. “You’re still… here,” Agatha said before she could stop herself. Rio didn’t pretend to misunderstand. “It’s quiet out here.” Agatha grunted. “Don’t get used to it. The fence breaks every other week.” “I like the quiet.”

    “I don’t.”

    “I noticed.”

    Agatha hesitated. Then nodded once, short and sharp.

    Another beat passed.

    Rio tilted her head. “Want me gone?”

    Agatha looked at her.

    Really looked.

    Then shook her head. “Nah. You’re fine.”

    She didn’t say: Stay.

    She didn’t say: I don’t mind this.

    But she didn’t have to.

    The silence between them said everything.

    Rio stepped back. Not far. Just a couple of paces. Enough to turn toward the ridge again, where the herd had started drifting toward the low shade. Agatha watched her go.

    Watched the way the wind caught the tail of her braid. The way her shadow stretched long across the ground. The way her presence didn’t feel like a disruption.

    She didn’t stop her.

    Didn’t call her back.

    Just stood there with her gloves hanging loose, heart beating in a rhythm she didn’t want to name, and let herself feel it for just a moment. Not the heat.

    Not the ache in her shoulders.

    Just the quiet.

    Just the quiet. And the soft thing growin’ in it, quiet as grass after rain, stubborn as mesquite roots.

    Chapter End Notes And that’s the chapter where Agatha got out-charisma’d by a cow and then emotionally steamrolled by someone standing three feet away just… existing.

    There’s no talking about the soft thing yet. No touching. No admitting. Just Agatha fixing fences and pretending she doesn’t feel something in her chest shift when Rio interacts with her.

    Anyway, It's fine. They’re fine.

    Thanks for reading!

    Chapter 5: A Question of Quiet

    Chapter Summary Agatha wanted nothing but quiet and routine. A morning in the barn, the comfort of her mare, the rhythm she could control.

    But when Rio walks in, and later, when a cut forces her to stay still and Agatha realizes some wounds don’t bleed the way you expect. Chapter Notes Welcome back to rural emotional repression.

    This one’s got Agatha being tender when no one’s supposed to be looking, Rio seeing way too much, and a minor injury that turns into major feelings (but no one’s admitting that). Expect long silences, unexpected softness, and agatha going into fight-or-flight over someone being nice to her.

    Anyway, thanks for being here. Let’s go ruin Agatha’s whole emotional equilibrium with one bandage.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes The barn was barely cooler than outside, but she took the quiet where she could get it.

    She hadn’t meant to spend this long in here. Just a quick check-in. Brushing out Honey’s coat before the heat climbed too high. The mare was patient, but her coat clumped in the summer if you didn’t stay on top of it and the last thing Agatha needed was a preventable rash turning into another vet bill.

    The brush moved in long, even strokes. Over the shoulder. Down the neck. Just behind the ear where Honey liked it best.

    She didn’t have to think about it. Her hands knew the rhythm, the weight and pace of it.

    “Good girl,” she murmured, more breath than voice. “Yeah, I know. Too hot lately. You’re not the only one sick of it.”

    Honey flicked an ear in response, eyes half-lidded and content. Agatha glanced around the barn once—automatically—then went back to brushing. No one was around. No footsteps. No voices. Just the low rustle of hay and the soft creak of leather. Peaceful.

    She let her guard down a little more.

    “I didn’t forget you yesterday,” she said, just under her breath. “Been too many damn things to do, not enough hours.”

    The horse bumped her hip gently with her nose.

    Agatha huffed out something like a laugh. “Pushy brat. You miss me or just the brushing?”

    Honey snorted. She always had been more dog than horse.

    Agatha reached up to adjust the bridle and paused, just for a second, to let her hand rest there, steady, familiar. It was the kind of contact she didn’t let herself have with anyone else. Not often. Not easily.

    She stayed like that longer than she should have.

    Because in here, in the quiet, it didn’t feel like weakness. It felt like breathing.

    “You’re lucky,” she muttered. “You don’t have to deal with people. You’d hate ’em.” The mare blinked slow, tail swishing lazily behind her. Agatha dropped her hand, leaned back just a little.

    “They’d probably hate you back.” she said softly. “You don’t pretend enough.”

    She didn’t mean to say it aloud.

    But it came out anyway.

    And that’s when she felt it.

    That tightening.

    That shift in the air. A presence behind her.

    Not the horse. Not Bandit.

    Something else, watching.

    Her spine went rigid.

    She didn’t spin around, wouldn’t give whoever it was the satisfaction of seeing her startle, but her whole body snapped back into place like a door slamming shut.

    She turned, slow and controlled, fingers still tight around the brush. And there she was.

    Rio.

    Standing just inside the barn’s open door, notebook under one arm, head tilted slightly like she’d been there longer than Agatha wanted to believe. Silent. Unapologetic. Just there, watching.

    Agatha’s throat went dry, jaw locking.

    She hated how her heart lurched.

    Hated that it wasn’t fear.

    It was something else.

    Something far too close to being known.

    Her eyes narrowed. Shoulders squared.

    “You need somethin’?” she asked, flat and cold.

    But the quiet between them didn’t shake.

    Rio didn’t flinch.

    Which somehow made it worse.

    Agatha didn’t move.

    Not even an inch.

    She stood with the brush still in her hand, Honey’s flank warm beside her, and stared at Rio like she could set her on fire just by thinking hard enough. And still the woman didn’t flinch.

    She just stood there, steady and quiet in the barn’s open mouth, with her hands at her sides and that unreadable look on her face. Not smug. Not startled. Just… present.

    Too present.

    Like she wasn’t sorry and didn’t need to be.

    Agatha’s jaw clenched. “You need somethin’?” she repeated, sharper this time, like tone alone could roll the clock back.

    But Rio didn’t blink. Didn’t shift.

    She just gave the tiniest tilt of her head, like she was still considering the question. Then, finally, her voice came, low and even.

    “You said I could borrow the wire cutters. Yesterday.”

    Right. She had.

    Tired and distracted, she’d waved in the vague direction of the barn with a grunt and a half-mumbled, “They’re in the toolbox. Left side.” “Already checked.”

    Agatha narrowed her eyes. “So you figured you’d ask while I was in the middle of somethin’?”

    “Door was open.”

    “You could’ve knocked.”

    “You could’ve locked it.”

    That shut her up for a second.

    Agatha clenched her jaw and went back to brushing, not because Honey needed it, not anymore, but because it gave her hands something to do. Something to hold. Something to press against.

    The mare leaned into her like nothing had changed, unaware of the woman a few feet away watching Agatha like something gentle.

    Agatha hated that, too.

    “You always this mouthy?” she asked after a beat, still not turning around. “Only when it works.” Rio said, without missing a step.

    Agatha let out a breath that was half a scoff, half surrender. She didn’t know if Rio was teasing or serious, probably both. Probably didn’t care how the words landed, so long as they were honest. She rubbed a little too hard just behind Honey’s ear. The mare didn’t seem to mind, just blinked slow and nudged her shoulder again, like she could feel Agatha winding too tight.

    “She don’t like strangers,” Agatha said, low.

    “Didn’t seem to mind me.”

    “She’s picky.”

    “Smart, then.”

    Agatha’s shoulders twitched.

    “She’s spoiled,” she muttered. “Doesn’t mean she knows who’s worth trustin’.”

    “I think she does.”

    That landed heavy. Too heavy.

    Agatha paused, her hand hovering over Honey’s flank.

    She swallowed the feeling—whatever it was—down fast and forced herself back into motion. She hung the brush up harder than necessary, metal clanging as it hit the hook.

    Behind her, Rio stepped farther in, not close enough to invade, but close enough to count.

    And that felt like something else entirely.

    Agatha turned around, arms crossing on instinct. Not because she was cold —God, she was burning—but because she didn’t know where else to put the ache gathering behind her ribs.

    “You always watch people like that?” she asked, brows low. “Only when they think no one’s watching,” Rio said easily. Agatha’s throat tightened.

    She wasn’t sure if it was anger or something worse.

    “You’ve got a habit of sayin’ too much,” she said stiffly. “You’ve got a habit of not saying enough.”

    The silence after that was thick. It hung in the rafters, sank into her boots. Agatha didn’t break eye contact, but she hated that Rio didn’t look away either.

    They just stood there, not arguing, not even talking anymore. Just existing too close, in too small a space, with too many words piled between them that neither of them wanted to touch.

    “You gonna get the damn wire cutters or not?” Agatha finally asked, voice a little rougher now.

    Rio nodded toward the far stall. “I will.”

    Neither of them moved.

    Honey gave another nudge against Agatha’s shoulder and let out a soft snort, like she could feel the tension too. Or maybe she just wanted more brushing.

    Agatha didn’t brush her away.

    She stood there, arms crossed, pulse in her ears…

    Pretending she wasn’t secretly glad Rio hadn’t left.

    That she wasn’t relieved someone had seen her quiet, soft moment and didn’t mock it.

    Didn’t flinch.

    Didn’t ruin it.

    Just… saw it.

    And stayed.

    And worse, that a small, selfish part of her was glad she had. Agatha didn’t move.

    Neither did Rio.

    And that was the problem.

    Because the silence between them had shifted. It wasn’t jagged anymore. Wasn’t cold or cutting or full of the kind of sharp edges Agatha had learned to wield like armor. It was quiet in a way that felt almost… still. Level. And worse, safe.

    She hated that.

    Rio wasn’t even looking at her now. Not directly. She’d turned slightly, posture loose and casual, gaze sweeping the far side of the barn like she had all the time in the world. Like there wasn’t tension buzzing in the walls. Like she hadn’t just caught Agatha whispering soft things to a horse like she was trying to convince herself she was still human.

    Agatha shifted her stance. Just slightly. One boot scuffing over the dirt floor, arms still folded too tight across her chest.

    Honey nosed at her again, her big warm face pressing lightly into the curve of Agatha’s shoulder.

    She didn’t shove her away.

    Didn’t even flinch.

    Just stood there and let herself be leaned on.

    Because it was easier than speaking.

    Easier than looking back at the woman standing six feet away like she didn’t know exactly what kind of moment this was.

    Rio finally spoke, voice low, unhurried. “She’s fond of you.” Agatha didn’t look up. “That’s because I feed her.”

    “She’s not the only one.”

    Agatha’s head snapped toward her.

    Rio met her gaze evenly. No smile. No judgment. Just calm observation, like she was stating the weather.

    The words sat heavy in the space between them. Agatha didn’t know what to do with them. She wanted to push them away. Scoff. Say something biting. Something that would reset the balance of power. Something that would make Rio leave.

    But her tongue felt heavy in her mouth, and all that came out was, “You think you know me?”

    “No.”

    That stopped her cold.

    “No?” she repeated, squinting at her.

    Rio shook her head, slow. “I just think you’re more than what you let people see.”

    Agatha swallowed hard. Her throat felt tight again.

    “You don’t know what I’ve let people see.”

    “I know what I saw.”

    That should’ve scared her.

    It did, if she was honest.

    Because she hadn’t meant for anyone to catch that moment. That soft voice. That easy touch. That part of her that only came out when the doors were shut and the only living things around her couldn’t hold it against her. “You don’t know what that means,” she said, voice flat.

    “I think I do.”

    Agatha looked away. Not because she was done, but because she couldn’t stand the way Rio didn’t look away at all.

    They stood in that quiet for too long. Not frozen. Just… suspended. Like the whole barn had become its own pocket of stillness, cut off from the sun and the herd and the world outside.

    Dust drifted in the shafts of light. The horse shifted her weight. The wind creaked faintly through a loose panel in the roof.

    But neither of them moved.

    And it hit Agatha all at once, that she wasn’t used to being seen like this and not being asked to explain it.

    That someone could look at her softness and just… let it be. Not demand more. Not push.

    Just see it. And stay.

    She almost told her to go.

    The words were right there on the edge of her tongue. A sharp, tired “You’ve got what you came for, haven’t you?” But they didn’t come out. Instead, she just breathed out slow.

    “Cutters’re in the bottom of the green box,” she said finally, voice low. Rio nodded. “Thanks.”

    She didn’t move right away.

    Didn’t rush.

    She turned her head and looked at the wall of tools near the stall. Walked slowly—no noise, no hurry—and crouched to open the box like this was just another morning chore.

    Agatha turned back to Honey, but her hands were still tight. She stared at the mare’s mane for a long moment, then reached up and brushed one last strand away from her eye.

    “Greedy,” she murmured.

    Honey blinked at her, content.

    Behind her, Rio stood again, cutters in hand. But she didn’t head for the door.

    Not yet.

    Agatha felt her pause.

    Like she wanted to say something else.

    She didn’t.

    Neither did Agatha.

    Another heartbeat. Another breath.

    Then Rio gave the smallest nod and turned, walking slowly out of the barn and into the sun.

    And Agatha was left standing there with dust in her lungs and something she didn’t want to name blooming behind her ribs.

    She didn’t move for a long time.

    Because something had shifted.

    And she wasn’t sure she wanted it to shift back.

    She stayed in the barn long after Rio left.

    Not doing anything. Just… standing.

    Honey had stopped nudging her. Had gone back to her feed like nothing had happened. Like Agatha’s chest didn’t feel too tight, like her arms weren’t crossed out of sheer survival instinct.

    Eventually, she moved. Slowly. Mechanically.

    The back gate needed closing. It always stuck halfway open, and if you didn’t latch it just right, the wind would throw it wide and the whole damn feed room would be full of curious livestock before noon. Agatha grabbed the latch without looking. Pulled it the way she always did —hard, fast, because it never budged easy.

    But this time, something caught.

    A sliver of rusted metal, maybe. Or a bent nail she hadn’t seen before. Whatever it was, it bit deep into the side of her palm as she yanked, splitting skin like old paper.

    The pain was sharp and instant.

    She hissed through her teeth and drew her hand back fast, blood already pooling along the ridge of her palm.

    “Goddammit—”

    The word came out low, more habit than anger.

    She clenched her fist on instinct—wrong move. Blood welled faster, warm and sticky, curling toward her wrist in lazy little trails. She stood there, hand raised, heart pounding with a stupid rush of adrenaline she didn’t ask for.

    It wasn’t a bad cut. She knew that. She’d had worse.

    But the timing—the timing—felt like a cosmic joke. Like the universe saw her standing in that barn, emotions still scrambled from being seen too clearly, and decided to throw in a little blood just for flavor. “Shit,” she said.

    She turned toward the wall where the old med kit was stashed, half-thinking she’d just patch it herself and move on. She didn’t need help. Didn’t want it. Just gauze, pressure, done.

    But the barn door creaked again behind her.

    Her stomach dropped.

    She didn’t even have to turn to know who it was.

    Rio’s voice was already there, quiet but firm. “What happened?” Agatha clenched her jaw. “It’s fine.”

    “You’re bleeding.”

    “I know.”

    A pause.

    “Let me see.”

    Agatha turned slowly, one shoulder squared, blood sliding down her palm in lazy red curves.

    “Said it’s fine.”

    Rio didn’t argue.

    She didn’t step back either.

    She moved in slow, measured steps, like approaching a spooked animal, and stopped just within reach.

    “I believe you,” she said, voice steady. “But let me look anyway.” Agatha wanted to argue. To yank her hand away. To tell her to back off and mind her own damn business.

    But she didn’t.

    Because something in Rio’s voice wasn’t patronizing.

    It wasn’t pity.

    It was calm.

    That same calm that had made Agatha nervous earlier. That still made her nervous.

    She exhaled hard and lowered her hand a fraction.

    Rio reached out carefully and caught her wrist in gentle, sure fingers. Her skin was cool. Her touch was firm but light.

    Agatha hated how still she had to stand just to keep from jerking away. “It’s not deep,” Rio said after a moment, examining the cut. “But it’ll sting like hell if you don’t clean it.”

    Agatha shrugged, trying not to look directly at her. “Not my first scrape.” “Still deserves to be taken care of.”

    That stopped her.

    Agatha blinked.

    It wasn’t what Rio said. It was how she said it, calm, practical, like this wasn’t about weakness or fussing. Just care.

    Simple. Earnest.

    And that was worse.

    That was so much worse.

    Rio reached for the canteen on her hip and unhooked it without letting go of her wrist. She poured a slow stream of water over the wound, and Agatha hissed through clenched teeth.

    “I warned you,” Rio murmured.

    Agatha glared at her. “You enjoy this?”

    “No,” she said, mouth twitching into something that might have been a smile. “But you’re a terrible patient.”

    Agatha didn’t argue.

    She was too busy pretending to breathe normally.

    Rio pulled a clean cloth from her bag and folded it into a tight square. Pressed it gently to the cut, then wrapped it in slow, neat movements, like she’d done this before. Like it was second nature.

    Agatha kept her eyes on the far wall. It was easier that way. But the weight of Rio’s hands stayed with her.

    Grounding. Unsettling. Kind.

    She didn’t know what to do with that.

    When it was done, Rio tied the cloth off with a clean knot. Then stepped back.

    Agatha flexed her fingers experimentally. The cloth tugged, snug and warm. “You okay?” Rio asked.

    Agatha didn’t answer right away.

    She wasn’t sure she could.

    Her throat felt tight again, but not from pain.

    So she nodded once.

    Short. Grudging.

    Rio accepted it without pushing.

    “Let me know if it reopens,” she said. Then, softer: “Please.” And then—quietly, cleanly—she turned and left.

    Leaving Agatha in the barn, heartbeat loud in her ears, hand bandaged, and spine locked too straight for comfort.

    She looked down at her fingers.

    Flexed them again.

    And for the first time in a long time, she wasn’t sure what hurt and what didn’t.

    The barn was quiet again.

    Rio’s footsteps had faded into nothing. The door creaked once behind her, then eased shut with the breeze.

    And Agatha was still standing in the middle of it, hand cradled loosely against her ribs, spine too straight, chest too full.

    She flexed her fingers again.

    The cloth pulled gently around her palm. Firm. Warm. Still damp at the edges from where the water had soaked through. She could feel the heat of her skin under it, the memory of Rio’s fingers—precise and careful— wrapping it tight.

    It didn’t even hurt anymore.

    Though it felt like it should.

    She looked down at her hand and scowled at it, like that would make the feeling go away.

    It didn’t.

    It just sat there, heavy in her chest, filling her up with something she didn’t have words for.

    Rio hadn’t said anything dramatic, hadn’t fussed or scolded. She’d just seen her bleeding and acted, no hesitation, no question, no expectation of thanks.

    And that?

    That was worse than any wound.

    That was something Agatha didn’t know how to hold.

    She stepped back toward the wall and leaned against it, her uninjured hand bracing on the old wood. Honey let out a soft exhale nearby, chewing lazily, tail flicking. The same as always.

    But she wasn’t.

    Agatha wasn’t the same anymore. Not after this morning. Not after that quiet moment at the horse’s side. Not after the brush, the silence, the not- looking-away.

    Not after that goddamn bandage.

    She let her head tip back against the beam behind her. Closed her eyes. Just for a second.

    The cool of the wood pressed into her scalp. The sounds of the barn filled in around her, gentle hoofsteps, birds outside, the faint rustle of hay. Nothing urgent. Nothing sharp.

    And underneath it all, the memory of Rio’s hands. Her voice. The steady, grounding weight of her presence.

    Let me look anyway.

    Agatha had wanted to say no. She always wanted to say no. But she hadn’t.

    And Rio hadn’t pushed, hadn’t taken advantage, hadn’t turned it into some moment of pity or power.

    She’d just… cared.

    Quietly.

    Cleanly.

    Like it was no big thing.

    Agatha hated how much it was.

    She opened her eyes again, blinking up at the rafters, then down at her hand.

    The cloth was white, already smudged pink with blood. Neatly tied, efficient and kind.

    She exhaled slow and dropped her hand back to her side.

    Didn’t unwrap it.

    Didn’t move.

    Just stood there and let the feeling sit, sharp and tender and stupidly, stupidly warm.

    Because care was dangerous.

    Care was a thing that could be used against you. She’d learned that lesson young, and she’d learned it deep.

    But now?

    Now it was stitched across her hand like a quiet promise. And she didn’t know what the hell to do with it.

    Chapter End Notes And that’s the chapter where Agatha gets caught being soft, gets gently patched up, and somehow survives both.

    This one is all quiet tension and accidental care and the kind of silence that says way more than words ever could. Agatha's doing great (she’s not). Rio’s just existing like a calm breeze that accidentally knocks over every emotional wall agatha’s ever built.

    Thanks for reading!

    Chapter 6: The Storm Breaks Quiet

    Chapter Summary The sky turns wrong long before it turns dark.

    When the storm finally breaks, Agatha finds Rio still in the field soaked, shivering, stubborn as ever. Dragging her inside should’ve ended it, but the silence that follows feels heavier than the rain. Some storms don’t shout, they settle.

    Chapter Notes And now we enter the storm chapter. Literally and emotionally. This one’s got thunder, concern-aggressiveness, a tragic notebook, and two emotionally constipated lesbians drinking black coffee while pretending not to fall in love. Agatha offers dry clothes like it’s a felony. Rio does some quiet house-snooping. No one says anything meaningful but also they say everything.

    Enjoy the rain. And the tension. And the firelight that’s definitely not a metaphor.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes It started quiet.

    The kind of quiet that crept in so slow you didn’t notice it until it was too quiet, until even the wind held its breath and the cicadas stopped their whining in the trees. Until the horses in the paddock turned their ears toward the horizon and didn’t twitch them back again.

    Agatha stood on the porch, one shoulder leaned against the post, thumbs hooked in the front pockets of her jeans. She wasn’t fidgeting. Not exactly. Just… shifting her weight more than usual.

    The sky wasn’t angry yet, just wrong.

    Off-color. Washed out. Like the world had gone pale with tension. The clouds weren’t dramatic, not the kind you’d paint or chase. Just a low haze building at the edges dark and heavy and quiet. The way you could feel a person getting mad before they raised their voice. That kind of sky. Agatha squinted into it, scanning the line of trees past the ridge. Nothing moved.

    No rustling leaves. No birds overhead. No buzz of bugs. The grass in the field didn’t dance in the wind the way it usually did in the early evening. Because there wasn’t wind anymore, just weight.

    The air sat thick on her skin, humid and still, like a blanket pulled too tight. It pressed down on her shoulders. Slowed her breath without permission. She exhaled slowly through her nose, and muttered, “Well, hell.” Back inside, the radar confirmed what her gut already knew. Or it tried to.

    Agatha hated the damn thing. Useless more often than not. It showed a mild patch of weather maybe twenty miles north, a few green streaks barely touching the outer edge of the county. No red. No yellow. Nothing worth a warning.

    But she knew better than to trust it.

    She tapped the screen anyway. Watched the little blip crawl eastward and darken slightly. Glanced out the window.

    The sky looked darker now.

    More certain.

    She clicked the radar off and let the screen go black.

    The kind of storms that scared her weren’t the ones painted in red on a map, they were the quiet kind. Slow. Patient. The ones that roared only once they were already overhead.

    She turned back toward the door, rolling her sleeves up as she went. The good flannel. The one soft with age and patched at the elbow. Her fingers moved without thinking, tightening the cinch before a ride, muscle memory from years of doing this alone. Preparing without panic. Not for anyone else’s benefit, just because it needed doing.

    Except this time, someone else was out there.

    She paused at the threshold, brow creasing.

    Rio hadn’t come back in yet.

    She should’ve. By now, she should’ve.

    They hadn’t set a schedule. There weren’t rules for this. Rio came and went on her own time, tracked her samples, chased her observations. She was quiet, independent, self-contained. Agatha respected that. Maybe a little too much.

    She muttered something sharp and stepped back onto the porch. The air hit different now, cooler at the edges, like a cold front threading through something warm. It slipped down the back of her neck and made the hairs on her arms stand up.

    She looked to the pasture again.

    Still nothing.

    No movement.

    No sign of her.

    Rio usually stayed close to the far field. That’s where she’d found most of her plant samples, she said. Some invasive species she seemed weirdly excited about. Agatha didn’t ask questions, she figured enthusiasm was harmless enough, but she also figured the woman would have enough sense to get inside when the sky started fixin’ to throw a punch like this. She hadn’t.

    Agatha glanced at the porch clock. Did the math without meaning to. Rio had been out there for over an hour. Maybe longer.

    The pressure behind her ribs pulled tighter.

    She’s fine, Agatha told herself.

    Then, less certain: Probably fine.

    But “probably” wasn’t good enough.

    Not with a sky like that and a wind like this and a woman too damn calm about her own safety.

    A sudden gust of wind blew across the porch, sharp and cold. The screen door creaked on its hinge. A few loose leaves danced across the ground and scattered into the field like they were running ahead of something. Agatha swore again, quiet but final.

    She grabbed her old canvas jacket from the hook, threw it over her flannel, and shoved her hat low over her brow. Her boots were already on. Had been all morning.

    Without another word, without even thinking to yell her name, she stepped off the porch and headed out.

    The air was heavier with every step.

    That quiet kind of electricity started to build, the sort that crawled up the back of your neck and made your jaw clench without warning. She didn’t like the way the trees looked now, still and shadowed, like they were waiting for something they couldn’t stop.

    The first rumble of thunder cracked low and distant, barely more than a growl.

    Agatha didn’t flinch.

    She just moved faster.

    Her boots sank slightly into the slick gumbo near the edge of the pasture. The storm wasn’t overhead yet, but it was close. She could feel the shape of it now. Could feel the distance shrinking.

    And somewhere out there, somewhere in all that silence, Rio was still crouched in the grass, chasing leaves like a damn fool, probably too focused to notice the sky turning black behind her.

    The knot in Agatha’s chest pulled tighter.

    It wasn’t panic.

    Not quite.

    It was something sharper.

    Something worse.

    It was care.

    Uninvited. Unwelcome.

    But rooted too deep now to ignore.

    “Goddamn it, Rio,” she muttered, scanning the ridge line again. And then, just barely, she saw a flash of movement near the edge of the field.

    A figure.

    Small.

    Still.

    And not moving fast enough.

    Agatha’s breath caught.

    And the sky cracked open.

    The first crack of thunder hadn’t even finished rolling before the rain started.

    No gentle build-up. No warning drops.

    It came all at once—a real frog-strangler—sharp, slanted,, cold as hell. Wind slicing sideways through the field, driving water into Agatha’s skin like needles. The kind of downpour that soaked through everything in seconds, whether you were ready for it or not.

    Agatha didn’t slow down.

    She didn’t even flinch when the cold hit her collarbone and slid down her back, carving a line of ice between her shoulder blades.

    Because she’d seen her.

    A figure, just beyond the ridge, still and hunched like someone trying to shield something instead of themselves.

    And that someone was Rio.

    Agatha pushed forward through the rain, half-running now, boots slipping on the wet grass, breath short with a mix of exertion and pure disbelief. Of course she was out here.

    Of course she hadn’t noticed the weather turning until it was already overhead.

    And of course she wasn’t moving fast enough.

    Agatha came over the ridge, wind whipping her hair into her mouth, and finally saw her clearly.

    Rio was crouched low near the edge of the field, one hand gripping a soaked notebook, the other bracing herself against the ground. Her hair was plastered to the sides of her face, and her shoulders were drawn tight, not from panic, but like she hadn’t even realized she was cold yet. Her glasses were streaked with water. Her clothes clung to her in heavy folds. She didn’t look scared.

    She looked annoyed.

    Like the storm was an inconvenience, not a problem.

    Somehow, that made Agatha angrier than fear would have.

    She stopped just a few feet away, rain pounding between them like a curtain.

    “Are you kiddin’ me?” she barked over the wind.

    Rio looked up, slow. Blinked water from her lashes. “What?” Agatha stared at her. For a second, she didn’t know what to say. The sight of her—soaked and shivering and weirdly calm—scrambled something behind her ribs.

    Then she recovered.

    “Get up,” she snapped. “You’ll catch somethin’ dumbass.”

    Rio blinked at her. “It’s just water.”

    “You’re soaked through.”

    “I noticed.”

    Agatha stepped closer, pulling her jacket off and tossing it over Rio’s shoulders before she could argue. It hung awkwardly, oversized, already damp, but it was warmer than whatever she was wearing.

    Rio didn’t shrug it off.

    Didn’t thank her, either.

    Just sat there, rain dripping from her nose. “It’s not that cold.” Agatha nearly choked. “Are you out of your mind? You’re shiverin’ like a newborn calf.”

    “I’m fine.”

    “You’re not.”

    “I don’t mind rain.”

    “This ain’t about mindin’ it,” Agatha growled, already reaching down and yanking her up by the elbow. “It’s about hypothermia, and common sense, and you bein’ too stubborn to come inside when you should’ve.” Rio didn’t resist. Didn’t argue. She stood slowly, the notebook clutched in one hand like it mattered more than her body temperature. Agatha stared at it. “That thing’s ruined.”

    Rio glanced down. “The ink might still hold.”

    “Jesus.”

    “Don’t worry,” she said, flicking wet hair from her face. “I don’t expect you to understand fieldwork.”

    Agatha’s jaw locked.

    She wanted to snap back. She could’ve. About how the land wasn’t just a lab. About how people who lived out here had to read the sky like scripture and knew better than to ignore a thunderhead when it came calling. But she didn’t.

    Because Rio looked miserable.

    And for all her calm, all her too-even voice, all her casual deflections, her fingers were shaking, just enough to catch a horseman’s eye. Agatha saw it. Clear as anything.

    That subtle tremble along the spine of her hand. The way she kept shifting her weight, like she was trying to hide how stiff she was. The line of tension in her jaw. Not fear. Not even discomfort.

    Just cold.

    Agatha let out a long breath through her nose and muttered, “C’mon.” Rio didn’t argue this time.

    She followed.

    They walked in silence across the field, the rain making their path slick and uneven. Agatha stayed close without meaning to, her jacket still hanging awkwardly off Rio’s shoulders, her steps slightly ahead but never too far. The wind pulled at them, relentless. Lightning lit the sky in irregular pulses. The house sat like a shadow on the horizon, too far away and not far enough.

    Agatha kept glancing over her shoulder, not that she’d admit it. Just making sure Rio hadn’t stopped. That she was still upright. Still shivering.

    Still quiet.

    And somehow that silence—the not-complaining, the not-asking for help— was worse than anything else.

    Because Agatha didn’t know what to do with someone who refused to be fragile.

    Didn’t know what to do with someone who looked like hell and still said I’m fine like it meant something.

    She bit the inside of her cheek.

    Focused on the house ahead.

    The jacket hung between them, something neither of them would talk about. By the time they reached the porch, Agatha could feel the weight of the rain in every thread of her clothes.

    Her flannel was plastered to her back. Her jeans clung to her legs like second skin. The hat had done its best, but her hair was soaked through and sticking to her neck. Everything itched. Everything ached. But none of that mattered.

    Not compared to the sound of Rio’s wet boots behind her. Slow. Measured. Dragging a little on the last step.

    Agatha didn’t say a word as she opened the door.

    Just shoved it wider and stepped inside, leaving muddy prints across the threshold.

    Rio followed.

    Didn’t hesitate.

    Didn’t look surprised.

    But when she crossed over the worn wood floor, something in her posture shifted.

    She paused just past the doorway.

    Dripping. Quiet. Still wrapped in Agatha’s jacket like it belonged to her now.

    And her eyes?

    They moved.

    Not frantically. Not with open curiosity. Just… with attention. The same kind she used when examining plants in the field. Intent and quiet and total. Agatha felt it like heat behind her ribs.

    Because Rio had been in the house before. Technically.

    She’d come into the kitchen a few times. Enough to grab a glass of water, maybe leave a thank-you on the counter for the coffee Agatha brewed in the mornings. But she’d never gone further. Never crossed that invisible line between shared space and private.

    Until now.

    Agatha said nothing.

    She stepped past her, peeled her hat off, and slung it on the hook by the door.

    “You can leave your boots on,” she said quickly. “Floor’s already wet.” Rio didn’t answer right away.

    She just scanned the room, really looked. Like she was cataloguing it, and Agatha was suddenly aware of every single thing she never thought twice about.

    The living room was plain. Sparse. Not messy, just empty. No photos, no art, no trinkets. Just the old armchair her grandfather had built, the patched couch she never used, a small wooden table near the fireplace that wobbled if you leaned on it too hard.

    Everything looked lived in but untouched.

    Useful, but not personal.

    There was a throw blanket over the back of the couch, sure, but it was threadbare as an old saddle blanket, washed too many times, barely more than decoration.

    Rio stepped toward the hearth. Her boots squeaked on the old wood floor. “You made this,” she said.

    Agatha turned. “What?”

    Rio was looking at the bookshelf.

    Simple, sturdy, stained dark. Not bought. Crafted.

    “Your bookshelf,” she said. “You built it.”

    Agatha shifted uncomfortably. “So?”

    “It’s good. Balanced.”

    Agatha blinked. “It holds books. That’s the point.”

    Rio didn’t smile. Didn’t press.

    Just kept looking.

    Her gaze moved from the bookshelf to the table. Then to the windows, bare except for light cotton curtains she’d sewn herself two winters ago because the drafts kept catching her neck when she read too late at night. Agatha hadn’t realized how obvious it was until someone else stood there, soaked and dripping, and looked at her house like it had meaning. It wasn’t supposed to.

    It was just a house.

    Four walls. Wood and nails. A roof that leaked once in the south corner but hadn’t caved in yet.

    But now Rio was looking at it like it meant something.

    Like it was a reflection.

    Like it said something about her.

    And Agatha hated how warm her face felt suddenly.

    She turned toward the kitchen, trying to regain control of the moment. “I’ll get the coffee on.”

    Rio didn’t follow.

    Just stood in the middle of the room, water still puddling at her boots, and kept looking like she was reading something Agatha hadn’t written down. Agatha reached for the tin of grounds, hands moving by muscle memory. She worked faster than usual, just to drown out the silence. Click of the stove dial. Strike of a match. The kettle set down hard on the burner.

    All normal sounds.

    All louder than they should’ve been.

    And still—behind her—Rio didn’t speak.

    Didn’t sit.

    Didn’t wander further.

    Just existed there, calm and quiet, soaking the floor and seeing far too much.

    “You can take the jacket off,” Agatha said finally, without turning around. A pause.

    Then the soft rustle of canvas, heavy and wet, being peeled away. It was still warm from Rio’s body when Agatha caught a glimpse of it out the corner of her eye, her own jacket, folded neat as Sunday tack over the back of the unused couch, like a placeholder. A memory.

    Agatha pressed her hand to the edge of the counter, grounding herself. She hated this.

    Not because Rio was in her house.

    But because she wasn’t doing anything wrong.

    She wasn’t digging. She wasn’t prying. She wasn’t asking questions. She was just here.

    Quiet. Observant.

    And not leaving.

    Agatha poured the boiling water slow.

    Listened to it hiss against the coffee grounds.

    Let the steam rise between them like a shield.

    Because if she had to look at her again—soaked and calm and too goddamn kind—she wasn’t sure what would come out of her mouth.

    And she wasn’t ready to find out.

    The coffee kettle hissed on the stove, loud and constant, a thin coil of steam twisting toward the ceiling.

    Agatha stood by the counter, arms stiff, back straight, doing her best impression of someone who wasn’t unraveling.

    The kitchen smelled like roasted earth and rain. Her shirt was still damp, clinging under her arms, and the heat from the stove had begun to dry her sleeves unevenly, hot on one side, clammy on the other.

    Behind her, Rio still hadn’t spoken.

    Agatha could feel her in the room, not just hear her, but feel her. That quiet steadiness like a rock in a creek, the kind of presence that didn’t make noise but shifted the current around it.

    The fire in the hearth crackled softly, just past the living room. She could see the flicker of orange light dancing along the walls from here, feel the shift in temperature, the edge of warmth pulling gently at the chill wrapped around her spine.

    It should’ve been comfortable.

    It wasn’t.

    Not with Rio close.

    Not with the space between them suddenly meaning something. Agatha cleared her throat without turning around. Her voice came out flat, too casual.

    “There’s…” She paused, shifted her weight. “There’s some dry stuff in the hall closet. Big. Probably still clean.”

    The silence behind her felt like a pause.

    Not hesitation.

    Just… understanding.

    Rio didn’t answer right away. Just the soft sound of a step backward, the creak of wood under her boots. Then her voice calm, level, soaked to the bone: “I’ll dry fast enough by the fire.”

    Agatha gave a slight nod like it didn’t matter.

    Like she hadn’t offered anything at all.

    The kettle began to hiss harder. She turned off the burner and poured, keeping her hand steady even though she could feel her pulse in her wrist. The old mugs clinked against the counter. She didn’t ask how Rio liked hers, just poured it black, hot, and strong.

    When she turned, Rio was already seated near the fire, her wet hair tucked behind one ear, her shoulders still stiff with lingering cold. Her hands were folded around the mug Agatha handed her without comment. She hadn’t asked for sugar. Just took it, quiet and even, like she understood that the gesture meant more than it looked.

    Agatha sat down across from her.

    Two mismatched mugs.

    Two sets of damp boots steaming by the hearth.

    A storm still raging outside, wind battering the windowpanes in sharp, hollow bursts.

    But in here?

    It was just silence.

    And heat.

    The fire popped once. A soft, splintering crackle that made Agatha glance up, even though she didn’t want to look.

    Because Rio was already looking at her.

    Not openly.

    Not in a way that demanded anything.

    But seeing her, all the same.

    Her eyes were dark in the firelight, catching gold and copper in places Agatha didn’t want to notice. Her posture was relaxed now, legs stretched out slightly, mug balanced between her palms like something sacred. Agatha took a slow sip of coffee.

    Bitter.

    Scalding.

    Perfect.

    She stared into the fire instead of at Rio. Watched the way the logs curled in on themselves. How the light flickered across the old brick of the hearth. She could hear the storm outside trying to press in, gusts of wind against the siding, spatter of rain on the roof, but it felt far away now. Too far.

    Because Rio was close.

    Not touching-close.

    Not even shoulder-close.

    But close enough.

    Close enough that Agatha could smell the rain still clinging to her skin. Could feel the heat from the fire reflecting off both of them in uneven waves.

    The silence wasn’t nothing. It was full, and she rode it like a green horse— loose reins, ready to bail.

    She cleared her throat.

    Rio didn’t blink.

    “You always that reckless?” she asked, low.

    Rio tilted her head slightly. “I wasn’t reckless.”

    “You stayed out in lightning with a damn notebook.”

    “I wasn’t under a tree.”

    “You weren’t in shelter.”

    Rio just shrugged. “I didn’t realize how fast it was coming.” “You should’ve.”

    Another silence.

    “I know.”

    Agatha clenched her jaw.

    It wasn’t about the notebook.

    It wasn’t about the storm.

    It was about the image she hadn’t been able to get out of her head since they stepped inside: Rio crouched in the field, hair soaked, lips pale, shoulders stiff from cold, and still saying she was fine like that meant something. Agatha gripped her mug tighter.

    “I ain’t draggin’ your body out of a ditch,” she muttered. “So don’t give me a reason to.”

    Rio’s lips twitched.

    Not a smile.

    Not quite.

    But something close.

    “I didn’t ask you to come find me.”

    Agatha’s eyes snapped to her.

    And there—finally—was a spark of heat that wasn’t from the fire. She set her mug down harder than she meant to.

    “You think I did that for you?”

    Rio didn’t flinch. “Didn’t say that.”

    “You’re implyin’ it.”

    “I’m not.”

    “Then what are you doin’?”

    Another silence.

    Then softly, steadily: “I’m just sitting with you.”

    Agatha didn’t know what to do with that.

    It hit somewhere deep in her chest. Some old, buried ache she didn’t like to think about. Something that sounded an awful lot like longing. And Rio just… sat there.

    Calm.

    Drying slowly.

    Not pushing.

    Just present.

    The storm growled louder outside.

    The fire crackled again.

    And Agatha realized—slowly, terribly—that this wasn’t tension waiting to break.

    This was tension settling.

    This was the moment after.

    After fear. After cold. After pretending she didn’t care. This was the moment something shifted.

    And she had no idea what the hell to do with it.

    So she picked up her mug again.

    Took a long sip.

    Didn’t speak.

    And across from her, Rio did the same.

    The only sound in the room was fire, rain and the deliberate act of not looking at each other.

    The air between them was thick, and changing. They both knew it. Chapter End Notes Anyway. They’re not making eye contact. The air’s weird now. Neither of them knows what to do with the shift.

    This chapter is 85% atmosphere, 10% repressed yearning, and 5% Agatha being like “You could die and that would inconvenience me, emotionally.”

    Chapter 7: Kindness, Uninvited

    Chapter Summary The storm’s gone, but it left its mark.

    Rio’s hurt. Agatha helps. Neither of them knows what to do with how gentle it feels, especially when gratitude sounds more dangerous than thunder.

    Chapter Notes This chapter is brought to you by: ankle injuries, quiet lesbians doing too much, and Agatha “I’m not strong I just carry people like they’re sacks of flour” Harkness.

    We’ve got: Unexpected manhandling.

    Flustered biologist panic.

    Tender bandaging as foreplay (shhh).

    Agatha scrubbing already-clean leather while emotionally combusting Also someone says thank you and it ruins everything. Enjoy. See the end of the chapter for more notes The sky was clearer than it had been in days.

    The storm had blown through overnight, taking the heat with it, and the morning air was crisp and dry, sharp-edged, the kind of clean that only came after thunder, like the land had finally exhaled.

    Agatha moved with the herd, slow and steady, the reins slack in her hand. The cattle were slow in the sun, lowing under their breath, tails swatting flies in rhythm. Dust rose in pale gold clouds beneath their hooves, catching the light like smoke.

    It should’ve been simple. Routine.

    She liked this kind of work. Hard, rhythmic. It gave her hands something to do. Gave her brain space to flatten out. The early sun pressed warm against her shoulders, and the saddle creaked in familiar ways.

    She wasn’t thinking about Rio.

    Not really.

    Not yet.

    She only looked toward the far fence line out of habit. That’s where Rio liked to work when Agatha had the herd out, far enough not to spook the cattle, close enough to see the pasture in full bloom. She’d usually be crouched by the tall grass or kneeling beside a cluster of prickly pears, notebook in hand, totally absorbed in whatever small thing had caught her attention.

    Agatha glanced toward the ridge line like she always did. And frowned.

    Because Rio was there.

    But she wasn’t crouched or kneeling.

    She was standing.

    Sort of.

    Her body tilted, off-balance in a way that sent something cold down Agatha’s spine. Not bent like she’d dropped something. Not resting on one hip like usual. But favoring her left side, like putting weight on the right leg cost her something.

    And the way she moved—slow, cautious, uneven—was wrong.

    Agatha blinked.

    Watched closer.

    Rio took another step.

    Winced.

    It was subtle. If Agatha hadn’t been looking at exactly the right moment, she would’ve missed it.

    But she saw it.

    That flicker of discomfort. The brief pause. The way she set her jaw just slightly tighter after shifting her weight.

    And suddenly the cattle didn’t matter anymore.

    Agatha turned her horse without a word, guiding it with her knees. No whistle. No call to the dog. Just a silent pivot and a straight, deliberate line across the pasture.

    She didn’t ride hard yet, but her grip tightened on the reins. Because she knew that walk. Had done it herself enough times to recognize it.

    You didn’t limp like that unless something hurt. And you didn’t hide a limp like that unless you didn’t want anyone noticing.

    Agatha’s jaw clenched.

    Rio hadn’t yelled for help. Hadn’t called her name. Hadn’t waved an arm or made a sound. Tough as a fence post and just as damn stubborn. And that—that—pissed her off more than the limp itself.

    Because she would’ve kept walking like that, wouldn’t she? Would’ve finished her field notes, taken her damn samples, pretending she wasn’t in pain, like maybe she could will the injury away if she just didn’t name it.

    Agatha knew that logic. She’d lived that logic.

    And now she hated seeing it on someone else.

    She rode slow, steady, drawing closer.

    Rio didn’t look up, still moving in careful steps, keeping her right foot angled like every bit of pressure hurt.

    Her notebook was tucked under one arm. Her free hand hung loose at her side, fingers twitching now and then like she wanted to reach for something, support, maybe, but wouldn’t let herself.

    The wind shifted.

    Agatha could smell the grass warming in the sun, the leather of the saddle, the faint hint of something sweet on the air, early mesquite blossoms or maybe that aloe Rio had pointed out last week.

    The horse huffed under her, ears flicking.

    Ten more paces.

    Rio still hadn’t noticed her.

    Or maybe she had.

    And just hadn’t acknowledged it.

    Agatha tightened the reins and stopped.

    Slid down from the saddle in one clean motion.

    The ground was warm beneath her boots. Dry. Easy.

    Rio didn’t look surprised when Agatha approached.

    Didn’t flinch or jump or spin around.

    Just… glanced sideways, like she’d been expecting this moment but still hadn’t planned for it.

    Agatha looked her over once, quick and sharp.

    Hair dry now. Same shirt, same boots. But her right leg bore less than half its weight.

    “You step in a hole?” Agatha asked, voice flat.

    Rio shrugged. “I think so.”

    “You think so.”

    “It was quick. I didn’t see it.”

    “An’ now you’re limpin’ like your leg ain’t attached.”

    “I’m fine.”

    Agatha blinked. The words barely made it through her skull before her hands curled into fists at her sides.

    There it was again.

    That damn phrase.

    I’m fine.

    “I can walk,” Rio added.

    “Doesn’t look like it.”

    “It’ll pass.”

    Agatha said nothing.

    She just exhaled slow, glanced once at the horse, then looked back at Rio. And made a decision.

    Not a loud one.

    Not a dramatic one.

    Just a clean, efficient shift from watching to acting, like turning a horse before it bolts.

    Agatha looked at Rio, then at the horse, then back again. She stepped forward without a word.

    Rio straightened automatically, that same defensive posture she always used when she didn’t want help. Her shoulders squared, her chin lifted, but the weight didn’t go on her right foot.

    Agatha caught that.

    Noted it.

    Didn’t say a damn thing.

    “You don’t have to—” Rio started.

    Agatha cut her off with a look.

    Just one. Steady. Quiet.

    Not angry.

    Not even annoyed.

    Just… done.

    “No, ma’am,” she said flatly. “You’re not walkin’ this off.” “I can walk,” Rio insisted.

    “Not well.”

    “I’m not asking to be carried.”

    “Good.”

    And with that, Agatha stepped in, reached out, and lifted her. There was no warning. No countdown. No dramatic gesture. Just two strong arms and a solid grip, one at the back of Rio’s thighs, the other steadying her spine and suddenly her boots weren’t on the ground. She let out a soft, startled breath. Didn’t even have time to yelp. Just up. The motion was smooth. Easy. Like Rio didn’t weigh a thing. Because to Agatha?

    She didn’t.

    She shifted Rio’s weight against her chest without a grunt or a stumble, like she’d done it a hundred times before. Like it wasn’t even worth blinking over.

    And Rio—

    Rio forgot how to breathe.

    Because what the hell.

    The strength wasn’t just obvious, it was effortless.

    She’d expected a grunt, a strain, something.

    Agatha didn’t even flinch.

    Just turned, carried her over to the horse, and hoisted her up like she was setting a feed sack across the saddle.

    Gentle, though.

    She was gentle.

    Agatha kept one hand steady at the small of her back, guiding her into the saddle without jostling her ankle. Then she stepped back, adjusted the stirrup, and checked Rio’s posture like it was any normal morning. Rio just sat there, stunned.

    Still not talking.

    Still not breathing quite right.

    Agatha swung up behind her, clean as ever. One boot in the stirrup, one strong motion, her body settling behind Rio’s like it belonged there. No rush.

    No breathlessness.

    Just done.

    And Rio?

    Rio was losing her mind in complete silence.

    Her hands hovered awkwardly near the saddle horn. Her spine was too straight. Her brain was short-circuiting.

    Because holy shit.

    Apparently this woman could lift her without breaking stride. And Rio didn’t quite hate it.

    She didn’t say it.

    Wouldn’t dream of saying it.

    But the thought was loud, and immediate, and way too hot for a sunny morning in the middle of a field.

    Agatha was close behind her now. Not touching. Not unnecessarily, at least. But her knees pressed firm against Rio’s thighs. Her presence was a wall— solid, steady, and very warm. And Rio could feel her breath at the edge of her jaw when she leaned forward slightly to take the reins. “You good?” Agatha asked.

    The question came low, rumbling near her ear.

    Rio nodded before she could think better of it. “Fine.”

    It came out high.

    Too quick.

    She wanted to kick herself. Couldn’t. Ankle.

    Agatha didn’t comment. Just clicked her tongue, nudged the horse with her heel, and started them toward the barn.

    The ride was smooth.

    Too smooth.

    And Rio was keenly aware that her thigh was touching Agatha’s. That every time the horse shifted, so did their bodies. That Agatha’s arm brushed her side more than once and did not shake.

    Strong. Still. Unapologetic.

    Rio had never been more flustered in her life.

    Because the truth was this: she could’ve limped back. She could’ve argued. Could’ve made a scene.

    But now?

    Sitting in the saddle, pressed against the kind of strength that didn’t ask for thanks and didn’t pretend to be anything it wasn’t?

    She was glad she hadn’t.

    Even if she’d never, ever admit that out loud.

    The ride back was silent.

    Agatha didn’t say a word. Didn’t ask questions. Just kept her focus on the path ahead, the feel of the reins in her hand, the solid warmth of the woman in front of her.

    Rio sat stiff in the saddle.

    Not from fear. Not from pain.

    But like she didn’t know what to do with herself.

    Agatha didn’t know what to do with it either.

    So she stayed steady. Kept her breathing even. Let the horse’s gait do the talking.

    When they reached the barn, she dismounted first.

    Rio didn’t try.

    Didn’t even make a move.

    She just sat there, face unreadable, ankle still tucked close like she was protecting it.

    Agatha stepped close, laid a hand on her thigh.

    “Hang on.”

    Before Rio could ask what she meant, Agatha reached up—

    —and lifted her down again.

    Slower this time. Careful.

    Rio’s arms looped around her shoulders on instinct, and Agatha didn’t flinch. Just let it happen. Let her body settle against hers for the space of a heartbeat. Her arms wrapped around Río’s back, one hand under her knees. Close.

    So close.

    But Agatha didn’t say a word.

    She carried her inside the barn, straight past the toolshed and tack wall, and set her down on an old hay bale by the stall. Not too soft. Not too low. Just… steady.

    Rio’s fingers hovered uselessly in her lap.

    Her voice came quiet. “You really don’t have to fuss.”

    Agatha didn’t look at her.

    “Not fussin’,” she muttered, already heading for the cabinet near the workbench.

    She grabbed the old red first-aid box. Worn at the edges. Smelled faintly of liniment and dust. She hadn’t used it on anyone but herself in years. She didn’t pause to think about that.

    Just opened it. Dug out the wrap. A pair of scissors. The salve she didn’t remember buying but kept around anyway.

    When she turned back, Rio was watching her.

    Not saying a word.

    Agatha crouched without asking. Rested her palm on Rio’s shin, gentle but firm.

    “Boot’s gotta come off.”

    Rio hesitated, then nodded Agatha reached for the laces, slow and methodical.

    Her fingers worked quickly, tugging wet knots loose, moving with the kind of focus you could only earn through repetition and worry. She eased the boot off with a grip that was firm but not rough, one hand bracing under Rio’s calf to keep the angle clean.

    Rio inhaled sharply, but didn’t pull away.

    The sock underneath was soaked and muddy. Agatha peeled it down with careful fingers, her thumb brushing over skin warmer than she expected. The ankle was already swelling.

    Not badly, but enough to make her jaw tighten.

    “Damn gophers,” she muttered under her breath.

    “Little demons dig more holes than a drunk post-digger.” Rio huffed out a faint breath that might’ve been a laugh. Agatha didn’t smile.

    She reached for the wrap.

    “This’ll burn,” she said, voice low, as she uncapped the salve. “But it helps.”

    Rio didn’t argue.

    Didn’t move.

    Agatha rubbed it in, fingers slow over the joint, brushing the side of Rio’s foot with more tenderness than she meant to show.

    She hated how exposed it made her feel, doing this. Quiet care, no room to hide in it. No distance.

    But she didn’t stop.

    Didn’t speak.

    Just wrapped the ankle tight and clean. Not too tight. Just enough. She smoothed the edge of the bandage down with the pad of her thumb, her hands finally still.

    And that was when Rio said it.

    “Thank you.”

    Soft.

    Quiet.

    No sarcasm. No awkwardness.

    Just two words, like a promise she didn’t mean to make.

    Agatha froze.

    Just for a second.

    Then sat back on her heels, eyes dropping to her hands.

    She didn’t know what to say.

    Didn’t know how to take that.

    The thank-you had weight. Not just politeness. Not just pain. It was gratitude without obligation.

    And Agatha had no goddamn idea what to do with that kind of softness. She cleared her throat. “Don’t mention it.”

    Rio tilted her head. “I mean it.”

    Agatha’s fingers twitched against her thigh.

    “I know.”

    And she did.

    Which was the problem.

    Because care, in her world, always came with cost. Always had strings. Even the quiet kind. Especially the quiet kind.

    But this?

    This wasn’t transactional.

    This was just trust.

    And it scared the hell out of her.

    So she stood. Brushed her hands on her jeans. Focused on the next task, the next motion, the next anything to keep from looking at Rio too long. “I’ll get you water,” she said.

    And walked toward the house like the barn wasn’t full of silence that now felt impossible to leave behind.

    She didn’t hurry on the walk back.

    Didn’t rush across the yard. Didn’t storm into the house. Just moved with steady, measured steps, hands in her pockets, boots hitting dirt with that familiar weight.

    But the calm was fake.

    Even she knew that.

    Inside the house, she reached for a clean glass without thinking. Opened the tap. Let it run too long. Cold water spilled over her fingers as she filled the glass to the top, the rim trembling slightly.

    She set it down harder than necessary.

    Not enough to break it, just enough.

    Her hand hovered for a second, then pulled away.

    She needed something to do.

    Something else to do.

    So she carried the glass out to the barn. Said nothing.

    Didn’t meet Rio’s eyes.

    Just handed her the water, fingers brushing lightly as she passed it over, like it was any other job needing done.

    Rio took it without a word.

    Didn’t say thank you this time.

    Didn’t have to.

    Then Agatha turned around.

    Walked out again.

    Back up the porch steps, into the stillness.

    Her eyes landed on the saddle soap on the counter by the window. Left there two days ago after a ride she hadn’t meant to take but needed anyway. She grabbed it. Found the old rag. Moved back outside and sat down on the bench just outside the door.

    Not because the saddle was in urgent need of cleaning.

    Because she needed to stop thinking.

    Only she didn’t.

    Because the second she sat down, Rio’s voice echoed back. Thank you.

    Soft. Steady. No cracks in it. No shame.

    Like she meant it.

    And meant her.

    Agatha’s hands were already moving, rag dipped in the tin, working over the worn leather in slow, circular motions, but her head was a million miles off.

    Or maybe twenty feet.

    Because Rio was still in the barn. Still sitting where she left her. Still perched on that hay bale like something temporary, like she wasn’t sure if she belonged there or anywhere else.

    Agatha’s jaw clenched.

    She didn’t like the image of Rio sitting there alone. Didn’t like thinking about her favoring that ankle again if she tried to stand. Didn’t like that she’d let her stay behind even if she knew damn well she’d needed the space.

    To breathe.

    To think.

    To feel.

    Which was the real problem.

    Because she hadn’t expected to feel this much.

    Not from a sprained ankle.

    Not from one soft-spoken thank you.

    Not from fingers brushing skin that wasn’t her own but felt like it mattered anyway.

    Agatha worked her way along the saddle’s edge, fingers tight around the rag. The leather creaked beneath her grip. She’d conditioned it so many times the material was practically molded to her. She knew where the give was. Where it stuck. Where it cracked in winter and swelled in the heat. She knew it better than her own damn reflection, every stitch and scar of it, like an old trail etched in her hands.

    She didn’t know Rio that well.

    Not yet.
