    But she was starting to.

    And that scared her more than anything else.

    Agatha leaned back against the porch post. Closed her eyes for a second. Let the breeze push damp hair across her forehead.

    What was she even doing?

    Getting worked up over a thank you.

    Getting rattled by carrying someone.

    By touching her.

    By the way Rio didn’t flinch.

    Didn’t resist.

    Didn’t look away.

    Agatha rubbed a hand down her face and let it hang over her mouth. The thing was, she could handle pain. Bruises. Bleeding. Blisters. She could patch herself up in the dark without blinking. Could ride out storms and droughts and nights when the house creaked too loud from the wind. But this?

    This quiet gratitude?

    This trust?

    It was worse than all of it.

    It was kind.

    And kindness didn’t come cheap. Not in her world.

    Not from her mother. Not from neighbors. Not from anyone she hadn’t bled beside.

    And Rio hadn’t bled.

    Not yet.

    She’d just limped.

    And sat still.

    And said thank you like it was nothing.

    Agatha reached for the tin again. Pressed the cloth in harder than necessary. The saddle was already clean.

    Didn’t matter.

    She needed to move.

    Because if she didn’t, she’d go back out there. Back into the barn. Back to the way Rio was looking at her when she wrapped that bandage. She’d go back and do something stupid.

    Like stay.

    Like say something.

    Like care a little too much out loud.

    So she scrubbed leather that didn’t need scrubbing and told herself it wasn’t about the girl with the soft voice and the bruised ankle. But it was.

    Of course it was.

    And the worst part?

    She didn’t even want to stop.

    She didn’t hear the barn door, didn’t need to, the shift of wind told her someone had moved. The way the dog perked up and didn’t bark said it wasn’t a stranger. And the faintest crunch of gravel on the path told her Rio was walking—limping—across the yard.

    Agatha didn’t look up.

    Just kept her hand moving over the saddle. Slow now. No more pressure behind it. Just going through the motions because they were easier than standing still.

    The footsteps stopped short of the porch.

    Silence followed.

    Thick.

    Weighted.

    Agatha kept her head down and let it stretch.

    She wasn’t trying to be cold.

    But she was trying not to ask how the ankle felt. Not to say you didn’t have to come out here. Not to show that her chest had gone tight the second she’d heard those footsteps.

    She didn’t need to give that away.

    Not yet.

    Rio cleared her throat softly. “You didn’t have to do all that.” Agatha paused.

    Just for a breath.

    Then resumed the slow, circular motion over the stirrup. “You were hurt,” she said flatly.

    “I mean it though,” Rio added, voice quiet. “It was probably more of a hassle than—”

    Agatha set the rag down.

    Deliberately.

    Turned her head.

    Met her eyes.

    Rio’s mouth closed mid-sentence.

    And Agatha said, calm but sharp, “Don’t.”

    Rio blinked. “Don’t what?”

    “Don’t apologize for bein’ in pain. Life’ll buck you hard enough without you helpin’ it along.”

    “I wasn’t—”

    “Yes, you were.”

    The words weren’t loud.

    But they landed.

    Agatha stood slowly, wiping her hands on her jeans, then leaning one hip against the porch railing.

    She didn’t cross her arms. Didn’t step forward. Didn’t do any of the things she usually did when she needed space.

    She just looked at her.

    And said, quieter this time: “You didn’t make a mess. You didn’t break anything. You got hurt. That happens.”

    Rio shifted her weight, wincing slightly. “Still. I know you’ve got work to do.”

    Agatha exhaled hard through her nose. “Work’ll keep.”

    “I just meant—”

    “You always do that?”

    The question came low.

    Even.

    But pointed.

    Rio tilted her head. “Do what?”

    “Downplay it. Shrink it. Act like takin’ up space is somethin’ you gotta earn.”

    That hit.

    Agatha saw it, just the flicker of something across Rio’s face. Something true. Something she wasn’t ready to admit but couldn’t deny. So Agatha stepped down from the porch, slow, boots hitting the dirt one at a time, and closed the distance without brushing too close. She didn’t crowd her.

    Didn’t loom.

    Just stood beside her with the space of intention between them. “You don’t owe me a sorry,” she said. “Not for bein’ human.” Rio didn’t speak.

    Didn’t look away either.

    Her throat worked once, like she was trying to swallow something. Agatha scratched the back of her neck. “You need help, you ask. That’s it. Don’t need to explain it or soften it or act like I’m doin’ you a favor.” Rio’s voice, when it came, was soft again. Careful. “You’re not?” Agatha met her eyes.

    And for once, didn’t look away.

    “No,” she said. “I’m not.”

    The air went still again.

    Not awkward.

    Not tense.

    Just… still.

    Settled.

    And that was worse, in a way.

    Because it wasn’t silence she could fight. Wasn’t sarcasm or distance or snark.

    It was closeness.

    Real closeness.

    Earned in bruises and bandages and the kind of honesty Agatha didn’t usually offer unless someone had bled beside her.

    But Rio?

    She’d just sat still.

    And that had been enough.

    Agatha sighed and looked toward the barn. “You rest a while. Ankle’s gonna stiffen if you keep movin’ on it.”

    Rio nodded slowly. “All right.”

    “Go sit on the porch, if you want.”

    “You’ll let me steal your good chair?”

    Agatha snorted. “Ain’t a good chair. It just leans the least.” That earned the smallest smile.

    Agatha didn’t let herself stare at it.

    Just turned back to the saddle, already clean, already done, and picked up the rag again like she hadn’t just carved herself open with those damn words.

    Chapter End Notes I regret to inform you that Agatha has officially started caring out loud. Rio’s trying not to apologize. Agatha’s trying not to feel anything. They’re both failing. spectacularly.

    As always, thanks for reading.

    Chapter 8: The Quiet Between

    Chapter Summary After the storm, a loose calf sends Agatha and Rio across the ridge and into a small argument neither of them meant to have.

    By the time the fence is steady again, so is something between them. Chapter Notes In today’s episode of “two stubborn women who do not know how to talk about their feelings,” I bring you: A runaway calf (symbolism? maybe.)

    Yelling that is definitely not about the calf.

    Agatha manhandling a fencepost like it personally offended her. And a porch scene so quiet it might actually kill me.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes The wind carried sound too well that morning.

    It wasn’t loud. Wasn’t even constant. Just a slow, steady push across the tall grass that made the whole pasture hum, like something old and half-asleep stirring in its bones.

    Agatha had been walking the north fence line, checking posts and tension where the storm had blown through days before. It was work she usually liked—simple, familiar. Pull, patch, reset. Wood and wire and sweat. She didn’t mind the solitude. Preferred it, most days.

    But today?

    Today she wasn’t alone.

    Rio trailed a few paces behind her, notebook in one hand, walking stick in the other. Her limp was still noticeable, less sharp than yesterday, but present. She moved carefully, as if she’d memorized every part of her body’s complaint and was negotiating with it one step at a time. Agatha hadn’t told her to stay behind.

    Hadn’t told her to come either.

    Just found her standing at the porch with boots on and that same calm look in her eyes like, I’m going. Unless you stop me.

    Agatha didn’t stop her.

    The sun was just high enough to throw gold across the ridge when Agatha heard it.

    That sound—sharp and high and distant.

    A calf, crying.

    It cut across the wind like a warning.

    Agatha stilled.

    Turned her head slightly, listening.

    It came again.

    This time closer. Too close for comfort.

    She scanned the field.

    Nothing obvious. The herd was further down, huddled near the southern edge of the pasture, mostly calm. But the sound wasn’t coming from them. It was coming from the other side of the hill. Near the stretch of fencing she hadn’t reached yet. The part with the old cedar posts and rusted wire that had already seen one too many winters.

    Agatha swore under her breath.

    And started moving.

    Not a run. Not yet. Just a faster stride, deliberate and quiet. Rio didn’t ask anything, just followed, notebook tucked under her arm now, eyes scanning the horizon.

    They crested the ridge together.

    And there it was.

    A small calf, maybe three weeks old, wandering just beyond the property line. Too far from the others. Ears twitching, head low, clearly looking for something, its mother, most likely. Spooked.

    Alone.

    The fence beside them sagged in two places. One post snapped clean at the base, likely from stormfall. Another had been weakened at the ground, tilted inward like a hand that had given up trying to hold the line. Agatha felt her stomach turn, low and hot.

    She hadn’t fixed it.

    Had meant to.

    Had seen it two days ago and told herself tomorrow.

    And now that calf was out there, just far enough off to be a problem. Coyotes, terrain, riverbed washouts, too many ways for it to get hurt or worse.

    She exhaled through her nose.

    And then—because she had to say something—muttered, “Damn fences.” Rio glanced toward the calf. “Can we get to it?”

    Agatha nodded slowly. “Yeah. Just need to loop around, haze it gentle. Ease it back in.”

    “Need help?”

    Agatha didn’t answer right away.

    She looked at Rio’s ankle, the way she stood with just a little too much weight on her left leg, and felt the words you should stay here rise in her throat.

    But she didn’t say them.

    She just gave a small shrug. “It’s your research.”

    Río’s mouth quirked. “So that’s a yes.”

    Agatha was already moving. “You go left. I’ll go down the ridge.” They didn’t need a full plan.

    Just angles.

    Just instinct.

    Just get the calf home.

    Agatha didn’t look back as they split paths. She knew Rio would follow. Knew her steps would be uneven, maybe slow, but steady. The woman didn’t quit. Hadn’t once, even when soaked to the skin or limping across the yard like it didn’t matter.

    And Agatha?

    She wasn’t sure when exactly that had started to matter more than she wanted it to.

    But it did now.

    Because the fence wasn’t the only thing that’d gone soft at the edges lately. The slope was steeper on this side of the ridge.

    Agatha rode the slope through loose dirt and dry weeds with practiced ease, boots digging in at just the right angle. The kind of muscle memory you earned from years of doing the same damn thing, chasing what didn’t want to be caught and pretending it didn’t wear you out.

    The calf had wandered farther than she’d thought.

    It wasn’t just over the fence, it was halfway to the next patch of scrub trees, picking its way through unfamiliar grass with wide eyes and twitching ears. It kept glancing back over its shoulder like it expected its mother to appear out of thin air.

    It wouldn’t stay still for long.

    She needed to move fast.

    She whistled, soft and sharp, trying not to spook it. Just enough to draw its attention. Just enough to make it pause.

    The calf flinched.

    Then lit out left.

    Agatha hissed a curse and cut wide to flank it, angling her path to steer it back toward the pasture. But it was fast—small legs, fresh panic, too much open space.

    And Rio—

    Rio wasn't where she should’ve been.

    Agatha glanced up and saw her halfway through the brush, trying to cut around, notebook long gone, hands up like she was trying to talk the calf into submission.

    It didn’t work.

    The calf turned again—sharp, skittish—and barreled in the wrong direction. “Damn it!” Agatha snapped, loud.

    Rio’s head jerked toward her, startled.

    “She’s spooked,” Rio said quickly, trying to follow without putting too much weight on her right foot.

    “I know she’s spooked!”

    “She’s heading into the ravine!”

    Agatha’s boots hit the ground hard as she cut sign and changed angle again, body low, breath heaving. “I see that, too, thanks!”

    Rio bristled. “Don’t yell at me.”

    “I ain’t yellin’—”

    “You’re raising your voice!”

    Agatha cursed again, dodging a thorn bush. “Because you’re—dammit, this was supposed to be simple!”

    “Sorry for trying to help!”

    “You’re limpin’, Rio!”

    “So?”

    “So stay the hell back!”

    That landed.

    Rio Stopped dead.

    Agatha chased the calf another ten feet before it finally hesitated, turning in a loose circle, bleating, confused. Agatha slowed her pace, her breath catching in her throat, trying to coax it without sound this time. Just posture. Just presence.

    The calf took a few tentative steps back toward the fence line. Then a few more.

    Agatha exhaled.

    Held her ground.

    Waited.

    And after a long moment, the calf trotted through the gap in the wire, back into familiar pasture, and took off toward the herd without another sound. Agatha watched it go.

    Felt the tension bleed out of her shoulders.

    Then turned.

    Rio was standing thirty feet away.

    Mud on her jeans. Dust on her sleeve. And an expression Agatha couldn’t quite read, somewhere between frustration and disbelief. Agatha pushed a hand through her hair. “You all right?”

    “I’m fine,” Rio said flatly.

    Agatha flinched.

    Not visibly. But inside?

    God, she flinched.

    Because it was the same I’m fine she’d thrown at her back in the pasture, days ago. Same tone. Same steel edge. Same don’t ask me again. And maybe she deserved that.

    She sighed and started walking over. “I didn’t mean—”

    Rio held up a hand. “Don’t.”

    Agatha stopped.

    Silence hung between them. The wind picked up again, brushing dust across the dry grass like it was trying to clean the slate. But it didn’t.

    Couldn’t.

    Because the words had already been said. The ones neither of them knew how to take back.

    Agatha’s voice came quieter this time. “You just scared me, is all.” Rio blinked. “What?”

    “You’re hurt,” she said, shrugging, like it wasn’t a big deal. “Could’ve made it worse.”

    Rio stared at her.

    For a long second, Agatha thought she wasn’t going to answer. Then softly, “You’re scared I’d get hurt?”

    Agatha looked away.

    “Maybe.”

    Rio didn't push.

    Didn’t step forward.

    Just stood there, wind tugging at the hem of her shirt, letting the silence settle again, but this time it felt different.

    Not full of tension.

    Not empty.

    Just… different.

    The kind of silence that came after something broke and didn’t fall apart. Agatha rubbed her face with the heel of her hand.

    “C’mon,” she muttered, glancing back at the fence. “I need to brace that post before we lose another one.”

    Rio nodded slowly. “I’ll help.”

    Agatha didn’t argue.

    Didn’t stop her.

    Didn’t offer to carry her back.

    And Rio?

    She didn’t apologize.

    They just walked, quiet, side by side.

    And for the first time in a while, it felt like the distance between them wasn’t measured in acres.

    They didn’t talk as they made their way back to the fence line. Not because there was nothing to say, but because too much had already been said.

    The wind had settled. The dust had too. The sun was higher now, and the air had lost its morning edge. Still warm. Still dry. But softer somehow, like even the land knew they were tired.

    Agatha walked with her head down, eyes on the uneven ground. Rio moved slowly beside her, still not favoring her ankle but not wincing like before. She didn’t speak. Didn’t offer commentary. Just kept pace in that quiet, steady way of hers.

    They reached the broken post and stopped.

    Agatha exhaled, crouched beside it, and laid a hand on the wood. It had cracked right through at the base, jagged and dry from years of sun. Probably weakened last season. She’d noticed it earlier in the week, meant to replace it. But one thing had led to another, and she hadn’t. Now here they were.

    She pressed her shoulder into it, testing the give.

    Too much.

    It leaned under her weight, and the barbed wire tugged loosely behind it. She stood again. Planted her boots. Looked out across the fence line that separated everything she knew from everything she didn’t. Rio stepped up beside her.

    Didn’t speak.

    Didn’t move.

    Just watched the same view.

    The calf was long gone now, folded back into the herd like it had never been missing. The rest of the pasture stretched quiet and wide beneath the midday sky, golden in places, rough in others.

    Agatha crossed her arms.

    “I’ll brace it,” she said, mostly to herself.

    “You want help?”

    “No,” Agatha said, voice steady. “I’ve got it.”

    She crouched by the post, setting her gloves against the splintered wood. The work came easy, measured, familiar. She wedged the brace against the base, pulled the wire tight until it sang low, then hammered the nails home one by one. Each strike felt like breathing again.

    Rio stayed close but didn’t interrupt. Just watched the sure rhythm of her hands, the clean precision of each motion. Agatha didn’t look up. Didn’t need to.

    When the last nail sank deep and the wire stopped groaning, she pressed her palm flat against the post. Solid now. Straight.

    She let out a breath she hadn’t realized she’d been holding. “Good as new,” she murmured.

    Rio’s voice came soft behind her. “You always do that?”

    Agatha glanced back, brow furrowed. “Do what?”

    “Fix things when you’re upset.”

    A faint huff escaped her, halfway between a laugh and a sigh. “Better than breakin’ ‘em,” she said.

    She stood then, brushing the dirt from her jeans, and for a long moment they just stayed there, close but not touching. Quiet, but not distant. Agatha glanced sideways at her.

    The sun caught at the edges of her face, tracing the curve of her jaw, the line of her brow drawn in thought. Her hair had come loose in places, soft strands clinging to her cheek. There was mud on her jeans and a smudge of dirt at her temple where she’d pushed her hair back without thinking. She looked tired.

    Winded.

    Still beautiful.

    Agatha looked away fast.

    Didn’t say a word.

    She reached up and wiped her forehead with the back of her arm. “You shouldn’t’ve chased after it like that.”

    “I didn’t chase,” Rio said calmly. “I followed.”

    “Same thing.”

    “No,” she said. “It’s not.”

    Agatha let out a breath.

    Then turned toward the post again. Rested both hands on the top of it. Let her head hang forward slightly.

    Rio stepped closer.

    Not all the way.

    But enough that Agatha could feel her presence, warm and grounded and somehow always still, even when the world wasn’t.

    They stood like that for a long moment.

    No one moved.

    No one breathed too loud.

    And Agatha didn’t know what the hell to do with the quiet. Because it wasn’t empty anymore.

    It was charged.

    Like something was going to break, but not in a bad way. Just… shift. Like if she reached out—if she turned just slightly—there’d be no turning back. She swallowed hard.

    “You gonna say anything else?” she asked finally, voice low. Rio looked at her.

    “I don’t think I need to.”

    Agatha let that hang in the air between them.

    Didn’t agree.

    Didn’t argue.

    Just closed her eyes for half a second and let the stillness wrap around her like a blanket that didn’t quite fit.

    The wind picked up again, slow and soft, brushing through the grass like breath.

    Agatha straightened up.

    Brushed her palms on her jeans.

    “C’mon,” she muttered. “Fence’ll hold now.”

    Rio didn’t ask if she was sure.

    She just nodded.

    And they walked back toward the barn.

    Not side by side this time.

    But not far apart either.

    And in that half-step of space between them, something settled. Not resolved.

    Not spoken.

    But real.

    And it stayed there, unspoken and alive, all the way home. They didn’t talk on the walk back.

    Didn’t need to.

    The silence between them wasn’t the brittle kind, not anymore. It wasn’t sharp at the edges or tight with heat. It just… was. Like the space between fence posts: held in place by tension neither of them wanted to cut. Agatha stepped up onto the porch first.

    Rio followed, slower.

    Her limp had worsened just a bit, tightened by too much movement, too much uneven ground, but she didn’t complain. Didn’t mention it. Just pressed one hand to the railing and hauled herself up the steps like she’d been doing it her whole life.

    Agatha opened the door and didn’t look behind her as she stepped inside. The kitchen was still and dim. Afternoon light filtered through the old linen curtain above the sink, casting gold across the countertop. Dust hung in the air, caught in shafts of sun like threads not yet pulled. Agatha walked to the sink on instinct. Turned on the tap. Let the water run cold.

    There was nothing urgent in the motion. Just habit. Familiarity. Her fingers curled around the lip of the chipped enamel sink, knuckles tightening until they turned pale. She didn’t realize she was holding her breath until the glass under the faucet started to overflow.

    She blinked. Pulled it back. Shut the water off.

    Footsteps behind her.

    Slow. Careful.

    Then the scrape of a chair leg on wood.

    Agatha didn’t turn around.

    Just set the glass down and grabbed another.

    She didn’t ask if Rio wanted it.

    Didn’t need to.

    She brought both glasses over, set one in front of her at the table, and sat across from her without ceremony. The chair creaked beneath her weight. The silence creaked louder.

    Rio didn't reach for the glass.

    Just looked down at her hands, mud-streaked, faintly red at the knuckles and then back up again.

    Agatha kept her eyes on the table.

    The wood grain had darkened with age. A long crack split one corner, filled in with glue from a half-hearted fix she’d made two winters ago. She needed to sand it down. Refinish it. Add it to the list. She cleared her throat.

    Didn’t speak.

    Rio was the one who broke first.

    “This land means a lot to you.”

    Agatha’s gaze didn’t move.

    “Yeah,” she said. “It does.”

    “You’ve taken care of it a long time.”

    Agatha shrugged. “Someone had to.”

    Rio nodded once, fingers tracing the rim of her glass. “You do a good job.” The words shouldn’t have landed like they did.

    But they did.

    Agatha blinked. Slow.

    “Fence didn’t hold,” she said.

    “No. But the rest did.”

    She glanced up then, just briefly. Rio’s expression was soft. Unassuming. Not trying to pry anything loose. Just… there.

    Agatha looked back down.

    “You’re not gonna write about that in your notes,” she said, dry. “Maybe I will.”

    “That your field partner’s bad at upkeep?”

    “That she’s got a working ranch and does it all herself.” Rio tilted her head. “There’s a story in that too.”

    Agatha didn’t know what to say to that.

    Didn’t want to say anything, really.

    Because if she did, she might say more than she meant to. Might say thank you. Might say don’t look at me like that. Might say please stay, even if you won’t.

    She picked up her water. Took a sip. Let it give her something to do with her hands.

    Rio did the same.

    The glasses clinked quietly when they set them down at the same time. More silence.

    But it wasn’t uncomfortable.

    Just... too aware of itself.

    Agatha leaned back in her chair. Let her legs stretch out under the table. One boot nudged against the edge of Rio’s.

    She didn’t pull back.

    Neither did Rio.

    They sat like that for a while, legs just touching, hands just still, breathing quietly enough to hear the wind against the window glass. Eventually, Agatha stood.

    Didn’t speak.

    Just took both glasses, rinsed them clean, and left them in the rack. She didn’t ask if Rio was staying for dinner.

    Didn’t need to.

    Because she already knew she was.

    And maybe that was the problem.

    Because knowing something didn’t make it easier to name. It just made it harder to not feel.

    The light had changed again by the time Agatha stepped out onto the porch. Later now. Softer. The kind of gold that didn’t last long, just a brief flicker between afternoon and dusk when everything looked warmer than it felt. Shadows stretched long across the yard. The old cedar near the fence cast fingers of gray that almost reached the porch steps.

    Rio was already sitting there.

    Second chair from the left. The one that creaked less when you leaned forward but groaned like hell if you leaned back. She had one leg propped up on the low railing, ankle resting carefully against the worn wood. The wrap was still clean. Tight. Her hands were in her lap.

    She didn’t say anything when Agatha stepped outside.

    Didn’t move either.

    Just looked out toward the pasture, where the herd had gathered again. Quiet now. Settled. Like the morning hadn’t happened. Like calves didn’t run off and fences didn’t break and silence hadn’t meant something hours earlier.

    Agatha hesitated in the doorway.

    Only for a second.

    Then stepped forward and sat in the chair beside her.

    Not her usual one.

    That one was closest to the post, where she could lean and watch the whole yard with one elbow cocked and a cigarette. But she didn’t take that seat now.

    She took the one next to Rio’s.

    Close enough for her knee to almost bump hers.

    Close enough to feel the heat coming off her skin, rising slow in the evening air.

    She leaned forward, resting her forearms on her thighs. The porch creaked beneath them. The breeze carried the scent of sage, old wood, and sun- warmed dirt.

    Still no one spoke.

    And it didn’t feel strange.

    Not anymore.

    Agatha let her eyes drift to the side, just enough to catch the profile of Rio’s face. She was watching the sky now, eyes half-lidded, jaw relaxed. Her shoulders weren’t tense like they’d been earlier. The lines around her mouth had softened.

    She looked…

    Peaceful.

    And that did something to Agatha’s chest she didn’t have a name for. She looked down at her hands. Scarred knuckles. Dirt under her nails. Calluses pressed into the creases of her palms from years of reins and rope and fencing wire.

    Hands meant for work.

    Not softness.

    Not touch.

    But Rio had let her touch her anyway.

    And hadn’t flinched.

    Agatha sat with that for a long moment.

    Let it settle into her ribs like something warm and weighty and new. “I should’ve fixed that fence days ago,” she muttered finally, voice low. Rio didn't look over. “But you didn’t.”

    “No.”

    “You got to it when it mattered.”

    Agatha exhaled slowly. “That your way of sayin’ I’m not a failure?” “Maybe.”

    Agatha huffed, something between a laugh and a sigh. “You’re a pain.” “I know.”

    But there was a smile behind it.

    Not wide. Not bright.

    Just there.

    Agatha leaned back in the chair, slow and careful. The wood groaned beneath her, but she didn’t care. The breeze picked up and pushed a loose strand of hair across her forehead. She didn’t bother fixing it. They sat like that for a while.

    Not counting minutes.

    Not watching the sky change colors.

    Just… sitting.

    Together.

    At some point, Rio shifted slightly and her knee brushed against Agatha’s. She didn’t move it.

    Agatha didn’t either.

    The touch was barely there. Just pressure. Just warmth.

    But it stayed.

    And so did they.

    The sun dipped lower.

    The land went gold, then amber, then bruised purple at the edges. And still they sat.

    Not touching more.

    Not saying anything else.

    But something quiet stayed behind in that silence. Something that didn’t leave when the breeze cooled or when the herd moved again in the distance. It stayed between them.

    Settled in the half-inch of space between their knees, in the half-spoken words, in the things neither of them had said but both of them felt. And when Agatha finally stood, slow and deliberate, she looked down at Rio and said only, “Come in when you’re ready.”

    Not asking.

    Not commanding.

    Just… offering.

    Rio nodded.

    Didn’t move.

    Agatha didn’t need her to.

    She turned, stepped back inside, and left the door open behind her. Just in case.

    Chapter End Notes Shout-out to the calf for being the emotional catalyst we didn’t know we needed.

    Agatha let someone sit beside her in her chair. Rio didn't flinch. They touched knees and nobody died. Character development, baby. Thanks for reading.

    Chapter 9: The Things Left Standing

    Chapter Summary The bunkhouse hasn’t held anyone in years.

    Agatha shows Rio what’s left of it and maybe, unintentionally, what’s left of herself.

    But when a phone call reminds her that Rio’s world is bigger than this ranch, silence takes on a new weight.

    Some things can be fixed. Some things can only be felt.

    Chapter Notes Agatha’s been fixing things she can’t explain, and Rio has a habit of noticing the things no one’s supposed to. That’s… pretty much the whole story.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes It started with a question that wasn’t a question.

    Agatha stood on the porch, one boot braced against the railing, eyes on the horizon where a few slow clouds gathered over the west ridge. The air was heavy, the kind that made your skin feel too thick for your bones. Not hot, just still. Like something was waiting to break.

    Behind her, Rio leaned in the doorway with a mug of coffee, watching the same line of sky. Not saying anything. Not asking anything either. Agatha glanced at her. Then toward the far end of the property. The bunkhouse sat quiet in the distance. Half-shadowed by the tree line, roofline just barely visible above the tall grass and half-fallen fence rails. She hadn’t meant to say it, but the words came anyway.

    “Come take a look at somethin’.”

    Rio didn’t ask what.

    Didn’t hesitate.

    Just stepped off the porch, boots crunching soft against the gravel, and followed.

    The walk was slow.

    Not for any particular reason. Just one of those silences that didn’t feel like it needed to be filled. A few birds called in the distance. The breeze rustled through the cottonwoods, brushing leaf against leaf with a sound like breath. The ground was still soft from the rain two days ago. The scent of turned earth lingered faintly in the air.

    Agatha kept her eyes ahead.

    Didn’t look back to see if Rio was still there.

    She knew she was.

    The bunkhouse came into view fully as they reached the lower slope, a small, weather-worn building of gray clapboard and rusted metal trim, half- swallowed by overgrown weeds and wildflowers. The front steps creaked when Agatha climbed them. The porch was solid, but only just. She made a mental note to replace the bottom stair.

    She reached for the door.

    Paused.

    “Sticks,” she said, more to herself than anything. “Takes some persuadin’.” Then she put her shoulder into it and shoved.

    The frame groaned. The latch scraped. And the door swung open with a reluctant sigh.

    Inside, the bunkhouse smelled of wood, dust, and time.

    It wasn’t falling apart, yet. But it was getting there.

    The windows were dirty but intact, letting in slanted beams of afternoon light that caught on floating particles in the air. There were two beds, both with stripped frames and no mattresses. A cracked washbasin in the corner. One shelf with old tools and half-used paint cans. A table near the back, battered and uneven.

    And on the far wall, a wide patch of freshly sanded wood where Agatha had been working.

    Rio stepped inside slowly, letting her eyes adjust. She didn’t speak. Just looked. Just took it in the way she always did, quietly, without judgment, like it was a story she was reading instead of a space she was standing in. Agatha leaned against the doorway, arms crossed, watching her without watching her.

    “It was for the ranch hands,” she said after a moment. “Way back.” Rio turned, brow lifted slightly.

    Agatha nodded toward the beds. “My ma kept it up when I was a kid. Said it made us look respectable, even when we couldn’t afford to pay anyone to stay in it.”

    “And now?”

    Agatha shrugged. “Don’t use it.”

    She didn’t mention the fact that she’d been out here the last few weekends, fixing the beams, sanding the floor. That she’d cleared the cobwebs. That she’d bought new hinges. That there was paint in the shed she hadn’t touched yet.

    Rio stepped toward one of the beds. Ran her fingers along the frame, worn metal, cool to the touch. She didn’t smile. But she didn’t frown either. “Why show me this?” she asked.

    Agatha looked away. Out the window. Toward the fields.

    “Figure it’s somethin’ you oughta know’s out here.”

    Rio didn’t press.

    Didn’t say for what? or who for?

    Just nodded once, like that answer was enough.

    But Agatha’s chest was tight.

    Because it wasn’t enough. Not really. Not the real answer. The real answer was: because you’re still here.

    Because you showed up with your limp and your quiet and your unshakable patience and I don’t know what to do with that. Because I don’t know how to offer anything without making it look like a job or a chore or a damn trap.

    Because maybe, just maybe, I want you to stay.

    She didn’t say any of that.

    Just crossed the room. Knocked on a wall stud near the corner where the floor had warped.

    “Gonna need bracin’,” she muttered.

    Rio looked over her shoulder. “You doing all this alone?” Agatha gave her a dry look. “You see anyone else holdin’ a hammer?” Rio didn’t answer. Just took a few more steps around the space, pausing in the square of light near the far window. Her face looked soft in that light. Still. Curious.

    She glanced back at Agatha. “You fixing it up for someone?” Agatha’s hand stilled on the stud.

    The air thickened.

    She didn’t turn.

    Just said, low and matter-of-fact: “Maybe.”

    And Rio nodded.

    Didn’t push.

    Didn’t pry.

    Just stood there in the bunkhouse Agatha hadn’t let anyone inside in years, looking at it like it was more than a pile of wood and old stories. Like it mattered.

    Like she did.

    Agatha didn’t move.

    She wasn’t sure she could.

    The light had shifted again.

    It always did, out here, moved slow across the floorboards like time had been told to take its boots off before coming inside. Slanted amber spilled through the windows now, catching on dust motes and sanding marks and the loose curls of wood shavings scattered near the far wall. Agatha watched Rio move through the space.

    Not directly.

    Just… sideways. Through half-lowered lashes. Through the sound of her steps. Through the way the boards creaked just a little differently under her weight than they did under Agatha’s.

    Rio didn’t fill the room. She didn’t try.

    But she belonged in it in a way Agatha hadn’t expected.

    She walked slow. Careful. Like she was tracing a memory she’d never been told. Her fingers grazed the edge of the table, the top of a windowsill, the chipped enamel on the basin that hadn’t held water in a decade. Agatha didn’t speak.

    Didn’t move.

    Just leaned against the support beam, arms crossed, chewing on the inside of her cheek.

    She watched Rio pause by one of the beds. It was little more than a frame, the springs rusted and groaning faintly when she nudged it with one knee. But she didn’t seem put off. Just tilted her head, examining the welds at the joints, the curve of the headboard, the initials scratched into the metal. “Who’s JW?” Rio asked softly.

    Agatha blinked. “What?”

    Rio pointed. “Initials. Scratched here. Real light.”

    Agatha stepped forward without meaning to, drawn in by the question. Sure enough, etched in the curve of the upper rail—so faint she’d almost missed it—were the letters JW and the year ’86.

    Agatha frowned.

    “Could’ve been Jim Walker,” she muttered. “Worked the ranch for a season, back when I was ten.”

    “Did he stay in this one?”

    “Yeah. Think so.”

    Rio nodded. “Guess it left a mark.”

    Agatha huffed a dry sound too soft to be called a laugh. “Some folks always do.”

    That silence fell again, but it felt different now.

    Thicker.

    Softer.

    Rio turned away from the bed and walked toward the far window. Her fingers touched the glass, smudging a line through the dirt. She looked out toward the trees, where a hawk circled overhead.

    Then—still not looking at her—she asked, “You ever think about hiring help again?”

    Agatha didn’t answer right away.

    Her hand closed loosely around the post beside her, the wood rough and solid under her palm.

    “Thought about it.”

    “But?”

    “Too much trouble. Not enough payoff.”

    Rio glanced back, brow raised. “Even for the company?”

    Agatha’s mouth twitched.

    Just slightly.

    “You think ranch hands make good company?”

    Rio shrugged. “You let me stay.”

    Agatha’s jaw clenched.

    That wasn’t the same and she knew it. But the words still sat there, heavy, waiting to be confirmed or denied.

    She walked toward the table, fingers trailing along the edge as she passed. When she stopped, she was only a few feet from Rio. Not close enough to touch. But close enough to feel it.

    “The bunkhouse’s just a building,” she said. “Don’t read too much into it.” “I’m not reading into it,” Rio said. “I’m just… noticing.” Agatha looked at her then.

    Really looked.

    Rio’s expression was unreadable, but not cold. Not closed. Just open in a way that made Agatha feel like someone had peeled the roof off her ribs and left her out in the sun.

    It wasn’t judgment.

    It was… understanding.

    And that was worse.

    Agatha turned away.

    Ran a hand along the wall again.

    “You always this good at makin’ people feel seen?” she muttered. Rio’s voice came soft. “Only when they let me.”

    That landed too clean.

    Too square in the center of her chest.

    She cleared her throat and walked toward the door. “We should head back. Storm’s movin’ in fast.”

    Rio didn’t argue.

    Didn’t tease.

    Just followed.

    And as Agatha shoved the sticking door closed behind them, she felt something stay behind. Something quiet. Something wordless. Like maybe the bunkhouse wasn’t empty anymore.

    Not really.

    Not after this.

    They hadn’t said anything since leaving the bunkhouse.

    The sky had dimmed by degrees, slow and golden, the kind of quiet dusk that settled on your shoulders before you noticed it. Clouds still hung out toward the western edge of the land, not quite threatening but present, waiting for something to push them forward.

    Rio had said she’d left her notebook near the barn and peeled off toward it. Agatha had nodded without looking. Truth was, she needed the second alone. Just one minute without those dark eyes watching her. Without that steady calm. Without the knowledge that she’d let someone into a space that hadn’t seen another soul in years and hadn’t hated it.

    She didn’t know what to do with that.

    Didn’t know what to do with her.

    Agatha leaned against the porch railing and tried not to think too hard. About the bunkhouse. About the way Rio had touched the old metal bedframe like it meant something. About the look she’d given her when she asked, “You fixing it up for someone?”

    About the fact that Agatha hadn’t had an answer.

    And about how Rio had never once asked if it was for her. Not out loud.

    Which made it worse, somehow.

    And then, just as the quiet was starting to settle into something manageable, Rio’s phone rang.

    Not loud. Not obnoxious. Just a clear little chime that came from somewhere out by the barn.

    Agatha stilled.

    She didn’t mean to listen.

    But she didn’t walk away either.

    The wind carried just enough of the conversation to reach the porch, muffled but audible. Not the words, exactly. Not most of them. Just the rhythm. The tone. The warmth.

    Spanish.

    Fast, fluid Spanish.

    Not like the clinical, measured voice Rio used when she was talking about data points or ecological runoff or soil acidity. This voice was bright. Quick. Familiar in a way that made Agatha ache.

    She couldn’t make out much.

    Just a laugh, low and real.

    And then words she didn’t know. A whole world she didn’t speak. “¡Ay Dios, no! Fue un hoyo. Ni lo vi venir.” (Oh God, no! It was a hole. I didn’t even see it coming.)

    “Estoy bien, lo juro. Agatha me ayudó.” (I’m fine, I swear. Agatha helped me.)

    “Sí, sí, ya me lo vendé. Está todo bien, Sofia.” (Yes, yes, I already wrapped it. Everything’s fine, Sofía.)

    Agatha froze at her name.

    Agatha me ayudó.

    She didn’t know what had come before it. Didn’t know what had followed. But hearing her name in Rio’s mouth, folded into that softer language, that real voice, it did something to her.

    She stood straighter without meaning to.

    Felt the railing under her palms like an anchor.

    She hadn’t realized how different Rio’s voice sounded when it wasn’t meant for her. How much warmer her voice got. How easy her laugh was. How close her world felt and how far.

    Another string of sentences followed. More fast Spanish. More words Agatha couldn’t translate. She caught a name, Sofia, maybe. Another laugh. And then—

    “Te extraño también. Llama cuando llegues, ¿sí? Okay. Besitos.” (I miss you too. Call me when you get there, okay? Okay. Kisses.) Silence.

    Agatha didn’t move.

    Didn’t blink.

    Just stared out across the pasture, where the sky had gone dark enough to blur the edges of the hills.

    She hadn’t understood a damn word of it.

    Not really.

    But she’d heard everything.

    Rio had someone. A voice on the other end of the line that made her smile. Someone to check in with. Someone who said goodbye with affection and meant it.

    Agatha had no idea what it would feel like to pick up a phone and expect love on the other end.

    She didn’t think she ever had.

    She stepped back from the railing, slowly, and reached for the screen door. Her hand stalled on the handle for a second too long.

    You’re not jealous, she told herself.

    You’re just lonely.

    And that was worse.

    Because jealousy meant wanting what someone else had.

    Loneliness meant not knowing if you could ever have it at all. The screen door creaked as she pulled it open and slipped inside. She didn’t hear Rio’s footsteps on the porch.

    Didn’t hear the way she paused outside before opening the door herself. But she did hear the wind rise, just enough to rattle the empty glasses in the sink.

    And for once, it was the silence inside the house that felt the heaviest. She didn’t want to sit still.

    Couldn’t, really.

    The kitchen felt too tight. The walls too close. The silence too full of things she didn’t know how to say. The sound of Rio’s voice still echoed somewhere behind her ribs, even though the words hadn’t been for her. Especially because they hadn’t.

    So she grabbed a work shirt from the hook by the door, pulled it on over her tank, and stepped out into the early dusk without a word. The barn wasn’t far.

    Her boots hit the dirt in a rhythm she didn’t try to soften. She welcomed the scrape, the crunch, the slight sting in her bad knee. It gave her something to focus on. Something that wasn’t the fact that Rio had someone who called her. Someone who existed outside the fence lines of this place and loved her.

    Agatha hadn’t even gotten the names of her own mother’s cousins right at the funeral.

    The barn door groaned as she shoved it open.

    The inside was still warm, air thick with the smell of hay and cedar and horse sweat. One of the geldings nickered in the far stall, nothing urgent. Just a soft acknowledgment. The wind outside stirred a bit of dust from the rafters, and it shimmered in the late light like gold caught midair. Agatha headed for the feed room.

    She didn’t need to be in there.

    But she needed something to do.

    The bracing on the far wall had been half-replaced a year ago, when one of the boards had warped in the rain. She’d meant to come back and reinforce the corner joint. Never had.

    So she found the hammer. Dug through the cluttered tool shelf for a handful of old nails. Pulled her gloves on with more force than necessary. Set to work without looking too closely at anything.

    Every swing of the hammer felt good.

    Grounding.

    It let her breathe in rhythm again.

    She was halfway through the second brace when she heard the soft crunch of footsteps behind her.

    Then the pause.

    Then Rio’s voice.

    “Barn this time?”

    Agatha didn’t turn around.

    “Somethin’ needed fixin’.”

    “That brace?”

    “Eventually.”

    A beat.

    “You’re not great at lying.”

    Agatha’s hammer froze mid-swing.

    She exhaled. Tight. Short.

    “Didn’t say I was.”

    Rio didn’t come closer.

    Just leaned, probably, against the doorway. The way her voice echoed slightly off the wood and hay made it easy to picture. Loose stance. Hands in pockets. That quiet presence that didn’t demand anything but still made the air feel like it meant more.

    “I didn’t mean to make you uncomfortable,” she said, voice low. Agatha glanced over her shoulder, just briefly.

    Rio’s face was unreadable in the fading light. But not cold. Never cold. “You didn’t,” Agatha muttered, turning back to the beam. “Just got work to do.”

    Another pause.

    “That bunkhouse… It’s not just a building, is it?”

    Agatha’s grip tightened around the hammer.

    She wanted to say it was.

    Wanted to brush it off. Make a joke. Dodge it like she always did. But instead she just said—

    “…It’s easier to fix a wall than explain it.”

    Rio didn’t respond right away.

    She let that sit there, like she respected how hard it had been to say it at all. Then she nodded once. “Yeah. I get that.”

    Agatha stared at the nail she hadn’t driven in yet.

    Her hands didn’t move.

    “I don’t offer things I don’t mean,” she said quietly.

    “I know.”

    “It’s just a building.”

    “Okay.”

    “But I wouldn’t’ve shown it to you if I didn’t…” Her jaw locked. “…If I didn’t trust you.”

    Another long silence.

    The kind where the only sound was breath and the occasional soft stomp from the horse down the aisle.

    Then Rio stepped forward.

    Just a little.

    Not close enough to crowd.

    But close enough for Agatha to feel the shift in the air. “Then I won’t take that for granted,” she said.

    Agatha didn’t look at her, didn’t thank her, didn’t move. But she set the hammer down—slow, deliberate—and let her shoulders drop just an inch.

    It wasn’t surrender.

    But it wasn’t distance either.

    And that, for her, was something.

    The rain came in hard, when it finally came.

    Thunder had been growling in the distance for over an hour, a low, drawn- out rumble that threaded itself into the floorboards and the air and the back of Agatha’s neck. It hit just as she finished putting the tools away, sudden and sharp, the sky cracking open like a split log, heavy drops hammering the tin roof in a quick, angry rhythm.

    She and Rio had made it back inside without saying much.

    They were both damp from the sprint across the yard. Not soaked, but close. Rio’s hair had curled tighter at the ends, small ringlets clinging to her jaw. Agatha didn’t look at her for more than a second.

    She didn’t trust herself to.

    Now they sat in the front room, not quite facing each other. Agatha was in her usual chair, battered, square-backed, worn smooth at the arms. Rio sat opposite on the low settee, legs folded underneath her, shoulders curled forward slightly. She held a chipped mug of tea Agatha had made without asking. Plain, hot, no sugar. It hadn’t needed words. The fire snapped and hissed, small but steady. Enough to push back the chill rolling in behind the rain.

    Outside, the storm battered the porch and gutters and the side of the house like it was trying to find its way inside.

    Inside, it was quiet.

    Not tense. Not awkward.

    Just still.

    The kind of stillness Agatha wasn’t used to letting anyone share with her. She sipped her own tea, barely tasting it. Mostly holding it for warmth. She let her eyes flick toward Rio in the low light, just long enough to catch the shape of her. The slope of her neck. The shine of her damp skin. The way her eyes had gone half-lidded, not tired, just quiet.

    “Sorry,” Agatha muttered after a while. “For… earlier.”

    Rio looked over.

    “Don’t be.”

    Agatha stared into the fire. “I didn’t mean to be short.” “You weren’t.”

    “I was.”

    Rio smiled a little, barely there. “Okay. You were.”

    They didn’t laugh. But something between them eased, just slightly. Agatha set her mug down on the table beside her. Leaned forward, elbows on knees, hands clasped between them like she was trying to keep them from doing anything else. She wasn’t good at sitting still with people. She wasn’t good at silence that didn’t come with work to do.

    But she stayed.

    So did Rio.

    Minutes passed.

    The storm didn’t let up.

    The windows rattled. The fire cracked again. Somewhere in the back pasture, a windchime rang once, sharp, distant, then gone. Rio shifted, just barely, and stretched her legs out. One foot brushed lightly against Agatha’s boot. Not quite intentional.

    Impossible to ignore.

    Agatha didn’t move.

    Didn’t look down.

    Just stayed exactly where she was, gaze locked on the flames, every muscle tight and careful and quietly open.

    It was the closest she knew how to be right now.

    And Rio didn’t ask for more.

    She sipped her tea again. Let her leg stay there, just touching. And Agatha—

    She didn’t pull away.

    She didn’t look over.

    But she didn’t move either.

    And maybe that was the point.

    Maybe stillness, shared, chosen, was its own kind of intimacy. And maybe, for tonight, it was enough.

    Chapter End Notes I love writing them in this stage, the quiet before either of them has language for what they’re feeling. Just touch, silence, and the smallest acts of trust.

    Thanks for reading!

    Chapter 10: One Step Forward

    Chapter Summary Rio makes bread.

    Agatha pretends she doesn’t care.

    Between touch that lingers too long, trust learned on the ground, and the kind of quiet that says more than either of them can, Agatha finds herself slipping, slowly, stubbornly, undeniably.

    Some things you can fix with your hands.

    Some things you can only feel.

    Chapter Notes This chapter is brought to you by: Agatha “I don’t want anything” Harkness getting offered something gently and immediately forgetting how to function.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes It was the bread that did it.

    Not a grand gesture. Not a formal invitation. Just a quiet clatter in the kitchen, barely audible from where Agatha stood in the hallway near the back of the house, followed by the smell of something warm. Fresh.

    Goddamn comforting.

    She didn’t move at first.

    Just stood there with her hand on the doorframe, one boot scuffed against the wall like she’d meant to leave and forgotten how. Her shoulders were tense. Her mouth set in a flat line. She hated the way her body betrayed her, pausing just to listen.

    The soft hum of movement.

    The clang of a pan.

    Then Rio’s voice, low and easy, not meant to carry.

    Not singing. Not talking.

    Just… present.

    Agatha inhaled once, slow.

    Then stepped into the kitchen.

    Rio was by the stove.

    Hair twisted up in a loose knot at the back of her neck. Rolled sleeves. Hands dusted with flour. There was a pot on the back burner and a towel over something on the counter, rising dough, maybe. The room was warm, and not only from the stove.

    She didn’t look surprised when Agatha came in.

    Just glanced over and said, “I found some yeast in the pantry. Hope you don’t mind.”

    Agatha grunted. “Ain’t my yeast.”

    Rio smiled without showing teeth. “Consider it borrowed, then.” Agatha hovered by the table.

    Didn’t sit.

    Didn’t ask what she was making.

    Didn’t know how.

    Because she wasn’t used to this. Wasn’t used to someone being in her kitchen, her space, her life, not with this kind of quiet confidence. Not doing something just because they could. Because it might make her day easier. Warmer. Better.

    She watched Rio move, calm, unhurried, competent. Like she’d grown up in kitchens like this. Like the old gas range didn’t bother her. Like the chipped counter and the wobbly drawer didn’t matter. Like she belonged. That unsettled Agatha more than she could admit.

    “You cook much?” she asked, aiming for neutral.

    Rio nodded, not looking up. “All the time.”

    “For work?”

    “For home.”

    Agatha raised an eyebrow. “You have time to cook when you’re out there collecting weeds?”

    Rio laughed, soft. “Botany’s more than weeds.”

    “Didn’t answer the question.”

    Rio looked over her shoulder then. Not sharp. Not smug. Just… open. “I make time. I like the process. Makes my brain stop spinning.” Agatha said nothing to that.

    Didn’t have anything to say.

    Because that was too familiar. Too close to home.

    Rio turned back to the stove and stirred something in the pot. “Growing up,” she said, tone lighter now, “my sister Sofia and I used to race to see who could cook faster. She was better at presentation. I was better at not setting things on fire.”

    “Important skill,” Agatha muttered.

    Rio smiled. “We had this old toaster that sparked every time we used it. Swore it was haunted. My sister made me unplug it before I even looked at the bread.”

    Agatha’s lip twitched.

    Just barely.

    It caught her off guard.

    She looked away before it could settle into a smile.

    “You talk to her a lot?” she asked.

    Rio nodded. “Almost every day.”

    Agatha said nothing.

    Didn’t know how to explain the hollow in her gut at that answer and she didn't want to.

    Rio opened the oven, peeked inside, and said, “Bread will be ready in about twenty. I made enough for two.”

    There it was.

    The offer.

    Small. Simple.

    But heavy, in the way that mattered.

    She didn’t say you should eat with me.

    She didn’t say I thought of you.

    She didn’t say I want you here.

    But she didn’t have to.

    Agatha stood frozen for a beat too long. The kind of pause that meant yes but didn’t know how to say it out loud.

    She shifted her weight. Looked at the table. Then at the door. Then back again.

    “I got somethin’ I need to check in the barn,” she said gruffly. Rio nodded. “Okay.”

    She didn’t look hurt. Didn’t look surprised.

    Just kept kneading gently, like she’d expected that answer, and was leaving the door open anyway.

    “Bread will be warm when you get back,” she said, voice easy. Agatha didn’t respond.

    She just walked out the door, boots heavy on the floorboards, heart heavier still.

    But she didn’t go to the barn.

    Not right away.

    She stood on the porch for a minute instead, staring out at the pasture, arms crossed tight over her chest like she was bracing for weather that wasn’t coming.

    She didn’t know how to accept softness.

    Didn’t know how to say thank you without feeling like she owed something back.

    But she knew—deep in her chest—that when she came back inside, she’d sit at that table.

    And she’d eat the bread.

    And she’d let it mean something.

    Even if she never said a word.

    She didn’t say anything when she stepped through the door. Just nodded once at the table, at the plate already set with two uneven slices and a jar of jam between them like it wasn’t something that mattered. She ate it without speaking.

    Rio didn’t push.

    They chewed in silence. No eye contact. But something had eased between them. Not all the way. But enough.

    It was after the bread, after the second slice, after Agatha had pretended she didn’t catch the way Rio had hesitated before offering her the last piece, that the words came out: “You ever been on a horse?”

    Rio blinked.

    Looked up slow.

    “Once,” she said. “Trail ride, touristy. I think the horse was half-asleep the whole time.”

    Agatha huffed. “Sounds about right.”

    Rio tilted her head. “Why?”

    Agatha shrugged. “Thought I’d teach you.”

    The offer hung there, too quiet to be casual.

    Rio smiled. Soft. “Sure.”

    They didn’t talk much on the walk to the corral.

    The storm had passed the night before, leaving the air damp and clean, the dirt packed just firm enough under their boots. The horses were scattered across the pasture, but Agatha gave a sharp whistle, and one of the mares lifted her head and started walking over.

    Rio watched with a quiet sort of reverence.

    “She always come when you call?”

    “Most of the time.”

    “She got a name?”

    “June.”

    “Like the month?”

    Agatha nodded. “Named her after the first warm week that didn’t break my back in half.”

    Rio laughed, low and breathy.

    Agatha led June into the corral and handed Rio the lead rope. “You ever haltered a horse before?”

    “Nope.”

    “Good. You’ll do it right.”

    She showed her, slow, steady, deliberate. Rio watched her hands more than her words. She picked it up fast, mimicking her grip, her posture, the way she stood at the horse’s shoulder instead of in front of her. “Don’t crowd her,” Agatha murmured. “You want her trust, not control.” Rio nodded once. Quietly focused.

    And Agatha—

    She watched her more than she should have.

    The way she moved, the slight furrow in her brow when the knot didn’t sit quite right, the careful way her fingers brushed the rope like she was trying not to make too much noise with them.

    “You’re good with her,” Agatha said before she could stop herself. Rio glanced up, surprised. “She’s doing all the work.”

    Agatha snorted. “Takes more than passin’ grades to get that calm.” She took a step forward then. Close enough to touch. Close enough that her hand rested lightly over Rio’s where it held the halter, correcting her grip. “Not like that,” she murmured. “Too tight.”

    She guided Rio’s fingers, slow.

    Rio didn’t flinch.

    And that was the problem.

    Agatha’s chest was suddenly too tight.

    She pulled her hand back, subtle. Pretended to adjust the lead. “We’ll work on groundwork first,” she said, voice low. “Trust starts on the ground.”

    Rio nodded. “Makes sense.”

    They circled the pen once. Then again. June moved slow, patient, adjusting her weight without protest. Agatha explained how to read her, ears, posture, stride. Rio followed each point with that same quiet intensity, eyes sharp, mind working.

    But Agatha’s focus kept slipping.

    To the way their hands brushed now and then on the lead. To the way Rio stood a little too close when she turned to ask something. To the faint scent of warm skin and flour and horse between them. She cleared her throat.

    “You’re a quick study,” she muttered.

    “I pay attention.”

    Agatha glanced at her.

    That was an understatement.

    Rio didn’t just listen, she saw. She read things the way Agatha read weather: tension in shoulders, hesitation in movement, weight behind silence.

    And right now, Agatha could feel herself being read like an open field. She hated it.

    She didn’t move away.

    June stood patient and solid as ever, shifting her weight from one hind leg to the other as Agatha led her to the mounting block.

    The mare was as steady as they came, good for beginners, smooth gait, hard to spook. But that didn’t stop the pressure that tightened low in Agatha’s ribs as she turned to look at Rio and realized, suddenly, that this next part was going to require touching her.

    Not brushing her hand.

    Not guiding a rope.

    Actual contact.

    And Rio was looking at her like she trusted her.

    Agatha cleared her throat.

    “Alright,” she muttered, voice rough. “Put your left foot in the stirrup—this one here. Grab the horn, then swing your leg over.”

    Rio gave her a look. “Swing?”

    Agatha smirked. “Climb with conviction.”

    Rio raised an eyebrow, but did as instructed. She braced her hands, adjusted her grip—

    And got halfway up before her foot slipped.

    Agatha stepped in on instinct—fast, hand at Rio’s back, steadying her with one palm pressed between her shoulder blades, the other catching her thigh just above the knee.

    She shouldn’t have touched her there.

    She hadn’t meant to.

    It was just instinct, hold her, keep her from falling, make sure she didn’t twist anything important.

    But then Rio looked down at her.

    And Agatha realized how close she was.

    Face inches from her boot. One hand on her thigh. The other still braced across her back. The weight of her between her arms. The smell of dust and sweat and skin.

    Agatha backed off fast—too fast—stepping away like she’d touched something hot.

    “You alright?” she asked, sharper than intended.

    Rio nodded, sliding the rest of the way into the saddle. “Yeah. Just caught my foot.”

    Agatha didn’t respond.

    She busied herself adjusting the stirrups instead, pretending like she wasn’t still on fire.

    “Boot heel’s gotta sit flat here,” she said gruffly. “Not jammed in. You want control, not entrapment.”

    Rio lifted her leg.

    Agatha caught it.

    Held it steady.

    She adjusted the stirrup slowly, fingers brushing the soft worn leather of Rio’s boot. Then her ankle. Then just the curve of her calf as she eased it into place.

    She tried not to think about it.

    About how solid Rio’s leg felt under her hand. How close her hip was to Agatha’s shoulder. How still she was, not stiff, not tense, just waiting. Letting her touch her.

    Agatha stood back with a breath like she'd been holding it for minutes. “Other leg,” she said.

    Rio shifted. Agatha moved to the other side and did it again. Too slow. Too careful.

    She felt ridiculous.

    When she straightened this time, Rio met her eyes.

    Calm.

    Steady.

    Unbothered.

    Like this was normal. Like she liked being looked at. Like she wanted to be seen.

    And Agatha—

    She swallowed once.

    “You’re sittin’ real pretty,” she said before she could slam the words back down where they belonged.

    The second the words left her mouth, her brain screamed. Fuck.

    She hadn’t meant it like that. Hadn’t meant to say it at all. It was just a phrase that popped out when the saddle looked clean and the posture was good and—

    Rio tilted her head.

    Smiled.

    Not wide. Not smug.

    Just warm.

    Like she’d noticed. Like she wasn’t going to let it slide. But wasn’t going to call her out on it either.

    Agatha looked away so fast she nearly gave herself whiplash. “I mean—you’re seated well. Balance’s good. That’s all I meant.” “Of course,” Rio said, utterly unbothered.

    Agatha turned away, walked a few paces toward the gate, hands on her hips like she could physically burn the embarrassment out of her chest. She wanted to crawl into the dirt.

    She wanted to grab a shovel, dig a trench, and disappear into it. Behind her, she heard the soft creak of leather as Rio shifted her weight. She didn’t laugh. She didn’t tease. But the silence was too full. “Do I get to learn how to move now?” she asked, voice light. Agatha cleared her throat.

    Twice.

    “Yeah,” she said. “We’ll start slow.”

    Rio nudged June forward with the gentlest shift of pressure. The mare responded without hesitation.

    And Agatha—

    Agatha walked beside them, jaw tight, heart louder than hoofbeats. She’d never flirted in her life.

    Not like that.

    Not on accident.

    Not while standing next to a woman in her saddle, all calm and composed and looking at her like she liked what she saw.

    She was going to die.

    But not today.

    Because today she had to teach Rio how to ride.

    And survive her own goddamn hands in the process.

    June moved at a steady walk, ears flicking forward and back like she was taking her own measure of things. The ground was still soft from the storm, but not too slick, and the morning heat hadn’t settled yet, so the air was light and moving.

    Agatha walked beside them.

    She kept her pace even with the mare’s front hooves, her eyes flicking between the rhythm of the stride and the set of Rio’s shoulders. She wasn’t sure what she was watching for.

    Mistake, maybe. Panic. The moment Rio lost her balance or stiffened too much or got thrown by the motion of something that wasn’t wholly predictable.

    But it didn’t happen.

    Rio rode like she listened.

    Not just to Agatha. To the horse. To the ground. To the rhythm underneath her.

    She was still new to it, hands a little too cautious on the reins, posture a little too upright, but she wasn’t afraid. And she wasn’t trying to control it, either.

    Just feel it.

    Trust it.

    “You’re leanin’ too much in your hips,” Agatha said after a few minutes. “Loosen your knees.”

    Rio adjusted without question.

    “How’s that?”

    “Better.”

    They went a few more laps around the corral. Slow, easy arcs. The mare kept her head low and relaxed, chewing softly on the bit, tail swishing. Agatha gave minimal direction, just the occasional muttered instruction, a short point or nod when Rio did something right.

    It was quiet in the way she liked best.

    Work quiet.

    No pressure. No talking just to fill the air. No noise that didn’t matter. Just the sound of hooves, the light creak of tack, and the occasional buzz of cicadas in the grass beyond the fence line.

    Agatha exhaled slowly. Let her hands rest on her hips.

    Rio turned the mare with an instinct that was better than it should’ve been. “You pick this up fast,” Agatha muttered.

    Rio smiled, keeping her eyes ahead. “It’s just a balance thing, right?” Agatha gave a dry laugh. “Yeah. Sure. Tell that to the folks who’ve fallen on their ass tryin’ to master it.”

    “I used to take dance lessons,” Rio said, surprising her. “Salsa. You’ve gotta feel the beat under you, not just follow it.”

    Agatha looked at her sidelong. “You’re sayin’ ridin’s like dancin’?” Rio glanced down at her. “Isn’t it?”

    Agatha opened her mouth. Closed it again.

    She’d never thought of it like that.

    But now she couldn’t unsee it, Rio’s hands light on the reins, her body loose but upright, her weight shifting in response to the horse instead of dictating it.

    Like a lead that listened.

    She cleared her throat.

    “That’s… not wrong.”

    Rio smiled.

    There was that quiet between them again, but it didn’t feel charged this time. Just still. Easy.

    Like maybe something had settled between them.

    “You trust her now?” Agatha asked after a while, nodding toward the mare. Rio looked down, thoughtful. Then reached forward and ran a hand gently along June’s neck.

    “She’s the one doing all the work,” she said. “But yeah. I think I do.” “Good.”

    “Do you trust me?” she asked, sudden, soft.

    Agatha stopped walking.

    Rio didn’t.

    The mare kept walking for another few paces, and then Rio pulled gently on the reins, halting her.

    She didn’t look back.

    Just waited.

    Agatha stared at the ground.

    Then at her boots.

    Then at the back of Rio’s shoulder.

    And finally said, “You ain’t given me a reason not to.”

    Rio nodded, still not turning.

    “Alright.”

    She clicked her tongue softly, nudging June forward again. Agatha fell back into step beside her.

    It wasn’t an answer.

    Not really.

    But it wasn’t a no.

    And for now it was enough.

    They brought June back to the barn without much fanfare. Rio dismounted with more grace than Agatha expected, swung her leg over, slid down slow, boots hitting the dirt with just a puff of dust. She patted the mare’s neck gently, murmuring something low that Agatha didn’t catch. It didn’t matter.

    June leaned into her.

    Agatha led them through the barn gate and into the aisle, already grabbing a lead to clip to the halter. She didn’t say anything when Rio stayed close, just handed her a curry comb without looking and nodded toward the flank. They worked side by side.

    Agatha took the front. Rio took the back.

    The soft rasp of metal teeth against hide was the only sound between them for a while. That, and June’s occasional shift of weight, the faint brush of her tail flicking away a fly, the muted creak of old wood settling around them.

    Agatha watched Rio out of the corner of her eye.

    She brushed slow. Careful. Focused. Her brow furrowed slightly in concentration, mouth pressed in a line that looked like thought, not tension. Agatha cleared her throat.

    “You did good,” she said.

    Rio looked up, surprised. “Thanks.”

    “You got a good seat. You listen.”

    “You’re a good teacher.”

    Agatha huffed. “I didn’t say much.”

    “Exactly.”

    That startled a sound from Agatha that wasn’t quite a laugh. Just a breath with weight behind it. She turned back to the horse, brushing a little harder than necessary.

    They finished in silence.

    Agatha leaned against the stall door while Rio unbuckled the halter and let June wander into her space. The mare nosed at the hay, satisfied, and gave one last swish of her tail before settling.

    Rio dusted her hands off. Turned to Agatha.

    “You’re quiet again,” she said.

    Agatha shrugged.

    “Too much talkin’ wears me out.”

    “Fair.” Rio said, easy Rio didn’t leave the barn.

    Didn’t walk away.

    Just stood there beside her, not touching, not crowding, just being there. Agatha stared straight ahead.

    Out toward the open doors, where light spilled across the dust in long streaks. The sky was going gold again. Second day in a row it had looked like that, like the sun was trying to apologize for something. “I was gonna make lentil stew tonight,” Rio said, voice casual. “With cornbread, if I can figure out how to light that oven without burning my eyebrows off.”

    Agatha didn’t respond.

    Rio glanced over.

    “You’re welcome to join.”

    Agatha’s hands clenched, just slightly, where they rested on the stall gate. She didn’t look at her.

    Didn’t have to.

    The offer hung there like it had earlier—gentle, open, without expectation. And it was worse because she knew it wasn’t pity. Rio wasn’t offering out of politeness or obligation. She just wanted to share something. And that was a lot harder to sidestep.

    Agatha opened her mouth. Closed it. Swallowed.

    Then finally muttered, “I’ll see how long the water line takes me. Could be a while.”

    Rio nodded once. “Alright.”

    No disappointment.

    No pressure.

    Just acknowledgment.

    “But,” Agatha added, too quiet, barely audible, “if the stew’s still warm when I’m done…”

    She trailed off.

    Didn’t finish the sentence.

    Rio smiled.

    Not wide. Not smug. Just real.

    “The offer’ll still be there,” she said.

    And Agatha—

    She didn’t smile.

    But she stayed in the barn for a minute longer.

    Just to find out what it felt like not to walk away.

    Chapter End Notes Thank you for reading! I swear every time I try to write something calm for them, Agatha immediately behaves like she’s been emotionally waterboarded by a single touch.

    Chapter 11: Two Steps Back

    Chapter Summary A simple supply run turns into something heavier when town eyes linger too long and say too much. Agatha shuts down, spirals inward, and tries to smoke the feeling out of her chest. But when Earl makes the mistake of talking about Rio like she’s disposable, Agatha doesn’t hesitate, she defends her without even thinking.

    The rest of the day unfolds in small silences and unspoken things: Rio giving her space, Agatha not knowing how to accept it, and both of them trying to navigate a kind of closeness that feels fragile and real. By nightfall, neither of them knows how to bridge the distance, but neither walks away from it, either.

    Chapter Notes Welcome to the chapter where the world edges back in.

    This one’s got: Town gossip doing what town gossip does.

    Agatha retreating into silence so hard she might implode. Rio staying calm and kind, even when it hurts.

    And Agatha finally stepping between her and something sharp. It's tense. It's quiet. it’s full of things they don’t know how to say. If you’re looking for the emotional equivalent of a pulled muscle, this is it.

    We’re in the middle stretch of the slow burn now, where every look and every pause hits harder than it should.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes They needed feed and fencing nails. That was it.

    In and out. Maybe twenty minutes, if the damn post office line wasn’t long. She could count the errands on one hand, had run through them twice before they even pulled onto the county road. Just errands. Just supplies. Just another day.

    But the second they turned onto Main Street, Agatha felt it. That shift.

    That quiet kind of noticing that settled behind her ribs and tightened her shoulders.

    It wasn’t anything dramatic. Not at first. Just too many glances that lasted half a second too long. People who said hello with too-bright smiles. The woman at the general store counter who looked from Agatha to Rio and then back again like she was trying to do the math.

    And it wasn’t like they were standing close.

    They weren’t even walking side by side most of the time. Rio wandered the far shelves, inspecting brands of feed like she could recite every ingredient list from memory. Agatha stayed near the front, talking fencing sizes with the clerk, mentally counting how much weight the truck could take on the drive back.

    But none of that mattered.

    Because people looked.

    And Agatha had lived in this town long enough to know what that look meant.

    It was the same one they used to give her when she was sixteen and sharp- eyed and didn’t smile for boys. The same one they gave her mother when she came in alone to buy whisky and feed and wouldn’t answer questions about her husband. The same one that always meant something but never had the guts to say it.

    And now they were giving it to Rio.

    Agatha caught it in the tilt of a chin. The too-quick glance away. The way someone asked, “And who’s your friend?” with a smile that was all sugar and teeth.

    She didn’t answer.

    Let Rio speak for herself. Let her introduce her full name, Rio Vidal, botanist, working on a research project for the season. She said it politely. Calmly. The way she always did.

    But Agatha could feel the shift behind the counter. The thin, polite judgement thick in the air. She could practically hear the questions they weren’t asking.

    She’s not from here, is she?

    She’s staying at your place?

    And what kind of name is “Rio” anyway?

    Agatha clenched her jaw.

    They left the store not long after. The fencing nails were in the bed of the truck. The feed was paid for. Nothing went wrong.

    Except everything had.

    They were halfway back to the truck when she heard it, low, just behind her, one voice to another.

    “Didn’t know she was hiring help again.”

    Agatha didn’t turn around.

    Didn’t say anything.

    But her spine went stiff, her hands in fists at her sides. “Don’t mind them,” Rio said gently, walking just behind her. “It’s just curiosity.”

    Agatha didn’t respond.

    She wanted to say it wasn’t. That she knew curiosity, and this wasn’t it. This was something sharper. Something more invasive. She wanted to say they weren’t looking at her like that before you showed up. But she didn’t.

    She kept walking.

    They stopped at the post office. Rio offered to go inside. Agatha let her. Stayed leaning against the truck, arms crossed, staring straight ahead like the silence might drown out the noise in her own head.

    The wind picked up.

    Dust swirled near the sidewalk.

    And still, she felt it, the weight of eyes on her back.

    When Rio came back out, letters tucked under one arm, she was smiling faintly. Like nothing had shifted. Like the world hadn’t tilted sideways in the span of a half-hour errand run.

    Agatha didn’t say a word on the drive home.

    And Rio—maybe wisely—didn’t ask why.

    The truck rolled to a stop in its usual place by the shed, but the silence didn’t shift.

    Not even when Agatha turned the key and the engine shuddered off. Not when the tick of the cooling metal started up beneath the hood. Not when Rio shifted in the passenger seat and reached for the door handle like it was her cue to leave first.

    Agatha stayed seated.

    One hand still on the wheel. The other already fishing in the glovebox for the pack she told herself she didn’t keep there anymore.

    She hadn’t smoked in front of Rio. Not directly. Not really. Maybe a few times, early on, when Rio had been off in the field and Agatha had taken a minute on the porch with her back turned. But she hadn’t let herself light one around her. Hadn’t wanted to see what kind of judgment it might bring.

    But now?

    Now her hands were shaking. And her lungs felt too tight. And her chest buzzed with the kind of ache that only ever got worse in stillness. She pulled a cigarette from the pack. Lit it one-handed with a flick of her old lighter. The smell hit her like memory: smoke, dried grass, smething almost metallic underneath.

    Rio didn’t comment.

    She just stepped out of the truck, boots soft in the gravel, and walked around back to unload the feed like nothing had happened. Like Agatha hadn’t gone full statue-mode the entire drive home.

    Agatha stayed where she was for another minute, door still open, smoke curling past her face, eyes unfocused on the barn in the distance. She inhaled deep.

    Held it.

    Then exhaled slow, through her teeth.

    The cigarette didn’t fix anything. It never did. But it gave her something to do. Something to hold. Something to keep her from clawing her way out of her own skin.

    By the time she got out and started helping with the unloading, she’d smoked most of it down to the filter. Stomped it out in the dirt near the steps. Didn’t look at Rio once.

    She didn’t offer an explanation.

    Didn’t say, It’s not you.

    Didn’t say, I just can’t stand being looked at like that again. She just worked.

    Faster than necessary. More force than needed. Her body moving in ways that said don’t ask.

    Rio didn’t.

    But Agatha could feel her watching.
