    Not judging.

    Not pushing.

    Just seeing her.

    And somehow, that made it worse.

    When the last of the feed was stacked in the shed and the gate was latched, Agatha turned and muttered something about needing to check the back line.

    She left without waiting for a reply.

    Didn’t know if Rio gave one.

    The walk to the fence felt longer than usual. Her boots kicked up dry clumps of grass, and the sun was hot on her neck despite the late hour. She pulled her hat down lower. Tugged her gloves on tight. Gritted her jaw against the weight of her own silence.

    It wasn’t about shame.

    It wasn’t.

    She wasn’t ashamed of Rio. Wasn’t ashamed of the bunkhouse, or the food they shared, or the way they’d started existing in the same spaces like it meant something.

    But the second someone else looked—really looked—she’d folded in on herself like a damn trapdoor.

    Because Agatha Harkness didn’t know how to be seen.

    Not like that.

    And survive it.

    She found a corner post she’d replaced just last year and tightening the wire again anyway.

    Didn’t need it. Didn’t matter.

    She just needed something in her hands that wasn’t care she couldn’t accept.

    She smoked another cigarette on the way back to the house. Didn’t bother hiding it.

    She smelled like sweat and cedar and smoke when she stepped into the kitchen. Rio wasn’t there.

    The only sign she’d ever been inside were the dishes in the sink and the still-warm cornbread tucked under a towel by the stove.

    Agatha didn’t eat it.

    She sat at the kitchen table for a while, silent. Tense. Fingers twitching against the wood like they wanted to reach for something that wasn’t there. Eventually she stood.

    Opened the drawer where she kept her matches and smokes.

    Pulled one out.

    Lit it slow.

    And smoked like it was the only thing keeping her grounded to the floor. The knock came just after midday.

    Agatha had been on the porch, boots up on the railing, fourth cigarette of the day half-smoked between her fingers and the sun sitting hot above the roofline. The air was thick with mid-July weight, humming with the lazy buzz of insects and the creak of the wood beneath her chair. She almost didn’t hear it.

    Three short raps on the side of the frame, not the front door. She turned her head, slow.

    It was Earl.

    Sixty-something. Leathered face, always smiling like he’d said something clever, even when he hadn’t. Used to help her mother out back in the day. Had a ranch of his own up the road, smaller than hers, half of it leased out now to some hay operation.

    “Afternoon,” he said, stepping up like the porch was his. Agatha didn’t stand.

    Just tipped her chin. “Earl.”

    “You got a minute?”

    She didn’t. Not really.

    But she gestured with the cigarette anyway. “You’re here already.” He gave that laugh she hated, half-gravel, half-smirk.

    “Still sharp as ever,” he said, shifting his weight as he leaned against the rail. “Was out checking the fence by the east lot this morning. Thought I’d swing by. See how the new help’s settling in.”

    Her cigarette froze halfway to her mouth.

    She flicked ash off the end without looking at him. “New help?” “You know. That gal. The botanist.” He smiled like the word tasted funny. “Saw her at the feed store last week. Not exactly what I’d expect from ranch help. Real pretty, though. That kind of pretty don’t usually last long out here in the sun.”

    Agatha didn’t answer.

    Didn’t blink.

    Didn’t move.

    Earl kept talking.

    “Not complainin’,” he added. “I mean, I get it. You been up here alone long enough. Hell, I’d probably hire a little thing like that myself, if I thought she’d last more than a week.”

    He chuckled.

    Loud. Pleased with himself.

    And something in Agatha snapped.

    Not with a shout. Not with fists or fire.

    Just a long, slow exhale as she stood.

    Boots to floorboards.

    Cigarette still in hand.

    She stepped off the porch and turned to face him, slow and deliberate. And when she looked up, her gaze was ice.

    “You want to walk that back?” she asked.

    Earl blinked. “Now don’t get riled—”

    “You think I let folks come on my porch and run their mouth about the people who live here?”

    His smile faltered.

    “I didn’t mean nothin’ by it,” he said. “Just jokin’ around. You know how folks talk.”

    Agatha took one step closer.

    Then another.

    Until she was close enough for the smoke from her cigarette to catch in his breath.

    “Yeah,” she said. “I do.”

    There was a moment then.

    Heavy.

    Earl’s posture shifted, subtle, but there. A lean back. A twitch in the jaw. A flicker of something behind the eyes that knew he’d gone a step too far. Agatha didn’t raise her voice.

    Didn’t need to.

    “She’s here to work,” she said, low and flat. “She pulls her weight. She’s smart. She’s respectful. That’s more than I can say for most folks who come sniffin’ around this place.”

    Earl opened his mouth.

    Agatha didn’t let him speak.

    “She’s not your business. What she looks like? Not your business. Where she sleeps? What she eats? How long she’s stayin’? None of it is your goddamn business.”

    She let the words sit.

    Like heat rising off the dirt.

    Then she stepped back.

    Dropped her cigarette. Ground it into the porch with her boot. “You’re on my land,” she said. “Act like it.”

    Earl straightened.

    His smile was gone now. Replaced with something tight and stiff. He cleared his throat. Adjusted his hat.

    “I’ll let you get back to your day,” he said, almost polite. Agatha didn’t respond.

    She watched him walk off the porch. Down the path. Back toward his truck. He didn’t look back.

    The screen door creaked behind her.

    She turned, slow.

    Rio stood there.

    Hands in her back pockets. Face unreadable.

    Agatha didn’t know how long she’d been standing there.

    Didn’t know how much she’d heard.

    Didn’t ask.

    She just said, “He was leavin’ anyway.”

    Rio didn’t smile.

    Didn’t nod.

    She just held her gaze for a second longer than usual, like she was searching for something in Agatha’s expression that she didn’t expect to find. Agatha looked away.

    Lit another cigarette with hands that shook more than she wanted them to. The cigarette burned down faster than usual.

    Agatha didn’t smoke it so much as let it go. Let the heat creep into her fingers. Let the smoke sting her lungs. Let her mouth stay tight and silent even after Rio had gone back inside.

    She stood there on the porch for a long time.

    Watching the dust trail settle where Earl’s truck had been. Listening to the faint creak of the screen door still swaying behind her, just a little.

    She didn’t regret it.

    Not the words. Not the tone. Not the way she’d stepped in like the heat in her chest had nowhere else to go. But that didn’t mean it didn’t cost something. Every time she spoke up, there was a part of her that twisted, like the air was thinner now, like too many people had tried to teach her that defending someone was a sure way to get hurt.

    But she hadn’t even thought.

    That was the worst part.

    She’d heard it, the way Earl said that gal like it meant something else. The way he smiled around the edges of her name. The way he looked too long and spoke too easy about what lasted and what didn’t.

    And the reaction hadn’t been conscious.

    It had been reflex.

    Rio didn’t say thank you when they passed each other again that night. Not when Agatha came in to wash up, and she was already standing at the sink rinsing something that smelled like beans and garlic and cumin. Not when Agatha hovered awkwardly by the door, still tasting ash and anger in the back of her throat.

    Rio didn’t look over.

    Didn’t speak.

    Just slid the pot off the burner, wiped her hands on the towel at her hip, and stepped away to give her room at the sink.

    It wasn’t distance.

    It wasn’t coldness.

    It was just, room.

    Agatha washed her hands in silence. Let the water run longer than she needed it. Watched the dirt spiral into the drain. Watched her knuckles flex under the faucet like maybe her body could tell her something she hadn’t figured out yet.

    She didn’t say anything.

    But she stood there a second too long.

    And Rio didn’t ask what was wrong.

    Didn’t poke at it. Didn’t press.

    She just stirred the beans again. Stepped to the side. Pulled two bowls down from the shelf like it was just another night. Like the world hadn’t shifted just a little when Agatha raised her voice for her.

    Agatha didn’t know what to do with that.

    She dried her hands and sat at the table without being asked. She didn’t thank her for the food.

    Rio didn’t seem to need it.

    And maybe that was what settled hardest in Agatha’s chest, this sense that Rio understood something unspoken. That she wasn’t waiting for an apology or explanation or gratitude. That she knew exactly what it had meant for Agatha to stand up like that in front of someone who thought they knew her.

    And she didn’t make a scene.

    She didn’t say a word.

    She just stayed.

    Agatha ate half the bowl. More than usual. The flavors were stronger this time, deeper. She didn’t ask what was in it. Didn’t comment on how it burned just a little, in a way that wasn’t unpleasant. Just kept eating, slow, measured. Every few bites, she caught Rio watching her from the corner of her eye.

    Not waiting for approval.

    Just watching.

    Like maybe she needed to see Agatha wasn’t going to run.

    And Agatha didn’t.

    She finished her bowl.

    Did the dishes without being asked.

    And when she stepped out onto the porch later that evening, cigarette in one hand, lighter in the other, she found the screen door already open. Propped gently. Left ajar like an afterthought or maybe an invitation. Rio didn’t say thank you.

    She didn’t need to.

    Because she stayed in the house that night.

    Didn’t go to the truck.

    Didn’t close the door between them.

    And that was all the thanks Agatha needed.

    The screen door creaked once more just after dark, but this time it was Agatha slipping back inside.

    The cigarette was burned to the end. Her throat ached from it, not from the smoke itself, but the quiet it was masking. She didn’t know how long she’d been out there. Long enough for the stars to come out. Long enough for the porch to cool under her boots.

    Rio was still awake.

    The light in the kitchen was off, but there was a glow under the frame of the guest room down the hall. Not bright. Just soft enough to suggest she was moving around in there. Or maybe just sitting. Waiting out the night with a book or a journal or the thoughts she didn’t say out loud. It still caught Agatha off guard sometimes, seeing that strip of light under that door.

    A reminder that a few days ago, Rio had been sleeping in her damn truck like a stray cat with a PhD until Agatha had muttered, “You’re not stayin’ out there, spare room’s open,” and walked away before she could take it back.

    Rio had moved in without a fuss. No assumptions. No questions. Just a quiet, “Okay,” like she understood it for what it was, an invitation Agatha didn’t know how to say twice.

    Agatha didn’t go to her.

    She thought about it.

    Thought about knocking, or just walking past on her way to the sink, making eye contact like it meant something. But the weight in her chest was too tight, and the words in her throat were still splintered. So she walked slowly down the hall and turned instead into the living room. No TV. No noise. Just a low lamp near the window and the sound of the old wood floor creaking beneath her as she moved.

    She sat on the couch like it didn’t belong to her.

    Not slouched. Not at ease.

    Just… placed.

    Half-stiff. Barely breathing.

    Her hands curled around a throw pillow she hadn’t used in years. Her eyes locked on the window like she could see something out there in the dark. But there was nothing, just the shapes of the trees near the fence line. The distance between them wasn’t physical anymore.

    It was worse than that.

    It was the space in the hall. The silence at the table. The way Rio hadn’t asked why Agatha flinched in town, because maybe she already knew. And hadn’t asked again why Agatha was silent now, because maybe she understood that too.

    But knowing didn’t fix it.

    Understanding didn’t close the gap.

    Agatha shifted. Crossed one leg over the other. Uncrossed it again. Rubbed her thumb against the seam of her jeans until her nail caught. She didn’t know how to say it hurt.

    Didn’t know how to say that the quiet between them now wasn’t like the quiet that had come before. It wasn’t peace. It wasn’t calm. It was just absence. Like something had been set down and then left, and they were both pretending they hadn’t noticed.

    She hadn’t meant to retreat. Not really.

    She just… didn’t know how to accept kindness without feeling like she owed something back. And with Rio, the weight of that debt was heavier than usual. Not because Rio expected anything, God, no. But because she didn’t.

    And somehow that made it worse.

    The clock ticked.

    The walls creaked like they always did when the temperature dropped at night. A faint breeze caught one of the curtains, just enough to make the room move in soft shadows.

    Agatha let her head fall back against the couch. Closed her eyes. She didn’t cry.

    Didn’t even come close.

    But her hands were clenched so tight they ached.

    And down the hall, she knew—without knowing how she knew—that Rio was lying in bed, turned toward the wall, still awake.

    They didn’t speak.

    Didn’t check on each other.

    Didn’t try to name the thing between them.

    But they both thought about the kitchen light.

    About the sound of the other moving through the house.

    About the fact that neither one had gone out to the porch again. And neither had left the door open.

    And maybe that was the worst part.

    Not the distance itself.

    But the fact that it felt familiar.

    And neither of them knew how to fix it.

    Chapter End Notes Thank you for reading.

    I promise they’re getting there, even if Agatha has to smoke half a pack and nearly bite Earl’s head off first.

    Chapter 12: This Is the Best I Can Do

    Chapter Summary After days of silence, tension, and the weight of town gossip, Agatha finally seeks Rio out, only to realize how much damage she’s done by retreating. What follows is a quiet reckoning: one apology spoken in the dirt of the south field, one moment of patience offered back, and the fragile start of something that might finally grow if neither of them walks away.

    Chapter Notes This chapter sits in the aftermath of the last one, where silence did more damage than words could. Agatha’s struggling with being seen, the townsfolk are doing what small towns do best (unfortunately), and Rio… is still there. This is the chapter where one of them finally walks toward the other on purpose.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes The house was too still.

    Not the peaceful quiet she liked. The late-night kind, where everything settled and the air felt heavy in a way that meant the work was done. This was the waiting kind. The kind where every room felt like it was holding its breath.

    Agatha stood in the doorway for too long.

    Half of her wanted to go back out to the porch, light another cigarette, pretend her ribs didn’t ache from being too full of unspoken things. The other half was already stepping into her boots again.

    She didn’t grab her hat. Didn’t need it.

    The sun had started to fall behind the ridge, long streaks of late-afternoon gold cutting across the yard. It was warm, but not brutal. The breeze carried the dry scent of hay and dust and the first edge of coming evening. She walked.

    Not fast.

    Not with purpose.

    She didn’t know where Rio would be. Hadn’t asked. Hadn’t looked all day, and now that she was, she realized how much she'd missed the signs of her, boots by the back door, the faint clink of a glass left in the sink, the smell of shampoo that didn’t belong to her lingering in the hallway. She passed the bunkhouse. Empty.

    The barn. Just horses, chewing slow and indifferent.

    It wasn’t until she crested the gentle slope near the south field that she saw her.

    A figure bent near the fence line, not far from where the dry creek bed cut through the pasture. Notebook in one hand, pen moving in little stuttering bursts. Her jacket tied around her waist. Hair pulled up messily. Sleeves rolled.

    Still working.

    Still here.

    Agatha stopped walking.

    Her boots crunched softly in the dirt, but Rio didn’t turn. She was crouched low, near a patch of wild barley that had pushed up near the fence. Studying it. Scribbling something.

    And Agatha?

    She just… stood there.

    Watched her.

    Watching the easy set of Rio’s shoulders, the way her body stayed relaxed even alone. Watched how she was present, not just filling time. She could’ve gone back to town. Could’ve packed her things and said it wasn’t worth it. Could’ve left without slamming a door, without a word. But she hadn’t.

    And something about that—

    Something about the fact of it—

    Made Agatha’s chest go tight.

    Because this wasn’t just about town talk anymore. Or silence. Or her own damn inability to speak when it mattered.

    It was about seeing someone stay.

    Even when you gave them every reason not to.

    She shifted her weight. Cleared her throat without meaning to. Rio looked up.

    Not startled. Not tense. Just… calm.

    Like she’d known Agatha would come eventually.

    Her eyes met hers across the space, twenty, maybe thirty feet. Not close enough to feel it. But enough for Agatha to see the set of her mouth. The way she didn’t smile. But didn’t frown, either.

    Just watched.

    Waited.

    Agatha stood there for a moment longer, then finally took a step forward. Every step felt too loud.

    Not in the world, but in her head, boots hitting dirt with the kind of weight that made her shoulders tense and her mouth go dry.

    She wasn’t built for this kind of thing.

    Not for talking.

    Not for explaining herself.

    Not for walking toward someone kind and steady when all she’d done for days was shut down hard and fast like a trap closing around her own ribs. But she kept walking.

    Rio hadn’t moved.

    Not even an inch.

    She’d just shifted her weight slightly, her posture still but alert, watching like she did when a calf was about to make a break for the wrong gate, like she expected something careful and unpredictable to come her way and was just… letting it.

    Agatha had seen people wait before.

    Had seen plenty of versions of it, impatience disguised as patience, polite interest masking suspicion. She’d grown up around the sharpness of it, the kind that came from people who only waited because they were winding something up to use against you.

    But Rio wasn’t wound.

    She was still.

    She wasn’t waiting to catch her.

    She was just—there.

    And that was what kept messing with Agatha’s head.

    She shoved her hand deeper into her pocket, fingers curled around the old cigarette lighter she hadn’t realized she’d grabbed. It was muscle memory. A way to ground herself. She didn’t light one. Not yet. Not with Rio looking at her like that.

    The closer she got, the more it hit her, how many times she could’ve spoken before now. That night in the kitchen. On the porch. After Earl. The number of moments where she could’ve reached across the space and said just one honest thing.

    But she hadn’t.

    She’d let the silence win.

    Let the old fear crawl back in and whisper that needing people was a mistake. That letting someone stay close meant giving them a knife and turning your back.

    And now—

    Now she was standing ten feet away from the one person who hadn’t used any of it against her.

    Rio didn’t smile.

    She didn’t soften or speak or shift to make Agatha more comfortable. But there was no distance in her expression either.

    No wall.

    Just calm. Present. Like she was offering the space without asking anything back.

    Agatha hated how much that made her want to run.

    She looked past her, at the fence line, at the field, at the slow roll of clouds edging low near the horizon. Storm weather, maybe. Another excuse to shut herself in the barn and pretend there was work that couldn’t wait. But even that felt empty now.

    There wasn’t anything left to do but this.

    She took another step.

    Then another.

    Her boots sank slightly into the patchy, dry grass. The air was thick with heat and late-summer weight. It smelled like dust and horse and sage, like work. Like home. Like her.

    And Rio?

    Rio was still just… there.

    Notebook closed. Pen tucked against the spine. Her arms folded loosely across her chest, not defensive, just held. Her whole posture quiet, listening without needing to be told.

    Agatha’s hands flexed at her sides.

    She wanted to speak.

    Wanted to say you didn’t deserve any of that.

    Wanted to say it wasn’t about you.

    Wanted to say I didn’t mean to make you feel like you were alone here. But the words wouldn’t line up right.

    They never did.

    So instead, she stopped just a few feet away. Closer now. Close enough to see the smudge of dirt on Rio’s jeans, the sunburn starting along her collarbone, the little mark on her thumb from where she must’ve pressed the pen too hard.

    All the details that meant she stayed.

    And maybe Agatha’s heart clenched because of that. Because despite everything, despite the silence, despite the town, despite the fence she’d rebuilt around herself—

    Rio was still here.

    Agatha drew in a breath.

    Held it.

    And then let it out, slow.

    One more step.

    Close enough to speak, if she could just figure out how.

    And Rio?

    Rio didn’t say a word.

    She just waited.

    The silence between them stretched.

    But this time it wasn’t empty.

    It was full of breath and heat and words that hadn’t been spoken yet. It was full of the fact that Agatha had come here, had walked across the pasture and stood in the open with nothing but a mess of guilt and whatever the hell had started knotting itself into her ribs every time she saw Rio’s shoulders tighten.

    She still didn’t know what she was going to say.

    But doing nothing wasn’t an option anymore.

    So she reached for the first thing that felt close to truth. “I didn’t mean to be cruel.”

    It came out lower than she expected. Tighter. Rough from disuse, like it scraped on the way out. She wasn’t even sure if Rio heard it at first, not until those brown eyes lifted again, catching hers like they always did. Not soft. Not forgiving. Just… open.

    Agatha looked away.

    “That’s what it was, though. I know that.”

    The words weren’t easy. They fought her, wanted to stay buried, wanted to twist into something safer, something meaner. But she didn’t let them. She stood there and said them anyway.

    “I ain’t good at this,” she muttered, jaw tight. “Ain’t good at explaining myself, or talking when things go sideways. I don’t—”

    Her voice faltered.

    She forced herself to keep going.

    “I get real quiet when I’m tryin’ not to say the wrong thing. Always have. It’s not about you.”

    She risked a glance up.

    Rio hadn’t moved.

    She was listening. Really listening. Not rushing to interrupt. Not trying to ease the tension.

    Just… letting her speak.

    And God, that made it harder.

    Agatha pulled in a breath.

    Scratched her hand along the back of her neck. She could feel the sweat there, warm and slick, and the cigarette lighter still tucked deep in her pocket like some kind of anchor.

    “I let the silence do the talkin’ for me,” she said. “And I know what that sounds like. I do. I’ve been on the other side of it, too.” She looked away again, toward the fence. Toward anything else. “I didn’t mean to shut you out. I just—didn’t know how not to.” That was the closest she’d ever come to saying I’m sorry. And she hoped—God, she hoped—that Rio would hear it for what it was. “I didn’t want you to leave,” she added, voice barely above a whisper. “Even when I was actin’ like I did.”

    She felt exposed.

    More than exposed.

    She felt raw. Flayed open.

    Like saying it out loud had peeled something back she couldn’t hide behind anymore. She didn’t cry, she didn’t do that, but her throat burned like she’d swallowed something too big, and her hands wouldn’t stay still. Rio hadn’t said a word.

    She just watched.

    Agatha took one more shaky breath.

    “Anyway,” she muttered, like it meant nothing, like it hadn’t just taken everything she had to speak, “that’s all.”

    She shifted back a half step.

    It felt like she’d already said too much.

    But before she could turn—

    Rio finally moved.

    Not fast. Not dramatic. Just… stepped forward a half pace. Enough to close the space.

    Enough to stay.

    And she didn’t say “I forgive you.”

    Didn’t say “It’s okay.”

    Didn’t say anything useless or sweet or easy.

    She just said, quietly: “I know.”

    And Agatha—

    God help her, she believed her.

    “I know.”

    That was all she said.

    No follow-up. No soft little laugh to take the edge off. No asking for more. Just two words.

    And somehow, it landed heavier than anything Agatha had prepared herself for.

    She wasn’t sure what she’d expected. A nod? A shrug? Maybe a quiet “thank you,” or worse, something generous. Something kind. Something that might make her feel like she didn’t deserve the air she’d just spilled between them.

    But Rio?

    Rio didn’t try to make it easier.

    She just stood there.

    One half-step closer than before. Still, balanced, solid. Like nothing Agatha had said had shaken her. Like she’d heard every word and filed it somewhere gentle.

    And that should’ve made Agatha feel better.

    Instead, it made her feel like her chest had cracked open. Because silence, in her world, had always been a weapon.

    It was what people did when they were done with you. What came after the yelling stopped and the door slammed and the anger turned into something that didn’t bother wasting itself on words anymore.

    Silence had always meant you’re not worth it.

    This silence was different.

    Rio didn’t look away. Didn’t fidget. Didn’t pretend she hadn’t heard. She just let the quiet be, like she trusted Agatha enough to sit in it with her. Agatha rubbed her palms along the thighs of her jeans, trying to shake the way her hands itched. Her pulse was still too loud in her ears. The weight of everything she’d just said was pressing into her spine now, settling in like regret even though she didn’t regret a damn word of it.

    She just… didn’t know what to do with it now that it was out in the open. “You don’t gotta say anything,” she said after a while, her voice quieter than before.

    “I wasn’t going to,” Rio replied, calm. Not sharp. Not unkind. Agatha blinked.

    She looked up, slow.

    Rio’s expression hadn’t changed. Still neutral. Still open. But now there was something else there too, something like warmth, except quieter. More restrained.

    More like certainty.

    “I figured if you were gonna talk,” Rio added, “it’d be when you were ready. Not when I asked.”

    Agatha didn’t answer.

    Because she didn’t know how to take that.

    Because no one had ever waited like that before.

    Because she wouldn’t have waited for her.

    And yet Rio had.

    Without chasing. Without sulking. Without punishment.

    She’d stayed in the guestroom. Cooked quietly. Kept to her work. Given space without turning it into distance. She hadn’t asked for Agatha’s heart on a platter, and she sure as hell hadn’t demanded closure. She’d just been there.

    And now she was here.

    Letting the moment be what it was without rushing to shape it into something else.

    Agatha didn’t realize she was breathing harder until she exhaled. It came out sharp, nearly a laugh, but not quite.

    “You always that damn patient?”

    Rio raised a brow. “No.”

    Agatha’s mouth tugged at the corner.

    “Lucky me, then.”

    A beat passed.

    Then another.

    And Rio, with the calm of someone who knew the moment didn’t need more than it had already been given, just said, “You wanna sit?” Agatha hesitated.

    Then nodded.

    And together, they moved toward the fence line. Sat in the low grass with knees bent, the world humming soft around them. Nothing else was said. Nothing else needed to be.

    Rio didn’t fill the silence.

    And that—more than anything—was how Agatha knew she was safe. They sat at the edge of the pasture, backs against the fence post, the sun pulling long shadows across the grass. It was the kind of late afternoon that felt like it could tip either way, into something gentle or something sharp and Agatha still wasn’t sure which direction this was going. But she hadn’t walked away.

    And Rio hadn’t filled the space.

    And that—somehow—was enough to anchor her right here.

    The grass was warm beneath her hands, the dirt dry and familiar beneath her boots. The wind stirred the taller weeds, rustling like it had a secret it wasn’t in a rush to tell. A hawk called somewhere overhead, far off but clear. The sky above was cloudless, wide. The kind of blue that only showed up after a heatwave had finally let go.

    Agatha shifted slightly, knees drawn up, arms resting loosely across them. Rio was beside her. Not close enough to touch. Not far enough to feel like she wasn’t with her. She sat cross-legged, one hand in the grass, fingertips absently brushing a patch of clover like she wasn’t thinking about it, like she just needed something to touch to stay grounded.

    Neither of them spoke.

    But the quiet wasn’t tense now.

    It wasn’t silence being held between them like a blade.

    It was… ease.

    Tentative. Fragile. But real.

    Agatha found herself glancing over more than once. Just little shifts of her eyes, like her body needed to confirm Rio was still there. That she hadn’t imagined this, that the moment was still intact.

    Every time she looked, Rio was looking at the horizon.

    Calm.

    Unbothered.

    Present.

    And Agatha could’ve wept from the simple mercy of it.

    Because no one had ever stayed this still next to her before. Not without expecting something. Not without needing her to explain, or apologize better, or open herself up like a wound for them to press into. But Rio didn’t need any of that.

    She was just there.

    Breathing easy.

    Not asking for anything.

    And God, it made Agatha ache.

    She didn’t know how to be this person yet, the kind that could talk about her feelings, who could look someone in the eye after saying something real and not feel like the world was tipping out from under her. But right now, she didn’t have to be.

    Right now, she just had to sit here. And not run.

    And Rio made that feel possible.

    Agatha let her eyes drift toward the fence line again. Let her back relax an inch deeper against the post. She wasn’t sure how long they sat like that, ten minutes, maybe twenty, but it didn’t matter. The sun dropped lower. The colors changed. The breeze kicked up.

    And still, neither of them moved.

    She cleared her throat once, low.

    Not to say anything.

    Just to make sure her voice still worked.

    Rio didn’t look over. But she did shift slightly, as if to say I’m still listening, if you’ve got more.

    Agatha didn’t.

    Not yet.

    But maybe soon.

    Her fingers curled in the grass.

    The silence pressed against her ribs, not heavy this time. Just there. And something in her chest… loosened.

    Not all the way. Not enough to call it peace. But enough that the weight of the last few days stopped feeling like it was going to crush her if she let her guard down for five damn minutes.

    She looked over at Rio again.

    The sun lit her profile, softened it.

    There was dirt on her elbow, a small grass stain on her knee. Agatha didn’t smile.

    But she looked.

    And she kept looking.

    And maybe that was all she could give.

    But Rio accepted it.

    And that, more than anything, was how Agatha knew something had changed.

    Something had started to settle.

    Not finished. Not solved.

    But real.

    And growing.

    Chapter End Notes Agatha’s apology isn’t pretty, but it’s honest, maybe the most honest she’s managed so far. And Rio doesn’t ask for more than she can give. Their quiet is changing, softening, becoming something that holds rather than hurts. Thank you for reading and sitting with these two through the slow, slow burn.

    Chapter 13: The Things We Learn in the Quiet

    Chapter Summary On a slow, heat-softened afternoon, Agatha and Rio fall into a rhythm neither of them are ready to name. A walk along the fence line turns into traded histories and unexpected honesty.

    Chapter Notes I’ve been looking forward to writing this chapter for so long. Agatha and Rio finally sit down and actually talk, about childhoods, coping, silence, and the things they don’t quite have the words for yet. There’s softness here.

    And tension.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes The afternoon settled over the ranch the way heat settles over memory, slow, deliberate, and impossible to ignore.

    Sunlight slanted through the wide barn doors in wide, golden strokes, turning dust motes into drifting constellations. Every movement stirred them: the soft stamp of a horse’s hoof, the shift of hay under a boot, even the long exhale that escaped Agatha before she realized she’d been holding her breath for too long.

    She wasn’t hiding.

    Not exactly.

    But she wasn’t doing anything useful either.

    She stood in the barn aisle with a bridle in her hands, one she’d already checked twice, oiled the day before, and hung properly on its hook. Now she was pretending it suddenly needed her attention again. Pretending she wasn’t listening for footsteps outside. Pretending her hands weren’t working faster than her thoughts.

    The barn smelled like warm leather and old wood. Like hay and dust and something faintly sweet drifting from the open pasture. The air was thick with late-summer weight, the kind that hummed with insects and made shadows stretch long across the floor.

    A horse nudged her shoulder from behind, breath warm against her neck. She reached up without thinking and scratched the soft space between its eyes.

    “There’s nothin’ here worth lookin’ at,” she muttered.

    The horse snorted like it disagreed.

    Agatha set the bridle down. Picked it up again. Adjusted the leather strap. Set it down once more.

    She was restless, and that wasn’t normal.

    Not the familiar restless, the one that came before storms when the cattle grew jumpy and the winds picked up. This was something else. Something in her ribs she didn’t have a name for. Something that had been building for days, ever since Rio had looked at her with that soft, quiet patience during her apology and said I know like the words weighed nothing at all. Agatha rubbed the heel of her palm over her sternum, frustrated that the motion didn’t make the tightness go away.

    She needed work. Real work. Something with structure, where every problem had an answer: fence rails, water lines, hoof trims, anything but this slow-burning ache in her chest.

    But the barn had been worked through already. She’d been picking at jobs for hours, pacing between stalls, rearranging tools that didn’t need rearranging, sweeping corners that were already clean. Every few minutes she caught herself glancing toward the door. Toward the sunlight. Toward the place where she’d last seen—

    Her.

    Rio.

    Out in the yard earlier, hair pulled low on her neck, sleeves rolled as she hung washed cloths on the line. The image had stuck in Agatha’s head like a tack, sharp and irritating, impossible to pull out.

    She didn’t know why she kept thinking about it.

    Or she did know, and she didn’t want to sit with the knowing too long. The barn felt too small suddenly. Too warm. Too full of all the things she wasn’t willing to say out loud.

    She shoved a hand through her hair and let out a breath that tasted like frustration.

    And then—

    A flicker of movement outside.

    A silhouette.

    Agatha paused mid-step. Leaned just slightly toward the open barn doors. Rio was crouched near the salt block just beyond the threshold, her back to the barn, head bowed in concentration. A handful of dried sage crumbled between her palms, the fragments falling like pale green dust onto the packed earth.

    She was humming.

    Softly.

    A low, unhurried tune with no sharp edges. Something that didn’t quite sound like a song but more like a thought carried on a breath. Agatha’s heart thumped once—hard—against her ribs.

    She had no business staring.

    But God help her, she couldn’t look away.

    Rio always moved like she belonged wherever she stood. Even in dirt. Even crouched beside a salt block meant for cattle. Even with sage dust on her fingers and sunlight splitting across her cheekbones. She held herself with this quiet self-assurance that made Agatha’s stomach knot. Not confidence like showiness.

    Confidence like honesty.

    Like she didn’t see the point of pretending to be anything she wasn’t. It unnerved the hell out of Agatha.

    She stepped forward, slow, boots scuffing the floorboards just enough to announce herself.

    Rio looked up.

    Her smile was small, but warm. The kind that curled rather than spread. The kind that didn’t demand anything in return.

    Agatha felt it like heat along the back of her neck.

    “You like that smell?” she asked, leaning a shoulder against the doorframe, trying not to look as off-balance as she felt.

    Rio lifted the sage to her nose, inhaling softly.

    “It’s calming,” she said. “Feels like a scent a place earns.” Agatha huffed. “Mostly smells like dust and cow shit out here.” “That’s one layer,” Rio said, shrugging. “Places have more than one.” She wasn’t looking at the barn when she said it.

    She was looking at Agatha.

    Agatha’s breath caught before she could stop it.

    Damn her.

    Damn the way she saw things.

    Damn the way she said them without sounding like she meant more, but always did.

    Agatha cleared her throat and dragged her eyes away.

    “Fence on the west side’s saggin’,” she said, a little sharper than she intended. “Gonna walk out and kick at it some. You can come if you want.” Rio stood, brushing dirt off her knees.

    “Yeah,” she said simply. “I’d like that.”

    She meant it.

    That alone made heat flare in Agatha’s chest.

    They started walking. The sun clung to the edges of everything, the fence posts, the tall grass, the loose strands of Rio’s hair that had pulled from her bun and were now catching the light like tiny threads of gold. For the first few steps, neither of them spoke.

    And somehow… the quiet didn’t hurt.

    It wasn’t brittle or sharp. It wasn’t the heavy kind that followed arguments or misunderstandings. It wasn’t the airless quiet Agatha had grown up with, where silence meant get small, stay hidden, don’t give ’em anything to hit. This silence breathed.

    This silence walked beside them like an animal that wasn’t dangerous unless cornered.

    Rio let her fingers brush the tops of the grasses as they moved. Agatha kept her hands in her pockets, thumbs hooking into worn denim like an anchor. A hawk circled overhead. The breeze carried the faint sweetness of warm earth.

    It should’ve been ordinary.

    Hell, it was ordinary.

    But with Rio at her side, nothing felt plain.

    The land felt… different. Not because it changed, but because Rio didn’t take it for granted. She looked at every rock and blade of grass like it mattered. Like she was cataloging the world quietly, respectfully, as if the ranch itself was something she wanted to learn rather than conquer. It made Agatha feel seen in a way she wasn’t used to.

    And that scared her more than she’d ever admit.

    She kicked at a rock, trying to ground herself.

    “You don’t gotta come watch me poke at some fence posts,” she muttered. “I didn’t come to watch the fence,” Rio said.

    Agatha’s pulse jumped.

    Rio added, softer, “I just like walking with you.”

    Heat rose at the back of Agatha’s neck again.

    She didn’t answer.

    Didn’t trust her voice to come out steady.

    They kept walking.

    The ranch stretched quiet around them. The sky was wide. The land breathed easy.

    And for the first time in days—

    So did Agatha.

    They kept walking until the barn was only a dark shape behind them and the west pasture opened wide, a stretch of sun-baked land dotted with sage, brittle grass, and fence posts leaning just enough to bother Agatha’s eye every time she passed.

    The path narrowed here.

    Not a real trail, just the line the cattle had worn into the earth over years, dipping slightly where the ground collected runoff after storms. Rio walked close enough that the backs of their hands brushed once, accidental, light, but enough to send a pulse of heat racing up Agatha’s arm. She didn’t pull away.

    She also didn’t let her hand drift back.

    Both felt equally dangerous.

    The silence settled between them again, but softer this time, easier. The kind that made Agatha aware of her breathing, of her boots scuffing earth, of Rio’s presence at her side like a steady hum.

    “You do this every day?” Rio asked eventually, her voice low like she didn’t want to startle the quiet.

    “Walkin’ fence?” Agatha shrugged. “Some part of it needs checkin’ most days. Heat shifts the rails. Wind weakens the posts. Animals lean where they shouldn’t. Always somethin’ coming loose.”

    Rio hummed thoughtfully, eyes drifting to the line of posts stretching into the distance. “Seems like a lot for one person.”

    “It is.”

    She didn’t sugarcoat it.

    Rio didn’t look surprised. She only nodded slowly, as if confirming something she already knew.

    “And you don’t take days off?” Rio asked.

    Agatha snorted softly. “Land don’t take days off. Animals don’t either.” “That’s not what I asked.”

    Agatha’s step faltered.

    Rio wasn’t pushing. Her tone wasn’t sharp. But the question hung there, simple, gentle, heavy in a way that made something inside Agatha bristle and curl at the same time.

    “I take evenings,” she muttered.

    “Evenings aren’t days,” Rio murmured back.

    Agatha’s jaw clenched, and she kicked a stray root sticking out of the dirt. Rio didn’t say anything else, which somehow made it worse. They walked a few more yards before Rio spoke again, lighter this time, almost curious.

    “You ever get bored of it?”

    “Of what?”

    “This.” She gestured vaguely to the land. “The routine. The repetition. The same tasks, same views, same fences needing the same repairs in the same places.”

    Agatha shook her head. “Ain’t boring when you’re doin’ it to keep things alive.”

    Rio considered that for a moment, then nodded.

    “Fair point.”

    They reached the sagging section of fence then, two rails bowed inward where the post had started to lean. Agatha crouched, checking the give in the lowest slat with a practiced hand. The wood creaked under her palm. Rio didn’t hover.

    She didn’t offer help, didn’t ask what needed doing. She simply folded her arms atop the top rail, leaning into it with her chin tilted thoughtfully over the field.

    “You ever think about leaving?” Rio asked, still watching the land. Agatha’s fingers stilled on the wood.

    The question wasn’t sharp. Wasn’t careless. But it lodged somewhere deep in her ribs.

    “…Not really.”

    Rio glanced down at her. “Not once?”

    Agatha swallowed. “Once was enough.”

    The wind shifted, carrying the smell of warm earth and distant cedar. A bird clicked somewhere in the grass.

    Rio waited.

    She always waited.

    Agatha stood, brushing dirt from her palms, keeping her eyes on the fence instead of the woman standing five feet away making her stomach act like it didn’t belong to her.

    “Why ask?” Agatha muttered, testing the angle of the post. Rio shrugged one shoulder. “Just trying to understand you.” Agatha’s hands froze.

    Rio caught it immediately, but she didn’t take it back. Didn’t soften it. Didn’t apologize.

    She just watched her.

    Looked at her like she was studying a plant she didn’t want to mishandle, gentle, careful, curious in a way that made Agatha’s throat tighten. “There’s not much to understand,” Agatha said, bending again so she wouldn’t have to meet her eyes.

    “I disagree,” Rio said softly.

    Agatha’s pulse kicked.

    She cleared her throat. “Well. That’s your mistake to make.” Rio smiled then, faint, but real. “I don’t think it is.” Agatha hammered the new nail in harder than necessary.

    They fell into a quieter rhythm after that, Agatha working, Rio watching the horizon like she belonged in that exact spot and nowhere else. Eventually, Rio said, “When I was a kid, I traveled a lot.” Agatha paused mid-swing.

    Rio kept her voice even, conversational. “Different cities, different schools. My mother kept thinking the next place would feel better than the last.” “You like movin’ that much?”

    Rio laughed softly. “No. Hated it. But I learned fast. How to pay attention. How to read people. When to keep my head down. When to leave first.” Agatha straightened slowly, wiping sweat from her brow.

    “Sounds lonely,” she murmured.

    Rio’s smile didn’t quite reach her eyes. “It was.”

    Agatha didn’t look away this time.

    Rio met her gaze calmly.

    “And you?” Rio asked. “You stayed.”

    “Didn’t have a choice.”

    “Still means something.”

    Agatha let that settle.

    Maybe too deeply.

    She turned back to the fence, but her hands felt warmer than the sun should’ve made them.

    “You always watch people this close?” she asked, trying to sound irritated. Trying to sound like her pulse wasn’t doing something humiliating. Rio leaned back against the rail, sunlight catching the line of her jaw. “Only the ones who matter,” she said.

    Agatha missed the nail head completely, the hammer head glancing off wood with a clumsy thud.

    She muttered a curse under her breath.

    Rio’s smile grew, not smug, not teasing. Soft.

    Like she’d meant every word and wasn’t planning on taking any of them back.

    Agatha forced her heartbeat to slow, forced her hands steady around the hammer.

    “Could warn a person before sayin’ things like that,” she grumbled. “Would you rather I lie?” Rio asked, tilting her head.

    Agatha glared at the fence as if it personally betrayed her. “I didn't say that,” she muttered, cheeks hot.

    Rio just kept looking at her, quiet, steady, gentle in a way that felt like a hand on the back of Agatha’s neck, grounding and terrifying all at once. And somewhere between breath and heartbeat—

    Something in Agatha cracked open.

    Not a break.

    A shift.

    Subtle, but real.

    The last nail sank into the rail with a satisfying thunk, the wood settling into place like it had finally decided to cooperate. Agatha stepped back, rolling her shoulders once, the ache spreading down her spine in a familiar pull. The fence looked right again.

    Straight. Steady. Strong.

    Simple, she thought. Wood was simple. Nails were simple. If something broke, you fixed it. If it split, you replaced it. If it leaned, you braced it before the whole damn thing became a problem.

    People weren’t like that.

    She wiped the sweat from her brow with the back of her hand, her throat tight. Rio was still leaning against the rail, arms folded loosely, watching the line of earth stretching west like it was a horizon worth studying. She wasn’t watching Agatha.

    Not now.

    And that—somehow—made it easier to speak.

    “Used to come out here with my mom,” Agatha said abruptly. Rio’s head turned. Not fast. Not surprised. Just attentive, as if she had felt the shift in the air before Agatha even said anything.

    “Yeah?” Rio asked softly.

    Agatha’s jaw worked once. She hooked her thumbs into her belt loops, grounding herself with the familiar pressure.

    “Storm nights were the worst,” she said. “We’d lose sections of fence. Debris would slam into the rails. Wind knocked down posts like toothpicks. Most folks waited till morning.”

    Rio didn’t blink. “You didn’t.”

    “No.” Agatha huffed out a humorless breath. “Mom’d drag me out of bed before the thunder even cleared. Lantern in one hand, boots barely laced, tellin’ me, ‘If you wait to fix somethin’ that’s breakin’, you just make more work for yourself.’”

    Rio smiled faintly. “Sounds practical.”

    “It was.”

    Agatha hesitated.

    “It was also the only time she ever talked to me.”

    That silence landed harder than any hammer strike.

    Rio’s arms lowered from the rail.

    She straightened, just slightly, giving Agatha her full attention without stepping closer. Not crowding. Not coaxing. Just listening. Agatha’s chest tightened. She looked away, out over the pasture where the cattle were little dark shapes moving through the grass. “She wasn’t… cruel,” Agatha said slowly. “Just… not soft. Didn’t have that in her. She could fix a pump in a hailstorm, mend a calf with her bare hands, negotiate with suppliers like she had a knife in her voice.” A pause.

    “But ask her to say somethin’ gentle? Ask her to tell me I did somethin’ right? Hell, ask her to sit beside me for more than ten seconds without a job to do?”

    Her mouth twisted.

    “That wasn’t her.”

    Rio didn’t interrupt.

    Agatha kept going, not because she meant to, but because the words, once cracked loose, didn’t seem interested in going back.

    “I think she wanted to.”

    Her voice wavered.

    “Sometimes, on good days, I could see it. Like she had a thought she wanted to say but didn’t know how. She’d stand in the doorway of my room, look like she was ’bout to speak, then just… leave. Walk out to the barn instead.”

    Rio’s brow softened. Not pity, never pity. Something quieter. Something like sorrow without the sharp edges.

    “That must’ve been hard,” she whispered.

    Agatha laughed, dry. “Hard? No. Hard’s when somethin’ breaks and you can’t fix it. Hard’s when you ain’t got money for feed. Hard’s buryin’ a calf in the mud.”

    A breath.

    “This was just… normal.”

    Rio didn’t contradict her.

    But she didn’t agree either.

    And that was somehow worse, because it left space for the truth to sit on its own without being dismissed or swallowed down.

    Agatha shifted her weight, scuffing the toe of her boot against a patch of dry dirt.

    “She died when I was twenty-one,” she said. “Heart gave out. Quick. Quiet. Like everythin’ she never said finally rushed her at once.” Rio stepped closer, not by much. Just enough that her presence nudged the air between them.

    “I’m sorry,” she murmured.

    Agatha gave a small shake of her head. “Don’t be. We weren’t… close. Not in the way most people mean.” A beat. “But she was all I had.” And that, somehow, was the closest she’d ever come to saying I was alone after that.

    Rio’s eyes softened even further.

    “What about your father?” she asked gently.

    Agatha’s jaw locked.

    “No such man,” she said. “Not in any way that mattered.” Rio nodded once, accepting the boundary. Not pushing. Not digging. She waited.

    Agatha exhaled slowly.

    “I learned early ain’t nobody comin’ to pick up the slack,” she said. “You break somethin’, you fix it. You fall behind, you catch up. You shut your mouth and do the work.”

    Her voice dropped lower.

    “Didn’t have room for cryin’ or complainin’ or… needing people.” When she looked over, Rio’s expression hadn’t changed, but something in her eyes had.

    Something like recognition.

    Something like understanding sharpened by experience.

    Agatha swallowed.

    “I don’t talk about her,” she admitted. “Not to anyone.” Rio’s voice was barely a whisper.

    “Why now?”

    Agatha didn’t know how to answer.

    Her eyes drifted out to the fence line again. The new rail she’d set gleamed pale in the late light. Straight. Solid. Fixed.

    Then she looked back at Rio.

    And the truth rose before she could stop it.

    “Because you were lookin’ at me,” she said quietly, “like you wanted to see the parts that ain’t pretty.”

    Rio’s breath caught, too soft to hear but visible in the shift of her shoulders. Agatha looked away again, heat crawling up her neck.

    “Most folks want the simple parts,” she muttered. “The strong pieces. The reliable ones. They don’t want the splintered edges.”

    Rio took one more step closer.

    Close enough that Agatha could feel the warmth of her even in the open air. “Splinters tell a story too,” Rio said. “Doesn’t mean they break the whole board.”

    Agatha’s throat tightened so hard it almost hurt.

    Rio held her gaze, steady and gentle.

    “You ever think,” Rio murmured, “that maybe you don’t have to fix everything before someone can love the pieces?”

    Agatha’s heart hammered so loud she felt dizzy.

    “I ain’t talkin’ about love,” she said, voice rough.

    “No,” Rio agreed softly. “You’re not. But you’re talking about fear.” Agatha looked at her sharply.

    Rio didn’t back down.

    “You think if someone knows the parts that hurt, they’ll run,” she said. “You think if you open that door, the whole damn house will collapse.” Agatha’s breath stuttered.

    Rio’s voice softened even more.

    “But I’m still here.”

    There it was.

    The sentence that hit deeper than any hammer, any storm, any memory of a mother who couldn’t touch softness without flinching from it. Agatha didn’t speak.

    She couldn’t.

    The air between them thickened.

    Warm. Quiet. Full of something that felt like a hand pressed gently over her ribs, holding the pieces still.

    Rio reached out, not to touch. Not yet. Just far enough that Agatha could feel the intention, the almost of it.

    “You opened a door,” Rio whispered. “I see it. I’m not walking away.” Agatha closed her eyes for one long, shaky breath.

    When she opened them again—

    Rio was still there.

    Still watching her.

    Still waiting.

    And for the first time in her life, Agatha didn’t slam the door shut again. She let it stay open.

    Just an inch.

    Just enough.

    The quiet that settled after Rio’s last words wasn’t empty. It wasn’t brittle or hot or waiting to break.

    It was… full.

    Full of things unsaid. Full of breath and heat and the way Rio stood so close now that the air between them felt like a shared thing rather than a divide. Agatha looked away first.

    Not out of shame. Not exactly.

    Just… overwhelmed.

    She hadn’t spoken about her mother in years.

    She hadn’t meant to, not in the barn, not on the walk, not while fixing a goddamn fence. But Rio had a way of looking at her, a way of listening without prying, that made space open where it shouldn’t. And now that the words were out, raw, clumsy, vulnerable, they felt too big to take back.

    Agatha rubbed her palms on her jeans, grounding herself in the rough denim. Her breath was uneven. Her throat felt scraped hollow. Rio didn’t fill the silence.

    She stepped back, not away, but out of the direct heat of Agatha’s confession, like she was giving her room to breathe.

    Then she sat down in the grass.

    Just folded her long legs beneath her and settled with the ease of someone accustomed to grounding herself in the earth.

    She didn’t look at Agatha yet.

    Just patted the grass beside her once, wordlessly.

    A quiet invitation.

    Not a demand.

    Agatha hesitated.

    That same old instinct pulled at her, to stay standing, to stay guarded, to keep height and distance between them. Sitting would mean being level. Being open. Being reachable.

    She hated reachable.

    And yet…

    She lowered herself slowly until she was sitting beside Rio, boots planted in the dry dirt, elbows resting loosely on her knees. Not relaxed. But not tense either.

    Just… suspended.

    Rio plucked a small stalk of dried grass and twisted it between her fingers. For a long moment, she said nothing, her eyes on the horizon where the sky had begun to shift toward evening gold.

    Agatha realized, slowly, that Rio was preparing herself. That alone made her breath catch.

    Rio, who rarely spoke about anything personal, who kept her history behind quiet eyes and steady hands, was bracing to give something back. “…You’re not the only one who grew up with silence,” Rio said softly. Agatha’s head turned sharply, but Rio didn’t meet her eyes yet. She kept her gaze on the field, the grass moving in small waves like it was breathing.

    “My mother, she is loud in all the wrong ways,” Rio continued. “Loud in chaos. Loud in worry. Loud in wanting things to be better than they are.” Agatha frowned, but stayed quiet.

    Rio’s voice was even. Not emotionless, controlled. Like she’d learned long ago that spilling everything at once only made the wound deeper. “She wants a life that keeps moving. New places. New starts. New scenery. She believes every problem can be outrun if she finds the right horizon.” A small, humorless breath.

    “But she isn't good at staying.”

    Agatha’s chest tightened.

    She didn’t interrupt.

    “She loves us,” Rio said. “Me and my sister. No doubt about that.” Her fingers twisted the dried stem until it split.

    “But love doesn’t always come with stability. Sometimes it comes in waves. Too much one year, too little the next.”

    Agatha felt something twist deep inside her.

    Because yes.

    She knew that.

    She knew it too well.

    Rio exhaled slowly and leaned back on her palms, tilting her face toward the sun like she was letting it burn away old shadows.

    “I learned to listen to people before I spoke to them,” she said. “Because talking didn’t fix anything. Talking just stirred water that needed to stay still.”

    She paused.

    “I learned to pack fast, to start over fast, to smile fast, because standing still meant feeling the things we didn’t have time to feel.”

    Agatha swallowed hard.

    That too felt like a mirror she didn’t want to look into. Rio continued, but her voice softened now, gentler.

    “Sofia she could talk to anyone. Laugh first, think later. She was the one who held all of us together when things got messy.”

    A faint smile tugged at her mouth.

    “She’d say I was the quiet one. The observer. The one who noticed everything and said nothing until it mattered.”

    Agatha snorted under her breath.

    “Still true.”

    Rio sent her a sideways glance, amused but muted.

    “Still true,” she agreed.

    Another pause. Another breath. Another shift in the grass. “When I left home,” Rio said, voice lower now, “I promised myself I’d only stay places that let me breathe.”

    She toyed with a small pebble, thumb brushing the surface. “Places that didn’t demand versions of me I couldn’t give.” Agatha felt the weight of that.

    The truth nested in it.

    Rio turned finally and looked at her, really looked at her. Deep. Steady. Unflinching.

    “This place,” Rio said quietly, “lets me breathe.”

    Agatha’s heart stuttered.

    Rio held the moment, didn’t rush, didn’t break eye contact. “And you,” she added, softer still, “don’t demand anything from me I’m not ready to give.”

    Agatha opened her mouth, then closed it.

    Because she didn’t know what to say to that.

    Didn’t know how to hold it without dropping it.

    Rio wasn’t done.

    “You talk like you’re hard to be around,” she said. “Like the pieces of you that splinter are a burden. Like someone would need gloves just to touch you.”

    Her gaze gentled.

    “But you don’t break people, Agatha.”

    Agatha’s breath hitched.

    Rio leaned in, barely, just enough that their shoulders brushed with the faintest whisper of contact.

    “Your quiet isn’t cold,” Rio said. “It’s cautious. It’s earned.” A beat.

    “And when you let someone in? When you speak?”

    Her voice went tender, almost reverent.

    “It feels like trust.”

    Agatha’s eyes burned.

    She looked away fast, blinking against the sudden sting. Rio watched her, not intrusively, not pitying, just steady, present. “I can give you space,” Rio went on. “I can give you quiet. I can wait.” Her hand shifted on the grass, knuckles brushing Agatha’s knee without fully touching.

    “But I won’t walk away because you get scared.”

    Agatha swallowed hard.

    She wanted to say something, anything.

    But her voice was gone.

    Rio’s gaze softened even further, if that was possible.

    “You opened a door today,” she said. “So I’m opening one too.” She inhaled.

    Slow.

    Intentional.

    Then: “I don’t stay places for long.”

    “But I want to stay here.”

    Agatha’s heart nearly broke open.

    Rio wasn’t done.

    “And not just for the work.”

    A pause.

    “Not just for the land.”

    Another pause.

    “For you.”

    The words landed like a storm finally breaking.

    Quiet.

    Heavy.

    Relieving.

    Agatha didn’t move.

    Couldn’t.

    Rio didn’t reach for her.

    Didn’t push the moment too far.

    She just sat there beside her in the grass, warm, solid, unhurried and gave Agatha something she didn’t know she’d been waiting her whole life to hear: Someone choosing to stay.

    Not out of obligation.

    Not out of habit.

    Not because they had nowhere else to go.

    But because they wanted to.

    Rio’s hand drifted closer again, not touching, but close enough that Agatha could feel the deliberate offer of contact.

    A question made of breath and patience.

    “I’m here,” Rio whispered. “If you want me to be.”

    And Agatha—

    Agatha didn’t flinch.

    She didn’t pull away.

    She didn’t shut down or shut out or bury herself in silence. She just breathed.

    For the first time all day, she breathed without feeling like it hurt. The grass held warmth like a secret.

    They sat close, not touching, but close enough that Agatha could feel the heat coming off Río’s shoulder, the soft rustle of her clothes when she breathed, the slight tremble in the air between them like a live wire stretched too thin.

    Dusk wasn’t settling so much as sinking around them, turning everything gold and then blue in slow waves. The kind of light that softened edges but sharpened truths.

    Agatha wasn’t sure when her breathing had gone shallow.

    Maybe when Rio shifted closer, thigh brushing hers for half a heartbeat. Maybe when Rio turned her head, slow, deliberate, and looked at her like she was something worth studying.

    Maybe long before that.

    Rio’s voice, when it finally broke the quiet, was barely above the breeze. “You’re quieter than usual.”

    Agatha huffed a breath she couldn’t quite call a laugh.

    “Don’t usually sit this close to someone.”

    Rio held her gaze.

    Not teasing.

    Not smiling.

    Just there, fully, painfully present.

    “And is it hard,” she asked softly, “sitting close to me?” Agatha’s pulse stuttered.

    Because that wasn’t flirtation.

    That wasn’t accidental.

    That was intention wrapped in gentleness.

    She swallowed hard.

    Her eyes dropped to the grass, then lifted again almost on instinct. “Yeah,” she said. “It is.”

    Rio’s breath hitched.

    Just a tiny, fragile sound, but real. Honest.

    Agatha watched the way Rio’s fingers curled in the grass, like she needed something to hold onto.

    Not her.

    Not yet.

    “Why?” Rio asked.

    Agatha’s laugh was barely a breath.

    “You know why.”

    Rio blinked, slow, expression unreadable… except for the way her lips parted just slightly, like she’d forgotten how to keep them sealed. “Say it anyway,” she murmured.

    Agatha’s chest tightened so sharply she had to look away for a second. Because saying it would make something real.

    Because not saying it would keep breaking her open.

    But Rio didn’t rush her.

    Rio never rushed her.

    She just waited, knees drawn up, palms in the grass, posture open, eyes steady.

    It made Agatha feel peeled open and held all at once.

    She took a breath, shaky and thin.

    “Because it feels different with you,” she said. “And I don’t always know what to do with that.”

    Rio inhaled like the words hit her somewhere deep.

    The silence that followed wasn’t gentle now.

    It was charged.

    Heavy.

    Rio shifted, closer.

    Not much, but enough for their knees to brush again, this time lingering for a heartbeat longer before Rio eased back just a fraction, like she was testing the air, testing Agatha’s breath, testing herself.

    “Agatha,” she said quietly.

    Her name sounded different in Rio’s mouth.

    Less like a label.

    More like an invitation.

    Agatha didn’t trust herself to answer.

    So she didn’t.

    Rio lifted her hand.

    Slow.

    Slow enough that Agatha could stop her if she wanted to. Slow enough that Agatha had time to realize she didn’t want to. Her fingertips hovered near Agatha’s jaw, not touching, but close enough that the warmth of her hand brushed her skin like a whisper. Agatha froze.

    Not out of fear.

    Out of recognition.

    Because she wanted this.

    God, she wanted this.

    Rio’s eyes flicked to her mouth.

    Not subtly.

    Not accidentally.

    Just a small, helpless movement, like she’d given up pretending she wasn’t thinking about it.

    Agatha’s breath stuttered.

    Her lips parted.

    She leaned in—

    Barely.

    A tilt.

    A shift of gravity.

    She didn’t even realize she’d moved until Rio did the same. Two inches closed.

    Then one.

    The world narrowed to heat and breath and the soft tremble of restraint. Rio whispered, “Tell me if you don’t want this.”

    Agatha’s voice broke.

    “I do.”

    Rio exhaled, a sound that was almost relief, almost ache. Their foreheads nearly touched.

    The grass whispered around them.

    The breeze cooled the back of Agatha’s neck.

    Her heart hammered like something wild and terrified.

    She wanted to close the distance.

    She wanted to lean forward that last inch.

    She wanted—

    Rio stopped.

    Her hand trembled once.

    Her breath faltered.

    And she whispered, voice breaking on the edges: “…Not yet.”

    Agatha flinched, not back, not away, but inward, like her ribs tightened around something too big.

    She didn’t hide it fast enough.

    Rio saw.

    And that hurt, too — the seeing.

    “I want to,” Rio said, barely a breath. “You know that.” Agatha swallowed so hard it almost hurt.

    “Yeah,” she whispered. “I know.”

    “And I don’t want the first time to happen because we were… caught in a moment.”

    Agatha blinked.

    Rio’s words weren’t pulling away.

    They were an anchor.

    A promise disguised as restraint.

    Her voice softened.

    “I want us to be ready. Both of us.”

    Agatha’s chest tightened again, but differently this time. She nodded, slow.

    Rio let her hand drop from the air, brushing the grass instead, grounding herself.

    But she didn’t move away.

    If anything, she leaned closer by a fraction, her shoulder brushing Agatha’s arm, the contact feather-light but unmistakably chosen.

    Agatha’s breath escaped on a long, uneven exhale.

    Something unclenched.

    Something surrendered.

    Something steadied.

    The almost-kiss hung between them like a lantern, not extinguished, just waiting.

    The tension didn’t break.

    It settled.

    Warmed.

    Promised.

    Agatha let her fingers graze the grass nearer to Rio’s, stopping just shy of touching.

    Rio noticed.

    Her lips curved, not a smile, not fully, just a soft, quiet acknowledgment. “We’ll get there,” she murmured.

    Agatha closed her eyes, letting the words settle against her ribs like something she hadn’t known she needed.

    “Yeah,” she breathed. “We will.”

    And for the first time, wanting didn’t scare her.

    Waiting didn’t either.

    Because now it wasn’t a question.

    It was inevitable.

    Chapter End Notes Thank you for reading.

    This chapter was all about opening doors. Not all the way, not yet, but enough to let the air in.

    Enough to change everything.

    Chapter 14: Hands Full Of Gentle Things

    Chapter Summary Blood, breath, and the quiet work of staying.

    When Agatha saves a calf, Rio bears witness, not just to the labor, but to the care beneath it. What grows between them is fragile, unspoken, and very much alive.

    Chapter Notes Agatha: “I don't care about anything.”

    Also Agatha: literally hand-delivers life into the world with tears in her throat and gentleness in her bones.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes She didn’t need anyone to tell her something was wrong.

    The cow had gone quiet too long.

    She’d been pacing yesterday, tail twitching, sides heaving in those uneven waves that always meant it was close. Twice this morning, Agatha had walked the pasture to check on her, boots damp with dew, voice low when she called out across the field. “C’mon now, mama. Let’s get it done.” But now, the rest of the cattle had gathered, not in panic, just in stillness. Watchful. The kind of stillness that meant they knew something was wrong before Agatha even got close.

    She didn’t run.

    Didn’t need to.

    But her pace quickened, sure and quiet. She grabbed the birthing kit from the shed on instinct—lube, gloves, twine, and a clean cloth she kept folded too neatly for something she always expected to ruin.

    The cow was down when she got there.

    Flat on her side, sides heaving, nostrils flared. She groaned low, a panicked sound she’d only heard once or twice before in her life, and Agatha’s chest cinched at the sound.

    “Shhh,” she murmured, crouching low. “Hey, hey now.”

    Her voice dropped, soft and even.

    “None of that. I’m here.”

    She pressed a hand gently to the cow’s flank a calming, steady pressure and let her eyes sweep over the scene.

    No hooves. No nose. No telltale push. Just tension. Just the tremble of a body working too hard against something too wrong.

    “Shit,” she whispered, too quiet for the cow to hear, too late to stop it. She didn’t hesitate. Just rolled her sleeves, pulled on the shoulder-length glove, and coated her arm in lube as she crouched at the cow’s hind end. The cow tensed. Her legs twitched in pain.

    Agatha pressed a hand to her side again, firmer this time. “I got you,” she said softly. “It’s gonna be alright.”

    And then she went in.

    The heat was suffocating. The pressure too tight. Her forearm met resistance almost immediately—no nose, no feet. Just the bend of a leg in the wrong direction, and another curled beneath it.

    Agatha closed her eyes for a second.

    Not to panic.

    To feel.

    She moved slowly, hands gentle. Found the first leg. Bent wrong. She nudged it into a better position, inch by inch, whispering under her breath the whole time.

    “You’re alright, sweetheart. Just breathe. I know it hurts. Almost there.” When she found the second leg, the calf shifted, just barely, but it was enough.

    Agatha worked her fingers along the narrow angle of bone, tried not to wince at the tightness of the space. She knew this kind of labor. She’d seen worse. She’d lost calves. But this one, this one had time left in it. She wasn’t gonna lose this one.

    “I’ve got you,” she said again, voice softer than she meant it. “I’m not lettin’ go.”

    She didn’t hear the footsteps at first.

    But she felt the shift in the air behind her.

    Looked up.

    And there was Rio.

    Standing still. Hands at her sides, brows drawn faintly inward, not in judgment. Not even in worry.

    Just present.

    Just watching.

    Agatha didn’t speak.

    Just held her gaze for a breath, her gloved arm still buried to the elbow in a cow she’d named but never told anyone.

    She could’ve sent Rio away.

    Could’ve grunted something sharp. Told her it wasn’t her business. Made it easy to leave.

    But she didn’t.

    “Kid’s breech,” she said instead. “Gotta get him turned.” Rio gave a single nod.

    Didn’t move closer. Didn’t walk away.

    Agatha looked back down. Slid her hand deeper. Let her voice drop to a whisper again, just for the cow.

    “That’s it,” she murmured. “We’re gonna make this alright.” And Rio stayed.

    Didn’t say a word.

    But watched, the way someone did when they were learning something sacred.

    The calf wasn’t turning easy.

    She’d done this enough times to know when something wasn’t going smooth. Knew how the leg should slide under her fingers, how the body should shift when things aligned. But this one was stubborn. Twisted in tight, hips wedged at the wrong angle, like it had decided the world wasn’t worth coming into unless it made things difficult on the way out. Agatha kept her hand steady.

    Wrist deep, then elbow, then a little farther still. The pressure was intense, and the cow groaned again, low, deep, pained.

    “Shhh,” Agatha whispered, brow furrowed. “I know, girl. I know.” Her other hand braced against the cow’s flank, thumb rubbing absent circles into coarse fur. The little touch wasn’t necessary—not medically, not mechanically—but she did it anyway. Couldn’t stop herself. Some part of her always needed to soothe.

    Even when she didn’t know how to say it out loud.

    She shifted her weight on her knees. The ground was damp here, a little softer from the shade, and it clung to her jeans like memory. Rio hadn’t moved. She could feel her, just off to the side, silently watching. Not watching like most people would. Not with nerves or discomfort or even interest.

    No, Rio watched like she was learning something.

    Like this moment meant more than blood and instinct and birth. Agatha found the calf’s back leg again, finally managed to nudge it free of the pelvis, careful not to tear anything. Her fingers worked slow, delicate as threading a needle. The space was too narrow, and the calf was big. “C’mon, sweetheart,” she murmured. “You’re doin’ fine. Just a little further.”

    The cow’s breathing was ragged now.

    Panicked.

    Agatha leaned forward, shifting her body to help hold the cow steady as another contraction rolled through. She didn’t flinch as the pressure clamped down on her arm, just closed her eyes for a second, jaw tight. When it passed, she moved again.

    “Almost got you,” she said. “You just stay with me.”

    There was mud in her boots. Blood on her hands. Her glove had started to roll at the wrist, and a streak of warm wet was sliding down to her elbow, soaking into her sleeve. She didn’t care.

    The calf shifted again.

    Just a little.

    Agatha’s breath caught.

    She could feel the change, the rightness of it. The angle lining up, the second leg sliding alongside the first, the pressure easing ever so slightly. She exhaled through her nose.

    “Alright,” she whispered. “Alright. Here we go.”

    She took hold.

    Gentle. Firm.

    Fingers wrapped around two small, slippery hooves like they were glass. And then she pulled.

    Not hard.

    Not fast.

    Just enough.

    Enough to help the calf into the world, slow and safe.

    The cow groaned. Her body convulsed. And then—

    With a final, wet slide, the calf came free.

    Back legs first, just like she’d feared. Long and dark and slick with afterbirth, still connected by the cord that pulsed faintly in the dirt. Agatha lowered him down carefuyl, like she was handling something sacred.

    And then she sat back on her heels.

    The blood cooled on her arms.

    The breath rattled out of her lungs.

    And she didn’t look up.

    Not yet.

    Not until the calf twitched once, just the ear. Then a leg. Then the chest rose, shallow and weak, but rising.

    She let out a low, shaking laugh.

    Not joy. Not surprise.

    Just… relief.

    “There you go, little one,” she whispered. “You’re here.” And for the first time in longer than she could remember— She was glad someone else had been there to see it.

    The calf twitched again. Stronger this time.

    One ear flicked. The spindly back legs kicked once in the dirt, awkward and wet and too long for a body that small.

    Then a cough, wet and gurgling, followed by a thin, reedy bleat that sounded more like protest than anything living.

    Agatha didn’t move at first.

    She just sat there, knees in the mud, hands resting on her thighs. Her arms were coated from shoulder to wrist in blood and afterbirth, her sleeves damp and clinging, her hair matted to her forehead. She wasn’t trembling. But she wasn’t steady either.

    Her chest rose and fell in heavy, uneven breaths.

    Not panic.

    Not exhaustion.

    Just… the come-down.

    The quiet, shivering release of effort after everything that could’ve gone wrong didn’t.

    She reached forward with one hand—bare now, glove peeled off and tossed behind her and gently cupped the calf’s narrow head. Her thumb brushed across the damp line between its eyes, careful, soothing. “There you are,” she murmured. “You made it.”

    The calf gave another half-hearted noise and tried to lift its head, only to flop back into the grass. Alive. Breathing. Small. Real.

    Agatha’s throat tightened.

    She didn’t cry. But it came close, an ache at the base of her ribs, a fullness behind her eyes that she swallowed down like grit.

    And then she remembered, she wasn’t alone.

    She turned.

    Slow.

    And found Rio still there.

    Still a few paces back. Still unmoving. But her expression, God, her expression.

    Something quiet had cracked open across her face. Her jaw was slack, eyes wide, but not in shock. In something deeper. Reverent. Like she was seeing something she hadn’t known she’d wanted to see.

    She hadn’t looked away once.

    Not during the mess. Not during the blood. Not when Agatha cursed low and panicked under her breath. Not even now.

    She was still there.

    Agatha’s first instinct was to look away.

    To make a joke. Get up. Brush it all off.

    But she didn’t.

    She held Rio’s gaze, heartbeat heavy behind her sternum, her fingers still curled against the calf’s warm skull.

    The silence between them pulsed.

    It wasn’t empty.

    It was full.

    So full it pressed against Agatha’s ribs and made breathing harder than it should’ve been.

    Rio blinked once. Slowly.

    Then stepped forward, just one pace. Not crowding. Not rushing. Just… closing the distance.

    Agatha didn’t stop her.

    Didn’t look away.
