    She turned back to the calf, smoothed a hand along its damp side, feeling the rise and fall of its tiny chest. The skin was hot, still slick, already beginning to pulse with the unmistakable rhythm of life.

    It was alive.

    She’d done that.

    With her hands. With her voice. With the part of herself she didn’t show to anyone, not unless they’d earned it.

    And Rio had earned it.

    Because she hadn’t looked away.

    Because she’d stayed.

    Agatha shifted back, knees creaking, legs stiff with effort. She braced herself on the ground and leaned her head back for a second, eyes closing against the late sun that was just beginning to crest low over the hills. She breathed in.

    Let it out slow.

    The kind of breath that made space in her ribs again.

    When she opened her eyes, Rio was kneeling too now. Not close, not touching. But near enough that Agatha could feel her there, calm and present.

    Rio looked at the calf.

    Then at Agatha.

    Then said, barely above a whisper, “You were—”

    She stopped herself.

    Agatha raised an eyebrow, too tired to hide anything now. “Was what?” Rio’s lips parted. Her brow twitched. Then her gaze dropped back to the calf.

    “…Never mind.”

    Agatha let the quiet linger a little longer, then gave a small, crooked smile. “Say it or don’t. I ain’t pressin’.”

    Rio didn’t answer.

    She didn’t have to.

    Because her silence wasn’t retreating this time.

    It was awestruck.

    And Agatha, still kneeling in blood, still covered in the evidence of her own care, felt seen in a way she hadn’t been in years.

    Maybe ever.

    The calf was still breathing steady.

    Not strong, not yet, but regular. Enough that Agatha could ease the knot in her chest. Enough that she could stop watching for the rise and fall of its tiny ribcage like it might vanish if she blinked too slow. It made another soft sound, eyes still shut tight, legs twitching with the instinct to stand. Agatha kept her hand where it was, resting just over the calf’s flank. She wasn’t ready to move yet.

    And Rio hadn’t moved either.

    They sat like that for a while, Agatha in the dirt, blood and sweat drying on her arms, and Rio close by, knees tucked beneath her, not saying a word. Agatha couldn’t bring herself to break the silence.

    It was too easy to ruin.

    Too tempting to fill it with something sharp, something dismissive. A joke. A shrug. Some quick remark about stubborn cows and breech births and how nothing ever goes smooth around here.

    But she didn’t say any of that.

    She just watched the calf.

    Watched the way the breeze caught its wet fur and made it shiver. Watched the cow—its mother—stir weakly, turning just enough to nudge its nose against her calf’s side.

    Agatha swallowed.

    Something stuck in her throat. Didn’t move.

    “I ain’t never really cared about any of this,” she said suddenly. Voice quiet. Rough.

    It came out before she could think to stop it.

    Rio looked at her.

    Agatha didn’t meet her eyes.

    She stared down at her hand on the calf’s side. At the way her fingers curled in just slightly, like she couldn’t help the shape of it. “Not like this,” she went on. “Not in that way where it makes your chest hurt.”

    Rio didn’t interrupt.

    Didn’t even shift.

    Agatha exhaled slowly, jaw working. She didn’t know why she was talking now. Maybe it was the blood cooling on her skin. Maybe it was the fact that Rio had stayed through all of it and hadn’t looked away once. Maybe it was the calf, alive and small and breathing like it didn’t know how close it came to not being here.

    She let her hand fall to her knee, rubbed at the dried smear along her jeans. Her voice was lower when she spoke again.

    “I’ve done this kind of thing plenty. Pullin’ calves. Patchin’ fences. Mornin’ chores, rain or shine. Grew up doin’ it. Kept doin’ it after. Never thought much on it.”

    She paused.

    Felt the dirt under her nails. The ache in her spine.

    “But right now?” she said, a little raw. “I care.”

    The words hung between them.

    Unpolished. Awkward. Honest.

    And then, softer: “Don’t think I’ve ever cared like this before. Except now, maybe.” She didn’t look at Rio when she said it.

    Didn’t clarify.

    Didn’t explain if she meant the calf. The cow. The land.

    Or the fact that Rio had seen her kneeling in the dirt with her hands full of blood and new life and hadn’t flinched.

    Maybe all of it.

    Maybe more.

    The calf bleated again, a little louder this time. The mother shifted in the grass and nosed her calf with a gentleness Agatha felt down to her ribs. And finally Agatha looked up.

    Rio’s eyes were on her.

    Not surprised.

    Not searching.

    Just seeing her.

    Not as the woman who kept to herself. Not as the rancher with calloused hands and a sharp tongue.

    But as someone trying, clumsily, quietly, to say: this matters. You matter. Agatha’s throat tightened again.

    She gave a short breath of a laugh.

    “Shit,” she muttered, rubbing her forehead. “I ain’t good at this.” Rio’s lips twitched.

    “You’re doing alright.”

    Agatha met her eyes.

    Held them.

    And for once, just this once, she let the softness stay.

    The calf had begun to wriggle now, awkward and stiff-legged, pushing against the grass in fits and starts. The mother was more alert, breathing steadier, her head curled protectively toward the calf as she licked at its back with slow, rhythmic strokes.

    It would be alright.

    They both would.

    Agatha sat back on her heels and let the weight of that truth settle low and full in her chest.

    She felt wrecked.

    Not tired, exactly, though her body ached with it. But there was a looseness in her limbs now, like everything tight inside her had given up the fight all at once.

    There was blood drying along her forearms, caked in places where the glove hadn’t quite reached. Her shirt clung to her in patches, dirt and sweat, straw stuck in the folds. Her knees were damp, boots thick with the stink of the pasture.

    And still, somehow, she felt at peace.

    Or as close to it as she could ever remember being.

    Rio didn’t move until Agatha did.

    And even then, she waited.

    Agatha stood slowly, brushing a hand down the back of her thigh out of habit, eyes still lingering on the calf. She swayed a little, too long on her knees, her spine protesting, but she didn’t let it show.

    “C’mon,” she said gruffly, more to herself than to Rio. “Need to rinse off before I dry solid.”

    Rio followed without comment.

    She didn’t offer help, didn’t ask what needed doing. She just fell into step beside her, not crowding, not trailing, walking with her, like it was natural. Like she belonged.

    They stopped at the metal trough near the barn, one Agatha had filled earlier in the day when she realized the heat was creeping back toward something nasty. The water inside was cold, clean, surface still except for a floating strand of straw.

    Agatha crouched again, this time with less urgency, and dipped her hands in.

    The water shocked her system. A full-body jolt. Her breath caught as the chill raced up her arms and into her shoulders, but she didn’t flinch. She just rolled her hands slowly under the surface, watching the blood and dirt bloom around her in slow, red curls.

    She scrubbed her forearms with the bar of soap she kept wrapped in cloth near the pump. The smell was sharp, clean, herbal. Familiar. When she glanced up, Rio was there, crouched beside her. Close enough to hand her the rag without a word. Close enough that their knees nearly brushed in the dirt.

    Agatha took it, their fingers grazing.

    Rio’s hand was warm. Steady.

    Agatha didn’t thank her out loud.

    She couldn’t, not with her throat full like it was.

    She scrubbed in silence. Fingers methodical. The cloth moving in slow circles along the meat of her palms, up her arms, against her wrists where the glove had left a line.

    Rio watched. Not intrusive. Just… present.

    Agatha rinsed again, let the water drip down her forearms, and sat back on her heels.

    “I usually do this part alone,” she said, quietly.

    Rio looked at her then.

    “But I didn’t leave,” she said.

    It wasn’t a question.

    Agatha nodded once. “No. You didn’t.”

    They stayed there a moment longer, the sounds of the pasture soft behind them, lowing cattle, the click of insects, the faint rustle of grass as the calf learned the shape of its legs.

    The quiet between them wasn’t awkward.

    It was tender.

    Something new.

    Something earned.

    Rio shifted then, only slightly and reached over to dip the cloth back in the water. She wrung it out and gently, without speaking, lifted Agatha’s hand and wiped at a spot near her elbow she’d missed. One gentle pass. Nothing more.

    Agatha didn’t stop her.

    Didn’t make a joke. Didn’t pull away.

    Just looked down at the hand cradling her arm and let herself feel it. She didn’t know what to do with the warmth rising up her spine. Didn’t know what to call it.

    But she didn’t want it to stop.

    And that was new, too.

    They rose eventually. Didn’t speak.

    Walked back side by side through the pasture, boots quiet in the dust. Rio didn’t reach for her again.

    Agatha didn’t ask her to.

    But when their hands swung too close, and Agatha’s pinky brushed against Rio’s by accident—

    Neither of them moved.

    Agatha cleared her throat once, low.

    Not because she had something to say.

    Just to remind herself she could.

    Rio noticed anyway. She always did.

    “You okay?” she asked, not worried. Not pressing. Just checking. Agatha nodded. “Yeah. Just—” She gestured vaguely at the pasture, the barn, the calf’s soft bleating behind them. “That one took it outta me.” Rio hummed. “You were steady.”

    Agatha snorted. “Didn’t feel like it.”

    “But you were,” Rio said. Then, after a beat, quieter: “You always are. When it counts.”

    Agatha didn’t know what to do with that.

    She kicked at the dirt with the toe of her boot. Watched a small cloud rise, then settle.

    “My mom used to be like that too,” she muttered.

    Rio glanced over. Didn’t interrupt.

    “She wasn’t… great at a lot of things,” Agatha went on. “But when shit went sideways, she knew how to keep her head. Guess that part stuck.” Rio absorbed that like it mattered. Because it did.

    “I’m glad it did,” she said.

    Agatha’s pinky brushed Rio’s again as they walked. Still neither of them moved it away.

    After a moment, Rio added, almost shyly, “I’ve never seen something like that before. Birth, I mean. Up close.”

    Agatha smiled faintly. “Most folks don’t stick around.”

    “I wanted to,” Rio said. Then, softer, like she wasn’t sure she should say it at all: “I like seeing the things you don’t show people.” Agatha’s steps slowed.

    She didn’t stop walking.

    But her voice dropped when she replied, “Yeah?”

    Rio nodded once. “Yeah.”

    That was it.

    Not a promise. Not a line drawn.

    Just an acknowledgment.

    They reached the porch. The screen door creaked in the familiar way it always did.

    Agatha paused, hand on the railing.

    Rio waited.

    Agatha glanced over, then back at the house.

    “…You can stay,” she said, like it was nothing. Like it wasn’t. Rio’s smile was small. Warm. Real.

    “I know.”

    And this time, when their hands brushed again—

    Neither of them pretended it was an accident.

    Chapter End Notes This chapter was a quiet one, but somehow louder than most. No grand declarations, no sweeping gestures, just blood, breath, and a softness Agatha didn’t mean to show.

    Chapter 15: Held Ground, Held Breath

    Chapter Summary A confrontation at the edge of the ranch forces Agatha to see Rio clearly for the first time, not as quiet or careful, but as deliberate, principled, and unyielding when it matters. What follows is not resolution, but recognition: of anger held in check, of silence that carries weight, and of the charged space that opens when two people finally stop misunderstanding each other. Some lines are crossed. Others are quietly redrawn.

    Chapter Notes This chapter leans into restraint, what it means to hold your ground without raising your voice, and what happens when anger is purposeful instead of loud. It’s also about Agatha seeing something she didn’t know she’d been missing, and not quite knowing what to do with that yet. As always, thank you for reading and sitting in the quiet with them.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes It started with the sound of her name, Rio’s name, sharp and loud, carried on the wind like a warning.

    Agatha was two pastures over, working on a corral rail that wouldn’t stay level no matter how many times she reset the damn thing. The late afternoon heat had settled heavy on her shoulders, thick and unmoving, but that voice cut through it like a blade.

    She stood up straight, hammer loose in her hand.

    Didn’t move at first. Just listened.

    She wasn’t used to hearing emotion in Rio’s voice. Let alone volume. Let alone anger.

    But that’s what this was.

    It came again, low, controlled, but definitely raised. Clipped syllables. That cool, measured tone Agatha had come to know so well, but now it had an edge. A bite.

    Agatha dropped the hammer and started walking.

    Not running. Not yet. Just fast enough that the dust kicked up behind her and her boots clipped against the dry earth with purpose. She found them near the property line, barely half a mile from where Rio had been monitoring that overgrown patch of brushland all summer. The place she’d marked with little flags and noted meticulously in her leather- bound notebook. The place she walked through every morning with reverence, like it wasn’t just land, it was proof.

    And now there was a truck parked crooked at the fence line. A man standing beside it, arms crossed, broad and sun-reddened and already defensive. Rio stood a few feet away, squared off like she wasn’t the smallest one in the clearing, like she wasn’t even a little outmatched. Her back was to Agatha.

    But Agatha could see the tension in her spine from here. Could see the set of her shoulders, stiff and controlled, like every inch of her body was trying to hold back something bigger.

    “I’m telling you,” Rio said, her voice sharper than Agatha had ever heard it, “what you’re doing is illegal. And if you think you’re going to clear that field without a fight, then you’ve clearly never read a conservation easement in your damn life.”

    The man laughed, short, unpleasant.

    Rio didn’t flinch.

    “You’re burning through species that don’t grow anywhere else in the state,” she went on, steady now. Cold. “And if I have to contact the regional agency myself, I will. With documentation. With timestamps. With full geotagged maps. I’ve been tracking this land for six months.” The man opened his mouth to interrupt, but Rio didn’t let him. She stepped forward.

    Not big. Not loud.

    But it was enough to make Agatha’s breath catch in her chest. “You think this is about shrubs?” Rio snapped. “This is a disappearing biome. This is erosion control. This is butterfly and bee pollination corridors, entire chains of life that depend on these exact ten acres, and you’re out here with a goddamn bulldozer like it’s all just dead weight.” There was silence for a beat.

    Just the wind in the grass.

    The man scoffed. “Jesus. You people—”

    “You people?” Rio echoed, quiet now.

    Dangerous quiet.

    Agatha saw the moment the man stepped back, not much, but enough. Rio didn’t say another word.

    Didn’t have to.

    The man glanced between her and the field, jaw working like he was trying to decide whether it was worth the trouble.

    And then, without a word, he turned back toward his truck. Climbed in. Slammed the door.

    The engine sputtered, then roared.

    He drove off without another glance.

    Rio didn’t move.

    Not right away.

    Her shoulders rose and fell once, then again. Her arms were still rigid at her sides, hands balled into fists so tight her knuckles had gone white. And that’s when Agatha realized, Rio had been holding it in. Not just this.

    All of it.

    The quiet. The restraint. The carefulness. It wasn’t because she didn’t feel things.

    It was because she felt them too much.

    And right now, it was all right there. Written in the stillness of her body. In the line of her mouth. In the way she stared down the fence post like it had betrayed her.

    Agatha took a step forward.

    Then another.

    She didn’t speak.

    Not yet.

    But the wind shifted between them, and Rio turned.

    Her face was flushed.

    Eyes bright. Jaw tight.

    But she wasn’t shaking.

    She was still.

    Like the center of a storm that had passed, but not finished. And Agatha, God help her, felt her knees go just a little weak. Because she’d never seen anything so furious and so beautiful at the same time.

    The dust hadn’t settled yet.

    Agatha could still hear the faint rumble of the truck retreating down the gravel road, its wheels spitting rock behind it like an accusation. But the noise was already fading, drowned out by the sound of Rio’s breath, measured, slow, barely shaking.

    She stood just a few feet away, still facing the field. Back straight. Hands clenched at her sides like she hadn’t noticed she was doing it. Agatha had never seen her like this.

    She wasn’t pacing. Wasn’t ranting. There was no chaos in her. No flailing, no fury thrown wide. Just a terrifying kind of precision. Controlled anger, leashed tight.

    Focused.

    Purposeful.

    Agatha cleared her throat, the only sound she could make around the pressure in her chest.

    “That happen often?” she asked, voice rough.

    Rio exhaled. Not a laugh. Not quite. More like a release that had been waiting for hours.

    “Often enough,” she said, wiping her palms on her pants absently. “Not always this close.”

    She looked back out across the brushland, what was left of it. Yellow flags still fluttered in the wind, marking the edge of her study zone. Beyond that, fresh tire tracks dug scars through the dry soil.

    Agatha followed her gaze.

    It was just land, to most people. Dry. Choked with undergrowth. No cattle would graze there. No crops would grow. But Rio had walked that space every morning with the kind of reverence Agatha usually reserved for fixing things that mattered.

    Now Agatha saw it clearly.

    That patch of nothing was Rio’s whole proof that something delicate could thrive if you just left it the hell alone.

    Agatha let the silence stretch a little longer.

    “You were real calm,” she said eventually. “Even when you were mad.” Rio’s shoulders lifted in a shrug.

    “Anger doesn’t help if you don’t aim it right.”

    Agatha chewed on that.

    Didn’t know what she expected. Maybe yelling. Maybe something sharp and defensive, now that the moment had passed. But Rio just… stood there. Solid. Unapologetic.

    Like this wasn’t something to explain.

    Like she didn’t owe anyone the softness Agatha had always assumed came natural to her.

    Agatha studied her for a long beat.

    Then said, low and quiet, “You’re not soft.”

    Rio turned her head just enough to meet her eyes. “No,” she said simply. And Agatha finally understood.

    “You just don’t waste your teeth.”

    It wasn’t a compliment. Not really.

    Not a judgment, either.

    Just a fact, spoken into the space between them like it had always been there.

    Rio didn’t answer.

    But something flickered across her face. A twitch at the corner of her mouth. A gleam in her eye. Not smug. Not amused.

    Something that looked like recognition.

    Agatha looked away first.

    She didn’t like how warm she felt under that gaze.

    Didn’t like the way her ribs seemed to shift, like her heart was trying to get out of its cage and into the space between them.

    “I didn’t know you had that in you,” she said, not quite a whisper. “I figured you did,” Rio replied. “You just didn’t want to see it.” Agatha blinked.

    That wasn’t just a truth. That was a cut, precise and clean, the kind that didn’t even sting right away.

    She let out a slow breath, paced a few steps toward the flags, crouched to pluck one from the dirt. The fabric was frayed. Half-crushed under a tire. She held it between her fingers for a second. Then handed it over. Rio took it without a word.

    Their fingers brushed again.

    Not long.

    But long enough.

    Agatha’s heart kicked hard once, then settled. Uneven.

    She didn’t pull away.

    Neither did Rio.

    They stood like that, close, quiet, still charged with something that hadn’t finished sparking yet.

    And this time?

    Agatha didn’t want it to end.

    The field was quiet again.

    The truck was long gone. The dust had started to settle. The flags still flapped, faint and uneven, like they hadn’t seen the moment everything tilted sideways.

    Agatha’s hand still tingled from where it had brushed Rio’s. She didn’t know what to do with that.

    Didn’t know what to do with any of this, really.

    Rio stood beside her, still holding the crumpled flag between her fingers. She hadn’t looked at it again. Just kept it there in one hand like she wasn’t ready to let it go.

    Her other hand twitched once. Subtle. Barely visible.

    That’s when Agatha noticed it, the tremor in her fingers. Not wild. Not panicked. Just enough to catch the light when it moved. It was the only part of her that wasn’t steady.

    Agatha didn’t say anything.

    She didn’t reach for her. Didn’t offer comfort. Didn’t even shift closer. But her gaze stayed locked on that small tremble, like it was telling her something the words couldn’t.

    She’s not soft.

    She just carries things quieter than most.

    “C’mon,” Agatha murmured finally, voice lower than usual. “Let’s head back.”

    Rio didn’t argue.

    She just nodded once, tight and controlled and fell into step beside her like they’d done it a hundred times before.

    They didn’t talk.

    Didn’t need to.

    The walk back wasn’t far, but it stretched out in Agatha’s head like something bigger than distance. The silence between them didn’t press, it hovered. Heavy, but not suffocating. Like heat before a summer storm. Like something waiting.

    Rio kept her arms folded now, flag still clutched loosely in one hand, the other tucked tight against her chest like it didn’t quite belong to her anymore.

    Agatha walked a half-step behind for the first few yards. Not to trail.

    Just… to watch her.

    She didn’t know how to look at Rio the same after what she’d seen. That fury, controlled and exact. That focus. The way she didn’t raise her voice to scream, but to cut.

    It had unsettled Agatha in ways she hadn’t prepared for. She’d expected Rio to fold. To soften. To maybe cry later, tucked somewhere safe.

    Instead?

    She’d stood her ground like it was all she knew how to do. And it stirred something in Agatha that sat way too deep for comfort. Something like awe.

    Something like—

    She pushed the thought down. Buried it where all the dangerous ones went. They reached the bend in the fence line near the barn. The wind picked up again, cooler now, with the edge of evening trailing behind it. Dust kicked up in low spirals, brushing against Agatha’s boots.

    She glanced sideways.

    Rio’s cheeks were flushed. Her jaw still tight. But her hands had finally stopped shaking.

    Mostly.

    They didn’t speak.

    Didn’t look at each other much.

    But their steps stayed in sync.

    And when Agatha’s shoulder brushed lightly against Rio’s on the narrow stretch of dirt near the cattle gate.

    Neither of them moved away.

    Not even a little.

    They just kept walking.

    And the space between them, tight and electric and quiet, felt like it might never go back to what it was before.

    They didn’t go straight inside.

    The barn was closer than the house, and the horses still needed checking, so Agatha veered off that way without saying a word. Rio followed, boots soft in the dust, the tension between them like static waiting for the right spark. Agatha didn’t know what she was doing.

    Didn’t know what she was letting happen.

    But she wasn’t stopping it.

    Not this time.

    The barn was warm, heat from the day still clinging to the rafters. It smelled like cedar shavings, old sweat, and something sweet from the grain Rio had insisted on helping mix last week.

    Familiar.

    Comforting.

    Too intimate now.

    Agatha unlatched the stall nearest the door, patting the old mare inside without really looking. Just motion. Just something to do with her hands. “I’ll check the feed,” Rio said, quieter than usual.

    Agatha nodded.

    Didn’t answer.

    She didn’t trust her voice not to crack on something.

    They moved like that for a while, parallel. Close, but not close enough to touch. Not yet. The sounds were small: the rustle of hay, the scrape of a boot against wood, Rio’s exhale when she leaned into a barrel to measure out feed by habit more than need.

    It was almost normal.

    Except it wasn’t.

    Because every time Agatha glanced over, Rio was already looking. And every time their shoulders brushed in the narrow aisle, neither of them backed off.

    Not once.

    Agatha didn’t realize she’d stopped working until her fingers brushed Rio’s. They were reaching for the same halter.

    Not an accident.

    Not really.

    Their hands touched, bare, brief, and wholly deliberate in that small, suspended way where neither moved.

    Agatha’s heart thudded once.

    Hard.

    She didn’t look down.

    She looked at Rio instead.

    And Rio looked back.

    No smile.

    No teasing remark.

    Just that open, steady gaze that never wavered. Not after the argument. Not during the silence. Not now, when Agatha’s breath had gone shallow and her throat was full of every thought she didn’t know how to say. The halter slipped between them and hit the floor.

    Neither of them looked at it.

    Agatha didn’t know who moved first.

    Maybe it was her.

    Maybe it was just the weight of every almost-touch that finally caught up with them.

    But suddenly they were close. Too close. Inches, maybe. Her hand still hovering near Rio’s. The air between them hot and too full. Agatha’s voice came out rough. Barely audible.

    “You gonna tell me to stop?”

    Rio blinked once.

    Then shook her head.

    Slow.

    “No.”

    Agatha swallowed hard.

    Her fingers twitched, just slightly and brushed the edge of Rio’s wrist. Not grasping. Just enough to feel the skin there. Warm. Steady. Alive. It wasn’t dramatic.

    It wasn’t explosive.

    It was so quiet it almost didn’t exist.

    But Agatha leaned in.

    Slow.

    Hesitant.

    Like she was stepping off the edge of something without knowing what waited at the bottom.

    She gave Rio time.

    A breath.

    A pause.

    Enough space to back away if she wanted to.

    She didn’t.

    And when their mouths finally met, barely, just the lightest press of lips, it was like something settled inside Agatha’s chest she hadn’t realized was off-balance for years.

    She didn’t pull away.

    Not yet.

    Just rested there, forehead tilted forward, her hand still not quite touching anything else.

    She felt Rio breathe.

    Felt her exhale against her cheek, soft and warm.

    And still, no one said anything.

    Because this wasn’t something they needed to name.

    Not now.

    Not when it had taken this long just to get here.

    Agatha opened her eyes slowly.

    Rio was already watching her.

    And Agatha, breathless, quiet, shaking with it, didn’t move. Didn’t apologize.

    Didn’t run.

    She just stayed there.

    And for the first time since this whole thing began, so did Rio. She didn’t pull away right after.

    Neither of them did.

    The kiss had broken, sure, just a breath ago, but they hadn’t stepped back. Hadn’t retreated into the silence they both knew how to survive in. They just stood there.

    Close.

    Still breathing the same air. Still feeling the warmth of it between them like the ghost of something that hadn’t left yet.

    Agatha felt like her skin didn’t fit quite right anymore. Like she’d been peeled back and left standing there, exposed, aching, calm. That was the strange part. How calm it felt.

    She expected panic. Regret. The familiar need to shove everything down before it could take root.

    But instead?

    It felt like standing in the sun after too long in the cold. Uncomfortable, sure. Blinding. But… right.

    Her mouth twitched.

    The nerves came slow, late, like they were fighting to catch up with the moment. Her fingers flexed once at her side, looking for something to do. And when she opened her mouth, what came out was habit.

    A defense.

    A thing she could hide behind.

    “You gonna gloat about this now?”

    Her voice was too rough, too low to sound casual, but she said it anyway. Tried to wear the smirk she didn’t quite feel.

    Rio didn’t smile.

    Didn’t deflect.

    She just tilted her head, eyes still steady, expression unreadable in the dim barn light.

    “I don’t win anything,” she said softly, “unless you let me.” Agatha’s heart didn’t skip.

    It staggered.

    That should’ve felt loaded. That should’ve made her bristle, like hell she let anyone win anything.

    But it didn’t.

    It hit her somewhere lower. Quieter.

    Because Rio wasn’t teasing.

    She wasn’t playing.

    She said it like a fact. Like she knew that whatever just happened wasn’t about conquest. Wasn’t about pride. Wasn’t about winning. It was about permission.

    Agatha didn’t realize she was holding her breath until she let it out in one long exhale.

    She took a step back, not away. Just enough to look at Rio’s face from a different angle. Her expression hadn’t changed. Calm. Present. Watching. Waiting.

    Agatha licked her lips. Thought about saying something sharp. Something to reclaim ground she hadn’t even lost.

    But instead, she looked at Rio’s mouth again.

    Then kissed her a second time.

    Slower.

    More sure.

    No hesitation in it this time, just the quiet, aching kind of want that had been building for weeks, coiled tight behind every glance, every almost- touch, every moment they’d refused to name for what it was. Rio kissed her back like she already knew this was coming. Like she’d been waiting.

    And maybe she had.

    When they parted again, Agatha stayed close. Let their foreheads brush. Let herself linger.

    She felt Rio’s hand at her waist, not possessive. Just there. Anchoring. “I didn’t think it’d be like this,” Agatha murmured.

    “Like what?”

    Agatha swallowed.

    “I don’t know. Real.”

    Rio smiled then.

    Small. Soft. Devastating.

    “It is.”

    Agatha closed her eyes.

    She didn’t have anything else to say.

    Didn’t want to move.

    Not yet.

    Because the truth of it, that she’d kissed her, and Rio hadn’t pulled away, and nothing had burned down around them, felt too new to walk away from. And for once?

    She didn’t need to.

    Chapter End Notes Agatha has spent most of her life believing silence was something you endured or used as armor. This chapter lets her learn something else: that silence can also be an invitation. That anger can be measured. That staying doesn’t always mean pushing. And sometimes, when you stop bracing for impact, you find yourself close enough to cross a line you didn’t know you were ready to step over. Thank you for staying with them as things finally, quietly, shift.

    Chapter 16: The Heat Makes Honest Things of Us

    Chapter Summary The days after the kiss don’t change everything, but they change enough.

    Between sunburned soil, a rusted water tank and laughter born of heat and impulse, something between them bends, quietly, deliberately, into something neither of them can pretend is accidental anymore. Chapter Notes This chapter lives very much in the space between almost and finally. It’s about heat, literal and emotional and what happens when you stop pretending you don’t feel it.

    As always, thank you for walking slowly with me through this story. These two don’t do anything quickly… but when they move, they mean it.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes They didn’t talk about it.

    Not the kiss. Not the way it happened. Not the way Agatha had leaned in, waited, and Rio hadn’t pulled away.

    Not the second kiss, either.

    The slower one. The one that had felt… deliberate.

    They’d stood there in the barn, close enough that Agatha could still smell hay on Rio’s collar, still feel the heat of her hands, but nothing had been said.

    No jokes.

    No clarifying statements.

    No “What now?”

    Just quiet. And breath. And the slow return to normal movement, except, it wasn’t normal anymore. Not really.

    Because everything felt different now.

    Not in any big, obvious way. They hadn’t started holding hands or brushing shoulders on purpose. There was no change in routine. Rio still spent her mornings with her survey kit and her worn leather notebook. Agatha still handled the cattle, the fence lines, the feed. She still smoked her first cigarette of the day out on the porch like she always did, watching the sun break across her fields like a war she knew how to win.

    But the way they moved around each other had changed.

    It was in the way Rio stood a little closer when she passed Agatha in the hallway. In the way her eyes lingered now, just for a breath longer, when Agatha handed her a mug of coffee in the morning.

    It was in how Agatha’s fingers twitched when Rio reached for something near her, like her body remembered the shape of contact before her brain did.

    They hadn’t touched again.

    But the space between them was buzzing.

    That kiss, they’d buried it. Or tried to.

    But it was still in the way Agatha’s chest tightened whenever Rio smiled. The kind of smile she gave quietly, when Agatha said something half-smart or muttered under her breath about the damn calves trying to escape again. It was still there in how Rio looked at her sometimes, like Agatha had split open something neither of them knew how to sew back up.

    Agatha noticed herself looking more now, too.

    Not just glancing. Watching.

    The line of Rio’s back as she bent over her equipment case. The curl of her hair at the nape of her neck when she tied it up to keep the sweat off her shoulders. The way she squinted when the sun hit just wrong. Every little thing was suddenly sharp.

    Alive.

    And Agatha hated how easy it was to imagine pulling her in again,, pressing a hand low on her spine, mouth soft at her jaw.

    She didn’t do it.

    But the thought was there.

    Louder by the day.

    By the time Thursday rolled around, Agatha couldn’t walk past Rio without wanting to turn around and look again.

    She didn’t.

    But she wanted to.

    And that was the thing, wasn’t it?

    It wasn’t the kiss that had changed anything.

    It was the wanting.

    The knowing.

    And now that it was out in the open, there was no putting it back. The heat came in early.

    By nine, the sky had bleached to a flat, searing blue, no clouds, no breeze, no mercy. The kind of heat that didn’t just sit on the land but sank down into it, heavy as wet wool. It made everything slower. Meaner. Agatha swore the cattle moved like they were drunk. Even the wind, when it came, felt like someone exhaling straight from their lungs, hot and shallow.

    She had her hat pulled low, sweat already darkening the back of her shirt as she walked the fence-line for the third time. A calf had gone missing that morning, and she was too proud to radio for help just yet. She kept moving. Boots dragging a little now. Mouth dry. Shirt sticking. And all the while, even as her mind wandered, water levels, shade, the ache in her hip, it kept drifting back to her.

    To Rio.

    Rio, who had taken her soil samples out past the dry creek bed this morning, muttering about erosion rates and gravel loam, her notebook tucked under one arm like it held the whole damn future.

    Rio, who had looked at Agatha when she passed her on the porch like something had already happened, not just the kiss, not just the quiet after, but something unspoken. Ongoing.

    And Agatha, sweating through her shirt and trying not to trip over sunbaked roots, couldn’t get the memory of Rio’s mouth out of her head. Not just how it had felt.

    But how easy it had been to reach for her.

    And how hard it was not to do it again.

    She found the calf eventually, pressed up against a low cedar, panting, ears twitching at flies. She gave it a sharp whistle and a few slow steps, nudging it gently toward the herd again.

    The damn thing followed without a fight. Too hot to resist. She wasn’t far from the east water tank now. The big metal one. The one she kept filled for overflow in case the main lines failed. The air shimmered above it like a fever.

    And maybe she was feeling a little fevered herself.

    Because just beyond the tank, crouched low near a patch of disturbed soil, was Rio.

    She was in a tank top now, her overshirt draped over a fence post, her skin flushed at the neck and shoulders. Sweat had darkened the center of her chest and curled loose strands of hair at her jaw.

    She looked… hot. Overheated. A little dazed.

    But still focused. Always focused.

    Her hand moved over her notebook with slow, scribbling strokes. Her lips moved, murmuring to herself. Her brow furrowed.

    Agatha didn’t speak.

    Didn’t announce herself.

    She just watched.

    Rio looked different in the heat. Less contained. Less composed. The way her collarbone rose and fell under the fabric of her shirt. The damp trail of sweat down her throat. The way her legs stretched long and lean into the dust, one boot tapping a slow rhythm in the dirt.

    Agatha swallowed.

    The heat pulsed against her skin.

    It wasn’t just the sun.

    And that realization, how easy it was to want, nearly made her stop breathing.

    She looked away.

    Forced herself to the tank, unlatched the spigot, and checked the water line. It was full, thank God. Still cold, too, fed straight from the well under the barn. It’d hold.

    She could go.

    She should go.

    But instead, she stayed.

    Wiped the sweat off her brow with the back of her hand, tilted her hat higher, and glanced over again.

    Rio had stood now.

    Not gracefully. A little stiff, like her knees were regretting all the crouching. She turned and saw her.

    Their eyes met.

    Agatha didn’t smile.

    Neither did Rio.

    But she didn’t look away.

    She let it stretch. Let it breathe.

    The heat hummed between them like a third presence. Like it knew exactly what was happening and didn’t plan to give either of them relief. Agatha tipped her head toward the tank.

    “You gonna pass out on me?”

    Rio exhaled through her nose. “Might. It’s ninety-six in the shade.” “You’re not in the shade.”

    “Yeah,” Rio said dryly, “I noticed.”

    Agatha snorted.

    Something in her chest eased.

    And even though her shirt clung, and her hands itched, and the air still felt like it was pressing her skin straight to the bone—

    She stayed.

    Rio hadn't moved far from the soil plot, just paced a few steps before sinking down beside the water tank. Her notebook hung limp in her hand, and her face was flushed high across the cheeks. Her braid stuck to the back of her neck.

    Agatha could tell she was pushing through it, like she always did. But the heat was too much.

    Even Agatha felt like her blood was starting to move slow. She flexed her fingers just to remind herself she could.

    She walked around the other side of the tank, giving herself a moment to think or not think, which was harder. Her own shirt was stuck to her spine. Her thighs felt damp beneath the jeans. Her hat was doing what it could, but her scalp was starting to prickle under the sweat.

    And Rio just sat there.

    Not complaining. Not asking.

    Not looking at her either.

    But Agatha felt her. That presence. That hum.

    It was starting to feel dangerous again.

    Or maybe it always had.

    The tin ladle hung on the nail beside the spigot. It was the kind the ranch used in the old days, broad and dented, good for a drink or a rinse. She took it down, filled it from the tank with one smooth dip.

    And then, without a word, without hesitation, Agatha walked around the curve of the metal tank and dumped it right over Rio’s head. Rio gasped.

    The sound she made was half-startled inhale, half strangled squeak. Her whole body flinched. Arms jerked up on instinct. The notebook went flying, forgotten, and her braid hit the back of her shoulder like a soaked rope.

    Water ran down her scalp, soaked the neck of her tank top, streamed in rivulets down her collarbones and into the hollow of her throat. Agatha didn’t move.

    Didn’t apologize.

    She just watched the way Rio blinked against the water dripping into her lashes, mouth open, eyebrows high.

    “Ay, Dios mío,” Rio finally said, voice strangled.

    Agatha raised one shoulder in a lazy shrug.

    “You looked like you were about to melt.”

    “You could’ve warned me!”

    “You’d’ve ducked.”

    A beat passed.

    And then Rio laughed.

    Sharp. Surprised. Real.

    The kind of laugh that took her whole body with it, shoulders shaking, head tipping back, mouth open.

    Agatha had heard her laugh before.

    But not like this.

    Not this.

    It caught her off guard. Made her forget how heavy the air was. How hot the sun still burned down on her shoulders. How close they were to something they hadn’t said out loud yet.

    Rio laughed like she didn’t give a damn about holding anything back. And Agatha—

    Agatha laughed, too.

    At first it was just a puff of breath through her nose. Then a little huff. And then it took her over like her body had been waiting for the excuse. Her hand braced on her thigh, head ducked, hat tipping forward as she let it come.

    They were both delirious.

    Soaked in sweat and half-covered in dust and soil, laughing like fools beside a rusted-out water tank in the middle of a nowhere pasture. And yet, Agatha couldn’t remember the last time she’d felt that light. Rio wiped her hands down her face, still grinning.

    “You are dangerous, you know that?”

    Agatha tilted her head, still catching her breath. “You say that like it’s news.”

    Rio gave her a look, mock-scolding, but softened around the edges. Agatha looked away. Not far. Just enough to keep her from saying something real. Something like I’d do it again just to hear you laugh like that. Something like I think about you too much when I’m alone. The tin ladle was still in her hand. She dipped it again, then held it out this time.

    “Truce?” she offered.

    Rio eyed it like it might be a trick. Then leaned forward and took a sip, lips brushing the rim without hesitation.

    Her throat moved as she swallowed. Water dripped from her jaw, trailed down to the open collar of her shirt.

    Agatha looked away again.

    But only for a second.

    Rio leaned back against the tank first, her knees drawn up, hands braced in the dirt behind her. Water dripped slowly from her hair, soaking into the collar of her shirt. Her skin gleamed at the throat, damp and flushed and kissed pink by the sun.

    Agatha hesitated only a second before joining her.

    The metal of the tank was still warm at her back, sun-heated, not scalding, just enough to pull the sweat from her spine. She let her arms rest loosely on her knees, fingers curling into the fabric of her jeans like she needed to hold onto something.

    They sat like that—close but not touching—with the heat thrumming around them and the hush that follows laughter still hanging in the air like smoke.

    It was stupid, she thought absently.

    How good it felt to be beside her like this. How easy it was to fall into step with her even when the world felt like it was cracking open under the heat. There wasn’t anything to say.

    And somehow, that made it better.

    The water tank creaked as it settled. A fly buzzed too close to her boot. The sky above was a blistering, endless blue.

    Agatha tilted her head to the side and let her eyes slide toward Rio. She wasn’t looking at her. Not exactly.

    But her mouth was still curled and her hands had gone still in the dust, fingers trailing small half-moons like she didn’t realize she was doing it. A bead of water slipped from her jaw to her collarbone. Agatha tracked it with her eyes until it disappeared into the fabric of her tank top. God, she looked beautiful.

    Not delicate. Not polished.

    But alive.

    Full of fire and dirt, and purpose. The kind of woman who didn’t need to raise her voice to draw blood. The kind who stood her ground and then laughed like hell when someone poured water on her mid-heatwave. And Agatha, who’d spent a lifetime building fences, literal and otherwise, could feel all of hers splintering.

    Not breaking.

    But bending.

    And that was somehow worse.

    “You gonna say something?” Rio murmured suddenly, eyes still forward. Agatha blinked. “Didn’t plan to.”

    “Hm.” Rio tilted her head. “You’ve been thinking too loud again.” Agatha huffed, but it was half a smile. “You know everything, huh?” “No,” Rio said. “But I know you.”

    That shut her up.

    Not in a bad way. Just in a what-the-hell-do-you-do-with-that kind of way. She felt heat crawl up her neck that had nothing to do with the sun. “I ain’t used to people sitting still with me this long,” she said finally. “Most folks get fidgety.”

    “I’m not most folks,” Rio said simply.

    And there it was again, that quiet certainty that lived in Rio’s voice. The kind that didn’t ask to be believed. The kind that just was. They didn’t move.

    Didn’t shift closer.

    But something in the air did.

    Agatha could feel it, low and slow and deep in her chest. A hum. A pull. Not the desperate kind of wanting, but the weighted kind. The kind that made you stay in place just to feel it longer.

    Rio turned her head finally, and their eyes met under the brutal sun and beside the half-empty tank, with sweat still drying in the lines of their skin. “You ever just… sit still?” Agatha asked, quieter now.

    Rio nodded once. “When it’s worth it.”

    Agatha’s throat went dry.

    And not from the heat this time.

    They didn’t speak again for a while.

    They just sat there, sun-stupid and sweat-slick, barely breathing, and aching with the kind of quiet that only ever comes when something important’s trying to bloom.

    The sun was dipping just low enough to soften. That golden hour warmth had started to stretch across the pasture, turning the dust to honey and the curve of Rio’s cheek to something glowing.

    Agatha hadn’t moved in minutes.

    She didn’t want to.

    Rio’s presence beside her was steady. Grounded. The kind of stillness that wrapped around Agatha’s ribs and made her want to stay still, too. The heat had drained the edges off her sharpness. Left her loose in her own skin. She let her boots stretch out in front of her, let her head fall lightly back against the tank. Let herself breathe.

    And when she turned, Rio was already watching her.

    Not shy about it. Not pretending otherwise.

    Just looking.

    And for once, Agatha didn’t look away.

    “Why’re you still here?” she asked, voice quiet, thick with sun and dust and something heavier.

    Rio blinked, unbothered.

    “I told you,” she said. “I’m not going anywhere.”

    Agatha licked her bottom lip. Her mouth was dry again. But her pulse was not.

    Rio’s gaze dropped to her mouth.

    And Agatha’s heart stuttered.

    Something broke loose behind Agatha’s ribs.

    Not sharp. Not painful.

    Just… soft.

    And in the next breath, she moved.

    Not far. Not frantic. Just a slow lean forward, watching Rio’s face like it might disappear if she moved too fast. Her hand reached out, fingers brushing the edge of Rio’s knee, sun-warmed, and still damp in places from the water.

    Rio didn't flinch.

    She melted.

    Just slightly.

    Her eyes fluttered half-closed, her mouth parted. And that was all the permission Agatha needed.

    She closed the space with a kiss that was, somehow, both deeper and gentler than the first two. One that sank in slow. One that tasted like salt and heat and everything they hadn’t let themselves say.

    Rio kissed her back like she meant it.

    Like she’d been waiting for this moment since the first fence-line glance. Agatha felt the world narrow, hot metal at her back, Rio’s mouth soft against hers, and nothing else.

    Their lips slid slow. Careful. Familiar now, but still exploring. Agatha angled her head, deepened the kiss. Rio sighed into her and Agatha swallowed it whole.

    One of Rio’s hands came up, palm pressing light to the curve of Agatha’s jaw, thumb just barely grazing the hinge of it.

    Agatha leaned into the touch.

    Let it anchor her.

    Then she moved closer.

    Shifted to her knees, boots scraping softly in the dirt, her free hand bracing beside Rio’s hip. Her body lined up along Rio’s side without hesitation. She kissed her again, deeper this time, still gentle, but not shy. And Rio—God, Rio—

    She let herself be kissed. Let her head tilt back slightly. Let her mouth open under Agatha’s, breath catching in a quiet gasp when Agatha slid her hand up the back of her neck.

    Their mouths moved together like they’d done this a hundred times in dreams they never dared to admit having.

    It wasn’t rushed.

    But it was hungry.

    All sun-warmed mouths and unspoken ache, hands that stayed respectful but trembled with the urge to do more. They kissed until Agatha forgot the heat. Until her hat tipped back off her head and she didn’t bother catching it.

    Until they were breathing hard against each other’s mouths and neither of them wanted to stop.

    Eventually, it was Rio who slowed it.

    Not pushed her away, just let the kiss fade gently, like a firebank settling into coals.

    She leaned her forehead against Agatha’s.

    Eyes still closed.

    Breathing through her nose.

    Agatha didn’t speak.

    Didn’t move.

    Just stayed there, heart thudding, thumb stroking absently over the curve of Rio’s knee.

    After a long moment, Rio whispered, “That’s thrice you’ve kissed me first.” Agatha smiled. Barely.

    “You gonna complain?”

    Rio let out a quiet, stunned-sounding laugh.

    “No.”

    Agatha kissed her again. Once. Soft. Pressed to the corner of her mouth like punctuation.

    And then she whispered: “Then hush.”

    Chapter End Notes And just like that… they’re making out next to a water tank in ninety- degree heat and neither of them is running for the hills. This chapter was a sun-soaked exhale, messy, hot, and full of wanting.

    Chapter 17: She Makes the Silence Gentle

    Chapter Summary Agatha and Rio fall into an easy rhythm, shared mornings, shared work, shared quiet. No declarations, no plans. Just presence. And the slow, undeniable realization that neither of them is leaving. Chapter Notes We’re in our soft domestic tension era, folks.

    They’re waking up at the same time now. Drinking coffee like they’ve always done it together. Riding fence lines. Brushing hands. Not calling it anything, but still knowing what it is.

    Also: Agatha speaks exactly two words in Spanish and it nearly kills them both. enjoy.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes Agatha wasn’t sure when it started.

    Not really.

    It wasn’t like Rio moved in, wasn’t like they’d had a conversation, signed anything, made a plan. But somewhere along the way, her days started with the sound of boots hitting wood that weren’t her own. With the smell of coffee she didn’t make. With the low rustle of paper as Rio took notes at the porch table while the sun yawned over the pasture.

    And Agatha didn’t mind.

    In fact, she found herself leaning into it.

    The quiet click of Rio’s pen. The shape of her in the porch chair, one leg bent, her shirt soft with wear and sun. The way she muttered to herself in the morning light, brow furrowed, fingers already stained with soil she hadn’t even gone out to collect yet.

    Agatha never interrupted.

    She just took the second mug when it was offered. Drank it black. Sat down a few feet away with her legs stretched out and her hat pulled low. They didn’t speak, not right away. Didn’t need to. The morning did the talking for them.

    It had become a ritual.

    By the time the sun crested full over the ridge, Agatha had already checked the southeast gate. Rio had already cataloged the weather patterns in that little beat-up field book of hers. She’d already added a few muttered notes about the soil shift on the north slope and the ants near the west fence that she claimed weren’t “just ants, actually.”

    Agatha didn’t argue.

    She just said, “Hm,” and handed her a bottle of water.

    Some days, they went hours without speaking more than a dozen words. But there was a rhythm now.

    Agatha knew Rio always started at the same fencepost near the streambed. She never stepped in the same place twice if she could help it, always careful where her boots landed. Agatha, trailing behind sometimes, would watch her trace the land like it was sacred.

    And maybe it was, to her.

    Maybe Agatha liked watching that more than she meant to.

    They’d pass each other sometimes in the middle of a task, Agatha hauling fence wire over one shoulder, Rio wiping sweat from her jaw with the inside of her elbow. A glance. A nod. Occasionally a faint smirk. “You always look like you’re about to fight the sun,” Rio had said once. Agatha had grunted. “Sun’s been tryin’ to pick a fight with me for years.” They didn’t flirt. Not outright. But something simmered in the space between their movements now. A hum beneath the quiet. The kind of thing that felt like a secret even when no one else was around to hear it. Routines had settled like dust.

    Shared coffee.

    Split chores.

    Parallel silences.

    And maybe the biggest shift of all: Agatha wasn’t waiting for it to end. Wasn’t guarding herself for the moment Rio would pull back. Say she needed space. Needed distance. Needed to remember whatever it was she came here to do before they ever tangled themselves up in each other. Because Rio didn't leave.

    She didn’t stay because she had to.

    And when Agatha realized that, that every night Rio came back to the porch, to the shared dinner, to the easy silence under the stars and the last mug of coffee poured by steady hands—

    Her chest went tight.

    And then eased.

    Just a little.

    It was Rio’s idea to ride again.

    They’d just finished their morning coffee, Agatha’s hands still warm around the tin mug when Rio turned toward her with a steady look and said, “I want to try the north fence today. On horseback.”

    Agatha blinked.

    Not in disbelief. Just surprise.

    She’d seen Rio ride once before. Stiff. Careful. More comfortable on the ground than in the saddle. Not ungraceful, just unsure.

    But there was no hesitation in her voice now.

    Just that quiet, thoughtful confidence she wore like a second skin. Agatha finished her coffee in one slow swallow.

    “All right,” she said. “Pick your horse.”

    Rios squinted toward the corral, one hand shading her eyes. “Which one won’t kill me?”

    “None of mine will kill you,” Agatha said. “But get yourself on the wrong side of June’s mood and she’ll make you wish you’d picked a bicycle.” Rio huffed a laugh.

    “Lady, then.”

    Agatha nodded once. “Good choice.”

    They walked down together, the heat already settling around their shoulders like a weight. The horses shifted lazily in the midday sun, tails flicking. Rio moved with more ease than she had weeks ago. Her boots no longer looked like they were trying to walk in someone else’s rhythm.

    Agatha opened the gate and clicked her tongue once. Lady trotted over, glossy and docile. Agatha ran a hand down her flank, murmured something low that Rio couldn't quite hear. She moved like she was built for this, solid and efficient and already knowing exactly what came next. Rio just watched.

    Still sometimes caught off guard by how natural she looked here. Agatha adjusted the stirrups, checked the saddle, gave the reins a tug. Then she stepped back and looked at Rio.

    “Well,” she said. “Get on up.”

    Rio hesitated for a beat.

    Not long. But long enough for Agatha to see it.

    Then she moved to the side of the horse and set her boot in the stirrup, hands firm on the saddle horn. She got about halfway up before the weight shifted beneath her and suddenly her balance tipped.

    Agatha was there before Rio could even make a sound.

    One hand steady at her waist, the other braced against the saddle And just like that, she lifted her.

    Not a grunt. Not a groan. No dramatic show of strength, just a clean, simple motion. One second Rio’s boot was slipping, the next she was in the saddle, back straight, breath caught in her throat.

    She stared down at Agatha, eyes wide, hands still gripping the saddle horn like it might vanish out from under her.

    Agatha didn’t step back immediately.

    Her hand stayed on Rio’s thigh, just above the knee.

    Not inappropriate. Not too much.

    But firm.

    Grounding.

    Rio blinked once.

    Then again.

    Agatha met her gaze, mouth twitching like she might say something. But she didn’t.

    She just held her there. Steady.

    Then said, quiet: “You all right?”

    Rio cleared her throat.

    “Yes,” she said. A little too fast.

    Then, softer: “I didn’t realize you were going to lift me, again.” Agatha shrugged. “You looked like you were gonna fall.”

    Rio swallowed. “I had it.”

    “Sure you did.”

    Agatha let her hand drop and gave the stirrup a final tug to check its position.

    But Rio still hadn’t stopped watching her.

    She wasn’t flushed from the heat this time.

    And Agatha—despite herself—felt the tug in her stomach again. That low, familiar hum that lived somewhere between pride and want. Without looking up, she muttered, “Next time, just ask. I’ll get you up there clean every time.”

    Rio didn't answer.

    But she didn’t look away, either.

    Agatha swung up on June a moment later, the saddle creaking under her weight. The horse shifted, but Agatha settled them both without a word. They started riding in silence.

    They didn’t talk much.

    Just enough.

    The horses trotted easily, hooves muffled in the dust, and Agatha found herself easing into it, her body, her thoughts, even her breath. Everything moved slower now. Gentler. Like the world wasn’t asking anything from her for once.

    She always was.

    Rio smiled. “Maybe.”

    Straightened.

    “You’re gonna have to translate, darlin’.”

    Rio smiled, real and bright and proud.

    Agatha was going to say something. Maybe.

    And maybe pretty.

    And maybe something else entirely.

    Or maybe she did.

    Didn’t speak.

    Just pulled a chair out slowly and sat beside her.

    Didn’t flinch.

    Just full.

    But soft and quiet and real.

    Like Rio herself.

    Agatha hadn’t realized how many she’d memorized until she sat this close. The breeze shifted. Rio reached up and pushed her hair back with her wrist, revealing the long slope of her neck. Another freckle. Just there. Hidden in the dip below her ear.

    She wasn’t trying to look. Not really.

    But something about being near her like this, without needing to do anything else, made it feel impossible not to notice.

    Everything.

    The way Rio smelled faintly like hay and soap and that old book she always carried.

    The way her knee bounced when she was thinking hard.

    The way her lips moved when she reread a line, like she was tasting the shape of every word.

    Agatha didn’t move.

    She didn’t speak.

    She just sat there, shoulder brushing Rio’s once, then again. And Rio didn't pull away.

    Didn’t flinch.

    Didn’t even stop writing.

    She just stayed.

    Close enough to feel the warmth of her. Close enough to count freckles in the porch light.

    And that was enough.

    For now.

    Agatha used to dread the in-between.

    Not the work, that was easy. The work had rhythm. Predictability. But the pause before things began or ended, when the day hadn’t decided what it wanted from her yet, used to feel like a kind of ache. Like absence.

    Now it just felt like breath.

    She didn’t look at Rio yet.

    Didn’t need to.

    She could feel her.

    In the soft clink of spoon against ceramic. In the faint scratch of her pencil against paper. In the way her knee bumped the underside of the table in a rhythm Agatha had memorized like song.

    There was no talking. No need for it.

    Agatha stared at the far pasture, her eyes unfocused, coffee warming her palms, and thought, this is what peace feels like.

    Not silence. Not solitude. But sharing it.

    The horses were moving slow in the distance, settling for the evening, tails flicking, coats already gleaming under the dipping sun. The chickens were raising hell somewhere near the barn.     Not when she was sitting here with her.

    Quiet. Barely louder than the wind slipping over the porch rail. “You’re always the last one to sit down.”

    Rio wasn’t looking at her.

    But they could.

    Agatha swallowed.

    And that was all she could say.

    Because it was already different. She’d gotten up today not because she had to, but because she knew Rio would be there. Because the porch wasn’t empty anymore. Because the quiet was shared.

    Because mornings didn’t start alone anymore.

    Agatha tilted her mug to take another sip.

    But before it touched her lips, Rio’s hand shifted just enough. Their pinkies brushed, just a light graze.

    Not held. Not grasped.

    Just there.

    And Agatha didn’t move.

    She didn’t dare.

    Because somehow, that, that feather-light contact in the quiet between them, meant more than every kiss they’d shared so far.

    Rio didn't speak again.

    She didn’t have to.

    Agatha just sat there, coffee cooling in her hands, heart warm in her chest, and thought—

    This is it. This is how it starts. Quiet. Small. And worth every goddamn second.

    Chapter End Notes They’re not just dancing around each other anymore.

    They’re building something.

    Routines. Rhythms. Language. Silence.

    Agatha wakes up expecting her there.

    She doesn’t drink coffee alone anymore.

    And now Agatha knows what terquedad bonita means.

    And maybe she’s starting to believe it.

    Chapter 18: Let Me Be the Quiet You Come Home

    To Chapter Summary A storm rolls in, heavy and slow, the kind that feels like it’s waiting for something to break. Agatha has weathered plenty in her life, some that soaked the land, some that hollowed her out, but this one is different. Chapter Notes Listen. Sometimes yearning is just two idiots sitting in a kitchen, drinking cold tea, staring at the rain like it might spell out their feelings for them.

    Agatha? terrified to ask for what she wants.

    Rio? Already knows and is just waiting for the words.

    The storm outside? Dramatic as hell.

    It's time. Let them want out loud.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes It started with the smell.

    That thick, electric edge in the air. Earth and ozone and the kind of humidity that pressed down on your chest like a hand. Agatha knew it before she even looked up, knew the weight of a storm not from the sky, but from the way the grass quieted. The way the cattle stood still. The way her own bones seemed to ache in anticipation.

    The air was holding its breath.

    She stood on the porch with one hand braced on the post, thumb rubbing at a crack in the wood she’d been meaning to fix for months. The boards creaked faintly under her boots. Below, the dirt path was starting to darken at the edges, just slightly. Not rain yet. But close.

    The sky had turned a dull, muddled gray, stretching far and wide above the pasture. Not dramatic. Just heavy. Like the light was tired of pretending to be anything else.

    She didn’t call for Rio.

    Didn’t have to.

    Rio shook her head. “Not like this. It rains, yeah. But this—” She motioned to the sky. “This feels like it’s going to mean something.” Agatha let out a quiet breath, more of a grunt than a laugh. “It usually does.”

    She didn’t say that the last time someone had stood beside her like this while the sky threatened to split open, they’d left.

    Rio nodded. “Then we’ve got time.”

    Didn’t even think she meant to.

    But the words curled low in her chest, heavy and slow, and wouldn’t leave. Thunder rumbled again, closer now.

    The first drop hit the porch rail.

    Then another.

    And another.

    Slow, like an apology.

    Agatha didn’t move.

    Neither did Rio.

    They just stood there side by side, not touching, not speaking, as the rain started to soak the edges of the day and the house behind them grew darker with the coming night.

    The storm had finally broken.

    Not all at once, no flash flood, no roaring wind, but steady, soaking rain that crawled down the porch roof like it had nowhere better to be. It tapped against the windows in slow rhythm, crept into the seams of her old flannel shirt, darkened the floorboards where it splashed through the screen door. Agatha didn’t say anything when Rio turned and stepped inside. She just followed.

    The house took on a different kind of quiet during storms. The kind that made it feel like something living, like the walls were holding their breath, like every creak of old wood was saying look, listen, feel this. Agatha shut the door gently behind her.

    The air inside was cooler than outside, but not by much. Still humid. Still heavy. Still thick with the smell of pine soap and rain-damp wood and the faint herbal notes of whatever Rio had made the night before, mint or rosemary or something that didn’t belong here, but had started to feel like it did.

    Rio was already at the stove, striking a match with one practiced motion. She didn’t ask, just lit the kettle and reached for two mugs. Mismatched. One of them Agatha’s. The other… hers now. The one with the faint crack near the handle and the faded blue stripe.

    Agatha leaned against the counter, watching her.

    She didn’t bother pretending not to.

    There was something different about Rio in this light. The kind of different that didn’t make sense unless you’d seen her in the sun first. She looked softer here, out of her field gear, her curls damp, loose at her neck. Her sleeves were rolled past her elbows, her fingers still a little stained from ink and dirt and the day.

    The kettle hissed low.

    Rio didn’t speak, but she glanced over once, eyes catching Agatha’s and staying.

    That was new.

    Not a challenge. Not a question.

    Just… there.

    When the water was ready, she poured the tea in silence. Placed Agatha’s mug on the table without a word. Sat down across from her, back straight, hands cupping her own mug like she needed the warmth more than she let on.

    Agatha moved slowly. Sat with care.

    The kitchen only had one light on, the one above the sink. Yellow and muted and soft. It didn’t quite reach the table. The storm shadows flickered against the walls when lightning sparked somewhere distant, long before the thunder followed.

    The rain was louder now.

    But inside, they were still.

    Agatha took a sip.

    It was strong, earthy, bitter at the edges. Not her usual. But she didn’t complain. She just let it settle in her chest, warm and solid. Rio traced a finger along the rim of her cup. Not really absentmindedly. Just thoughtfully.

    She wasn’t fidgeting.

    She was waiting.

    Agatha didn’t know for what.

    Or maybe she did, and just didn’t want to name it.

    The room felt fuller than it should’ve. Like the air between them was made of something heavier than breath. The rain picked up against the windows. A gust of wind stirred the old curtain by the pantry door. The wood stove creaked, even though it hadn’t been lit in days.

    Agatha looked at Rio again.

    And thought: this is what it feels like when something wants to be said. She cleared her throat once. Quietly.

    Rio didn’t flinch. Didn’t look away.

    The clock on the wall ticked twice, then stuck again, old, temperamental, unreliable like everything else in this house. Except Rio. Rio, who was still here long past dusk, with rain on the roof and a mug in her hand and her eyes steady across the table.

    Agatha opened her mouth.

    Closed it.

    Looked at her hands.

    Then back up.

    “You don’t mind it,” she said, voice low. “The quiet.”

    It wasn’t a question.

    But Rio answered anyway.

    “No,” she said. “I never did.”

    And Agatha believed her.

    Because she was still here.

    Still sitting across from her in the dark with the storm curling around the windows, and one light on in the house, and everything unspoken still waiting.

    The storm curled tighter around the house.

    Rain slipped down the windows in thin rivers. The wind picked up just enough to make the eaves groan. Agatha could hear it now, how the roof let the water drum softly above the kitchen. It wasn’t loud. Wasn’t violent. Just constant.

    A sound she used to dread.

    When she was a girl, storms meant cold floors and tighter silences. Her mama never said much when it rained, just smoked by the window, eyes far away, barely registering Agatha’s presence. The quiet back then hadn’t felt like peace. It’d felt like a warning. Like something waiting to snap. Agatha had carried that kind of silence into adulthood. Built a life out of it. A house where nothing broke unless she said it could. A porch no one stood on unless she let them. A kitchen table that hadn’t seen a second mug in years.

    And now Rio was sitting across from her in the dark.

    Like it was the most natural thing in the world.

    Like she belonged here.

    Agatha hadn’t touched her tea in minutes. It had gone lukewarm in her hands. She didn’t notice. All she could focus on was the woman in front of her, head tilted slightly toward the window, like she was listening to the rain’s story.

    She wasn’t trying to fill the space between them.

    She was honoring it.

    Agatha didn’t know what to do with that.

    Didn’t know how to hold it without breaking something inside herself. Because the silence still scared her sometimes. Not because it was empty. But because it wasn’t anymore. Because it was full of Rio now. Of the way she breathed quietly through her nose when she was thinking. Of the way she sipped her tea like it was ritual, not habit. Of the weight of her gaze when it landed on Agatha and didn’t flinch.

    She looked at her now.

    Not for long.

    Just enough.

    And Agatha felt her throat close.

    She shifted in her chair, like movement could loosen the feeling clawing at her ribs. But it didn’t. Nothing about this moment wanted to be moved through quickly. It demanded stillness.

    Rio didn’t push. Didn’t speak. Just kept sitting there, her presence a kind of warmth that wrapped around the edges of the room like another layer of insulation. Like home.

    Agatha stared down at her mug.

    Then at her own hands.

    Rough. Calloused. Still smeared faintly with dirt from the fenceline earlier that morning. She hadn’t bothered to wash up properly. She’d been too tired. Too distracted. Too busy noticing Rio with that faint smile and that little tilt of her head, like it meant more than she was letting on. Her hands didn’t look like the kind of hands someone like Rio should feel safe sitting across from.

    But she was sitting there.

    No not just sitting.

    Settled.

    Agatha blinked once. Twice.

    And realized: she wasn’t scared of the storm anymore.

    Not because it had gotten quieter. It hadn’t.

    But because Rio made the dark feel safe.

    Because for the first time in her life, someone had walked into the house when the sky opened up and stayed.

    Didn’t flinch at the cracks in the walls.

    Didn’t leave when the lights didn’t reach every corner.

    Didn’t run when Agatha couldn’t quite speak the things that mattered most. She was still here.

    Still listening.

    Still watching the rain with a kind of reverence Agatha had only ever seen in animals before lightning struck.

    Agatha’s throat burned.

    She took a breath.

    Didn’t say anything.

    Not yet.

    But her hand inched forward on the table, slow and cautious. Rio noticed.

    Didn’t reach back.

    Didn’t pull away.

    Just watched her.

    And somehow, that was even better.

    The storm had settled into a steady rhythm now, less of a threat, more of a lullaby. Rain tapping gently on the roof, wind sighing against the windows. Somewhere in the distance, a tree groaned as the wind shifted, but inside the house, everything stayed still.

    Too still.

    The kind of stillness that came after a long exhale. After too many things had gone unsaid.

    Agatha could feel the clock behind her ticking, every second louder than the last. Not rushed. Not insistent. Just reminding her.

    It was late.

    Late enough that any other day, Rio would’ve packed up, said something polite, and made her way to the guestroom.

    But tonight… she stayed.

    They were still sitting at the kitchen table, empty mugs between them, the light over the sink casting soft amber shadows across the tile. The tea had long since gone cold. Neither of them moved to refill it. Agatha’s arm still rested along the table’s edge, fingers barely brushing the wood. Earlier, she’d reached out just slightly. Not enough to touch. Not enough to mean anything obvious.

    But enough that Rio noticed.

    She hadn’t looked startled. Or confused. Or even surprised. She’d just looked back.

    Like she was waiting.

    Like she was saying, I’m still here. Say what you want to say. But Agatha couldn’t say it.

    Not yet.

    She shifted slightly in her chair, restless without meaning to be. Her fingers twitched, then stilled. The wood grain beneath her palm felt too rough. Her throat too tight. She stared at the slant of Rio’s wrist where it rested lightly against the side of her mug.

    Rio hadn’t looked away. Not completely. She was watching the rain now, gaze flicking toward the window like it held some kind of answer. But she was aware. Agatha could feel it in the air between them. The listening. The waiting.

    And she was tired of waiting.

    Not for Rio. But for the part of herself that might finally be brave enough to ask.

    She drew in a breath, quiet, careful and slid her hand further forward. Inches.

    Then a little more.

    Her fingers were calloused, rough from years of work and weather and wanting things she’d never dared reach for. Rio’s hands were smaller. Narrower. But sure. Steady in a way that unsettled her.

    Agatha didn’t touch her.

    Not yet.

    Just let her fingertips hover near the edge of Rio’s hand. So close she could feel the heat of her skin. So close it almost burned.

    And still—

    Still she didn’t know.

    She didn’t know if she was allowed to hold her. Not really. Not outside the heat of a kiss or the haze of a storm or the hush that followed silence. Not in the kind of stillness that demanded nothing but presence. Because Agatha could hold someone for a moment. She could kiss like it was a promise. She could be strong when the world fell apart. But this—this quiet wanting, this staying—was harder.

    More dangerous.

    She wanted to ask.

    What are we?

    Do you want this, too?

    Can I keep you?

    But the words stayed locked behind her teeth, heavy and sharp and terrifying.

    So she reached instead.

    A whisper of motion. A question written in the space between skin. And just before her fingers could brush Rio’s—

    Rio spoke.

    “I should go.”

    Agatha froze.

    Her hand didn’t move.

    She wanted to say no.

    Wanted to say, You should’ve left hours ago, but you didn’t. You stayed. Don’t leave now, not when I’m almost brave enough to hold you like I mean it.

    But she didn’t.

    Couldn’t.

    So instead, she looked up.

    Met Rio’s eyes.

    And said nothing.

    Not with her mouth.

    But her hand stayed there, open, waiting, trembling slightly from the effort of hoping.

    And Rio didn’t stand.

    Didn’t move toward the door.

    She just looked at Agatha.

    And didn’t look away.

    Agatha had known silence all her life.

    She’d been raised in it. Lived with it. Loved it, even, when it meant solitude instead of scorn.

    But tonight, silence felt like a blade pressed to the soft of her neck. Not because Rio was using it against her, Rio never had. No, it was because she knew what came after this kind of quiet. She’d been here before. She’d felt this same stillness when her mother used to walk away from a room mid-sentence.

    She’d felt it the first time someone kissed her and never looked her in the eye again.

    She’d felt it every time she let herself believe something good might stay and it didn’t.

    And now Rio was sitting across from her, and saying she should go. “I should go.”

    The words echoed louder than they were spoken. Louder than the thunder that had already passed.

    And the worst part? She said it like she meant it. Not cold. Not cruel. Quiet. Like maybe she expected Agatha to nod and let her.

    Like that’s what Agatha always did.

    She stared at their hands, her own hovering in that small, impossible space between reaching and retreating. The air between them felt like pressure, like if she moved wrong, it would all collapse.

    Say something. Do something.

    Her heart pounded like she’d just been thrown from a horse. Her throat burned with all the things she hadn’t said for years. Maybe decades. And still, she couldn’t move.

    Until the thought hit her, sharp and sudden: If I don’t, she’ll walk away. And I’ll have no one to blame but myself. That did it.

    She reached across the table, fast and graceless, the way you grab something precious just before it slips off the edge. Her hand closed over Rio’s before she could think better of it.

    And Rio, still and soft and watching, didn’t flinch.

    Her hand was warm. Her pulse steady under Agatha’s palm.

    But Agatha’s wasn’t.

    Her hand shook.

    Just a little.

    She curled her fingers tighter, not holding Rio so much as clinging to her. Trying not to look desperate. Failing.

    She didn’t speak.

    Couldn’t yet.

    So Rio did.
