    Her voice was barely audible above the quiet hum of rain still dripping from the eaves.

    “Agatha.”

    One word.

    Tender. Steady. Knowing.

    That was almost worse.

    Agatha’s jaw clenched. Her throat thickened. She looked down at their joined hands, afraid if she looked Rio in the eye, she’d fall apart right there. “I’ve had a lot of folks leave,” she said at last, voice like gravel, like every word cost her something. “Some didn’t even bother to tell me. Just… faded. Like they were never there in the first place.”

    Rio didn’t interrupt.

    Didn’t shift.

    Didn’t let go.

    Agatha went on, low and rough and unraveling: “I’ve been good at lettin’ it happen. Pretending I didn’t care. Telling myself it’s just easier that way.” She dragged in a breath. “But it ain’t. Not really. It just leaves you starin’ at an empty chair and convincing yourself that’s the life you chose.”

    Another breath. Shaky this time.

    “But you’re not like them.”

    She dared to glance up.

    Rio’s eyes were locked on hers, open, soft, but strong. No pity. Just presence.

    “You’re not,” Agatha said again, firmer. “You sit with me in the quiet and don’t ask for more than I can give. You… you see me, and you don’t turn away.”

    Rio’s fingers curled around hers now, holding back.

    And still, she said nothing.

    Because she didn’t have to.

    Agatha blinked hard, jaw tight.

    “I don’t know what this is supposed to look like,” she admitted. “I don’t got the words for all of it. But I know what I feel. And I know I don’t want this to end with me watching the rain by myself.”

    She took one last breath.

    And finally, she looked her square in the eye.

    “I want you to stay.”

    Not a question.

    Not a plea.

    Just truth.

    Rio’s eyes glistened, but she didn’t cry. She didn’t gasp. She didn’t say a single damn word.

    She just got up, slow and certain, and walked around the table. Agatha turned in her chair, heart threatening to split her open, unsure of what would happen next—

    Until Rio knelt beside her, took both of Agatha’s hands in hers, and pressed their foreheads together. No kiss.

    No fanfare.

    Just the kind of closeness you don’t get unless you’ve earned it. And when Rio finally spoke, her voice shook, just a little. “I was always going to stay.”

    Agatha broke then.

    softly.

    She leaned into her, forehead against forehead, hands held tight, and let herself believe it. Let herself have it.

    Because tonight, for the first time in her life—

    She asked someone to stay.

    And they did.

    Chapter End Notes Agatha reached out. She said it.

    And Rio? She was already holding the door open.

    From this point forward, it’s not just longing, it’s choosing each other. Over and over.

    Even in silence. Even in storms. Especially in the soft, terrifying quiet after.

    We’re in it now.

    Chapter 19: The Quiet After Asking

    Chapter Summary Agatha has learned how to survive silence. What she’s never learned is how to trust it.

    In the aftermath of the storm, she begins to trace the patterns that taught her to expect loss and, for the first time, chooses not to retreat from what’s been offered instead.

    Chapter Notes This chapter is less about what happens and more about what’s finally named, the patterns Agatha has lived inside for years, and the moment she realizes she doesn’t want to keep choosing them.

    Thank you, as always, for sitting with this story and these two women so patiently.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes Rio stayed kneeling.

    She didn’t pull away after saying it. Didn’t straighten, didn’t retreat, didn’t soften the moment by adding anything else to it. She just stayed there, close enough that Agatha could feel her breath warm against her cheek, close enough that the space between them had nowhere to go.

    “I was always going to stay.”

    The words lingered like a held note.

    Agatha didn’t answer.

    She couldn’t, not yet. Her throat felt thick, her chest tight in that way that came after something broke open and didn’t immediately know how to settle again. She kept her forehead pressed to Rio’s, eyes closed, hands still caught between Rio’s palms like she’d reached out and forgotten how to let go.

    Her breathing was uneven. Not panicked. Just off. Like her body was still trying to figure out what to do now that the worst part hadn’t happened. Now that the leaving she’d braced for hadn’t come.

    Rio noticed.

    She always did.

    But she didn’t comment on it. Didn’t shush her. Didn’t say anything soothing or instructive. She just adjusted—barely—tilting her forehead so it rested more comfortably against Agatha’s, easing the angle so Agatha didn’t have to hold herself so rigid to maintain the contact. A small kindness.

    Agatha felt it all the way down her spine.

    The storm outside had softened to a steady rain. Not the sharp, rattling kind anymore. Just water sliding off the roof in slow sheets, tapping the window in a rhythm that felt almost deliberate. The house creaked as it settled, old wood stretching and sighing like it always did when the temperature dropped.

    Agatha was aware of all of it.

    The smell of wet earth seeping in through the cracks. The faint herbal steam rising from the forgotten mugs on the table. The warmth of Rio’s hands, steady and real, grounding her in the present moment instead of the ghosts she’d just named out loud.

    She let out a breath she hadn’t realized she was holding. It came out shaky.

    Rio didn’t tighten her grip.

    Didn’t loosen it either.

    She let Agatha breathe through it in her own time.

    After a moment, long enough that the silence stopped feeling sharp, Agatha shifted. Not away. Just enough to change the angle of her neck, enough to rest her forehead more comfortably against Rio’s temple instead. It wasn’t a decision. Just instinct.

    Rio adjusted with her, like it was the most natural thing in the world. They stayed like that for a while.

    No talking.

    No pressure to do anything else.

    Agatha became aware, slowly, of how tired she was. Not the bone-deep exhaustion of long days or hard labor, but the quieter kind, the kind that came from holding yourself together for too long. From bracing for impact that never came. From finally admitting you wanted something and surviving the saying of it.

    Her hands had gone slack between Rio’s, fingers no longer curled tight, no longer clinging. Rio noticed that too. She shifted her grip just enough to keep Agatha’s hands supported, even as they relaxed.

    Eventually, Rio drew back just far enough to look at her. Not searching. Not assessing.

    Just checking.

    Agatha opened her eyes.

    The kitchen light cast Rio’s face in soft amber, gentler than the harsh daylight, kinder than the storm-dark shadows from earlier. Her expression was open, unguarded in that way she rarely let herself be. No smile. No tension.

    Just presence.

    Agatha swallowed.

    “Hey,” she murmured, the word barely there.

    Rio nodded once. “Hey.”

    That was all.

    It was enough.

    Rio shifted then, careful, slow, easing herself up from her knees without breaking contact entirely. She didn’t stand right away, just moved to sit back on her heels, then onto the floor beside Agatha’s chair, shoulder resting lightly against Agatha’s knee.

    Close, but not demanding.

    Agatha let her.

    The chair creaked as Agatha shifted her weight, angling herself just enough that Rio fit more comfortably there, like the space had always been meant to accommodate her. She rested one forearm on the table, the other hanging loose at her side, fingers brushing Rio’s shoulder without quite meaning to. The contact sent a small, quiet jolt through her.

    Not panic.

    Recognition.

    Rio leaned into it without comment.

    The rain outside grew steadier, heavier for a moment, then eased again. The clock on the wall ticked and stuck, ticked and stuck, useless but familiar. Somewhere deeper in the house, a pipe knocked softly as the temperature changed.

    Agatha stared down at the tabletop, at the water rings from the mugs, the small chip along the edge she’d meant to sand down years ago. Her life, laid out in unfinished details.

    And Rio, sitting beside her anyway.

    “I’m gonna—” Agatha started, then stopped. Cleared her throat. Tried again. “I’m gonna lock up in a minute.”

    Rio nodded. “I can help.”

    Agatha shook her head gently. “You don’t have to.”

    “I know.”

    The words weren’t defensive.

    Just factual.

    Agatha huffed a quiet breath, something almost like a laugh. “You always do that.”

    “Do what?”

    “Help,” Agatha said, without looking at her. “Without makin’ a whole thing out of it.”

    Rio didn’t answer right away.

    When she did, her voice was low. Thoughtful.

    “It doesn’t need to be a whole thing.”

    Agatha let that settle.

    She reached out then, finally, really reached and rested her hand on Rio’s shoulder. Not gripping. Not tentative.

    Just there.

    Rio closed her eyes for a brief second at the contact. Exhaled. Agatha felt it.

    The house felt different now.

    Not quieter, just fuller. Like something had been placed gently back where it belonged. Like the night had wrapped itself around them and decided to keep watch for a while.

    Eventually, they did move.

    Slowly. Together.

    Agatha stood, joints stiff, and Rio rose with her, close enough that their arms brushed as they crossed the kitchen. Agatha checked the back door, the windows, the latch she always double-checked out of habit more than necessity.

    Rio followed, unasked.

    When Agatha turned off the kitchen light, the room fell into shadow, but not darkness. The glow from the storm-muted moon filtered in through the window, silvering the edges of things.

    They paused in the doorway for a moment.

    Agatha looked out at the rain-soaked porch, the night heavy and alive beyond it.

    Then she looked at Rio.

    “Storm’s passed,” she said quietly.

    Rio met her gaze. “Yeah.”

    Agatha hesitated.

    Then, just as quietly: “You can—”

    “I know,” Rio said again, gentle but sure. “I’m here.”

    Agatha nodded.

    Didn’t argue.

    Didn’t test it.

    She just turned and headed down the hall, Rio’s footsteps soft behind her, not falling away, not retreating, just there.

    The night closed around them, not as an ending, but as a pause. A held breath.

    Rio took the space beside her without ceremony, settling in like it had always been allowed. No touching. No questions. Just the quiet agreement of sharing a bed and nothing more.

    And for once, Agatha let herself rest inside it.

    Morning came in quietly.

    Not the abrupt kind, the blaring sun, the birds screaming like they were late for something, but slow and careful, as if it remembered what the night had been. The sky beyond the window was pale, undecided, the color of something still thinking about what it wanted to be.

    Agatha woke before the sun cleared the ridge.

    She always did.

    Her eyes opened without urgency, body already aware of the room before her mind fully caught up. The steady tick of the clock down the hall. The faint drip of water off the eaves outside. The smell of rain-soaked wood and clean dust and—

    Someone else.

    The realization didn’t hit her like a shock.

    It settled.

    Rio was still asleep beside her.

    Not sprawled. Not tangled. Just there, on her side of the bed, facing slightly toward Agatha, one arm bent beneath her head, the other resting loose between them like it hadn’t known what to do overnight and decided to wait it out.

    Agatha didn’t move.

    She let herself register it properly. The rise and fall of Rio’s breathing. The faint crease between her brows. The way her hair had escaped its tie and spread across the pillow in uneven curls, darkened slightly from the humidity.

    Still here.

    Agatha felt something tight in her chest, not fear, exactly. More like awareness. Like her body was bracing for a reflex it didn’t need anymore. She stayed very still.

    Listened.

    The house sounded the same as it always did in the morning. No new noises. No disruption. No evidence that the night before had cracked anything open too wide.

    And yet—

    Everything was different.

    Agatha stared at the ceiling, at the faint water stain she’d never bothered fixing, and let the quiet stretch. Her mind didn’t jump immediately to the worst thing, which was new. Instead, it circled, careful, slow, around the feeling that had followed her into sleep and woken up with her. The pattern.

    Not the memory.

    Not the ache.

    Just the shape of it.

    She exhaled slowly through her nose.

    Beside her, Rio shifted. Not waking, just adjusting, her arm sliding an inch closer, the back of her fingers brushing Agatha’s wrist in an absent, half- asleep movement.

    Agatha froze.

    Not because she didn’t want it.

    Because she did.

    Her first instinct, automatic and ingrained, was to pull away. To give space. To step back before something could be expected of her. Before the quiet turned into something heavier. Before she had to reckon with what staying actually meant when the night was over.

    She didn’t.

    She stayed.

    Let the contact remain. Let the warmth settle. Let her breathing even out again without changing position.

    Rio didn’t wake.

    But she didn’t move away either.

    Agatha closed her eyes for a moment.

    When she opened them again, the ceiling looked the same, but she didn’t. Eventually, she slipped out of bed.

    Slow. Careful. She didn’t rush it, didn’t move like someone sneaking away. She just eased herself upright, feet touching the floor one at a time, weight shifting quietly so she didn’t disturb Rio’s sleep.

    She pulled on her jeans, her old shirt, her boots.

    Normal things.

    Anchors.

    The kitchen greeted her the way it always did in the early morning, cooler than the bedroom, smelling faintly of yesterday’s tea and clean rain. She moved through it on instinct, setting the kettle on the stove, filling it without thinking, striking a match with practiced ease.

    The flame caught.

    The sound grounded her.

    She leaned against the counter while the water heated, arms folded loosely, gaze drifting toward the window. Outside, the pasture looked newly born, grass darker, soil rich and damp, the air still holding that post-storm hush that made everything feel suspended.

    She liked mornings like this.

    But she’d learned not to trust them.

    She poured the water. Made the coffee strong. Took the first mug out to the porch without thinking, then paused, hand tightening slightly around the handle.

    She went back and poured a second.

    By the time Rio appeared in the doorway, Agatha was sitting on the porch steps, elbows braced on her knees, mug steaming between her palms. The morning was still quiet enough that the screen door’s soft creak sounded louder than it should’ve.

    Agatha didn’t turn right away.

    But she felt Rio there.

    “Morning,” Rio said, voice rough with sleep.

    Agatha nodded. “Mornin’.”

    Rio stepped out beside her, barefoot, hair still loose, wearing one of Agatha’s shirts without comment or ceremony. She took the second mug when Agatha handed it over, fingers brushing once, light, familiar now. They sat.

    No rush.

    No awkwardness.

    Just the morning doing what it did best, arriving whether anyone was ready for it or not.

    They drank in silence for a while. The kind that didn’t ask anything of them.

    Eventually, Agatha spoke.

    Not looking at Rio.

    Not dramatic.

    Just… honest.

    “I don’t do well with mornings after,” she said.

    Rio stilled, but only slightly. Not a flinch. Just attention sharpening. “After what?” she asked gently.

    Agatha huffed a quiet breath. “After anything good.”

    She took a sip of her coffee, grimaced at the bitterness, then kept going. “I always wake up thinkin’ it’s already gone. Like if I don’t move fast enough, it’ll slip out from under me.”

    Rio didn’t interrupt.

    Agatha nodded to herself, like she was confirming something she’d already suspected.

    “It ain’t about you,” she added. “Not really.”

    “I know,” Rio said.

    That nearly undid her.

    Agatha swallowed. Pressed her tongue to the inside of her cheek. “I’ve got this habit,” she went on. “When something starts matterin’, I start pullin’ back. Like if I make myself smaller, it won’t hurt as bad when it ends.”

    She glanced sideways then, just briefly.

    Rio was watching her, not intensely. Not clinically.

    Just there.

    Agatha looked back out at the pasture.

    “I don’t think about why much anymore,” she said. “I just… do it.” Rio nodded once. “Patterns don’t need reasons to keep repeating.” Agatha snorted. “You say that like you’ve seen it before.” Rio smiled faintly. “Once or twice.”

    The air between them shifted, not heavy, not light.

    Aware.

    Agatha rested her forearms on her knees, mug dangling loosely from her fingers.

    “I ain’t ready to talk about the first time it happened,” she said quietly. “Not yet.”

    Rio didn’t miss a beat. “That’s okay.”

    “But I am ready to say this,” Agatha continued. “I didn’t stop wantin’ things because they hurt. I stopped because they left.”

    She breathed in.

    Out.

    “And I learned real early that if you don’t want too much, you don’t lose as much.”

    Rio’s voice was steady when she spoke. “And what do you want now?” Agatha didn’t answer right away.

    She stared out at the land she’d built her life around. The fences. The gates. The lines she knew how to maintain.

    Then she said, very quietly, “I want to stop punishin’ myself before anyone else gets the chance.”

    Rio didn’t respond with reassurance.

    Didn’t say I won’t leave or you can trust me or anything else that might’ve felt too big, too fast.

    She just said, “Then we can go slow.”

    Agatha let out a breath she hadn’t realized she was holding. “Slow I can do,” she muttered.

    Rio nudged her shoulder gently with her own.

    They sat there, watching the sun finally crest the ridge, light spilling over the wet grass, turning the world gold and ordinary and possible. Agatha took another sip of her coffee.

    Then, softer—almost to herself—she added, “I don’t know how to unlearn it yet.”

    Rio’s reply was simple.

    “You don’t have to. Not all at once.”

    Agatha nodded.

    That felt right.

    Not a fix.

    Not a promise.

    Just a name for the shape of the thing.

    And for now?

    That was enough.

    The sun had risen fully by the time Agatha spoke again.

    Not high yet, just enough to clear the ridge and set the pasture glowing, dew burning off in thin wisps like breath leaving a body. The light spilled across the porch boards, caught in the grain of the wood, warmed the edge of Agatha’s boots.

    She watched it for a long time.

    Rio didn’t rush her.

    That was becoming a pattern, too.

    “I didn’t learn this from my mother alone,” Agatha said at last. Her voice was steady. Not flat. Not shaking.

    Just… set.

    Rio turned her head slightly, not fully, not like she was bracing for impact. Just enough to show she was listening.

    Agatha rolled the mug slowly between her palms, heat soaking into her skin.

    “There was someone else,” she went on. “A long time ago.” Rio didn’t ask who.

    She didn’t say you don’t have to or only if you want to. She waited.

    Agatha huffed a breath that might’ve been a laugh in another life. “Her name was Wanda.”

    Saying it didn’t hurt the way she’d expected.

    It didn’t crack anything open. Didn’t send a spike through her chest. It just… settled.

    “She came through town one summer,” Agatha said. “Wasn’t supposed to stay long. Said it like that made it harmless.”

    She stared out at the land, eyes tracing fence lines she knew by heart. “She was loud in ways I wasn’t. Smiled at strangers. Asked questions like the answers were already hers. I remember thinkin’—” She paused, mouth tightening. “—that she felt like trouble I might actually enjoy.” Rio smiled faintly at that, just once.

    Agatha noticed.

    “She didn’t know the rules here,” Agatha continued. “Didn’t know how quiet meant somethin’. Didn’t know that if you stayed too long, folks would start expectin’ things of you.”

    Her thumb rubbed the rim of her mug, slow and absent.

    “She asked me what I wanted,” she said. “Like it was a real question.” Rio’s gaze softened.

    Agatha swallowed.

    “No one’d ever done that before. Not without already knowin’ what answer they preferred.”

    She leaned back slightly on her hands, boots braced against the porch step. “I didn’t trust her,” she admitted. “Not at first. Figured anyone who asked that easy was either gonna leave or take somethin’ with them when they did.”

    She glanced sideways at Rio then, just briefly.

    “Turns out I was right.”

    The silence that followed wasn’t sharp.

    It was heavy, but held.

    “She stayed longer than she meant to,” Agatha said. “Weeks turned into months. We didn’t name it. Didn’t need to. She slept over. Left clothes in my drawers. Talked about places she’d been like she was showin’ me postcards from a life I wasn’t sure I could step into.”

    Her jaw clenched, just a little.

    “I started thinkin’ maybe this time was different. Maybe if I didn’t ask too much. Maybe if I didn’t lean too hard—”

    Her voice faltered.

    Just barely.

    “She left while I was out working,” Agatha said.

    No drama in it.

    That was the worst part.

    “I came home and the house felt wrong. Quieter than it should’ve been. Like it knew somethin’ I didn’t yet.” She swallowed. “She didn’t leave a note. Didn’t say goodbye. Just… took her things. Took the sound with her.” Rio’s hand shifted on the porch board between them.

    Not reaching.

    Not pulling back.

    Present.

    “I found out later she’d always planned to go,” Agatha said. “Said it wasn’t personal. Said she didn’t wanna make promises she couldn’t keep.” Agatha snorted quietly.

    “Funny thing is,” she added, “I don’t remember cryin’. Not really. I remember cleanin’. Fixing things. Putting the house back in order like if I got everything just right, it wouldn’t hurt so much.”

    She looked down at her hands.

    “At some point, I realized I’d stopped expectin’ anyone to stay.” Rio let the moment breathe.

    Then, softly, she said, “You didn’t shut down because she left.” Agatha frowned slightly.

    “You shut down because she left without seeing what it would cost you.” Agatha’s throat worked.

    “That,” she muttered, “sounds about right.”

    They sat with it.

    The sun climbed higher. A bird landed on the fence rail, then took off again. The world continued in its quiet, indifferent way.

    After a while, Rio spoke again.

    “Can I tell you something?” she asked.

    Agatha nodded. “Yeah.”

    Rio took a breath, not deep, not steadying. Just enough to choose her words. “When people get close to me,” she said, “I don’t pull away.” Agatha glanced at her.

    “I get… precise,” Rio continued. “Controlled. I start editing myself. Making sure I’m not too much. Not too intense. Not asking for something they didn’t offer.”

    Agatha’s mouth twitched.

    “That’s you bein’ careful,” she said.

    Rio smiled faintly. “That’s me being afraid.”

    She let that sit.

    “I’ve learned how to be easy to keep,” Rio said. “Low-maintenance. Useful. Quietly dependable.”

    Agatha shifted, discomfort blooming, not at Rio, but at recognition. “And what happens?” Agatha asked.

    Rio shrugged. “People stay. For a while.”

    Agatha waited.

    “They stay,” Rio repeated, “until they realize they don’t actually know me.” Agatha exhaled slowly.

    “That’s a hell of a trick,” she murmured.

    “Yeah,” Rio said softly. “We both learned different versions of the same lesson.”

    They looked at each other then.

    Not searching.

    Not testing.

    Just… seeing.

    “I don’t want to do that with you,” Rio added. “I don’t want to be smaller so you don’t leave. And I don’t want you to disappear so it hurts less.” Agatha’s chest tightened.

    “You’re not,” she said quietly. “You’re here.”

    Rio nodded. “So are you.”

    Agatha stared out at the pasture again, then back at Rio. “I don’t know how to do this right,” she admitted. “I might flinch. I might go quiet. I might mess it up.”

    Rio’s answer was immediate.

    “I can live with that,” she said. “As long as you don’t do it alone.” Agatha closed her eyes for a second.

    When she opened them, the world felt, if not safer, then at least more honest.

    She nodded once.

    “That’s… fair,” she said.

    Rio smiled. Not wide. Not triumphant.

    Just warm.

    They sat together as the morning finished waking up around them, coffee cooling, sun rising, patterns named but not yet broken.

    They didn’t move for a long time after that.

    The sun climbed higher. The coffee went untouched. The world kept on with its gentle insistence that life continued whether you were ready for it or not.

    Agatha sat with her back against the porch post, boots planted, arms loose at her sides. Rio sat cross-legged beside her, shoulder warm where it brushed Agatha’s sleeve when the breeze shifted.

    They had said the important things.

    That didn’t mean they were finished.

    Rio was the one who broke the quiet.

    Not abruptly. Not with weight.

    Just enough to let Agatha know she was still there.

    “When you talk about her,” Rio said softly, “you don’t sound angry.” Agatha huffed. “That obvious?”

    Rio smiled faintly. “A little.”

    Agatha stared out at the pasture again, eyes narrowing, not defensive, just thinking.

    “I was,” she said. “For a while. Or maybe I thought I was.” She shifted, rubbing one hand over her thigh, slow and absent. “But anger wasn’t what stuck.”

    Rio tilted her head, listening.

    “What stuck?” she asked.

    Agatha took a breath.

    Long. Measured. Like she was stepping into cold water.

    “She didn’t break my heart,” Agatha said. “Not really.”

    Rio’s brows knit, just slightly.

    Agatha noticed.

    “It’s not that she left,” Agatha continued. “I’d survived people leavin’ before. Hell, I was used to it. Folks come through town. Folks move on. That’s just how things go.”

    Her jaw tightened.

    “What broke me,” she said, “was realizing I let myself believe she wouldn’t.”

    Rio didn’t interrupt.

    Didn’t soften it.

    Didn’t rescue her from the thought.

    Agatha kept going.

    “She didn’t promise anything,” Agatha admitted. “Didn’t lie. Didn’t cheat. Didn’t even pretend she was somethin’ she wasn’t.”

    She let out a quiet, bitter breath.

    “I filled in the rest myself.”

    The words landed between them, heavy, unmistakable.

    “I thought if I stayed steady,” Agatha said, “if I didn’t ask too much, didn’t need too loud, she’d see that I was worth choosing.”

    Her fingers curled loosely into the porch board.

    “But when she left, I realized somethin’ worse than bein’ abandoned.” Rio swallowed.

    “What?” she asked.

    Agatha turned her head then. Looked at Rio fully.

    “I realized I’d taught myself that wanting more was the reason people left.” The silence after that was different.

    Not just full.

    Sharp.

    Rio’s chest rose and fell slowly. She held herself still, not pulling away, not leaning in, like she understood the moment demanded presence, not comfort.

    “So I stopped,” Agatha said.

    Rio’s voice was barely above the wind. “Stopped what?”

    “Stopped wanting,” Agatha replied. “Or at least, stopped lettin’ anyone see it.”

    She shook her head once, a small, rueful motion.

    “I learned how to be fine with whatever people offered. Learned how to make it look like I didn’t notice when they didn’t offer much at all.” Rio’s hand twitched, just once.

    Agatha saw it.

    “That’s when I started leavin’ first,” Agatha said. “Not physically. Just… emotionally. Pullin’ back the moment things got real. Turning quiet into armor.”

    Her mouth tightened.

    “I figured if I never let anyone see how deep it went, they couldn’t take anything with them when they left.”

    Rio exhaled slowly.

    “And did it work?” she asked.

    Agatha let out a soft, humorless laugh.

    “No,” she said. “But it kept me functional.”

    They sat with that truth for a while.

    Then Rio spoke again.

    “You don’t talk about her like she hurt you,” she said carefully. “You talk about her like she taught you a rule.”

    Agatha blinked.

    That… landed.

    “Yeah,” she said slowly. “That’s exactly it.”

    She glanced down at her hands.

    “She didn’t mean to,” Agatha added. “I know that now. But the lesson stuck anyway.”

    Rio nodded once.

    “And the rule was?” she asked.

    Agatha swallowed.

    “If you want someone enough,” she said quietly, “you give them the power to leave you hollow.”

    Rio didn’t argue.

    Didn’t contradict.

    Didn’t tell her that wasn’t true.

    She just said, “That’s a hard rule to live by.”

    Agatha snorted. “Tell me about it.”

    Another stretch of quiet.

    The breeze kicked up just enough to rustle the grass. Somewhere down the fence line, a gate creaked softly.

    Rio shifted then, just a little closer.

    Not touching.

    But nearer.

    “Can I ask you something?” she said.

    Agatha nodded. “You always can.”

    Rio hesitated, not out of fear, but out of care.

    “Do you think,” she asked, “that part of you still believes that rule?” Agatha didn’t answer right away.

    She stared out at the land. At the fence posts. At the place where the pasture dipped and rose again like a steady breath.

    “Yes,” she said finally. “I do.”

    Rio waited.

    “But,” Agatha added, “I also think part of me is real tired of livin’ like it’s the only truth there is.”

    Rio smiled, not wide, not relieved.

    Just… honest.

    “I’m not asking you to forget it,” she said. “Or undo it. Or pretend it didn’t shape you.”

    Agatha glanced at her.

    “What are you askin’, then?” she asked.

    Rio met her gaze.

    “I’m asking you not to disappear when it shows up,” she said. “Not with me.”

    Agatha’s throat tightened.

    “You’re askin’ a lot,” she murmured.

    Rio nodded. “I know.”

    Agatha studied her face, the steadiness of her eyes, the quiet certainty there. “You ain’t scared I’ll push you away?” Agatha asked.

    Rio smiled softly.

    “I am,” she said. “But I’m staying anyway.”

    That hit harder than any promise.

    Agatha looked down at the porch, then back at Rio.

    “I don’t know how to be wanted without feelin’ like I’m about to lose somethin’,” she admitted.

    Rio’s voice was gentle but sure.

    “Then we’ll learn it slow,” she said. “Together.”

    Agatha closed her eyes for a second.

    When she opened them, she nodded.

    “Okay,” she said. “Okay.”

    They didn’t seal it with a kiss.

    Didn’t need to.

    They just sat there, shoulders almost touching, patterns named, wounds acknowledged, rules finally spoken aloud.

    And for the first time since Wanda—

    Agatha understood that what broke her wasn’t love.

    It was believing love only ever ended one way.

    Chapter End Notes Agatha’s fear of being left doesn’t come from one place, it’s layered, learned, reinforced over time. This chapter isn’t about healing that fear all at once, but about recognizing it without flinching. And choosing anyway.

    Thank you for reading. Truly.

    Chapter 20: The Shape of Staying

    Chapter Summary Agatha and Rio spend the day moving through familiar rhythms, work, quiet, shared space, only this time, something has shifted. Watching turns into asking. Distance turns into closeness. And without needing to explain or define anything, they choose each other again, softly, deliberately, and without fear.

    Chapter Notes A small scheduling note before we begin: this chapter is up a day later than planned because working rotating shifts has officially dissolved my sense of time. Also I got out of a night shift yesterday morning, slept straight through most of the afternoon, woke up around 4:30 PM feeling very confused, and realized I didn’t have enough time (or brain cells) left to do a proper final reread and last round of edits. Rather than posting it half-asleep and letting future me regret everything, I chose sleep and clarity.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes Agatha leaned against the fence post with her arms folded loose across her chest, hat tipped low enough to shade her eyes but not so low she couldn’t see.

    Rio was about twenty yards out, crouched near the edge of the dry creek bed, notebook balanced on one knee. The sun hadn’t climbed high yet, but the heat was already settling in, heavy, patient, the kind that promised to stay. Dust clung to Rio’s boots and the hem of her jeans, darkened where she’d knelt earlier. Her overshirt was tied around her waist now, sleeves flapping faintly when the breeze bothered to show up.

    She worked the way she always did.

    Careful. Deliberate. Like every step was chosen instead of taken. Agatha had watched her like this before. Plenty of times, actually. From the porch. From the barn doorway. From the far side of the pasture while pretending to be busy with something else. Back then, it had felt like observing a storm from a distance, fascinating, contained, something she could appreciate without stepping too close.

    But this was different.

    Agatha wasn’t standing back.

    She wasn’t braced for impact.

    She was just watching.

    Rio brushed her fingers through the grass, slow and precise, as if she were listening through her skin. She paused, tilted her head, and shifted a few inches to the left before making another note. Her pencil moved in short strokes, quick, efficient, then stopped. She frowned faintly, erased something with the side of her thumb, and wrote again.

    Agatha felt a small, unexpected warmth settle in her chest. Not sharp. Not startling.

    Just there.

    Rio hummed under her breath as she worked. Not a song, exactly, just a low sound, barely audible, like her body was keeping itself company. Rio straightened slowly, stretching her back with a quiet exhale, one hand braced on her thigh. She rolled her shoulders once, then twice, and Agatha caught the way her face softened during it, how the intensity slipped for just a second when she thought no one was watching.

    Except Agatha was.

    Rio glanced over.

    Not sharply. Not checking.

    Just looking.

    Their eyes met across the distance.

    Agatha didn’t move.

    Didn’t lift a hand. Didn’t nod.

    She just stayed there, leaning into the fence post like it was the most natural place in the world to be, and let the corner of her mouth tilt up, barely. Rio’s lips curved in response.

    Not a full smile. Just enough.

    And then she went back to work.

    That did something to Agatha.

    The ease of it. The certainty. The way Rio looked over because she expected Agatha to be there, not because she needed reassurance, not because she was checking for approval, but because that’s where Agatha belonged now. Right there. Watching.

    Agatha shifted her weight, boots scuffing lightly in the dirt. The fence post creaked under her shoulder. She adjusted her stance and felt the familiar ache in her hip, dull and grounding.

    She didn’t mind it.

    Her gaze followed Rio as she moved farther along the creek bed, marking something with a small flag before stepping back to study it. Rio tilted her head again, sunlight catching on the curve of her cheekbone, the line of her jaw.

    She looked settled.

    Not just focused. Not just competent.

    Settled.

    Agatha realized she wasn’t cataloging Rio’s movements the way she used to, like she was gathering evidence, learning patterns, figuring out where the danger might be. She wasn’t waiting for the moment Rio would straighten up, close the notebook, and say she was done for the day. Or for the season. Or for good.

    She wasn’t waiting for the leaving.

    She was just there for the being.

    That realization landed slow, but it landed deep.

    Rio crouched again, this time closer to where the grass thinned out into brittle earth. She pressed her palm flat against the ground, fingers splayed, eyes narrowed in concentration. Agatha watched the muscle in her forearm shift as she leaned more of her weight into it, watched the faint line of tension appear between her brows.

    And then, without looking, Rio spoke.

    “Don’t let me forget to check the west slope later,” she said, voice carrying easily across the distance. Calm. Certain.

    Agatha answered without thinking. “I won’t.”

    The word felt natural in her mouth. Easy. Like it didn’t require any effort to keep that promise.

    Rio nodded once, still not looking up.

    Agatha stayed where she was.

    She didn’t light a cigarette. Didn’t feel the urge to pace or fidget or find something productive to do. The ranch would still be there when Rio finished. The fences would still need mending. The world would keep turning.

    Right now, this was enough.

    Watching Rio work, really watching her, felt like learning the shape of something familiar all over again. Not because it had changed, but because Agatha had.

    She saw the small things now. The way Rio avoided stepping on certain patches of ground. The way she took the long way around instead of cutting straight across. The way she paused before writing, like she was making sure the words matched what the land was telling her.

    It wasn’t reverence exactly.

    It was respect.

    Agatha felt something loosen in her chest at that.

    Rio finished marking the section she was on and stood again, wiping her hands on her jeans. She looked over once more, this time holding Agatha’s gaze a beat longer. Sunlight flashed in her eyes, warm, open, unguarded. Agatha lifted two fingers in a lazy salute.

    Rio shook her head, smiling fully now, and turned back to her work. Agatha stayed at the fence, watching, feeling the quiet hum of the moment settle around her like it belonged.

    She wasn’t observing from the outside anymore.

    She was part of the stillness.

    And for once, that didn’t scare her at all.

    Rio didn’t notice Agatha leave the fence right away.

    She was bent over again, one knee in the dirt, pencil moving with that same measured certainty, slow enough to be careful, fast enough to be confident. The morning had crept toward midday without much ceremony. The sun sat higher now, heat pressing down in earnest, cicadas starting their low, persistent chorus somewhere out of sight.

    Agatha crossed the distance without hurry.

    No boots stomping. No clearing of her throat. Just the soft crunch of dirt underfoot, the sound familiar enough that it didn’t trip Rio’s focus. Agatha stopped a few steps away and waited.

    She didn’t interrupt.

    She’d learned that part.

    Rio finished her note, put her pencil down, and only then looked up. Her expression shifted, not startled but open, like she’d known Agatha would get there eventually.

    “You’re hovering,” Rio observed mildly.

    Agatha huffed. “Wasn’t aware I needed permission.”

    Rio’s mouth curved. “You don’t. I was just saying.”

    Agatha stepped closer, stopping at Rio’s side now, close enough that she could see the lines on the page, though the words themselves meant nothing to her. Charts. Symbols. Little arrows pointing to things Agatha wouldn’t have noticed in a hundred years.

    “You’re done with this section?” Agatha asked.

    “For now,” Rio said. “I’ll come back later when the light shifts.” Agatha nodded once. Then, after a beat, asked, “Why?”

    Rio blinked. Just once.

    “Why what?”

    Agatha shrugged, hands loose at her sides. “Why come back later. Why not just finish it now.”

    Rio studied her for a momentl. Then she closed the notebook and tapped it lightly against her knee.

    “Because I’d miss things,” she said simply. “This land doesn’t look the same at noon as it does in the evening. Or after rain. Or in different seasons. Patterns show up when you give them time.”

    Agatha considered that.

    “Huh,” she said. Then, quieter, more deliberate: “Guess that makes sense.” They stood there together for a moment, the quiet settling easily between them. Rio shifted her weight, dusting off her hands, and started toward the path that led back up toward the house.

    Agatha fell into step beside her without thinking.

    They walked like that for a few minutes, side by side, not touching but close enough that Agatha could feel the heat of Rio’s body through the air between them. The path was narrow here, grass brushing against their boots, and Agatha adjusted her pace automatically to match Rio’s shorter stride.

    She’d done that once without noticing.

    Now she noticed.

    They passed the bend where the creek dipped out of sight, and Agatha slowed, something tightening, not uncomfortably, in her chest. She glanced over at Rio.

    “You ever think,” she said, voice casual but not careless, “about doing something else?”

    Rio looked at her then.

    Really looked at her.

    Agatha didn’t flinch.

    “Sometimes,” Rio said. “Why?”

    Agatha shrugged again, but it didn’t feel like deflection this time. More like honesty without polish.

    “Just wonderin’,” she said. “You could’ve picked a lot of places. A lot of jobs. But you came here. You picked this.” She gestured vaguely at the land around them. “Why?”

    Rio didn’t answer right away.

    She slowed her steps, then stopped altogether. Agatha stopped with her, instinctively, turning to face her now. The sun caught Rio’s profile, lit the fine hairs at her temple, the quiet seriousness in her eyes. “This?” Rio said, echoing the word.

    Agatha nodded. “Yeah. This.”

    Rio exhaled slowly, gaze drifting back toward the pasture they’d just crossed. Her shoulders eased settling into herself.

    “I like places that don’t need me,” she said finally. “Not really. Not in the way people think.”

    Agatha frowned faintly. “Explain that.”

    Rio smiled a little at the tone. Not amused. Fond.

    “I grew up around people who always wanted something from me,” she said. “Opinions. Explanations. Reassurance. Proof that I was doing something worthwhile.” She paused, fingers brushing absently against the seam of her jeans. “Land doesn’t ask for that.”

    Agatha felt that land somewhere deep.

    “So you like it ‘cause it leaves you alone?” she asked.

    Rio shook her head. “No. Because it tells the truth whether I’m there or not.”

    Agatha swallowed.

    “That’s… not what I expected you to say.”

    Rio glanced back at her. “What did you expect?”

    Agatha hesitated. Not long, but enough.

    “I dunno,” she admitted. “Something about saving things. Or fixing them. Or proving a point.”

    Rio’s smile softened, but she didn’t tease.

    “I do want to protect things,” she said. “But not because they’re broken. Because they’re already and people don’t notice.”

    Agatha let the words sit. Felt them press gently against something old and familiar inside her. She shifted her stance, one hand hooking casually into her belt.

    “So this job,” she said slowly. “This study. It’s not about you bein’ needed.” “No,” Rio said. “It’s about listening.”

    Agatha snorted quietly. “You picked a hell of a place for that.” Rio’s eyes flicked to her, sharp and warm all at once. “I did.” They stood there in the sun for another moment, the space between them unhurried, unforced. Agatha realized she wasn’t itching to fill it. Wasn’t scrambling for the next question or the right response.

    She just wanted to know.

    “And your family?” Agatha asked, the question emerging before she could second-guess it. “What’d they say when you told ‘em you were comin’ out here?”

    Rio blinked again. This time, slower.

    “That’s two questions,” she said gently.

    Agatha smirked. “You can answer one.”

    Rio considered her for a long beat. Then nodded.

    “They think I’m stubborn,” she said. “They’re not wrong.” Agatha huffed. “Yeah. I gathered that.”

    “But they also know I don’t stay where I don’t feel right,” Rio continued. “So when I told them about this place, about the land, the scope of it, the work, they understood. Even if they didn’t fully get it.” Agatha tilted her head. “They worried?”

    Rio met her gaze. “They always do.”

    Something in her voice, quiet, steady, unashamed, made Agatha’s chest tighten.

    “Still,” Agatha said. “You left ‘em. Came all the way out here. Took a job that doesn’t exactly scream comfort.”

    Rio smiled, small but certain. “Comfort isn’t the same as safety.” Agatha felt that one lodge deep.

    She nodded slowly, eyes drifting back toward the house in the distance, the porch just visible over the rise. She thought of storms. Of silence. Of the way Rio had knelt in front of her and said she was staying. “Yeah,” Agatha murmured. “I’m learnin’ that.”

    Rio didn’t comment.

    They started walking again, this time closer. Their arms brushed once as the path narrowed, and neither of them moved away. Agatha felt the contact like a quiet spark, not electric, not demanding, just present. She let it happen.

    By the time they reached the first stretch of fence near the house, Agatha spoke again, voice lower now.

    “I’m glad you picked this,” she said.

    Rio glanced at her, surprised. “The land?”

    Agatha shook her head. Just once.

    “This,” she repeated. And this time, it was clear what she meant. Rio didn’t answer right away.

    But her hand drifted, almost unconsciously, close enough that her knuckles brushed Agatha’s.

    And that felt like answer enough, for now.

    They didn’t head straight back to the house.

    Rio angled off the path instead, veering toward the lower stretch of pasture where the land dipped and changed texture, where grass gave way to scrub, where the soil darkened and cracked differently underfoot. She moved with intention now, the casual ease of someone slipping back into rhythm. Agatha followed without asking.

    Not because she knew where they were going, but because she wanted to. The sun had climbed higher, heat settling deep into the bones of the place. Cicadas buzzed louder now, relentless and electric. Dust clung to the cuffs of Agatha’s jeans, to the edge of Rio’s boots. The land smelled dry and alive all at once.

    Rio stopped near a shallow rise, knelt again, and set her notebook down carefully on a flat rock like it mattered. Agatha lingered a step behind her, hands loose, posture open.

    She watched.

    Rio worked differently up close than she did from a distance. Agatha had seen her kneeling before, head bent, pencil moving, but standing this near, she noticed the smaller things. The way Rio paused before every mark, eyes scanning first. The way she touched the ground, not grabbing, not digging, just brushing the surface with her fingertips like she was listening through her skin.

    Agatha leaned her weight onto one leg, resting her forearm casually on her thigh.

    “You always start here?” she asked, quiet enough not to break the moment. Rio nodded without looking up. “This patch shifts first. It’s like a warning sign.”

    Agatha huffed softly. “Of course it is.”

    Rio smiled at that, brief, almost private and continued working. Agatha stayed silent after that.

    She didn’t need commentary. Didn’t feel the itch to fill the space. She just tracked Rio’s movements, the steady reach, the careful note, the brief pause where she tilted her head and squinted like the land had said something under its breath.

    At some point, Rio stood and brushed dirt from her knees. Agatha instinctively stepped closer, offering balance without thinking, hand hovering near Rio’s elbow in case she needed it.

    Rio noticed.

    Didn’t comment.

    But when she straightened, her arm brushed Agatha’s forearm, slow, deliberate contact this time. Not accidental.

    Agatha felt it travel.

    They moved on together, Rio leading, Agatha half a step behind. Rio pointed things out as they went, not explaining so much as naming. “This grass shouldn’t be here.”

    “That ant trail means something’s shifted.”

    “This patch is healthier than it looks.”

    Agatha nodded, absorbing tone more than content. She didn’t need to understand every detail to understand he, the care, the precision, the way Rio’s whole body leaned toward the work instead of away from it. They crossed a shallow gully where the ground dipped unexpectedly. Rio stepped carefully, weight testing before committing. Agatha watched her slip just slightly on loose gravel.

    Without hesitation, Agatha reached out.

    Her hand closed around Rio’s wrist, not tight, not panicked, just steady. Rio’s breath caught.

    She didn’t pull away.

    She let Agatha hold her there until her footing was secure again. Their hands lingered longer than necessary, skin warm, pulse present beneath Agatha’s thumb.

    “You okay?” Agatha asked quietly.

    Rio nodded. “Yeah. Thanks.”

    She didn’t move her hand away right away.

    Neither did Agatha.

    Eventually, Rio eased her wrist free, not retreating, just continuing, and Agatha let it happen without tension. But something had shifted. They were closer now.

    Not in distance—though that too—but in awareness.

    Rio worked through another section, crouching again, jotting notes. Agatha knelt beside her this time instead of standing, her knee pressing into the dirt, posture mirroring without mimicry.

    She studied the notebook, not the words, but the shape of Rio’s handwriting. Precise. Controlled. A little sharp at the edges. “You ever get tired?” Agatha asked suddenly. Not accusatory. Just curious. Rio paused, pencil hovering. “Of this?”

    Agatha shrugged. “Of always paying attention.”

    Rio considered that, then smiled faintly. “Sometimes. But I get more tired when I don’t.”

    Agatha nodded slowly.

    “That makes sense.”

    The sun beat down harder now. Heat shimmered faintly off the ground. Rio wiped her brow with the back of her wrist, smearing dust across her skin. Agatha noticed and without comment, reached into her back pocket, pulled out a folded bandana, and held it out.

    Rio blinked up at her. “You sure?”

    Agatha lifted one shoulder. “You’re sweatin’ into your notes.” Rio laughed quietly and took it, fingers brushing Agatha’s as she did. She wiped her face, then, after a beat, handed it back, neatly refolded. “Thanks,” she said.

    Agatha tucked it away again like it was nothing. Like her chest hadn’t tightened just a bit.

    They finished the section in companionable quiet. Rio stood, stretching her back, and Agatha rose with her. Their shoulders brushed again, this time neither if them pretended it wasn’t intentional.

    Rio glanced over. “You don’t mind tagging along?”

    Agatha shook her head. “No. I like seein’ it up close.”

    Rio smiled at that. Real, unguarded.

    They turned back toward the house together, pace unhurried. The land stretched wide and familiar around them, but something about walking it side by side made it feel new.

    Halfway up the path, Agatha’s hand drifted, just slightly, close to Rio’s. Rio noticed.

    She didn’t comment.

    She just let her fingers brush Agatha’s knuckles once, then again, before threading them loosely together.

    Agatha inhaled.

    Didn’t look down.

    Didn’t speak.

    She just let herself follow, not the land this time, but the quiet certainty of that shared step forward.

    They were walking slow by the time Rio spoke.

    Not because the path demanded it, but because neither of them seemed in a hurry anymore. The afternoon light stretched long across the dirt, turning the dust gold where it kicked up under their boots. Their hands were still loosely joined, no pressure, no claim. Just contact.

    Rio slowed half a step.

    Not enough to stop them. Just enough to change the rhythm. Agatha felt it immediately.

    “You always walk like you’re bracing for something,” Rio said. It wasn’t accusatory.

    It wasn’t even sharp.

    It was observational. The kind of statement that came from long watching, not sudden judgment.

    Agatha’s thumb stilled against Rio’s knuckle.

    She didn’t pull her hand away.

    “Yeah?” she said, voice even. Neutral. The way she answered questions that might have teeth.

    Rio nodded once. “Yeah.”

    They took a few more steps before Rio continued, giving the space time to settle.

    “Like you’re ready to catch something before it falls,” she added. Agatha exhaled slowly through her nose.

    The pasture stretched wide and familiar around them, the fence line, the rise of the hill, the old oak at the bend. Nothing here was threatening. Nothing here was running.

    And yet.

    “That bother you?” Agatha asked.

    Rio shook her head immediately. “No.”

    Then, after a beat: “But it makes me curious.”

    Agatha’s jaw tightened, not defensively, not sharply. Just the reflex of someone unused to being the subject of careful attention. She kept walking.

    Kept holding Rio’s hand.

    “That a problem?” she asked.

    Rio glanced at her then, not searching, not probing. Just looking. “I don’t ask questions unless I’m prepared for the answer,” she said. “And I don’t ask them if I think they’ll hurt someone unnecessarily.” Agatha huffed once. “You’ve got rules for everything.”

    Rio smiled faintly. “I have patterns.”

    Agatha looked down at their hands, then back up at the path ahead. She felt the urge, to deflect, to joke, to say something half-smart and move on. She didn’t.

    “What’s the question, then?” she asked quietly.

    Rio didn’t answer right away.

    They reached the edge of the porch, the shadow of the house falling across them like a line drawn gently in the dirt. Rio stopped there, just at the threshold, still holding Agatha’s hand.

    She turned fully this time.

    “Why don’t you let people help you,” she asked, “until you’re already exhausted?”

    Agatha blinked.

    Once.

    Twice.

    That one slipped past her guard, not because it was cruel, but because it was precise.

    She laughed softly, reflexively. “That your scientific assessment?” Rio didn’t smile back.

    Not because she was upset, but because she wasn’t joking. “I’ve watched you,” Rio said. “You take on everything first. You wait until it’s heavy before you say anything. And even then, you don’t ask. You just… allow.”

    Agatha’s grip tightened, just slightly.

    Rio noticed. Didn’t pull away.

    “Like you expect help to be temporary,” Rio added. “Or conditional.” Agatha swallowed.

    The porch boards creaked under her weight as she shifted, staring out over the land like it might give her an answer.

    “You’re good at this,” she muttered.

    “At asking?” Rio asked.

    “At seein’ too much.”

    Rio considered that. “I don’t think it’s too much.”

    Agatha scoffed softly. “That’s easy to say when you ain’t the one bein’ seen.”

    Rio nodded. “That’s fair.”

    She didn’t push.

    Didn’t rephrase.

    She just waited, still, patient, present.

    And Agatha realized, dimly, that this was the part she always ran from. Not the questions themselves—but the quiet afterward, when someone stayed long enough for the answer to matter.

    “I don’t know how to… stop,” Agatha said finally. The words came out low, rougher than she meant them to. “It’s easier if I do it myself.” Rio tilted her head. “Easier for who?”

    Agatha didn’t answer right away.

    She stared at the nail head in the porch rail. The crack in the wood she’d been rubbing earlier.

    “For everyone,” she said eventually.

    Rio’s brows drew together, not in disagreement, but in recognition. “That sounds lonely,” she said.

    Agatha’s chest tightened.

    “Yeah,” she admitted. “It is.”

    The word hung between them, unadorned, unsoftened.

    Rio stepped closer, not crowding, just closing the space. She lifted their joined hands slightly, grounding the moment back in the body. “You don’t do it because you don’t trust people,” Rio said carefully. “You do it because you don’t trust needing them.”

    Agatha’s breath stalled, just for a second.

    She looked away, jaw tight, eyes tracking the fence line like it might give her cover.

    “That’s not—” she started, then stopped.

    Rio didn’t interrupt.

    Didn’t correct her.

    She just waited.

    Agatha scrubbed her tongue against the inside of her cheek. “Needing someone puts things outta balance,” she said finally. “Means I’m not the one holdin’ it together.”

    Rio nodded slowly. “And that feels dangerous.”

    “Feels… exposed,” Agatha said. “Like if I lean too hard, something’ll give. And I don’t get to choose what.”

    Rio absorbed that.

    “You’re not afraid people won’t help,” she said. “You’re afraid of what it costs you if they do.”

    Agatha swallowed.

    “That sound about right?”

    Agatha huffed once, breathless. “You really are good at this.” “At listening,” Rio said.

    Agatha glanced at her, something wry and tired in her eyes. “At not lettin’ me dodge.”

    Rio smiled faintly. “You’re not dodging.”

    She lifted their joined hands slightly, grounding, steady. “You’re explaining,” Rio said.

    That landed harder than anything else had.

    Agatha stared down at their hands, thumb brushing once over Rio’s knuckle without thinking.

    “I don’t like feeling… beholden,” she said quietly. “Or like I owe someone for showin’ up.”

    Rio nodded. “So you make sure you never have to.”

    “Yeah.”

    The word came out softer than Agatha expected.

    They stood there for a beat, no rush, no pressure, just the land breathing around them.

    Then Rio said, gently, “You don’t have to untangle that all at once.” Agatha exhaled, something loosening just a fraction in her chest. “Good,” she muttered. “Wasn’t plannin’ to.”

    Rio smiled then, warm, fond, unmistakably there.

    They stepped onto the porch together.

    And Agatha realized, with quiet surprise, that being asked the question hadn’t taken anything from her.

    It had just made room.

    They didn’t move right away.

    That was the thing about it, how nothing had to happen for a moment to feel full. The porch was quiet again, the air warm and heavy, the land stretched out before them like it was listening.

    Agatha stood there with Rio’s hand still in hers.

    Not gripping.

    Not holding tight.

    Just there.

    Rio’s thumb rested against the side of Agatha’s hand, warm and steady. No pressure. No test. Just contact that stayed. Agatha had spent years bracing herself for the moment when touch would mean something she couldn’t control. For the moment it would tip into expectation or demand or consequence.

    This didn’t.

    It felt like a door left open on purpose.

    Agatha let out a breath she hadn’t realized she was holding. Her shoulders eased, not all the way, but enough that Rio noticed. Rio always noticed. She shifted closer, not stepping into Agatha’s space so much as aligning with it. Same direction. Same line of sight. Their shoulders brushed this time, not accidental, not quite deliberate either. Just inevitable. Agatha turned her head.

    Slowly.

    Not like she was afraid Rio would vanish if she moved too fast, just like she wanted to make sure she was choosing it. Rio’s face was close now. Close enough that Agatha could see the soft curve of her mouth when she wasn’t speaking, the calm in her eyes that never felt bored or distant, only present. Rio didn’t lean in.

    Didn’t close the distance for her.

    She waited.

    Agatha huffed a quiet breath through her nose. “You’re doin’ that thing again.”

    Rio tilted her head. “What thing?”

    “Letting me decide.”

    Rio’s mouth curved, not a smile exactly. Something gentler. “I like when you choose.”

    That did it.

    Agatha didn’t rush. Didn’t grab. Didn’t make it a statement or a claim. She lifted her free hand instead, slow and careful, fingers brushing against Rio’s wrist before sliding up to her cheek.

    Rio leaned into the touch without thinking.

    Agatha felt it, the moment Rio stopped holding herself still and simply received. The way her breath changed. The way her eyes softened, lids lowering just a fraction.

    “Okay?” Agatha murmured.

    Rio nodded. “Yeah.”

    Agatha closed the last inch between them.

    The kiss wasn’t dramatic.

    It wasn’t urgent or hungry or searching for reassurance.

    It was soft.

    Certain.

    Her mouth pressed to Rio’s like it had always known where to land. No rush. No desperation. Just the quiet certainty of two people who had already done the hard part, staying, listening, choosing again and again without needing to say it out loud.

    Rio made a small sound when Agatha kissed her. Her hand tightened slightly in Agatha’s, grounding, answering without pulling. Agatha kissed her again, still gentle, still unhurried. A kiss meant to be felt, not proven. She tilted her head just enough to fit better, her thumb brushing along Rio’s jaw where she still held her.

    Rio kissed her back with the same care.

    Not tentative, but deliberate.

    Like this wasn’t a question.

    Like it wasn’t something she was afraid to lose.

    The porch creaked faintly as Agatha shifted closer. Rio’s other hand came up then, resting at Agatha’s waist. Not pulling her in. Just anchoring them together, body to body, presence to presence.

    They stayed like that longer than necessary.

    Longer than polite.

    The world didn’t intrude. The land didn’t demand anything. The quiet held. When they finally pulled back, it wasn’t abrupt. Their foreheads rested together for a moment, breath mingling, hands still where they’d landed like neither of them felt any need to move them.

    Rio smiled first.

    Not wide. Not teasing.

    Just warm.

    Agatha swallowed, heart steady in her chest in a way that surprised her. She hadn’t lost herself. She hadn’t tipped into something she couldn’t manage. The kiss hadn’t taken anything from her.

    It had settled something.

    “Hi,” Rio said softly.

    Agatha huffed a quiet laugh. “Yeah. Hi.”

    They didn’t say anything else.

    They didn’t need to.

    They stood there on the porch, hands still linked, shoulders brushing, the land stretching out before them like a promise neither of them had asked for, but both were finally ready to accept.

    And for once, Agatha didn’t brace for what came next.

    She just stayed.

    Chapter End Notes As always thank you for reading.

    Chapter 21: What Stillness Can Hold

    Chapter Summary They don’t rush into it.

    They don’t need to.

    It happens slowly, without ceremony, without urgency, without any of the usual chaos Agatha’s used to bracing for. What starts as a quiet night becomes something more grounded, more tender, more real than anything she expected.

    Chapter Notes This one’s been sitting in my chest for a while. I wanted their first time to feel tender, careful, and completely deliberate, no rush, no spectacle, just the slow unfolding of trust and want.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes They stayed like that.

    Not frozen. Not hesitant. Just… still.

    Forehead to forehead, the space between their mouths warm and faintly charged, hands still exactly where they’d landed, Agatha’s curled loose at Rio’s waist, Rio’s resting firm and sure against Agatha’s ribs like she’d placed it there on purpose and seen no reason to take it back. The world didn’t rush in to reclaim them.

    The porch boards were quiet beneath their boots. The wind moved through the grass in a low, patient sweep. Somewhere far off, a gate creaked and settled again. Ordinary sounds. Familiar ones. None of them demanded attention.

    Agatha breathed out slowly through her nose.

    Her body felt present.

    Not buzzing. Not tipped over the edge of something sharp or reckless. Just warm. Anchored. Like she was fully inhabiting her own skin again instead of bracing it for impact.

    She became aware, gradually, of the details.

    The heat of Rio’s hand through her shirt, steady, not roaming, not tentative. The faint press of Rio’s thigh where it lined up with hers. The way Rio’s breath slowed to match Agatha’s without either of them saying a word about it.

    No one pulled away.

    Agatha realized, distantly, that she was usually the one who did. She was usually already stepping back by now, clearing her throat, making a joke, breaking the moment into something manageable. She’d been doing that for years. Decades, probably.

    But her body didn’t do it this time.

    It stayed exactly where it was.

    Rio shifted first, not away, just closer, the smallest adjustment of weight that brought her chest more fully in line with Agatha’s. It was an unconscious movement. The kind you made when you weren’t calculating outcomes. When you trusted the ground beneath your feet.

    Agatha felt it immediately.

    Her breath caught, not sharply, not panicked. Just enough to register. Rio noticed.

    She didn’t comment.

    She simply tipped her head a fraction, temple brushing Agatha’s, the contact gentle enough to ask rather than take. The question was written in the movement itself.

    Agatha answered by staying.

    By letting her hand flex once at Rio’s side, fingers spreading, then settling again like they’d found the right place and were content to remain there. The kiss lingered in her body like an afterimage.

    Not demanding to be repeated. Not fading either.

    Just there.

    She could feel it in the slow thud of her pulse. In the way her shoulders had dropped without her noticing. In the absence of that familiar coil of tension she usually carried high between her ribs.

    Rio shifted her forehead just enough to look at her again. Agatha didn’t flinch under it.

    Didn’t brace. Didn’t put anything up.

    She let herself be seen the way she was in that moment, quiet, steady, open in a way she hadn’t planned for and didn’t feel the need to correct. Rio’s mouth curved again, small and private, like the smile wasn’t for the world or even for Agatha’s benefit. Like it was simply the natural result of being here.

    Her thumb moved once, slow and deliberate, tracing a short arc against Agatha’s side. Not exploratory. Not suggestive. Just… grounding. Agatha swallowed.

    She became aware of how close Rio actually was, not in the abstract, but physically. The alignment of their hips. The soft brush of fabric at their thighs. The fact that if either of them leaned even slightly, there would be no space left at all.

    The thought didn’t make her pull back.

    It made her still.

    She rested her forehead more fully against Rio’s, eyes half-lidded now, and let herself feel the quiet weight of another person choosing not to leave the moment first.

    Rio breathed her name, not aloud, not quite. Just a shape of breath against Agatha’s cheek that carried intention without sound.

    Agatha felt it anyway.

    Her other hand lifted before she consciously decided to move it, coming to rest at Rio’s shoulder. She didn’t grip. Didn’t pull. She simply laid her palm there, thumb settling into the hollow at the base of Rio’s neck like it belonged.

    Rio exhaled.

    A soft sound. Barely there.

    That was when Agatha understood something important, not as a revelation, but as a bodily truth: This wasn’t escalation.

    This was continuation.

    They weren’t rushing toward the next thing. They were letting this one finish unfolding in its own time.

    The porch light behind them hummed faintly. The sky had shifted while they weren’t paying attention, deepening into that rich, late-evening blue that blurred the edges of the land and softened every line. Agatha stayed exactly where she was.

    And for the first time in longer than she could remember, she didn’t feel like she was waiting for the moment to end.

    She was inside it.

    Choosing it.

    Rio leaned in again, not to kiss, not yet, but to rest her brow more firmly against Agatha’s, a quiet press that felt like punctuation. Agatha’s mouth curved, almost without her permission.

    “Still here,” she murmured. Not an announcement. Not a question. Rio’s answer was immediate and unspoken.

    She stayed.

    And Agatha, heart steady, hands warm, the land wide and patient in front of them, stayed right there with her.

    They didn’t announce the shift inside.

    It happened the same way everything between them seemed to lately, without ceremony, without urgency. One moment they were still standing on the porch, the land wide and darkening in front of them, the air cooled just enough to settle the heat off their skin. The next, Agatha was turning slightly, angling her body toward the door, her hand still warm at Rio’s side. She didn’t let go.

    Rio noticed.

    Agatha felt it in the way Rio adjusted her grip, not tightening, not loosening, just staying with the movement. Matching it.

    “Come on,” Agatha said quietly, the words barely more than breath. Not an invitation framed like a question. Just a direction. A continuation. Rio followed without hesitation.

    The screen door creaked as Agatha pushed it open with her shoulder, the sound familiar enough that it barely registered anymore. The house welcomed them the way it always did, wood settling, air cooler inside, the faint scent of soap and old pine and something cooked earlier that still lingered in the corners.

    Agatha stepped aside to let Rio pass first.

    It was a small thing.

    But Rio noticed that too.

    She moved into the kitchen with the same careful ease she brought everywhere, eyes already cataloging the space, the counters worn smooth from years of use, the hooks along the wall holding cast iron and dented ladles and a knife block that looked older than both of them combined. Agatha shut the door behind them, slower than usual, and leaned back against it for a moment like she needed the contact. She rolled her shoulders once, then straightened.

    “Didn’t plan ahead,” she said, nodding toward the fridge. “But there’s enough for somethin’.”

    Rio smiled softly. “There’s always enough.”

    Agatha snorted. “That’s optimism.”

    “Experience,” Rio corrected gently, already moving closer to the counter, setting her bag down by the table without being told. She washed her hands at the sink on instinct, sleeves pushed up, fingers careful and thorough. Agatha watched her do it.

    Not in the way she’d watched her in the field, with distance, with awe. This was closer. Domestic. The kind of watching that came from imagining how someone fit into a space rather than observing them move through it. She pushed off the door and crossed the kitchen, opening the fridge and surveying its contents with a habitual frown.

    “Got vegetables,” she said. “Eggs. Leftover rice.”

    Rio leaned against the counter beside her, close enough that Agatha could feel the warmth of her through her sleeve. “That’s a meal.” “That’s ingredients.”

    Rio tipped her head, amused. “Trust me.”

    That was all the encouragement Agatha needed.

    She reached for a pan, set it on the stove with a practiced clatter, and handed Rio a knife without thinking, handle first, the way you handed something sharp to someone you trusted.

    Rio accepted it just as naturally.

    They fell into rhythm almost immediately.

    Agatha cracked eggs into a bowl, her movements efficient and sure. Rio chopped vegetables at the counter, her knife work precise but unhurried. Neither of them spoke much. They didn’t need to.

    The kitchen filled with small, shared sounds, the sizzle of oil heating, the steady tap of the blade against wood, the clink of ceramic when Agatha set the bowl aside. Ordinary noises. Familiar ones.

    And yet—

    Agatha felt it, low and constant: the awareness of Rio beside her. The way her elbow brushed Agatha’s once, then again, neither of them apologizing. The way Rio leaned just slightly closer when she reached for a spice, her shoulder pressing into Agatha’s arm for a brief, grounding second. Agatha didn’t step away.

    She adjusted to make room.

    “Salt?” Rio asked quietly.

    Agatha slid the jar toward her without looking.

    Their fingers brushed.

    Not accidental this time.

    Neither of them reacted like it was.

    Rio’s hand lingered a fraction longer than necessary, her thumb grazing the side of Agatha’s finger before she took the salt and turned back to the pan. Agatha felt the touch travel up her arm and settle somewhere deep and steady in her chest.

    She exhaled slowly.

    Cooking with someone else felt different than she remembered. She’d done this before, years ago, sharing space, sharing tasks, but it had always felt temporary, like something that belonged to a version of her life she wasn’t meant to keep. Like a borrowed moment you enjoyed because you knew it wouldn’t last.

    This didn’t feel borrowed.

    It felt weighted.

    Anchored.

    Rio moved behind her to reach the stove, close enough that Agatha could feel her breath at the back of her shoulder. She murmured, “Careful,” as she stirred the pan, not a warning so much as a presence. Agatha shifted automatically, making space, her hip brushing Rio’s thigh as she did. Neither of them commented on it.

    They worked like that for a while, side by side, bodies moving in quiet coordination. Agatha added eggs to the pan; Rio folded the vegetables through, steam rising and fogging the window above the sink. The scent of food filled the room, warm and grounding.

    At some point, Agatha realized she’d stopped thinking about what came next.

    She wasn’t anticipating an ending. She wasn’t measuring the moment, bracing for the way it would inevitably conclude.

    She was just here.

    Rio set the knife down and turned toward her, eyes warm in the low kitchen light. “You always cook like this?”

    Agatha blinked. “Like what?”

    “Like you’re not in a hurry,” Rio said. “But you know exactly what you’re doing.”

    Agatha huffed softly. “That’s just cookin’.”

    Rio smiled. “It’s not.”

    Agatha shook her head, but she didn’t argue.

    They plated the food together, hands passing dishes back and forth, their movements easy now. Agatha pulled out two plates from the cupboard without thinking, something she noticed only after she’d done it. She didn’t correct herself.

    They sat at the table across from each other, knees almost touching beneath the wood. Agatha took a bite, nodded in approval despite herself. “All right,” she conceded. “You win.”

    Rio laughed quietly. “I wasn’t competing.”

    Agatha looked at her over the rim of her plate and felt something warm spread through her chest. “Yeah,” she said. “I know.”

    They ate slowly.

    Not because they were talking, there wasn’t much of that, but because neither of them seemed eager to finish. To clear the plates. To signal that this part of the evening was done.

    The house held them gently, the light low and steady, the outside world reduced to the faint chorus of night sounds pressing against the windows. Agatha rested her forearms on the table, fingers loosely curled around her fork. She watched Rio chew, the way her expression softened when she liked something, the small crease at the corner of her mouth that appeared when she was thinking.

    Domestic, her mind supplied again.

    And for the first time, the word didn’t make her flinch.

    It felt like gravity.

    Pulling her gently toward something she didn’t need to resist. Rio caught her looking and didn’t tease her for it.

    She just smiled, warm, unguarded, and reached across the table, her fingers brushing Agatha’s knuckles in a quiet, unmistakable echo of the porch. Agatha didn’t pull away.

    She turned her hand slightly, letting their fingers rest together on the wood. And they stayed like that, sharing dinner, sharing space, letting the weight of it settle, slow and inevitable and exactly where it was meant to be. They didn’t rush to clear the table.

    The plates sat there a while longer than necessary, forks crossed, crumbs cooling in the quiet. The house felt fuller now, warmed by food, by shared motion, by the low hum that had settled between them sometime after dinner began and never quite left.

    Agatha stood first, gathering the plates with an absent efficiency that didn’t match the way her shoulders were held, looser than usual, like she wasn’t bracing for anything. Rio followed, carrying the cups to the sink, standing close enough that their elbows bumped once, then again.

    Neither of them apologized.

    They washed up together in the same quiet rhythm they’d cooked in. Agatha rinsed; Rio dried. Their hands brushed when they reached for the same plate. Rio’s knuckles skimmed Agatha’s wrist as she passed her a towel. Small things. Ordinary things.

    Heavy things.

    By the time the last dish was set aside, the kitchen light felt too bright. Too exposed.

    Agatha shut it off without comment, leaving only the softer glow from the living room lamp spilling down the hallway. The shadows lengthened, gentler now, turning the house into something private again. Rio lingered by the counter, fingers resting lightly on the edge like she was deciding something.

    Agatha felt it before she understood it, that familiar tightening in her chest, the one that came right before she either closed herself off or said something real. She wiped her hands on a towel she didn’t need and leaned back against the counter across from Rio.

    They stood there for a moment.

    Just looking.

    “You tired?” Agatha asked, voice casual enough that it might’ve passed for nothing if you didn’t know her.

    Rio tilted her head. “A little.”

    Chapter 23: Out in the Open

    Chapter Summary The morning doesn’t undo anything.

    No hesitation.

    No scanning the horizon to see who might be watching.

    Just the motion.

    Rio didn’t freeze.

    Didn’t look around either.

    She leaned into the touch slightly, small and unconscious. Agatha let her hand fall again.

    There was a time—not that long ago—when being seen like that would’ve tightened something under her ribs. When the thought of neighbors interpreting, measuring, forming opinions would’ve made her jaw set hard enough to crack teeth.

    But today?

    It felt different.

    Not lighter.

    Just solid.

    She wasn’t pretending this wasn’t something.

    She wasn’t pretending Rio was just help, or temporary, or passing through. Tom had looked.

    He’d seen.

    And Agatha hadn’t corrected him.

    Hadn’t softened it.

    Hadn’t distanced herself physically or verbally to make it easier for anyone else.

    That mattered.

    She started walking again, heading toward the far shed where feed sacks were stacked high against the interior wall.

    Rio fell in beside her.

    “You didn’t answer his question the way you used to,” Rio said after a moment.
