    Agatha glanced sideways.

    “How’s that?”

    “You didn’t make it sound like I was just passing through.” There was no accusation in it.

    Just recognition.

    Agatha considered that as they crossed the open yard.

    “No,” she said finally. “I didn’t.”

    “Why?”

    Agatha slowed near the shed door, one hand bracing against the frame before she pushed it open.

    Because I’m not trying to manage perception anymore.

    Because I’m not ashamed.

    Because you’re not temporary.

    But she didn’t say any of that like a speech.

    She just looked at Rio and said, calm and level: “Because you’re not.”

    Rio held her gaze for a long second.

    Not searching for reassurance.

    Not doubting.

    Just absorbing the fact of it.

    Inside the shed, the air was warmer, carrying the scent of grain and sun- heated wood. Agatha moved toward the feed stack and lifted one bag onto her shoulder without strain.

    Rio stepped forward to take another.

    “You don’t feel…” Rio started, then stopped.

    Agatha waited.

    “Exposed?” Rio finished.

    Agatha shifted the weight on her shoulder and walked back toward the yard. “Not exposed,” she said over her shoulder. “Just visible.” “And that’s alright?”

    Agatha stepped into the sunlight again.

    The sky was open and endless above them.

    She set the bag down near the pen and dusted off her hands. “It should be,” she said.

    That was the difference.

    It wasn’t bravado.

    It wasn’t pretending the attention didn’t exist.

    It was deciding that existing plainly wasn’t something she needed to brace against.

    They worked side by side, distributing feed, checking latches, moving through the motions of the morning with practiced rhythm. At one point, Rio reached for the same bucket Agatha did. Their hands bumped.

    Neither of them pulled away quickly.

    They just adjusted and continued.

    The contact was casual.

    Comfortable.

    Normal.

    Agatha felt it, that slight awareness that this wasn’t private anymore in the way it used to be. That if someone drove up again, they wouldn’t scramble apart.

    And she didn’t resent it.

    Didn’t resent the way it meant something had shifted.

    She glanced toward the road once, not anxious, just aware and then back to Rio.

    “You good?” she asked.

    Rio nodded.

    “Yeah.”

    Agatha studied her for a beat longer.

    “You didn’t look bothered.”

    “I wasn’t.”

    Rio wiped her hands against her jeans and leaned her hip against the fence post.

    “I’m used to being looked at,” she said. “For different reasons. This one didn’t feel hostile.”

    Agatha absorbed that.

    “You sure?”

    Rio met her eyes steadily.

    “I’m sure.”

    That steadiness did something quiet inside Agatha.

    Not reassurance.

    Not relief.

    Alignment.

    She stepped closer, close enough that their shoulders brushed naturally as they leaned against the same fence rail.

    Open field in front of them.

    No walls.

    No concealment.

    Just daylight and land and the weight of being known.

    Agatha rested her forearms on the top rail.

    “He’ll tell his wife,” she said casually.

    Rio’s mouth twitched. “About the baler?”

    “About us.”

    Rio considered that.

    “And?”

    Agatha shrugged once.

    “Then they’ll know.”

    She didn’t say it like a challenge.

    She said it like weather.

    Unavoidable. Neutral. Not worth fighting.

    Rio watched her for a moment, studying the line of her jaw, the way her posture remained loose instead of guarded.

    “You’re not shrinking,” Rio observed quietly.

    Agatha turned her head slightly.

    “Should I be?”

    “No.”

    Agatha nodded once.

    They stood there another long moment, wind brushing through the grass in low waves.

    Agatha felt it, that subtle shift in identity that comes not from declaration, but repetition.

    Not from saying this is mine.

    But from acting like it already is.

    She reached out and hooked her finger loosely through one of Rio’s belt loops.

    Not tugging.

    Not claiming.

    Just touching.

    Rio glanced down at the contact, then back up.

    Didn’t comment.

    Didn’t tease.

    She just rested her hand lightly over Agatha’s knuckles where they hooked there.

    Grounding.

    Present.

    Visible.

    Agatha exhaled slowly through her nose.

    The ranch stretched wide around them.

    And for the first time in a long time, being seen didn’t feel like something that might take something away.

    It felt like something she’d chosen.

    And kept.

    Agatha didn’t let go.

    Not immediately.

    Her finger stayed hooked in Rio’s belt loop, casual and unforced, like it had always belonged there. Rio’s hand still rested lightly over Agatha’s knuckles, warm and steady.

    The wind moved again, lifting dust and grass and the faint scent of feed from the open pen behind them.

    Agatha breathed in.

    The ranch didn’t feel different.

    That was the thing.

    The sky was the same sky. The cattle shifted in the same slow rhythm. The barn doors still creaked at the same hinge.

    But something in her chest had shifted its weight.

    She’d spent so many years believing that visibility meant vulnerability. That being looked at meant being measured. That any attachment made in daylight would be tested by it.

    But standing here, shoulder to shoulder, hip to hip, hands loosely linked in plain view of the open field, she didn’t feel exposed.

    She felt aligned.

    She gave Rio’s belt loop a small, absent tug.

    “C’mon,” she said.

    Rio’s fingers slid from Agatha’s hand but not away. They brushed down her wrist instead, slow and deliberate before falling to her side. “Where to?” Rio asked.

    Agatha tipped her chin toward the north pasture.

    “Salt block needs replacin’. And I want to check the yearlings near the far trough.”

    She didn’t phrase it like a command.

    But she moved first.

    Rio followed.

    The gravel shifted beneath their boots as they crossed the yard. The sun had climbed higher now, warming the back of Agatha’s neck. She rolled her shoulders once, settling into the rhythm of movement.

    Work felt different today.

    Not lighter.

    Just shared.

    She grabbed the fresh salt block from the stacked pallets near the barn and hefted it easily. Rio reached automatically for the second one without being asked.

    Agatha didn’t thank her.

    Didn’t need to.

    Their stride fell into sync naturally as they headed toward the pasture gate. The latch squealed faintly when Agatha lifted it. She pushed the gate open with her hip, stepping aside to let Rio through first.

    It wasn’t chivalry.

    It wasn’t ceremony.

    It was habit formed over months of working alongside someone who moved at her pace.

    Rio brushed past her shoulder on the way through.

    Agatha’s hand came up instinctively to steady her at the waist, brief contact, firm and easy.

    She didn’t pull away quickly after.

    She just let her palm rest there for a second longer than necessary. Rio’s glance flicked sideways, not surprised.

    Agatha removed her hand only when they were both clear of the gate. Inside the pasture, the cattle lifted their heads at their approach. A few took slow steps forward, curious but unbothered.

    Agatha scanned them automatically.

    Posture.

    Gait.

    Ears.

    Breathing.

    She walked with authority, not tension. The kind of authority that didn’t need to be loud.

    Rio kept slightly behind her, watching the herd with sharp focus. “You see that one?” Agatha said quietly, nodding toward a smaller black heifer lingering near the fence line.

    Rio squinted slightly.

    “Left hind leg?”

    “Yeah.”

    “She’s favoring it.”

    Agatha nodded once.

    “Not bad yet. But I’ll keep an eye.”

    Rio adjusted her angle, circling slightly to get a better view without pushing the animal into motion.

    Agatha watched her move.

    There was something deeply satisfying about it, not in control, not in dominance, but in the seamlessness of it. Rio didn’t disrupt the space. She read it.

    Agatha stepped closer to the salt lick post and set the block down carefully. She crouched to wedge it securely into the holder.

    Rio knelt beside her without comment, steadying the base while Agatha adjusted the metal bracket.

    Their knees brushed in the dirt.

    Neither of them shifted away.

    Sunlight hit Rio’s forearm, highlighting faint lines of muscle and the freckles scattered along her skin.

    Agatha’s eyes lingered there a fraction too long.

    “You’re staring,” Rio murmured quietly.

    Agatha didn’t deny it.

    “Yeah.”

    Rio’s mouth twitched.

    “You gonna explain that?”

    “Nope.”

    She tightened the bracket, stood, and brushed dirt from her palms. Rio rose too, slower.

    When she did, Agatha reached out and wiped a streak of dust from Rio’s cheek with her thumb.

    The touch was firm. Practical.

    But her thumb lingered.

    Rio leaned into it, just slightly.

    Out here, in full sun, no walls, no shadows.

    Agatha didn’t feel the urge to check the horizon for witnesses. She didn’t care if someone drove by on the ridge road.

    She didn’t drop her hand immediately.

    Instead, she let it slide down the side of Rio’s neck briefly before stepping back.

    “Good,” she said.

    Rio blinked. “What?”

    “Just… good.”

    Rio studied her a moment longer, something quiet and understanding passing between them.

    They walked further into the pasture together, inspecting the trough and checking the fence tension along the east side.

    The work was steady.

    Measured.

    Agatha took the lead naturally, pointing out a loose wire, handing Rio pliers, adjusting the line while Rio held it taut.

    Their movements were wordless coordination.

    When Agatha stepped back to test the fence line, Rio’s hand settled briefly at the small of her back, steadying, grounding.

    Agatha didn’t tense.

    Didn’t shrug it off.

    She just accepted it.

    Claimed it by not rejecting it.

    That was the difference now.

    Not loud affection.

    Not dramatic gestures.

    Just the absence of avoidance.

    They finished the line and began walking back toward the gate. The cattle had settled again, heads lowered, unbothered by the interruption. Agatha swung the gate shut behind them and dropped the latch back into place.

    She stood there a moment longer than necessary.

    Rio paused beside her.

    “You good?” Rio asked softly.

    Agatha looked out across the pasture.

    Sunlight stretched long and bright across the grass now. The day was fully awake. The ranch moving in its usual rhythm.

    “I am,” she said.

    And she meant it.

    Not because nothing could go wrong.

    Not because no one would talk.

    But because she wasn’t hiding from any of it.

    She started walking back toward the house.

    Halfway across the yard, she reached sideways and took Rio’s hand. Not tentatively.

    Not like a test.

    Just took it.

    Rio’s fingers tightened around hers without hesitation. They didn’t swing their arms.

    Didn’t exaggerate the contact.

    They just walked like that.

    Boots crunching gravel.

    Wind brushing their sleeves.

    Sun warming their joined hands.

    Agatha felt it settle in her bones, not defiance, not rebellion, but ownership. Of the land.

    Of her place on it.

    Of the woman walking beside her.

    Not possession.

    Not control.

    Choice.

    When they reached the porch steps, Agatha didn’t release her hand immediately.

    She turned toward Rio instead.

    Studied her face in full daylight.

    No shadow.

    No softness of morning haze.

    Just clear sun and open sky.

    “You hungry?” she asked.

    Rio nodded once.

    “Yeah.”

    Agatha squeezed her hand once before letting go.

    “Good. Then we eat. Then we finish the north line.”

    Matter-of-fact.

    Forward-looking.

    Rio smiled faintly.

    “Yes, ma’am.”

    Agatha snorted and climbed the porch steps.

    But before she reached the door, she paused.

    Turned back.

    Stepped down one step so she stood level with Rio again. Without preamble, without checking the yard, without softening it — She leaned in and kissed her.

    Slow.

    Certain.

    Daylight on their skin.

    No urgency.

    No secrecy.

    Just contact.

    Rio’s hand came up to her waist automatically. The kiss deepened slightly, then softened again before either of them lost the thread of the moment. When they parted, Agatha rested her forehead briefly against Rio’s. “Day’s ours,” she murmured.

    Not romantic.

    Not grand.

    Just true.

    Then she stepped back, opened the door, and walked inside. And this time, she didn’t feel like she was bracing for anything. She was simply living in it.

    Chapter End Notes I love writing them working side by side. There’s something intimate about shared labor, especially when it’s no longer something to hide.

    Chapter 24: Making Space

    Chapter Summary Agatha has always known where everything belongs. But when she finds Rio’s things in her drawer, she doesn’t push them aside. She makes space. And that small, practical choice changes more than either of them says out loud.

    Chapter Notes A chapter about drawers, habits, and the kind of change that doesn’t need to be announced.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes Agatha woke to the sound of the kettle, not the shriek of it, not yet, just that low, building murmur of water beginning to think about boiling. For a second, she didn’t move.

    Didn’t open her eyes.

    Just listened.

    There were other sounds layered beneath it. The soft thud of cabinet doors closing without being slammed. The scrape of ceramic against wood. The faint shuffle of bare feet crossing the kitchen floorboards she knew by heart, the ones that dipped slightly near the stove, the ones that creaked near the pantry door.

    Not hers.

    She lay still a moment longer, staring at the ceiling beams washed pale with early light. The house smelled like coffee already, strong, dark, unapologetic. And underneath it, something warmer. Toast, maybe. Or just heat meeting old wood.

    She rolled onto her side and pushed herself up, the sheet sliding down her waist. The air was cool against her skin, but not sharp. Late-summer cool. Temporary. The kind that would burn off by midmorning.

    Her boots weren’t by the bed.

    They were by the door.

    Because that’s where they belonged.

    Because someone had moved them there last night when she’d kicked them off halfway across the room and forgotten.

    Agatha swung her legs over the side of the bed and sat there for a beat, elbows on her knees, palms hanging loose between them. The kettle clicked louder now. The sound of a spoon tapping once against a mug. She didn’t rush.

    That was new enough she noticed it.

    Usually she’d be up before any of it, before sound, before movement, before the house even remembered it was meant to hold more than one body. But now she sat there, listening to someone else move first. And the world hadn’t collapsed for it.

    She stood, pulled on yesterday’s jeans from the chair, shrugged into a worn white tank, and walked barefoot down the narrow hallway toward the kitchen.

    The light hit her first, soft and angled, coming in through the east window above the sink. Dust hung in it like it always did, slow and unbothered. The table had a thin stripe of gold across it.

    Rio stood at the stove.

    Barefoot.

    In Agatha’s flannel.

    It hung low on her shoulders, sleeves rolled unevenly to her forearms. The hem brushed mid-thigh over a pair of sleep shorts Agatha didn’t recognize but had stopped cataloging as foreign sometime last week. Her hair was tied up in a loose, messy knot that had already started to slip. A pencil was tucked behind her ear like she’d been writing before she’d come in here. She was stirring something in a pan. Eggs, from the look of it. She hadn’t noticed Agatha yet.

    Agatha leaned her shoulder against the doorway and watched. Rio moved differently in the kitchen than she did in the field. Less measured. Less rigid. Still deliberate, but softened. She reached for the salt without looking. Knew where the pepper was. Opened the drawer beneath the counter and found the spatula without hesitation.

    She’d learned the house.

    Or maybe the house had learned her.

    The kettle began to whistle.

    Rio turned, lifted it without flinching, and poured hot water into the chipped blue mug.

    Agatha’s mug.

    Or—she supposed—not exclusively.

    Rio didn’t look guilty holding it.

    Didn’t look like a guest borrowing something she meant to return. She just wrapped her fingers around it, lifted it to her mouth, and took a slow sip like she’d done it a hundred times.

    Agatha felt something small and quiet settle in her chest. The dogs scratched lightly at the back door. Rio glanced over her shoulder and said, “I fed you guys already.”

    Her voice was rough with sleep.

    She still hadn’t seen Agatha.

    Agatha pushed off the doorway and stepped into the kitchen fully. The floorboards creaked under her weight.

    Rio turned then.

    There was no surprise in her face. No flustered apology. Just that steady, assessing look she always wore in the mornings.

    “Morning,” she said.

    Agatha grunted something that passed for agreement and moved to the cabinet for her own mug. The plain white one with the crack under the handle.

    Rio didn’t offer to pour for her.

    Didn’t hover.

    She just stepped slightly to the left, giving her space at the stove without thinking about it.

    Agatha poured her coffee. Black. Strong enough to burn.

    She leaned against the counter and took a long swallow, eyes drifting across the room as she did.

    Rio’s boots were by the door.

    Not lined up neatly like they’d been placed with care. Not tucked under the bench like someone trying to be polite.

    Just there.

    Slightly angled. One lace trailing loose across the floor. Her canvas field bag hung from the hook beneath the hat rack. Not slung over a chair. Not resting near the door like it was waiting to leave. It had weight in it.

    She could tell by the way the strap pulled slightly against the nail. Rio turned back to the stove and scooped the eggs onto two plates without asking whether Agatha wanted any.

    She set one down at the table. The other she carried with her as she sat. Agatha joined her a second later.

    They didn’t make a show of eating. Just sat across from each other in the quiet kitchen while the light widened across the table.

    Rio reached for the salt again. Sprinkled some onto her eggs. Took a bite. Agatha watched her for a moment longer than necessary.

    “Cattle’re up by the north line,” she said finally. “Heard ’em before sunup.” Rio nodded. “I saw them when I let the dogs out.”

    Of course she had.

    She’d stepped outside barefoot earlier, Agatha could see the faint trail of dust on her ankles now. The hem of the flannel was smudged faintly at the bottom from brushing against the porch railing.

    Agatha ate slowly.

    Rio’s notebook lay open at the corner of the table. A pen rested diagonally across the page like she’d paused mid-thought. There was a pressed sprig of something small and purple tucked between two sheets of paper, flattened but not yet brittle.

    It didn’t feel intrusive.

    It didn’t feel like something left behind.

    It felt… used.

    After they finished, Rio gathered the plates without being asked and carried them to the sink.

    Agatha didn’t stop her.

    Didn’t insist.

    Just watched her rinse the ceramic under warm water and set them in the rack to dry.

    “You got plans this mornin’?” Agatha asked, voice even.

    “Just organizing samples,” Rio replied. “Labeling, mostly.” She dried her hands on the dish towel and draped it back over the oven handle without ceremony.

    Agatha nodded once.

    She moved toward the back door, grabbed her boots, and sat on the bench to pull them on. Rio stepped around her easily, reaching up to grab her hat from the rack.

    Not asking permission.

    Not looking to see if it was okay.

    Just moving like she knew she belonged in the space between those walls. When she stood, Rio was already halfway through the door, sunlight catching in her hair where it had slipped loose from the knot. Agatha followed.

    They stepped out onto the porch together.

    The morning was already warming. The grass beyond the steps shimmered faintly with heat rising. The barn door stood open. A chicken strutted across the yard like it owned the place.

    Rio paused at the top step and stretched her arms over her head, the flannel pulling tight across her shoulders.

    Agatha looked at her boots again when she passed them.

    Still by the door.

    Still dusty.

    Still there.

    She didn’t comment.

    Didn’t tidy them.

    Didn’t move them out of the way.

    She just stepped around them like she would her own.

    And that was the whole difference.

    It was well past noon by the time the irritation arrived. Not the loud kind. Not the kind that flared hot and fast and made her snap. This one crept in sideways, quiet and small and practical. The house was warm now, heat pressing low against the walls. The air inside held the faint smell of coffee long gone cold and sun-warmed wood. Rio had taken her samples into the spare room near the back, the one with the narrow desk and the crooked window that never quite shut all the way. Agatha could hear the faint scratch of pen against paper, the occasional soft click of a camera being reviewed, the quiet hum of someone thinking through something.

    Agatha moved through the kitchen alone.

    She’d come in for twine. Or she thought she had. Something small. Something she’d kept in the same place for years.

    The drawer beside the stove.

    She pulled it open.

    It stuck halfway.

    She frowned and tugged harder.

    It slid the rest of the way out with a dull scrape.

    Inside, the drawer looked wrong.

    Not wrecked. Not chaotic.

    Just crowded.

    Her roll of baling twine sat pushed to the back corner. The box of matches she kept tucked along the side had shifted forward. The small tin of fence staples she liked within easy reach was buried under—

    A stack of sample labels.

    Neatly bundled.

    Rio’s handwriting stretched across the top one in small, slanted script. Latin names Agatha couldn’t pronounce if she tried.

    There was also a coil of measuring tape. A spare pencil. A folded cloth bag holding something that felt like dried soil.

    Agatha stared at it.

    Her jaw tightened before she meant it to.

    This drawer had always been hers.

    Not in a sentimental way.

    In a functional way.

    She could open it blind and know where everything sat. Could reach in without looking and grab what she needed without knocking something loose.

    Now she had to move things.

    She shifted the bundle of labels aside, careful not to bend them, and dug her fingers into the back corner for the twine.

    The measuring tape caught on her knuckle.

    She exhaled sharply through her nose.

    It wasn’t anger.

    It was disruption.

    The old, familiar resistance flared in her chest, the reflexive tightening that came when something of hers shifted without warning.

    Mine.

    The thought wasn’t loud, but it was there.

    This is mine.

    She closed the drawer halfway and stood there, staring down at it. From the spare room, a chair leg scraped softly against the floor. Rio murmured something to herself, low and thoughtful. The sound drifted down the hallway like it belonged there.

    Agatha rubbed her thumb along the edge of the counter.

    The irritation sharpened slightly.

    Not because Rio had taken over the drawer.

    But because she hadn’t asked.

    Because she hadn’t made a show of it.

    She’d just used it.

    Like it was natural.

    Like it wasn’t something temporary.

    Agatha pulled the drawer open again and looked at it properly this time. It wasn’t messy.

    It wasn’t careless.

    The labels were stacked tight and squared. The pencil sharpened. The cloth bag folded neat.

    Rio hadn’t claimed space recklessly.

    She’d simply filled what was available.

    Because that’s what you did in a place you were working in. You used it.

    Agatha’s shoulders eased half an inch.

    She reached in and lifted the bundle of labels out first. Held them in her hands.

    The paper was warm from the house heat. The edges slightly curled from humidity. Rio’s handwriting ran clean and steady across the top one, milkvetch. wild heliotrope. beardtongue.

    Agatha didn’t know what most of it meant.

    She set the stack gently on the counter.

    Then she removed the measuring tape.

    The cloth bag.

    The pencil.

    One by one.

    She lined them up carefully along the edge of the counter, parallel to each other.

    The drawer looked larger immediately.

    Her twine sat at the back. The staples were still slightly buried. She hesitated.

    The old instinct whispered again.

    You could just put them back.

    You could move them somewhere else.

    The spare room had a desk.

    There were shelves in the hall closet.

    This didn’t have to be shared.

    She rested her palms on the counter and leaned into them. The house hummed faintly around her.

    From the back room, she could hear the faint flip of a notebook page. Rio wasn’t claiming territory.

    She was working.

    Agatha straightened.

    Instead of pushing Rio’s things back into the drawer—

    She reached in and removed her own.

    The tin of staples first.

    The matches.

    The small square of sandpaper she kept tucked near the corner. She emptied the drawer entirely.

    Laid everything out on the counter in two loose groups without labeling them as such.

    Hers.

    And not hers.

    She stared at the empty wood.

    The drawer bottom was scratched deep from years of use. A dark ring from a bottle that had once leaked. A faint split in the back right corner. It had never been precious.

    It had just been familiar.

    Agatha reached for the labels.

    Placed them back inside first.

    Flat and centered.

    Then the measuring tape, coiled neatly.

    The pencil tucked along the side.

    The cloth bag near the front.

    She arranged them with space between each item, not crammed, not forced. Then she closed the drawer slowly.

    Opened it again.

    It slid smooth this time.

    Her things remained on the counter.

    She looked at them.

    The tin of staples felt heavier than it should have.

    She carried them to the pantry instead.

    Set them on the second shelf near the spare nails.

    The matches went into the cabinet above the fridge.

    The sandpaper into the tool caddy by the back door.

    It wasn’t exile.

    It was redistribution.

    She came back to the stove and opened the drawer one more time. Rio’s things sat there.

    Not borrowed.

    Not misplaced.

    Just present.

    Agatha ran her hand along the edge of the wood.

    There was no tightness in her chest now.

    No flare.

    Just a faint awareness of the shift.

    This wasn’t about twine.

    It wasn’t about a drawer.

    It was about the fact that the house no longer operated solely around her muscle memory.

    It had another rhythm inside it.

    She closed the drawer and wiped her hands on her jeans out of habit. The irritation had been small.

    Smaller than she would have expected, given how she used to be. Years ago, she would have snapped.

    Made a pointed comment.

    Shifted everything back before Rio noticed.

    Now she stood there in the quiet kitchen, the drawer changed, and felt something steadier settle into place.

    From the spare room, Rio laughed softly at something in her notes. Agatha glanced down the hallway.

    She didn’t call out.

    Didn’t announce what she’d done.

    She just moved back toward the porch door, stepped around the boots again, and went back out into the yard.

    The drawer stayed the way it was.

    And that mattered more than she’d expected.

    Agatha didn’t think about it again until late afternoon, when the sun had shifted west and the heat had turned dense and close. She came back inside to grab a rag and a glass of water, boots thudding lightly against the floorboards, dust trailing in behind her.

    The house was quieter now.

    No pen scratching.

    No chair scraping.

    Rio stood at the counter instead, sleeves pushed up, hair fallen halfway out of its knot, a faint smudge of dirt streaked across her cheekbone like she’d forgotten it there.

    The drawer slid out smooth and full, sunlight catching the pale edges of the labels inside. Rio’s fingers hovered just above them, then dipped in without hesitation.

    She pulled out the measuring tape.

    Paused.

    Her eyes flicked across the contents.

    Then she looked at the counter.

    Then at the pantry.

    Then back at the drawer.

    Agatha didn’t stop walking.

    She moved to the sink, ran the tap, drank from the glass she’d grabbed without comment. The water was lukewarm but welcome.

    Behind her, the drawer slid closed.

    “Did you reorganize?” Rio asked.

    Not accusatory.

    Not sharp.

    Just curious.

    Agatha wiped her mouth with the back of her hand and set the glass down. “Drawer was a mess.”

    It wasn’t.

    But the words came easy.

    There was a quiet behind her that felt heavier than the question had been. Agatha could feel Rio looking at her. Not hard. Not pressing. Just taking inventory the way she always did, of land, of weather, of plants. Of her.

    “You moved your staples,” Rio said after a moment.

    Agatha shrugged. “Didn’t need ’em there.”

    She reached for the rag hanging by the stove and wiped down a spot on the counter that didn’t need wiping.

    Behind her, silence again.

    Not cold.

    Just considering.

    Rio’s feet scuffed once against the floorboards as she shifted her weight. Agatha could picture the exact expression on her face without turning, the slight tilt of her head, the narrowing of her eyes not in suspicion but in thought.

    “You didn’t have to,” Rio said finally.

    Agatha set the rag down.

    “That drawer’s closer,” she replied, tone even. “You use it more.” She still didn’t turn around.

    She didn’t want to see the reaction too clearly. Didn’t want to track the micro-shifts in Rio’s mouth or eyes. She’d made the adjustment. That was the end of it.

    But Rio didn’t let it rest there.

    There was the soft sound of the drawer sliding open again. A beat.

    Then it closed.

    Footsteps crossed the kitchen, slow, measured, deliberate. Agatha felt the shift in air before she felt the touch.

    Rio stopped just inside her space.

    Not crowding.

    Not demanding.

    Close enough that the heat of her skin registered through denim and cotton. “You didn’t move my things somewhere else,” Rio said quietly. Agatha swallowed.

    “No.”

    “Just yours.”

    “Yeah.”

    There was no defense in it.

    No justification.

    Just fact.

    Rio was quiet again.

    Agatha could feel her attention moving, not across the room, not toward the sink or the table, but inward.

    Toward what the action meant.

    Not the drawer.

    The choice.

    Agatha kept her eyes on the countertop.

    Her reflection ghosted faintly in the window above the sink. Rio’s shape behind her, darker, steady.

    “You always know where everything is,” Rio said, almost to herself. “You don’t like it changing.”

    Agatha huffed softly. “Didn’t say I liked it.”

    “But you left it.”

    Agatha didn’t answer.

    Because that was the part that felt louder than the rest. Rio stepped closer.

    Her fingers brushed the back pocket of Agatha’s jeans, light enough it could’ve been accidental. Then they stayed there, resting, not gripping. Agatha’s spine straightened slightly at the contact.

    “You didn’t make it smaller,” Rio said.

    Agatha frowned faintly. “What?”

    “The space.”

    Rio’s hand slid around her hip now, thumb hooking lightly at the seam where denim met skin.

    “You didn’t make it smaller for me,” she clarified. “You made it bigger.” Agatha’s jaw tightened, not in anger, but in awareness.

    She hadn’t thought of it like that.

    She hadn’t thought of it at all beyond the physical act. But hearing it spoken back to her shifted something.

    She turned then.

    Slow.

    Rio was watching her the way she watched a patch of land she didn’t want to misread. Not eager. Not expectant. Just attentive.

    There was no smile on her face.

    No gratitude.

    Just recognition.

    “You use it,” Agatha said, gesturing vaguely toward the stove with her chin. “Makes sense.”

    Rio searched her face.

    “For now?” she asked.

    The question wasn’t loaded.

    It wasn’t a trap.

    It was logistical.

    Agatha felt it land in her chest anyway.

    “For when you’re workin’,” she said, careful. “It’s easier.” Rio’s eyes didn’t flicker.

    She held the look steady.

    “And you’re okay with that.”

    It wasn’t a question this time.

    Agatha considered it.

    She thought about the first flare of irritation.

    About the drawer sticking halfway.

    About the instinct to reclaim.

    Then about the moment she’d laid everything out and chosen otherwise. “Yeah,” she said.

    It came out simple.

    Not strained.

    Just true.

    Rio exhaled softly.

    Not relief.

    Not triumph.

    Something quieter.

    Her hand left Agatha’s hip and slid up along her waist, fingers resting just under the hem of her tank.

    “You didn’t flinch,” Rio said quietly.

    Agatha frowned faintly. “At what?”

    “At making room.”

    The words landed softly between them.

    Agatha swallowed.

    She hadn’t flinched.

    She’d felt it, yes, the old reflex tightening, but she hadn’t let it dictate. She hadn’t corrected.

    She hadn’t reclaimed.

    Rio stepped closer still, closing the final inch of space between them. Their hips aligned naturally. The contact was gentle, steady.

    “You could’ve,” Rio continued, voice low but even. “You didn’t.” Agatha’s hands hovered uncertainly for half a second before settling at Rio’s waist.

    Light.

    Testing.

    Rio didn’t hesitate.

    She leaned into it, weight shifting forward, hands sliding more securely around Agatha’s sides.

    There was something grounding about the contact.

    Something that made the kitchen feel smaller in the best way. Agatha felt her own shoulders drop again.

    “Wasn’t a big deal,” she muttered.

    Rio’s mouth curved faintly.

    “It was.”

    Agatha looked at her then.

    Really looked.

    There was no awe.

    No stunned quiet.

    Just something softer.

    Something certain.

    Rio lifted her other hand and brought it to Agatha’s jaw, fingers brushing the edge of her cheekbone. The touch was warm, deliberate, unhurried. Agatha didn’t tense.

    Didn’t brace.

    She leaned into it.

    Barely.

    But enough.

    Rio’s thumb stroked once along her cheek.

    “You don’t have to explain it,” Rio said softly.

    “I’m not tryin’ to.”

    “I know.”

    The silence that followed wasn’t heavy.

    It wasn’t the kind that pressed against Agatha’s ribs and demanded she fill it.

    It was open.

    Spacious.

    Rio’s forehead tipped forward until it brushed lightly against Agatha’s. The contact was gentle.

    Their breaths mingled.

    The house seemed to quiet around them, like it understood not to interrupt. Agatha’s hands tightened slightly at Rio’s waist.

    Not gripping.

    Just confirming.

    Rio’s fingers slid from her jaw to the back of her neck, palm warm against skin.

    The gesture wasn’t heated.

    But it wasn’t casual either.

    It held intention.

    A quiet acknowledgment.

    You made space.

    I see it.

    Agatha exhaled slowly, breath brushing against Rio’s mouth. Rio didn’t kiss her immediately.

    She stayed there first.

    Let the closeness settle.

    Let the meaning breathe.

    Agatha felt the steadiness of it in her bones.

    No rush.

    No urgency.

    Just choice.

    Then Rio’s mouth found hers.

    Soft.

    Unhurried.

    It wasn’t the kind of kiss that devoured.

    It wasn’t driven by leftover heat or restless hands.

    It was measured.

    Certain.

    Her lips moved slowly against Agatha’s, like she was memorizing the shape of this moment rather than chasing it.

    Agatha’s fingers curled slightly into the fabric at Rio’s waist. She answered the kiss without hesitation.

    No hesitation at all.

    The contact deepened, not in hunger, but in weight. Rio’s thumb traced along the edge of Agatha’s neck while their mouths stayed close, breathing the same air.

    Agatha felt something quiet and solid settle into place inside her chest. Not excitement.

    Not adrenaline.

    Something steadier.

    Rio’s hand slid down her back, palm flattening between her shoulder blades, pressing her closer.

    Agatha stepped forward without thinking, closing whatever space remained. The kitchen felt smaller now.

    Warmer.

    The clock ticked on the wall.

    Outside, a bird called once and fell silent again.

    The kiss slowed further.

    Softened.

    When Rio finally pulled back, she didn’t go far.

    Their foreheads remained touching.

    Their hands still resting where they’d settled.

    Rio’s thumb brushed once more along the line of Agatha’s jaw. “Okay,” she said again.

    But this time it felt like more than acknowledgment.

    It felt like agreement.

    Agatha swallowed.

    “Okay,” she answered.

    And neither of them moved away immediately.

    They just stood there in the quiet kitchen, hands resting easily at each other’s waists, the drawer unchanged, the house unchanged— Except it wasn’t.

    And that was enough.

    It was Rio who stepped back first. Not abruptly. Not to break anything. Just a small shift of weight, a slow slide of her palm down Agatha’s side before her fingers loosened their hold.

    She didn’t say anything else.

    Didn’t add to it.

    She picked up her notebook from the counter where she’d set it and gave Agatha one last look, steady, quiet, unreadable in the way that wasn’t withholding but simply self-contained.

    Then she disappeared back down the hallway.

    The scratch of pen resumed again a moment later.

    Agatha stayed where she was.

    The kitchen air felt different now, not warmer, not cooler. Just settled. Like something had clicked into place without needing to announce it. She reached absently for her glass, found it empty, and set it back down without refilling it. There was no urgency in her movements. No sense that she needed to reassert anything or tidy something back into its original shape.

    She opened the drawer once more.

    It slid open smooth and easy.

    Rio’s labels lay centered. The measuring tape coiled neatly. The pencil tucked along the side. The cloth bag resting near the front where fingers could reach it without digging.

    Nothing about it looked temporary.

    It didn’t look invasive either.

    It just looked used.

    Agatha closed it.

    Left it that way.

    The afternoon drifted into evening slowly. Heat softened. Light shifted from bright gold to something lower and amber. The house breathed with it, wood expanding, then easing, the walls holding warmth from the day. When Rio finally emerged from the back room, her hair had fallen completely loose. A smudge of graphite streaked the side of her hand. She didn’t speak when she passed through the kitchen. She just reached for a glass, filled it from the tap, and leaned against the counter like she’d done it a hundred times before.

    Agatha didn’t watch her openly.

    She noticed her in pieces.

    The way her hip fit against the cabinet.

    The way she nudged her boots aside with her foot without thinking, clearing the doorway as she moved.

    The way she reached for the dish towel hanging from the oven handle and used it like it belonged in her hand.

    There was no choreography.

    No careful stepping around each other.

    They moved through the same space and adjusted naturally. Later, when the light began to thin and the sky outside the kitchen window turned soft and violet at the edges, Agatha made her way through the house one last time before night settled fully in.

    She checked the latch on the back door.

    Turned the stove knob once, twice, even though she hadn’t used it since morning.

    She passed the spare room.

    Rio’s field bag sat on the floor beside the desk now. Unzipped. Papers peeking out slightly at the edges. A pressed flower left drying between two pages of a book.

    Not packed.

    Not gathered.

    Just resting where it had been set down.

    Agatha didn’t pause long.

    She walked into the bedroom.

    The flannel Rio had worn that morning lay folded over the back of the chair. Not draped carelessly. Not hung up formally. Just placed there. A pair of socks lay near the bed.

    Not hidden.

    Not tucked away.

    Agatha stepped around them without irritation.

    She walked back into the kitchen.

    Rio was still there, now perched on the edge of the counter, finishing her water. She looked up when Agatha entered, eyes soft in the low light. Agatha reached for the lamp near the sink and switched it on. Not the overhead light.

    Just the lamp.

    It cast a small circle of warm yellow across the counter, leaving the rest of the kitchen in a soft shadow.

    Rio watched her.

    Didn’t ask why.

    Agatha didn’t explain.

    Agatha moved to the front door and checked the bolt. Not because she thought someone would come through it. Just because she always did. Routine.

    Habit.

    But she didn’t shut off the lamp in the kitchen when she passed it again. She left it glowing.

    Soft.

    Steady.

    Rio slid off the counter and followed her down the hallway without being told to.

    Agatha paused near the bedroom door, letting Rio step in ahead of her. The house had settled fully now. Outside, the crickets had started up. A low hum of night rising in layers.

    She stood there for a second in the dim hallway, listening to Rio move inside the room. The rustle of fabric. The mattress dipping slightly. Agatha glanced back toward the kitchen.

    The lamp cast a gentle glow through the doorway. Warm. Inviting. Not harsh.

    It wasn’t a signal.

    It wasn’t a promise.

    It was just light left on in a house that no longer felt like it needed to be guarded from intrusion.

    She walked into the bedroom and closed the door behind her. The house didn’t feel borrowed anymore.

    It didn’t feel like it was holding space temporarily, waiting for something to be reclaimed.

    It felt lived in.

    Not rearranged.

    Just shared.

    Agatha lay down beside Rio without comment.

    Didn’t ask.

    Didn’t explain.

    The kitchen lamp stayed on a little longer than necessary. And no one rushed to turn it off.

    Chapter End Notes Sometimes the biggest shifts aren’t confessions.

    They’re adjustments.

    Chapter 25: Dust and Sugar

    Chapter Summary Rio asks Agatha to go to the county fair with her.

    It’s not dramatic. It’s not framed like a risk. It’s just a question and Agatha says yes.

    Under string lights and livestock banners, between sugar-dusted fingers and dust-heavy boots, they walk through town together for the first time since everything changed. People notice. Some stare. Some don’t. The sky doesn’t fall.

    Agatha doesn’t shrink.

    And when the night winds down and the gravel crunches under their boots, she makes a choice that feels small and monumental all at once. Chapter Notes Back in town, but this time it’s different.

    No bracing. No shrinking. Just choosing each other in the open. See the end of the chapter for more notes The idea came in the middle of something ordinary.

    Agatha was at the sink, sleeves rolled to her elbows, scrubbing dirt from beneath her nails with the edge of a small brush she kept tucked behind the soap. The day had been long but not difficult, fence line checked, cattle rotated, a loose latch fixed on the south gate. The house smelled faintly of sun-warmed wood and whatever Rio had been drying on the table earlier, pressed stems and paper and dust.

    Rio stood near the doorway, leaning one shoulder against the frame, notebook in hand but not writing.

    She’d been quiet for a few minutes.

    That was what made Agatha glance up.

    “Spit it out,” she said, not unkindly.

    Rio looked at her over the edge of the notebook. Not guarded. Just thinking. “There’s a fair,” she said.

    Agatha snorted softly. “There’s always a fair.”

    “County one,” Rio clarified. “Livestock judging. Local produce. Probably bad music.”

    Agatha rinsed her hands and shook them dry. “You hate crowds.” “I don’t hate them,” Rio corrected calmly. “I just don’t prefer them.” Agatha reached for the towel and wiped her palms slowly, buying time she didn’t need.

    “You want to go?” she asked, already knowing the answer. Rio closed the notebook.

    “Yes.”

    No hesitation.

    Just yes.

    Agatha leaned back against the counter, arms crossing loosely over her chest. “Thought you had labeling to finish.”

    “I do.” Rio paused. “It can wait.”

    That made Agatha’s eyes narrow slightly.

    “Why?” she asked.

    Rio held her gaze evenly.

    “Because I want to go with you.”

    The kitchen went still for half a second.

    Not heavy.

    Not dramatic.

    Just still.

    “With me,” Agatha repeated.

    Rio nodded once. “Yes.”

    Agatha pushed off the counter. “You can go without me. You know that.” “I know.”

    Rio stepped further into the kitchen now, closing some of the distance between them.

    “I don’t want to,” she said.

    The words weren’t shy.

    They weren’t playful either.

    Just direct.

    Agatha felt something low in her chest shift slightly.

    “You asking?” she said, tone almost neutral.

    Rio’s mouth curved faintly. “Yes.”

    There it was.

    Clear as anything.

    Not a suggestion.

    Not a passing thought.

    A question.

    Agatha studied her.

    Rio wasn’t dressed for town. She was still in work jeans, boots dusty, hair loose from the knot she’d tied that morning. There was a faint smudge of dirt along her jawline she hadn’t noticed.

    “You know folks’ll stare,” Agatha said.

    Rio didn’t flinch.

    “They always do.”

    “That don’t bother you?”

    Rio considered that.

    “It used to,” she said. “Doesn’t much anymore.”

    Agatha tilted her head slightly.

    “And you?” Rio asked quietly.

    That was the real question.

    Agatha held her gaze.

    The first time they’d gone into town together, she’d felt every pair of eyes like a weight. Every pause in conversation. Every glance that lingered too long.

    She hadn’t known what to do with Rio beside her then.

    Now?

    She exhaled through her nose.

    “Don’t bother me,” she said.

    Rio watched her carefully, like she was measuring the truth of it. “You sure?” she asked.

    Agatha stepped closer.

    “Yeah,” she said simply.

    The silence stretched a moment longer.

    Then Rio nodded once, satisfied.

    “Okay,” she said. “Will you go with me?”

    The phrasing made something warm curl low in Agatha’s stomach. Not are we going.

    Not we should go.

    Will you go with me.

    Agatha let the words settle.

    “Yeah,” she said.

    Rio’s shoulders eased slightly.

    Not relief.

    More like confirmation.

    “Good,” she replied.

    There was no squeal. No grin. No sudden burst of energy. Just that steady acceptance.

    Agatha cleared her throat. “You plannin’ on wearin’ that?” she asked, nodding vaguely at Rio’s dust-streaked jeans.

    Rio glanced down at herself. “Is there a dress code?”

    Agatha’s mouth twitched. “It’s a fair, not a gala.”

    “Then I’m fine.”

    Agatha shook her head faintly and moved toward the bedroom. “Don’t wanna hear you complainin’ when someone mistakes you for a farmhand,” she called over her shoulder.

    Rio followed her down the hallway.

    “I am working your land,” she said mildly.

    Agatha paused at the bedroom doorway and turned.

    “That don’t make you hired help.”

    Rio’s gaze flickered at that.

    Something unreadable passed through her eyes.

    Agatha disappeared into the room without elaborating.

    The bedroom was warm with late-afternoon light. The window stood cracked open, curtain stirring faintly in the breeze. Agatha moved toward the dresser and pulled open the top drawer.

    Her hands hovered for a second over her usual work shirts. Then she pushed them aside.

    Reached deeper.

    Pulled out the dark button-down she only wore when she had to go somewhere that wasn’t strictly functional.

    Not fancy.

    Just cleaner.

    Less worn at the cuffs.

    She held it up for a second, examining it like she hadn’t seen it in months. Behind her, Rio leaned against the doorframe, watching. “You don’t have to dress up,” Rio said.

    “I ain’t,” Agatha replied. “This is as dressed up as it gets.” She shrugged out of the tank she’d been wearing and pulled the shirt on, leaving it unbuttoned at first.

    Rio’s eyes tracked the movement without apology.

    Agatha caught her looking.

    “What?” she asked.

    “Nothing,” Rio said, though the corner of her mouth betrayed her. Agatha rolled her eyes and began fastening the buttons slowly. Rio stepped into the room fully now, crossing to the chair where the flannel she’d borrowed still hung.

    “Can I—?” she started.

    Agatha glanced over.

    “Take it,” she said.

    Rio slipped it on over her own shirt without ceremony.

    It hung oversized on her frame, sleeves brushing past her wrists. She rolled them up neatly, exposing her forearms.

    Agatha watched the motion without meaning to.

    Rio reached for her boots and sat on the edge of the bed to pull them on, fingers working the laces tight.

    The room felt smaller now.

    Quieter.

    Not tense.

    Just charged with something unspoken.

    When Rio stood, she adjusted the flannel at her shoulders and looked at Agatha expectantly.

    “Ready?” she asked.

    Agatha grabbed her hat from the hook near the door and set it firmly on her head.

    “Yeah,” she said.

    Rio hesitated for half a second.

    Then she stepped closer.

    Close enough that their chests almost touched.

    “Good,” she murmured.

    Not flustered.

    Not shy.

    Just pleased.

    Agatha felt it in her bones.

    Not because they were going to a fair.

    Because Rio had asked.

    And she had said yes.

    They walked toward the door side by side.

    They didn’t talk much on the drive in.

    Not because there was tension.

    Because there wasn’t.

    The truck rolled down the long stretch of county road with that familiar low hum, tires kicking up pale dust that curled behind them like a lazy tail. The fields blurred gold on either side, fence posts ticking past in steady rhythm. The sun had dipped just enough to soften the light, turning everything a little warmer, a little slower.

    Agatha drove with one hand on the wheel, the other resting near the gearshift.

    Rio sat angled slightly toward the window, elbow propped against the door, watching the land pass like she was cataloguing it even now. She looked calm.

    Comfortable.

    That did something quiet to Agatha’s chest.

    The closer they got, the more the landscape shifted.

    More trucks parked along the shoulder. More dust hanging thicker in the air. The faint echo of distant speakers testing microphones, feedback whining briefly before settling.

    Agatha slowed as they reached the gravel entrance to the fairgrounds. There were handmade signs nailed to posts: WELCOME. 4-H LIVESTOCK SHOW. PIE CONTEST 6 PM.

    The kind of lettering that looked permanent even though it wasn’t. She pulled in without hesitation.

    The gravel crunched loudly under the tires as she steered toward the back of the lot. The field was already half-full. Pickups lined up in uneven rows. Families unloading folding chairs. Teenagers hovering near the livestock pens pretending not to care.

    Rio leaned forward slightly, peering through the windshield. “Bigger than I expected,” she murmured.

    Agatha huffed softly. “You haven't seen the turnout for church fish fries.” Rio’s mouth curved faintly.

    Agatha parked near the edge, nose facing out toward the road like she always did.

    Engine off.

    Silence rushed in.

    For a second, neither of them moved.

    The fair noise reached them through the open windows now, laughter, a generator humming, music bleeding faintly from a tinny speaker somewhere deeper in the grounds.

    Agatha reached for the door handle.

    Paused.

    Looked over at Rio.

    Rio was already looking back at her.

    Not questioning.

    Just present.

    “You good?” Agatha asked.

    Rio nodded once. “Yeah.”

    Agatha studied her for a beat longer.

    She opened the door.

    Heat hit immediately.

    The air was thicker here, full of churned dirt and fryer oil and livestock and sweet kettle corn drifting low over everything. The late-afternoon sun sat heavy and gold over the field, casting long shadows under the trucks. Agatha stepped down first.

    Rio followed.

    The truck door shut with a solid thunk behind them.

    And just like that, they were visible.

    Not hidden behind windshield glass.

    Not tucked into the quiet of the ranch.

    Out in it.

    Agatha adjusted her hat slightly, scanning the lot out of habit. She recognized most of the trucks. Knew the plates. Knew the dents. A few heads turned.

    Not sharply.

    Not dramatically.

    Just that small-town pause when something familiar rearranges itself. Agatha didn’t look away.

    She didn’t square her shoulders either.

    She just stood.

    Rio stepped up beside her, close enough that their sleeves brushed. She didn’t reach for Agatha’s hand.

    Didn’t cling.

    She just stood there, shoulder to shoulder.

    The message in it was quieter than hand-holding.

    And stronger.

    Agatha started walking.

    Rio matched her pace without comment.

    The gravel shifted under their boots as they crossed toward the entrance. Kids ran past them with sticky fingers and sunburned cheeks. A little boy dragged a red wagon too big for him, wheels wobbling dangerously. They passed under a wooden arch strung with lights that wouldn’t come on until dusk.

    WELCOME TO THE COUNTY FAIR.

    The letters were slightly crooked.

    Inside, the grounds were alive.

    Rows of white tents. Livestock pens arranged in uneven lines. Folding tables stacked with jars of preserves and homemade quilts pinned up for judging. The smell of hay mixed with caramelized sugar and grease. Agatha felt it all register in her body.

    The noise.

    The movement.

    The eyes.

    Someone called her name from somewhere near the cattle pens. She lifted her chin in acknowledgment without breaking stride. Rio’s gaze flicked around carefully, taking it in. Not overwhelmed. Not shrinking.

    Just observing.

    A group of older men near the lemonade stand went quiet for half a second as they passed.

    Agatha felt it.

    She didn’t slow.

    Didn’t speed up either.

    She kept her pace steady, boots landing firm in the dirt. Rio walked beside her like she’d done it her whole life. At the livestock pens, a teenage girl in oversized boots struggled to guide a stubborn lamb into position.

    Rio slowed slightly, watching with open interest.

    Agatha noticed.

    “You wanna stop?” she asked.

    Rio glanced at her.

    “Yes.”

    No apology.

    Agatha turned with her toward the railing.

    They stood side by side, arms resting casually along the wooden fence. The wood was warm from the sun, rough beneath their palms.

    The lamb bleated indignantly as the girl repositioned it. Rio leaned in slightly, studying the animal.

    “Her hooves look good,” she murmured. “Healthy.”

    Agatha watched Rio instead.

    The way her brow furrowed faintly when she focused. The way her mouth moved as she thought aloud.

    Someone brushed past behind them.

    A whisper followed.

    Agatha heard it.

    She didn’t react.

    Rio’s shoulder pressed lightly against hers, whether by accident or design, Agatha couldn’t tell.

    She didn’t move away.

    The announcer’s voice crackled over the speakers, distorted but enthusiastic, calling participants to the next judging round. The crowd shifted like a living thing.

    Agatha straightened.

    “Come on,” she said quietly.

    Rio nodded and stepped back from the fence.

    As they moved deeper into the fairgrounds, the sky shifted toward a softer orange. Lights flickered on overhead, one strand at a time, humming faintly as dusk began its slow descent.

    They passed the pie contest table, ribbons already pinned beside lattice crusts and glossy fruit fillings.

    Rio paused briefly in front of a jar of honey labeled in careful cursive. Agatha watched her read it like it was something important. “You ever enter?” Rio asked without looking up.

    “Once,” Agatha said.

    Rio turned, surprised. “What happened?”

    “Lost.”

    Rio’s mouth twitched. “To who?”

    “Mrs. Caldwell. Peach cobbler.”

    Rio nodded solemnly. “Understandable.”

    Agatha huffed a quiet laugh.

    It felt normal.

    Not strained.

    Not like they were performing something for anyone.

    Just walking.

    Just talking.

    They moved toward the food stalls next. The smell of fried dough hit them full force. Oil popping loudly in metal vats. Powdered sugar dusting the air. Rio wrinkled her nose faintly.

    “Overwhelming,” she admitted.

    Agatha glanced sideways at her. “Too much?”

    Rio shook her head. “Just a lot.”

    Agatha angled her body slightly, creating a small pocket of space between Rio and the thickest part of the crowd without making it obvious. Rio noticed.

    She didn’t comment.

    But her fingers brushed briefly against the back of Agatha’s hand. Not a grab.

    Not even a hold.

    Just a quiet acknowledgment.

    They cleared the densest part of the food line and stepped into a more open patch of dirt near the livestock barn.

    The noise dropped slightly.

    The sky had deepened further now, streaked pink and violet near the horizon.

    Agatha looked around at the fairgrounds.

    The same place she’d walked a hundred times before.

    But this time—

    She wasn’t scanning for exits.

    Wasn’t bracing.

    Wasn’t calculating who might talk tomorrow.

    She was just here.

    With Rio.

    Rio tilted her head slightly toward her.

    “You still good?” she asked.

    Agatha met her gaze evenly.

    “Yeah,” she said.

    And she meant it.

    They stood there for a second longer, lights warming overhead, the murmur of the crowd rising and falling around them like tide.

    Then Agatha nodded toward the deeper rows of tents.

    “C’mon,” she said.

    They walked further in.

    Past the preserves and pies. Past the folding tables with crocheted blankets and jars of pickled okra. Past the hand-painted sign advertising a raffle for half a hog.

    The crowd thickened toward the center of the grounds, where the main ring had been roped off for evening demonstrations. A few rows of metal bleachers stood half-full already, people claiming spots with jackets and paper cups of lemonade.

    Agatha slowed without meaning to.

    Not hesitating.

    Just adjusting.

    Rio matched her pace easily, close enough now that their arms brushed with every other step.

    The lights overhead hummed faintly, strung in long, slightly sagging lines between poles. They cast a warm wash over everything, over dust particles suspended midair, over the sheen of livestock coats, over the faces turning toward the ring.

    Agatha felt it first in her spine.

    The shift.

    The weight of eyes.

    Not hostile.

    Not necessarily.

    Just aware.

    Someone nudged someone else near the bleachers.

    A look passed.

    Then another.

    Agatha didn’t look away.

    She didn’t stare either.

    She just kept walking.

    Her boots hit the dirt in steady rhythm. The brim of her hat cut a clean line against the glow of the lights. Her jaw stayed loose. Her shoulders relaxed. She didn’t shrink.

    Rio’s hand brushed against hers again.

    This time it lingered a fraction longer.

    Not quite a hold.

    But close.

    Agatha glanced sideways.

    Rio wasn’t looking at her.

    She was looking ahead.

    Chin lifted.

    Eyes clear.

    Like she understood exactly what this was.

    Being seen.

    Not in the quiet of the ranch.

    Not in the safety of distance.

    But here.

    In the middle of everything.

    They stopped near the outer edge of the ring, where a few older ranchers stood with arms crossed, discussing something low and technical about feed ratios.

    One of them—Mr. Henderson—noticed Agatha first.

    His eyes narrowed slightly.

    Then shifted to Rio.

    Then back.

    The pause wasn’t dramatic.

    It was long enough.

    Agatha gave him a nod.

    Polite.

    Neutral.

    He hesitated.

    Then nodded back.

    Not warm.

    Not cold.

    Just acknowledgment.

    It was enough.

    Rio shifted her weight slightly closer to Agatha’s side. Not hiding.

    Not clinging.

    Just choosing proximity.

    Agatha let her shoulder brush deliberately against Rio’s. Not subtle.

    Not exaggerated.

    Just clear.

    A teenager nearby dropped his phone when he realized he’d been staring. Agatha didn’t react.

    The announcer’s voice crackled again over the speakers, calling for volunteers for the calf-roping demonstration.

    The crowd adjusted, people stepping forward, stepping back, rearranging in loose clusters.

    A woman from the quilting table walked past them, slowed, and offered a small, tight smile.

    “Evenin’, Agatha,” she said.

    “Evenin’,” Agatha replied evenly.

    The woman’s eyes flicked to Rio.

    There was a beat.

    Then—

    “And you.”

    Rio met her gaze without blinking.

    “Evening,” she said calmly.

    The woman nodded once and moved on.

    Not dramatic.

    Not explosive.

    Just… noted.

    Agatha felt the old instinct flicker somewhere deep in her chest. The one that used to brace.

    The one that used to calculate.

    But it didn’t take root.

    It didn’t spread.

    Because she wasn’t alone in it anymore.

    Rio’s hand found hers then.

    Fully.

    No brush.

    No accident.

    Just fingers sliding into her palm and lacing there.

    Openly.

    In the middle of the fairgrounds.

    Agatha’s pulse jumped once.

    Not with panic.

    With clarity.

    She didn’t look down at their hands.

    She didn’t check who might be watching.

    She just tightened her grip slightly in return.

    The message was simple.

    I’m not stepping away.

    A murmur rippled through the bleachers as someone climbed into the ring. A calf bolted briefly before being guided back into position. Laughter broke out from one corner of the crowd.

    The world continued.

    Unbothered.

    Agatha stood there, hand in Rio’s, the hum of the fair wrapping around them like it always had.

    The difference was—

    She wasn’t bracing for impact.

    She wasn’t waiting for the sting of it.

    She was just standing.

    Solid.

    Present.

    Rio leaned slightly toward her.

    “You okay?” she asked quietly, barely audible over the noise. Agatha turned her head.

    Met her eyes directly.

    “Yeah,” she said.

    Rio searched her face.

    Not for cracks.

    For truth.

    Agatha held steady.

    After a second, Rio nodded.

    Satisfied.

    They stayed like that through the first round of the demonstration. Agatha half-watching the technique, half-aware of the warmth of Rio’s hand in hers. The calluses along her fingers. The steady pressure. A group of younger ranch hands wandered closer, whispering among themselves.

    One of them—Eli, barely out of high school—cleared his throat awkwardly. “Hey, Agatha.”

    She glanced over.

    “Eli.”

    He shifted his weight.

    Eyes flicking briefly to their joined hands before snapping back up. “Cows look good this season,” he said.

    “They better,” she replied. “You’re the one who helped with feed last month.”

    He flushed faintly.

    “Yeah.”

    There was an awkward pause.

    Then he nodded once, quick and decisive, like he’d made up his mind about something.

    “Good to see you,” he said.

    And then, to Rio, hesitant but clear, “Ma’am.”

    Rio inclined her head slightly.

    “Good to see you too.”

    He left.

    The tension that might have followed didn’t.

    The air didn’t split open.

    The sky didn’t crack.

    It was just—

    Fine.

    Agatha felt something settle deeper in her ribs.

    Not defiance.

    Not triumph.

    Just steadiness.

    Rio squeezed her hand once.

    “See?” she murmured.

    Agatha glanced at her.

    “See what?”

    “That the sky didn’t fall.”

    Agatha huffed softly.

    “Wasn’t worried about that.”

    Rio’s mouth curved faintly.

    “No,” she agreed. “You weren’t.”

    They turned their attention back to the ring.

    The lights overhead flickered as the generator adjusted to the growing demand. The sky darkened fully now, stars beginning to blink into visibility above the fairground glow.

    The crowd shifted again, people rotating toward the food stalls, others settling deeper into their seats.

    Agatha felt the weight of being seen still there.

    But it didn’t feel sharp.

    It didn’t feel like a spotlight.

    It felt—

    Normal.

    Like part of the air.

    She adjusted her grip on Rio’s hand, threading their fingers more securely together.

    A choice.

    Made once.

    And then again.

    And again.

    She didn’t need to prove anything.

    Didn’t need to perform comfort or courage.

    She just needed to stand.

    And she did.

    Beside her, Rio’s thumb traced a slow line across the back of her hand. Grounding.

    Present.

    The announcer called for the final round.

    The crowd cheered.

    Agatha didn’t look around this time.

    Didn’t check who was watching.

    Because she already knew.

    They were.

    And she was still here.

    Still standing.

    Still holding on.

    They didn’t leave the ring right away.

    The final round wrapped with a burst of clapping and a few good-natured groans from the losing side. The announcer’s voice crackled something cheerful about participation ribbons and next year’s improvements. People began to peel away in slow waves, bleachers clanging as folks stood, boots scuffing dirt, conversations overlapping and dissolving into the night air. Agatha stayed where she was.

    Rio’s hand still threaded through hers.

    The hum of the lights above them had grown steadier now that full dark had settled in. The bulbs cast halos against the deep blue sky, insects orbiting lazily in their glow. The air had cooled just enough to take the edge off the day’s heat. It smelled like hay and dust and sugar and something faintly metallic from the generators.

    Agatha flexed her fingers slightly, feeling the shape of Rio’s hand against hers.

    Rio didn’t let go.

    She shifted closer instead, shoulder brushing against Agatha’s upper arm. Not seeking protection. Not hiding.

    Just near.

    The crowd thinned enough that space opened around them. The pressure of being watched softened into something less direct. Not gone. Just diffused. Agatha exhaled.

    Rio turned her head slightly toward her.

    “Hungry yet?” she asked quietly.

    Agatha glanced at her.

    “Maybe.”

    Rio’s mouth curved. “That’s the closest I’ll get to enthusiasm, isn’t it?” Agatha’s lips twitched. “Don’t push your luck.”

    Rio laughed softly under her breath.

    The sound stayed between them.

    Not loud.

    Not for anyone else.

    Agatha felt it land somewhere low and warm in her chest. A breeze moved through the fairgrounds, stirring dust at their boots and lifting the edges of shirts and banners. It brushed cool against the back of Agatha’s neck. She reached up absently to adjust the brim of her hat. Rio watched the motion.

    Then, without comment, she reached up too.

    Her fingers didn’t adjust the hat.

    They brushed along Agatha’s jaw instead.

    Light.

    Almost absentminded.

    But deliberate.

    Agatha went still.

    The touch wasn’t theatrical.

    It wasn’t meant to be seen.

    It wasn’t bold.

    It was small.

    And that was what made it matter.

    Rio’s thumb lingered briefly near the hinge of Agatha’s jaw, just beneath her ear. The kind of touch that could have been mistaken for brushing away dust.

    But it wasn’t.

    Agatha’s breath shifted slightly.

    “Got somethin’ on me?” she asked quietly.

    Rio shook her head once.

    “No.”

    Her hand dropped slowly back to Agatha’s.

    Fingers threading again like they hadn’t left.

    Agatha swallowed.

    There were still people nearby.

    Close enough to notice if they were looking.

    Far enough that the moment belonged to them.

    The lights overhead buzzed softly. Somewhere behind them, a child protested loudly about wanting one more ride. Laughter followed. Agatha turned slightly, angling her body so that she faced Rio more directly. Their boots nearly touched now, toes brushing in the dirt. Rio looked up at her.

    Open.

    Unflinching.

    The fair noise blurred at the edges for a second.

    Agatha wasn’t thinking about who might see.

    She wasn’t thinking about tomorrow.

    She wasn’t thinking about what this meant.

    She was thinking about how simple it felt.

    Standing here.

    In the middle of everything.

    With Rio’s hand in hers.

    She reached up.

    Not rushed.

    Not dramatic.

    Her knuckles brushed lightly along Rio’s cheek, tracing the faint warmth there from the night air and the crowd. She let her thumb follow, slow and steady.

    Rio’s eyelids lowered slightly at the touch.

    She didn’t step back.

    She didn’t glance around.

    She just leaned into it.

    Barely.

    But enough.

    Agatha felt something soften deep in her chest.

    Not the unraveling kind.

    The settling kind.

    “You good?” she asked quietly.

    Rio’s eyes opened fully again.

    “Yeah,” she said.

    The answer was easy.

    Agatha nodded once.

    There was a cotton candy stand a few yards away, pink sugar spinning in delicate threads under bright light. A couple of teenagers were arguing about funnel cake versus fried Oreos. A man with a camera slung around his neck was trying to convince his wife to pose by a prize-winning heifer. Life went on around them.

    Ordinary.

    Unbothered.

    Agatha let her hand fall from Rio’s face, but didn’t let go of her entirely. She shifted closer instead, her hip brushing lightly against Rio’s thigh. Rio adjusted without thinking, stepping in so their sides aligned fully. A small thing.

    Barely visible.

    But unmistakable.

    Agatha felt the old reflex stir again, check the perimeter, measure the reaction.

    She didn’t.

    She stayed.

    Rio tilted her head slightly, studying her.

    “What?” Agatha asked.

    “Nothing,” Rio said softly.

    But her expression said something else.

    Something like—

    This is different.

    Agatha understood.

    Because she felt it too.

    Not the loud kind of different.

    Not the kind that announces itself.

    The quiet kind.

    The kind that sits in your bones and doesn’t leave.

    Rio shifted her weight again, this time resting her free hand lightly against the small of Agatha’s back.

    Not possessive.

    Not showy.

    Just there.

    Agatha inhaled slowly.

    She could feel the warmth of that hand through her shirt. The steady presence of it.

    A small moment.

    In a loud place.

    And it felt bigger than anything else that had happened tonight. “Dance floor’s open,” Rio said softly, nodding toward a patch of dirt near the main stage where a few couples had started swaying to a slow, slightly off-key country song.

    Agatha glanced over.

    A few older couples moved carefully in small circles. A pair of teenagers laughed as they tried to match steps.

    She looked back at Rio.

    “You askin’?” she said.

    Rio’s mouth curved faintly.

    “Maybe.”

    Agatha considered it.

    Then shook her head slightly.

    “Not tonight.”

    Rio didn’t look disappointed.

    She nodded.

    “Okay.”

    No pressure.

    No push.

    Agatha appreciated that more than she could put into words. Instead, she did something else.

    She leaned in.

    Not for a kiss.

    Not yet.

    She pressed her forehead lightly against Rio’s temple.

    A quiet, grounding contact.

    Rio exhaled softly.

    The world didn’t stop.

    The song played on.

    Someone whooped from the edge of the makeshift dance floor. Agatha stayed like that for a second longer than necessary. Then she pulled back.

    Not far.

    Just enough to look at her.

    “You want somethin’ sweet?” she asked.

    Rio tilted her head.

    “You offering?”

    Agatha nodded once.

    “Yeah.”

    Rio smiled.

    “Okay.”

    They didn’t rush.

    They didn’t separate much.

    They walked toward the food stalls still close enough that their shoulders brushed with every step.

    Still visible.

    Still together.

    The small moment lingered behind them like something sacred. Not loud.

    Not dramatic.

    Just true.

    And it carried with them as they moved.

    They didn’t stay until the last song.

    They stayed long enough.

    Long enough for sugar to stick to Rio’s fingertips. Long enough for the livestock barns to empty and refill. Long enough for the lights overhead to burn steady and the sky to deepen into that velvet blue that only showed up once the sun was fully gone.

    Agatha tossed the crumpled napkin from the fried dough into a trash barrel and glanced toward the outer edge of the grounds.

    “You ready?” she asked.

    Rio licked a faint line of powdered sugar from her thumb and nodded. “Yeah.”

    There was no reluctance in it. No unfinished business. Just the natural end of something that had been fully lived.

    They turned toward the gravel lot.

    The fair had shifted into its later rhythm now. The sharp noise of early evening had mellowed into something looser. Conversations slurred slightly at the edges. Laughter carried farther in the cooler air. A few children, overtired, dragged their heels behind patient parents.

    The lights strung between poles hummed faintly overhead as they walked beneath them. Dust rose around their boots, softer now in the dark. They didn’t hold hands immediately.

    They walked close.

    Close enough that the backs of their fingers brushed with each swing of their arms.

    Agatha felt it.

    The deliberate lack of urgency.

    The absence of proving.

    She glanced sideways at Rio.

    Her face was lit unevenly by the fair lights, one cheek warm gold, the other dipped in shadow. Her hair had loosened slightly from earlier, a few strands falling against her temple. She looked flushed. Alive.

    At ease.

    Agatha swallowed.

    “You had fun,” she said, more statement than question.

    Rio nodded. “Yeah.”

    A beat.

    “Did you?”

    Agatha considered that.

    The old version of her might’ve deflected. Might’ve said it was fine. Might’ve shrugged.

    Instead, she said, “Yeah.”

    And meant it.

    They stepped off the packed dirt of the fairgrounds and onto the looser gravel of the parking field. The noise softened immediately, swallowed by open space and distance. Engines turned over sporadically. Headlights blinked on, slicing pale lines through dust.

    Agatha’s truck sat where they’d left it, near the back row. Familiar. Solid. A quiet anchor in the shifting glow of the fair.

    They slowed as they approached it.

    Not because they needed to.

    Because the moment did.

    Rio stopped first.

    One hand reached for the passenger door handle, but she didn’t pull it open yet.

    She turned instead.

    The fair lights flickered behind her, outlining her silhouette in soft gold. Agatha stopped too.

    The gravel crunched once under her boot as she shifted her weight. For a second, they just stood there.

    Not speaking.

    The fair behind them still moved. Someone shouted a goodbye across the lot. A car door slammed. The generator hummed faintly.

    But the space between them felt separate.

    Contained.

    Rio’s eyes searched Agatha’s face.

    Not questioning.

    Just looking.

    Agatha stepped closer.

    Slowly.

    Her hand lifted without hesitation and settled at Rio’s waist, fingers spreading over denim. Not gripping. Not claiming. Just resting there like they belonged.

    Rio’s breath changed.

    Subtle.

    Her free hand slid up Agatha’s chest, stopping just beneath her collarbone. Neither of them rushed.

    Agatha tilted her head slightly.

    The kiss wasn’t sudden.

    It wasn’t sharp.

    It began as a brush.

    Mouth to mouth.

    Warm and deliberate.

    Rio answered it immediately, stepping in that final inch so their bodies aligned fully.

    The fair didn’t stop.

    Headlights swept briefly across them as someone pulled out of a nearby row.

    Agatha didn’t step back.

    Didn’t break it.

    Her hand tightened slightly at Rio’s waist, thumb pressing into the curve there. Rio’s fingers slid from Agatha’s collarbone to the back of her neck, steady and grounding.

    The kiss deepened slowly.

    Not hungry.

    Not desperate.

    Just certain.

    Agatha felt it in her chest more than anywhere else, the steadiness of it. The absence of bracing. The complete lack of hesitation.

    She wasn’t kissing in spite of the setting.

    She was kissing because of it.

    Because they were here.

    Because they had walked through it.

    Because she had stood in the middle of the ring and not let go. Rio’s mouth softened against hers. Opened slightly. Closed again. A slow exchange of breath.

    Agatha leaned into it fully.

    Gravel shifted under their boots.

    Somewhere behind them, a truck engine revved too loudly before settling. The world continued.

    They didn’t hurry.

    When they finally pulled apart, it wasn’t abrupt.

    It was gradual.

    Foreheads touching lightly.

    Breath shared in the narrow space between them.

    Agatha kept her hand at Rio’s waist.

    Rio kept her hand at Agatha’s neck.

    Neither moved first.

    “You good?” Rio murmured softly.

    Agatha let out a quiet breath.

    “Yeah,” she said.
