    A faint smile ghosted across Rio’s mouth.

    “Me too.”

    Agatha brushed her thumb once along the line of Rio’s jaw. Then she stepped back just enough to open the passenger door. She didn’t make a show of it.

    Just held it open.

    Rio slid into the seat, boots scuffing lightly against the truck floor. She paused before closing the door and looked back at Agatha once more. There was no question in her expression.

    No uncertainty.

    Just warmth.

    Agatha closed the door gently.

    Walked around the front of the truck, gravel crunching underfoot. The night air felt cooler now, cleaner, less thick with sugar and noise. She climbed into the driver’s seat and shut the door.

    For a moment, they sat there in the dim interior light. Not starting the engine yet.

    Just breathing.

    Rio reached across the console without looking and took Agatha’s hand. Not dramatic.

    Not tight.

    Just interlaced their fingers and rested them there.

    Agatha glanced down at their hands.

    Then back at Rio.

    She squeezed once.

    The engine turned over.

    Headlights flared to life.

    Dust lifted behind them as they pulled out of the lot.

    Agatha didn’t look back at the fairgrounds.

    She didn’t need to.

    Her hand stayed laced with Rio’s across the console as long as the road allowed it.

    And the silence between them wasn’t heavy.

    It was full.

    Chapter End Notes Sometimes growth isn’t loud.

    Sometimes it’s just not letting go of someone’s hand when people are watching.

    Thank you for staying with them through this arc.

    Chapter 26: Future, Said Plainly

    Chapter Summary A question asked quietly.

    An answer given just as plainly.

    Somewhere between checking fence posts and watching cattle move across the pasture, Agatha realizes that the future she’s been planning for the ranch no longer belongs only to her.

    Chapter Notes This is the ranch equivalent of a relationship check-in. There are cows. There is a fence. There is a conversation about the future that is extremely Agatha in the sense that it mostly happens while looking at the pasture.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes Morning came in slow bands of gold across the pasture.

    Agatha was already outside when the sun cleared the low ridge beyond the eastern fence line. The air still held that early cool that never lasted past midmorning this time of year, but it was enough to make breath visible in faint wisps when she exhaled.

    She stood by the truck bed with one boot braced against the bumper, tightening the strap on a mineral tub.

    The plastic drum was heavier than it looked, packed dense with the salt and supplement mix she rotated through the herd every few weeks. She’d hauled three of them out of the barn already and lined them up in the dirt like stubborn barrels waiting for instructions.

    Behind her, the pasture rolled wide and quiet.

    Cattle grazed near the upper fence line, slow heads dipping through the grass. The breeze barely moved the tall stems, just enough to ripple the surface of the field like a breath across water.

    Agatha gave the strap one last pull and stepped back.

    The sun warmed the back of her neck.

    Boots approached through the gravel.

    She didn’t turn.

    Rio came to stand beside her a moment later, carrying two metal tags and a coil of baling wire she’d probably found in the tool caddy by the barn. “You’re already halfway done,” Rio observed.

    Agatha shrugged.

    “Didn’t feel like waiting.”

    Rio glanced over the row of tubs, then toward the herd. “They’ve been working through the last one pretty fast.” Agatha nodded.

    “Grass is thinning on the lower slope.”

    Rio crouched briefly near the nearest tub, testing the weight with one hand. “Still full.”

    “Yeah,” Agatha said. “This one’s for the north pasture.” Rio stood again.

    They didn’t speak for a moment.

    The ranch woke slowly around them, fence wires creaking faintly in the morning heat, a distant crow calling somewhere beyond the treeline, the rhythmic sound of cattle shifting their weight in the grass. Agatha reached into the truck bed and lifted one of the tubs. Rio stepped forward automatically.

    “I’ve got it.”

    Agatha shot her a look.

    “You say that every time.”

    “And every time you pretend you’re not lifting forty pounds of salt.” Agatha’s mouth twitched.

    She handed it over.

    Rio balanced the tub against her hip easily enough, adjusting her grip with practiced efficiency.

    “Where?” she asked.

    Agatha nodded toward the upper pasture.

    “Near the cedar stand.”

    Rio started walking.

    Agatha grabbed the second tub and followed.

    They crossed the dirt track that cut through the property and stepped into the taller grass beyond it. Dew still clung low along the stems, darkening the cuffs of their jeans as they moved.

    The cattle noticed them almost immediately.

    A few lifted their heads.

    Not alarmed.

    Just aware.

    Agatha walked ahead now, pushing through the grass with the steady pace of someone who knew exactly where she was going.

    Rio fell in beside her after a few steps.

    “Think the rain’s coming early this year?” she asked.

    Agatha squinted toward the horizon.

    Clouds sat low and thin along the far hills. Hard to read this early. “Maybe,” she said.

    They reached the cedar stand, a small cluster of stubborn trees that had held their ground through decades of wind and drought.

    Agatha set the tub down near the edge of the clearing.

    Rio placed the other beside it.

    The cattle began drifting closer almost immediately, drawn by instinct more than smell.

    Agatha dusted her hands off.

    Rio watched the herd move.

    “They’ll burn through that in a week.”

    “Maybe less.”

    Rio nodded.

    Then she crouched and secured the tub with the baling wire she’d brought, looping it through a nearby post so it wouldn’t get tipped. Agatha leaned against the fence rail while she worked.

    “You’ve done that before,” she noted.

    Rio didn’t look up.

    “I pay attention.”

    Agatha huffed quietly.

    “Yeah. You do.”

    Rio finished tying the wire and stood.

    The cattle had gathered closer now, their hooves shifting softly in the dirt as they nudged toward the mineral.

    Agatha scanned them automatically, counting without counting, checking coats, watching gait.

    Routine.

    Rio leaned her forearms on the top rail beside her.

    They stood there a while.

    Just watching.

    “You’re going to rotate them again soon,” Rio said after a minute. Agatha nodded.

    “Pasture’ll need the break.”

    Rio glanced toward the far fence line.

    “That lower field could probably take them for a few weeks.” Agatha followed her gaze.

    “Yeah.”

    A breeze moved through the cedar branches overhead, scattering soft shadows across the ground.

    Agatha pushed off the fence.

    “Come on.”

    They started walking again, this time along the fence line itself. The posts stretched in a long, uneven row toward the ridge. Agatha checked each one as they passed, habit more than necessity. Some leaned slightly.

    Some held firm.

    Rio matched her pace without comment.

    They moved like that for several minutes, quiet except for the soft crunch of dirt under their boots.

    Then Agatha stopped at one of the posts and tested the wire tension with her hand.

    Still solid.

    She nodded to herself.

    “Fence’ll need tightening before winter,” she said.

    Rio looked at her.

    Agatha kept her eyes on the wire.

    “Storms’ll hit harder this year.”

    Rio followed the line of posts toward the hill.

    “Probably.”

    Agatha tugged once more at the wire, satisfied, and let it snap back into place.

    Then she straightened and glanced toward the herd again. “They’ll need to rotate again before the fall rains,” she added absently. Rio didn’t answer right away.

    Agatha had already turned and started walking again down the line. Rio fell in beside her after a beat.

    The grass swayed around their knees.

    The sky above them stretched clear and wide.

    Agatha continued talking as she walked, voice steady, practical. “We’ll move ’em down to the south pasture once the creek fills back in. That grass holds better through October.”

    She stepped over a shallow rut in the ground.

    “Gives this field time to recover.”

    Rio watched her.

    Not interrupting.

    Agatha kept going, scanning the fence.

    “Then come November we’ll probably reinforce the east corner. Wind comes through there worse than anywhere else.”

    She kicked lightly at a loose stone near the post.

    “We’ll need to replace two boards by then anyway.”

    She reached the next fence post and leaned on it briefly. She just pushed off the post and kept walking.

    Beside her, Rio said nothing.

    But her eyes stayed on Agatha a little longer than before. And the fence line stretched ahead like something already shared. Agatha walked it like she always did, eyes scanning automatically, body moving with the quiet efficiency of someone who had memorized every dip in the land years ago. Her boots sank slightly into the dry dirt where the grass thinned, kicking up faint curls of dust that drifted lazily behind them. She didn’t think about the word she’d used.

    Not consciously.

    The work kept her mind occupied: checking tension, noting where the ground sloped just enough to strain the posts when rain finally came, remembering the places where winter wind always cut hardest across the ridge.

    Routine.

    Familiar.

    The kind of work that filled the body so completely there was no room left for overthinking.

    Rio walked beside her.

    Not ahead.

    Not trailing behind.

    Just there.

    Her hands brushed through the tops of the grass as they moved, fingers catching seed heads that clung briefly before slipping free again. She wasn’t examining anything in particular now, no tools out, no notebook in her hand. The fieldwork portion of her day had ended earlier that morning. Now she was simply walking the land.

    With Agatha.

    The cattle had drifted further upslope behind them, their slow movements barely audible over the soft hush of wind through the grass. Agatha stopped at another post and leaned forward slightly, pressing a boot heel against the base to test its stability.

    It held firm.

    She straightened.

    “Good enough,” she muttered under her breath.

    Rio glanced at the post, then back at her.

    They started walking again.

    The land dipped gently toward the creek ahead, the grass growing thicker in the shallow basin before rising again toward the far ridge. Agatha rested her thumbs in her belt loops as she walked. The sun sat higher now, warm against her shoulders.

    She felt steady.

    Comfortable in the quiet.

    Comfortable in the work.

    Comfortable with Rio beside her in a way that no longer startled her every time she noticed it.

    They walked another dozen steps before Rio spoke.

    “You always plan this far ahead?”

    Agatha glanced sideways.

    The question wasn’t accusatory.

    Not even particularly curious.

    Just observational.

    Agatha shrugged one shoulder.

    “Kind of have to.”

    Her gaze moved back toward the pasture ahead.

    “If you run land.”

    Rio nodded slightly, absorbing that.

    Agatha continued without slowing.

    “Grass cycles. Rain patterns. Fences break if you wait too long. Cattle get stubborn if you move ’em too late.”

    She nudged a loose stone out of the path with the toe of her boot. “You get used to thinking ahead.”

    They reached a shallow rise in the land where the fence angled toward the creek.

    Agatha rested her forearms on the top rail briefly, looking out across the lower pasture.

    The grass there shimmered in the heat.

    A few dragonflies skimmed the air above it.

    Behind her, Rio stepped up beside the post and leaned lightly against it, mirroring Agatha’s stance without quite touching.

    “Always been like that?” Rio asked.

    Agatha squinted toward the horizon.

    “Pretty much.”

    She considered it for a moment.

    “Land teaches you quick.”

    “How?”

    Agatha tipped her chin toward the pasture.

    “You don’t think ahead, you end up reacting instead.”

    She ran a hand slowly along the rough wood of the fence rail. “And reacting usually means you waited too long.”

    Rio watched her.

    Not just listening.

    Watching.

    Agatha didn’t notice at first.

    She was too busy scanning the pasture again, habit pulling her attention outward the way it always did.

    Then Rio spoke again.

    “You said something earlier.”

    Agatha frowned slightly.

    “Did I?”

    Rio nodded.

    “You said ‘we.’”

    Agatha blinked once.

    Her eyes dropped briefly to the dirt at her boots.

    Then lifted again.

    The wind moved softly across the grass between them.

    She didn’t answer immediately.

    Not because she was defensive.

    Not because she wanted to take it back.

    Mostly because she hadn’t realized she’d said it at all. The word had slipped out of her mouth as naturally as breath. We.

    Rio didn’t push.

    She shifted her weight against the fence post, arms folded loosely now across the top rail.

    Agatha’s jaw worked once as she thought.

    “Probably just talkin’,” she said finally.

    But there was no real conviction in it.

    Rio tilted her head slightly.

    “Maybe.”

    She studied Agatha for another quiet second.

    Then asked the question.

    Calm.

    Even.

    Direct.

    “You planning on me being here for it?”

    The breeze slowed.

    Agatha felt the weight of the words settle between them, not heavy, not sharp, just present.

    She didn’t look away.

    That alone marked the difference between now and months ago. Old Agatha might have deflected.

    Changed the subject.

    Made a joke about cattle schedules or weather patterns. But the version of her standing here now simply stayed where she was. Thinking.

    She rested one hand on the fence rail and stared out across the pasture while she considered the question.

    The land stretched wide and patient beneath the sun.

    Grass.

    Fence.

    Cattle.

    The same things she had looked at every morning for years. And beside her, Rio.

    Still waiting.

    Not anxious.

    Not pressing.

    Just giving the question the space it deserved.

    Agatha let out a slow breath through her nose.

    “You don’t run a ranch day-to-day,” she said eventually, voice steady, “without thinkin’ a few seasons ahead.”

    Rio nodded once.

    “That makes sense.”

    Agatha’s fingers tapped lightly against the fence rail. Once.

    Twice.

    The sound was small against the quiet of the field.

    She shifted her weight and straightened from the fence. Then looked at Rio fully.

    Not guarded.

    Just honest.

    “You asked if I plan on you bein’ here,” she said.

    Rio held her gaze.

    Agatha didn’t rush the rest of the thought.

    The answer was there.

    She just needed to say it right.

    So she took a moment.

    Looked once more across the land.

    Then back at Rio.

    And let the silence hold for another beat longer before anything else followed.

    The wind moved softly across the pasture.

    It carried the dry scent of grass and warm earth, bending the taller stems in slow, quiet arcs around their boots. Somewhere beyond the slope, a cow shifted and snorted, the sound low and unhurried.

    Agatha stood with her weight balanced evenly between her heels. She had never been someone who rushed an answer that mattered. And this one—

    This one mattered.

    Not because it was complicated.

    But because it wasn’t.

    Her gaze lingered on the land one moment longer.

    The fence line stretching out across the pasture.

    The cedar stand where the mineral tubs now sat.

    The herd moving slowly across the grass like they always did this time of morning.

    The future of a ranch rarely arrived in sudden moments. It came in seasons.

    In small adjustments.

    In plans made quietly months before they were needed.

    And somewhere along the way, without her noticing exactly when it had happened, Rio had begun to exist inside those plans.

    Not as an idea.

    Not as a possibility.

    Just there.

    Agatha turned her head back toward her.

    Rio hadn’t moved.

    She was still leaning against the fence post, arms resting loosely across the rail, her expression steady in the sunlight. She wasn’t watching Agatha like someone waiting to be reassured.

    She was watching her like someone who trusted the answer would come when it was ready.

    Agatha appreciated that.

    The honesty of it.

    The patience.

    She pushed away from the fence again, boots settling into the dirt as she faced Rio fully.

    Her hands slipped into her belt loops.

    A familiar stance.

    Grounded.

    Practical.

    But her voice, when she spoke, carried something quieter underneath it. “Yeah.”

    The word landed gently in the space between them.

    Not dramatic.

    Not hesitant.

    Just simple truth.

    For a moment neither of them moved.

    The breeze lifted again, brushing across Rio’s shirt and tugging lightly at the loose strands of hair at her temples.

    Agatha watched the way the sunlight caught along the edge of Rio’s jaw. Watched the small shift of breath in her chest.

    Then she added, just as plainly, “If you are.”

    The second part came easier than she expected.

    Because it wasn’t a condition.

    It wasn’t caution.

    It was respect.

    Agatha had spent too many years watching people try to decide things for others, try to shape outcomes before the other person had even spoken. She wasn’t going to do that.

    Not with Rio.

    Not with something like this.

    The ranch had taught her plenty of things about responsibility. But it had also taught her something else.

    You didn’t force what wanted to stay.

    You made space for it.

    And then you let it choose.

    Rio’s expression didn’t change much at first.

    But something softened in the set of her shoulders.

    Something quiet.

    Like a tension she hadn’t realized she carried finally easing loose. She didn’t rush to fill the silence either.

    She simply held Agatha’s gaze.

    And in that shared stillness, the answer settled deeper than the words themselves.

    Agatha shifted her weight slightly, the toe of her boot pressing a shallow line into the dirt.

    The moment didn’t feel fragile.

    It didn’t feel like something that might break if either of them moved too quickly.

    It felt grounded.

    The way the land felt beneath their feet.

    The way the fence posts held steady even when the wind leaned hard against them.

    Agatha glanced briefly back toward the pasture.

    The cattle had drifted further along the slope while they’d been standing there.

    One of the younger calves trotted awkwardly to keep up with the others, its ears flicking in the sunlight.

    She let out a slow breath.

    Then looked back at Rio.

    There was no uncertainty in her expression now.

    Just the quiet certainty of someone who had already considered the weight of what she’d said.

    Running land meant thinking ahead.

    Planning for storms months before the clouds appeared.

    Preparing the ground for seasons that hadn’t arrived yet. And somewhere between checking fences and rotating pasture, between long mornings and slow evenings on the porch—

    The idea of Rio being here had stopped feeling like speculation. It had simply become part of the landscape.

    Agatha didn’t say anything else.

    She didn’t need to.

    The answer had already been given.

    Clear.

    Plain.

    Enough.

    The pasture held its quiet around them.

    Nothing dramatic followed the words Agatha had spoken. The sky did not shift. The cattle did not suddenly lift their heads in alarm. The wind did not die or rise.

    The land simply continued being what it had always been, wide, steady, patient.

    Agatha stood where she was, boots planted in the dirt beside the fence line, the warmth of the sun settling across her shoulders and down the back of her neck.

    She had said what needed saying.

    She didn’t feel the urge to dress it up with more words. Beside her, Rio was still.

    Not frozen.

    Just absorbing.

    Her weight rested lightly against the fence rail, one elbow hooked over the top board while the other hand hung loosely at her side. Her gaze hadn’t left Agatha’s face since the answer landed between them.

    For a long moment, neither of them spoke.

    The silence didn’t strain.

    It expanded.

    Like the land did after rain.

    Agatha felt something ease in her chest, not relief exactly, but the quiet sense of something settling into its proper place.

    She shifted her stance slightly, one boot scuffing a shallow line through the dust near the fence post.

    Rio noticed.

    Her eyes followed the movement, then lifted again.

    And then she pushed away from the fence.

    It wasn’t a sudden motion.

    Just a small shift of weight forward as her forearms left the rail and her boots settled more firmly into the grass.

    The distance between them had never been large.

    Two steps, maybe.

    But now Rio closed it.

    One step.

    Then another.

    Agatha stayed where she was.

    She didn’t brace.

    Didn’t retreat.

    Her hands remained hooked casually in her belt loops, shoulders relaxed, gaze steady as Rio stopped in front of her.

    Up close, the warmth of the morning sun had left a faint flush across Rio’s cheekbones. A loose strand of hair had fallen free from where it had been tied back earlier, curling against her temple in the breeze. Agatha noticed things like that.

    Small details.

    Always had.

    Rio’s hands hovered briefly near Agatha’s waist before settling—lightly, deliberately—against the sturdy denim at her hips.

    Not gripping.

    Just resting.

    The touch was simple.

    Warm.

    Certain.

    Agatha felt the contact immediately.

    Her breath shifted slightly in her chest, though she didn’t otherwise move. Rio’s thumbs brushed slowly along the seam of Agatha’s jeans where the fabric met the curve of her hips.

    Still not speaking.

    Still not rushing.

    Agatha studied her face.

    There was no urgency in it.

    No hesitation either.

    Just a quiet clarity.

    “You’re sure about that?” Rio asked.

    The question wasn’t doubt.

    It sounded more like a final check, something offered gently, the way you might check a knot before trusting it to hold.

    Agatha didn’t look away.

    She gave the smallest shrug.

    “If I wasn’t,” she said, “I wouldn’t have said it.”

    Rio’s mouth curved faintly at one corner.

    That answer seemed to satisfy her.

    The breeze lifted again across the pasture, pushing a soft ripple through the grass around their boots.

    Agatha slid her hands out of her belt loops.

    Slowly.

    Not abruptly.

    They came to rest at Rio’s waist, fingers settling naturally along the fabric of her shirt.

    The contact felt familiar.

    Not new.

    Not uncertain.

    Just another quiet confirmation of something that had already taken shape between them.

    Rio stepped a little closer.

    The movement closed the last space between their bodies. Agatha felt the warmth of her through the thin layers of fabric, the steady rise and fall of her breath.

    She tilted her head slightly.

    Rio leaned in.

    The kiss wasn’t hurried.

    It wasn’t tentative either.

    Their mouths met the way two things that already understood each other tended to meet, without needing to negotiate the moment first. Agatha felt the warmth of Rio’s breath as their lips parted slightly, the brush of it against her skin before the kiss deepened just enough to draw a quiet exhale from her chest.

    Her hands tightened slightly at Rio’s waist.

    Not possessive.

    Just steady.

    Rio’s fingers slid along the sides of Agatha’s hips before settling at the small of her back, the contact anchoring them together. The pasture stretched wide around them.

    Wind in the grass.

    The faint creak of the fence wire shifting behind them. Somewhere downhill, the herd continued grazing, unaware of the quiet moment unfolding along the fence line.

    Agatha leaned into the kiss a little more.

    Her thumb brushed slowly along the fabric at Rio’s side. She could feel the warmth of Rio’s skin beneath it.

    The kiss lingered.

    Not searching.

    Not urgent.

    Just present.

    When they finally pulled apart, it happened slowly.

    Their foreheads came to rest together for a brief second, breath mingling in the narrow space between them.

    Agatha exhaled softly.

    Rio’s hands remained where they were, warm against her back. The quiet returned.

    But it felt different now.

    Less like something waiting to be broken.

    More like something they were both standing comfortably inside. Agatha tipped her head slightly, looking down toward Rio again. Her expression had softened.

    Not dramatically.

    Just enough to show the ease settling into her posture. She brushed her thumb once more along Rio’s waist.

    Then said, almost casually, “Guess we better fix that fence before winter.”

    The words carried the faintest trace of dry humor.

    Rio huffed a quiet laugh against her shoulder.

    “We probably should,” she said.

    Neither of them moved away right away.

    They stood there a moment longer, hands resting, bodies close, the pasture stretching out around them in patient silence. The land didn’t rush them.

    It never had.

    And neither did the future they had just quietly acknowledged. For now, the confirmation was enough.

    For a while, neither of them moved.

    The fence post pressed lightly against Agatha’s shoulder blade where she had drifted half a step back again, but her hands hadn’t gone far. One still rested loosely at Rio’s waist, thumb brushing slow, absent lines against the side seam of her shirt. Rio’s palms remained at the small of Agatha’s back, warm through the denim, steady without tightening.

    They weren’t holding each other tightly.

    They were simply staying.

    The breeze passed over the pasture again, lifting the taller grass into quiet waves that traveled downhill toward the creek. The sound was soft but constant, a hush of movement that filled the open space between fence posts and scattered cedar trees.

    Agatha finally let her gaze drift away from Rio’s face. She looked out across the land again.

    It was something she did instinctively when she needed to think, not turning away from a person, but turning toward the thing that had always steadied her.

    The ranch spread wide beneath the sun.

    The herd had moved farther along the slope while they stood there. A cluster of cattle grazed near the mineral tubs they’d set out earlier, their heads bent low, tails flicking lazily at flies. One of the calves wandered a little too far toward the creek before turning again to follow the others. Agatha tracked their movement without realizing she was doing it. Habit.

    Years of it.

    But today the watching felt different.

    Not because the land had changed.

    Because the moment had.

    She shifted her stance slightly, weight settling more comfortably through her boots as she leaned one forearm on the fence rail again. Rio followed the movement without question.

    Her hands slid from Agatha’s back but didn’t fall away entirely. One came to rest lightly on the top rail beside Agatha’s arm, their shoulders brushing as they both faced the pasture.

    The quiet between them held.

    Not empty.

    Full.

    Agatha tipped her chin toward the grazing herd.

    “They’ll stay up there another hour or so,” she said.

    Her voice had returned to its usual low, practical cadence. The tone she used when talking about work.

    But there was something softer underneath it now.

    Rio followed her gaze.

    “Plenty of grass left.”

    “Yeah.”

    Agatha watched the wind move through the upper field.

    The sunlight had shifted slightly while they stood there. The angle of it now caught the tall grass in long streaks of pale gold, making the entire slope shimmer.

    She leaned her forearms more firmly on the rail.

    Her hands folded loosely together.

    Rio mirrored her posture again without thinking.

    It had become an easy rhythm between them, these small, unconscious adjustments that brought their bodies into quiet alignment. Agatha spoke again after a moment.

    “Storm season’s gonna be rough this year.”

    She said it the way someone said something they had already accepted as fact.

    Rio tilted her head slightly, studying the horizon the way Agatha had earlier.

    Thin clouds had begun forming low along the distant hills. Barely visible.

    But enough to hint at change.

    “Probably,” Rio said.

    She rested her chin briefly against her forearms.

    The fence creaked softly beneath the added weight.

    Agatha’s eyes followed the line of the pasture toward the far ridge. For a moment she pictured it the way she always did when thinking ahead. The grass thinning under colder winds.

    The fence lines rattling when the winter storms rolled through. The creek rising after heavy rain.

    All the small, constant adjustments the ranch demanded. It had always been that way.

    Work.

    Preparation.

    Maintenance.

    The future on land like this wasn’t something you waited for. It was something you shaped quietly, piece by piece.

    Beside her, Rio shifted slightly.

    Agatha felt the brush of her shoulder again.

    Not deliberate.

    Just natural.

    Then Rio said, simply, “We’ll manage.”

    The words settled easily in the air.

    Agatha turned her head a little.

    Not fully.

    Just enough to glance sideways at her.

    The use of the word didn’t surprise her.

    Not anymore.

    A faint breath escaped through her nose, something almost like a laugh, though quieter.

    “Yeah,” she said.

    Her gaze returned to the pasture.

    The cattle moved slowly across the slope, the herd adjusting itself without direction, each animal drifting where the grass led it. Agatha watched them the way she always had.

    But this time, she was aware of something else too.

    The presence beside her.

    The second pair of eyes watching the land with her.

    The second set of footsteps that had begun to trace the same paths across the ranch.

    Not replacing hers.

    Just joining them.

    She straightened slightly from the fence and pushed her hat back a fraction, the brim lifting enough to let the sunlight hit her eyes more directly. The sky stretched wide above them.

    Blue.

    Endless.

    The kind of sky that made the future feel less like a distant thing and more like a long road already under your feet.

    Agatha didn’t say anything else.

    She didn’t need to.

    Rio stayed beside her, forearms still resting on the rail. The wind moved again through the grass.

    The herd continued its slow, steady drift across the pasture. And the two of them stood there together, watching the land they would both now plan for.

    Chapter End Notes And that’s where we leave them for now.

    The next chapter will be the final one for this story. I’ve loved writing this little world and these versions of them, but this felt like the right moment to begin bringing it to a close.

    Chapter 27: What the Land Holds

    Chapter Summary The morning looks the same.

    The land hasn’t changed. The work hasn’t changed. The rhythm of the ranch still moves the way it always has.

    But Agatha is no longer standing in it alone.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes The morning began the way it always did.

    Not with birdsong or peace or anything so poetic it would’ve annoyed Agatha on principle, but with the small, practical sounds of a ranch already existing before she was fully awake. The distant lowing of cattle. The wind nudging somewhere at the loose edge of the porch screen. The house settling around dawn the way old wood always did, each beam and board adjusting itself slowly to light and warmth.

    Agatha opened her eyes to the ceiling she knew by heart. Same water stain in the corner near the window. Same hairline crack running through the plaster above the wardrobe. Same soft, pre-sunlight gray sitting low across the room, not bright enough yet to call it day, but enough to make the outlines of everything feel solid and present. For a second she didn’t move.

    She just lay there, one arm bent beneath her head, breathing into the quiet. There had been a time when mornings like this felt like bracing. Not because anything was wrong. Because everything needed doing. Because the instant she woke, her body already seemed halfway to the next task. Boots. Coffee. Feed. Gates. Water. The thousand small responsibilities that made up a day before the sun had even cleared the ridge. For years, she had woken like someone answering a call.

    Not dramatic. Not resentful.

    Just immediate.

    But this morning the urgency didn’t arrive all at once.

    It was there, yes. Somewhere in her bones, old and reliable as weather. But it didn’t hit hard enough to push her upright. It sat farther back, quieter than it used to, leaving room for the strange and still unfamiliar luxury of noticing other things first.

    The warmth beside her, for one.

    Not pressed close anymore, sometime in the night they’d both shifted, loosened, taken what space their sleeping bodies wanted without ever fully moving apart, but still undeniably there. Heat gathered in the mattress. A dent in the pillow. The faint trace of another person’s breathing, soft and steady, disappearing and returning in the silence like the tide. Agatha turned her head slightly.

    Rio was still asleep.

    Or near enough to it that it counted.

    She lay turned partly onto her stomach, one arm curled beneath the pillow, hair spread loose and dark against the faded pillowcase in a way that made it look softer than it ever did tied back. The sheet had slipped down to the middle of her back. Morning light, what little there was of it yet, had found the line of her shoulder and the soft hollow just above it. Agatha looked at her for longer than she meant to.

    There was no spike of alarm in it. No instinct to look away before the tenderness got too visible, even if only to herself.

    Just looking.

    That was one of the things this story between them had changed most completely, she thought. Not that she wanted. She’d wanted plenty before, in one form or another, though she hadn’t always been honest about it. Not even that she touched more easily now, or was touched without flinching. It was the looking.

    How ordinary it had become.

    How unguarded.

    Rio made a small shift in her sleep, not enough to wake fully, just enough to pull the sheet lower and expose more of her shoulder blade. Agatha’s eyes tracked the movement without thinking. The shape of her. The ease of her. The complete absence of tension in a body that knew, at least in this moment, exactly where it belonged.

    Agatha exhaled slowly through her nose.

    The room smelled faintly of sun-warmed cotton, dust, and the lingering trace of the soap Rio had used the night before. Nothing remarkable. Nothing heightened.

    Just lived-in.

    She pushed herself up eventually, careful with the mattress, instinctively moving in the slow, controlled way people did when they did not want to wake the person beside them. She sat for a moment at the edge of the bed, feet flat on the floorboards, forearms resting on her thighs. The boards were cool beneath her soles.

    The day beyond the walls waited.

    She knew exactly what she’d see when she stepped outside. The angle of the light over the eastern pasture. The trough near the south line that needed checking. The place where the grass by the creek had come back stronger after the last rain. The work waiting in its usual shape, patient and unromantic and real.

    But she also knew, without needing to turn and confirm it, that when she came back inside, the house would not be empty.

    That knowledge sat low and steady in her chest.

    Not dramatic. Not fragile.

    A fact.

    She stood, reached for her jeans from the chair, and dressed in the dimness with the kind of practiced ease that came from years of mornings spent moving before language. Shirt. Belt. Boots. She dragged a hand once through her hair and glanced back over her shoulder as she crossed toward the door.

    Rio hadn’t moved much.

    One knee now bent slightly under the sheet. Mouth still soft with sleep. The pillow pulled closer into her chest.

    Agatha paused with her hand on the doorframe.

    It struck her then, not as revelation, but as contrast, how different this was from the mornings that had come before. Not just before Rio. Before all of it. Before she’d let another person into the shape of a day without immediately measuring what it cost her.

    There had been mornings, once, when waking beside someone meant calculation.

    How long before she had to leave. How quickly she could make coffee and turn the moment practical. What expression to put on before anything soft could be mistaken for need.

    And even before that, before she knew the names for any of it, there had been the childhood version of morning: Evanora already sharp-edged and unsmiling, the house full of expectation before breakfast, every day beginning as if it were already disappointed in her.

    Agatha had built her adult life in opposition to that.

    She had built it around competence. Around self-sufficiency. Around routines so exact they held her upright even when nothing else did. And yet here she was.

    Standing in a room with another person asleep in her bed, and the feeling that rose in her wasn’t panic.

    It wasn’t dread.

    It wasn’t the old, corrosive need to put distance back in place before sentiment had time to thicken.

    It was something quieter.

    Something that didn’t demand defense.

    She reached down, picked up her boots, and carried them into the hallway before pulling them on. The laces scraped softly under her fingers. From here she could hear the ranch waking more fully now, one of the dogs giving a low bark from somewhere near the side yard, then another answering further off. A trough chain clinking faintly in the breeze. She moved down the hallway and into the kitchen.

    The house was cool still, the air carrying that brief hour of mercy before the day’s heat settled in. Light came through the east window in a pale band, hitting the sink and the scarred stretch of countertop beside it. The chipped blue mug sat where it had been left to dry the night before. Rio’s notebook rested open on the table, pages weighted down by a folded feed receipt and a smooth creek stone.

    Agatha stopped at the threshold.

    Looked.

    This, too, was different.

    Not because there were objects that weren’t hers. There had been visitors before. People passed through houses all the time leaving traces, intentional or otherwise. A forgotten shirt. A glass in the sink. A book face-down on the arm of a chair.

    But these things didn’t look left behind.

    They looked used.

    The notebook on the table. The mug by the sink. A pencil shaved down nearly to half its original size beside a stack of neatly sorted plant labels. None of it read as intrusion.

    It read as continuation.

    Agatha crossed to the counter, filled the kettle, and set it on the stove. The match struck on the second try. The flame caught blue and steady beneath the burner.

    She moved around the kitchen in the half-automatic rhythm of long habit, but even here the shape of the morning had changed. Not in its function. In its company. She reached for coffee and found herself glancing once toward the second mug before she’d consciously decided to use it. A small thing.

    But then, so many of the important changes had turned out to be. The kettle began its quiet build toward sound. Agatha leaned one hip against the counter and folded her arms loosely, gaze drifting toward the window over the sink.

    The yard outside glowed faintly silver-green in the early light. Beyond it, the first stretch of pasture ran open and low before the land lifted. She knew exactly where the herd would be at this hour. Knew where the shadows stayed longest. Knew the route she’d take after coffee, and likely which gate would complain when she opened it.

    The old life of her body still remained.

    That was the thing.

    She hadn’t become softer in the sense people liked to mean. She still woke early. Still thought in tasks. Still read the land before she fully read herself most mornings. The ranch still asked things of her, and she still answered. But she no longer felt like the only person inside that answer. The kettle hissed louder.

    Agatha pushed off the counter, reached for the mugs, and poured. Steam lifted between her face and the window, clouding the view for a second before clearing again.

    She carried both mugs to the table and set them down.

    Paused.

    Then one corner of her mouth tugged upward despite herself. There was something almost absurd about it, if she’d let herself stand back far enough to see. Agatha Harkness, who had spent half her life making sure no one had cause to assume permanence from proximity, now pouring coffee for someone before they’d even entered the room.

    But it didn’t feel absurd.

    It felt right.

    That was more unsettling than it would’ve been if it felt foolish. She sat down with her own mug and wrapped both hands around it, letting the heat settle into her palms.

    The kitchen held steady around her.

    A minute passed. Maybe two.

    Then she heard it: the soft tread of bare feet in the hallway, unhurried and familiar enough now that her body recognized it before her mind had to. She looked up.

    Rio had stopped in the doorway, one hand braced lightly against the frame, still caught somewhere between sleep and waking. Her hair was loose and rumpled. One sleeve of Agatha’s old shirt hung lower than the other. There was a pillow crease faint against her cheek.

    She looked at the table.

    At the second mug.

    Then at Agatha.

    Something warm and unreadable moved through her expression, not surprise exactly. Not gratitude in the polished, social sense. Something smaller. Truer.

    “Morning,” she said, voice rough with sleep.

    Agatha took a sip of coffee before answering.

    “Morning.”

    Rio walked in slowly and sat down across from her, fingers curling around the mug as if it had been waiting exactly there for her. She didn’t ask if it was for her.

    Agatha liked that.

    The silence between them settled easily. Not empty. Just inhabited. Outside, the first real sunlight hit the pasture hard enough to turn the grass from silver to gold.

    Agatha watched it happen over the rim of her mug.

    Same morning.

    Same ranch.

    Same work waiting.

    And yet not the same at all.

    They didn’t rush the morning.

    Not in the sense that the work wasn’t waiting, it always was. The ranch did not care for sentiment or slow pacing. It asked the same things every day, in the same quiet, unrelenting way. Feed needed checking. Water needed running. Gates needed opening and closing at the right time or not at all. But there was no sharp edge to it now.

    No sense that the moment before stepping into the day had to be cut short before it became something else.

    Agatha finished her coffee first.

    Not because she meant to, just because she always had. Years of mornings trained into her hands and throat, drink while it’s hot, don’t linger, get moving.

    She set the mug down with a soft, familiar sound against the wood. Rio was still halfway through hers.

    She wasn’t slow exactly. Just unhurried. Taking it in small, measured sips, eyes drifting occasionally toward the window, as if she were already cataloging the way the light was changing outside.

    Agatha watched her for a second.

    Didn’t comment.

    Then pushed her chair back and stood.

    “Gonna check the south trough,” she said, voice low, practical. Rio nodded.

    “I’ll come with you.”

    It wasn’t a question.

    Agatha didn’t make it one.

    She just gave a short nod in return and reached for her hat by the door. The screen creaked as she pushed it open. The air outside had already warmed a little, that early edge of cool giving way to something softer, thicker. The kind of heat that settled gradually, not all at once, but enough that you could feel it building in the ground beneath your boots. Agatha stepped onto the porch.

    The boards flexed slightly under her weight, familiar and steady. She didn’t stop there this time.

    Didn’t lean against the railing and watch from a distance the way she once had, holding herself apart from the thing she was looking at. She stepped down into the yard.

    Rio followed a moment later, the screen door swinging shut behind her with that same tired, weather-warped sigh.

    Agatha didn’t turn.

    She heard the steps anyway.

    Felt them.

    They moved across the yard together, gravel crunching under their boots in a rhythm that wasn’t perfectly matched, but close enough to settle into something shared.

    The pasture opened ahead of them.

    The morning light had shifted while they were inside, no longer pale and cautious but full and direct, catching on the grass in long stretches of gold. The herd had spread out further across the slope, bodies moving in slow arcs as they grazed.

    Agatha took it in automatically.

    Checked spacing.

    Noted the direction of movement.

    Work.

    Always work, even when it looked like stillness.

    But she didn’t stop walking.

    Didn’t plant herself at the edge of the porch to observe from a distance. She crossed the threshold into it.

    Rio stayed at her side.

    Not trailing behind.

    Not moving ahead.

    Just there.

    They cut across the yard and into the grass.

    The ground dipped slightly near the old fence line, and Agatha adjusted her stride without thinking. Rio matched it after half a step, not because she was watching Agatha’s feet, but because she’d learned the land enough now to anticipate the shift.

    Agatha noticed that.

    She didn’t say anything.

    But she noticed.

    The trough sat where it always did, just beyond the low rise near the southern edge of the pasture. The metal basin caught the sunlight hard, reflecting a bright, almost blinding strip across the surface of the water. Agatha stepped up to it and leaned over slightly.

    Still full.

    The water line sat just below the rim, surface disturbed only by the faint ripple of wind.

    She reached out and dipped her fingers into it, testing temperature without looking like she was doing it.

    Cool enough.

    Rio came up beside her.

    Close enough that their shoulders brushed lightly as she leaned in to look. “Still holding,” Rio said.

    Agatha nodded.

    “Yeah.”

    She straightened and wiped her damp fingers against her jeans. The moment could have ended there.

    Check done. Move on.

    That was how it would have gone before.

    Instead, she stayed.

    Not because there was more to inspect.

    Because she wanted to.

    Rio shifted slightly beside her, stepping a half-turn away from the trough. Her attention moved outward now, to the land, the grass, the small, easy details that had nothing to do with immediate tasks.

    She crouched near the edge of the pasture, fingers brushing through a patch of low-growing plants that had pushed through the soil near the trough. Not pulling.

    Not harvesting.

    Just touching.

    Observing.

    Agatha watched her.

    Not from the porch.

    Not from a distance that gave her space to retreat if the moment grew too soft.

    From right there.

    Close enough to see the way Rio’s fingers moved, careful, deliberate, the same attention she gave everything she worked with. Close enough to hear the quiet shift of her breath as she leaned forward, the faint sound of fabric moving against fabric when she adjusted her weight.

    Agatha’s chest tightened.

    Not sharply.

    Not in a way that warned her to step back.

    Just enough to remind her that she was feeling something. And that she wasn’t running from it.

    Rio tilted her head, studying the plants.

    “These weren’t here last time I checked,” she said.

    Agatha moved closer.

    She didn’t think about it.

    Didn’t hesitate.

    She just stepped forward until she stood just behind and slightly to the side of Rio, close enough that her shadow fell across the patch of ground. “Rain pulled ’em up,” Agatha said.

    Her voice came out low, even.

    Rio glanced back at her briefly, then returned her attention to the ground. “They’re spreading,” she said. “Good sign.”

    Agatha hummed quietly.

    Her gaze wasn’t on the plants.

    It stayed on Rio.

    On the line of her back where the fabric of her shirt stretched slightly as she leaned forward. On the way her weight shifted through her knees as she adjusted her balance.

    She reached out without thinking.

    Her hand came to rest lightly at the center of Rio’s back. The contact was small.

    But it grounded the moment in a way that words wouldn’t have. Rio didn’t startle.

    Didn’t pause.

    She leaned into it, just slightly, as if that touch had already become something expected.

    Something understood.

    Agatha’s fingers spread a fraction against the fabric, thumb brushing once, absent and slow.

    She stayed like that.

    Watching.

    But not from afar.

    From within it.

    From a place that no longer required distance to feel safe. Rio shifted again, pushing herself up from her crouch.

    Agatha’s hand slid naturally with the movement, trailing from her back to her side before falling away.

    They ended up standing close.

    Closer than they had been when they first stepped into the pasture. Rio looked out across the land again, eyes tracking something in the distance, maybe the herd, maybe the line of the fence further out. Agatha followed her gaze this time.

    Because she wanted to see what Rio was seeing.

    The pasture stretched wide in front of them.

    Wind moving through the grass.

    Light settling into the dips and rises of the land.

    It hadn’t changed.

    Not really.

    But the way she stood in it had.

    She wasn’t apart from it anymore.

    And she wasn’t alone inside it either.

    Rio’s shoulder brushed hers again.

    Neither of them moved away.

    Agatha let her arm rest loosely at her side, close enough that their hands nearly touched without quite closing the space.

    The moment held steady.

    Like the land itself.

    And for the first time, watching it didn’t feel like something she had to do on her own.

    The moment didn’t break.

    It shifted.

    The wind moved again through the pasture, carrying that dry, sun-warmed scent of grass and soil, bending the taller stems just enough to catch the light in motion. The trough behind them settled back into stillness after the small disturbances of their presence, the surface of the water smoothing into a quiet, reflective sheet.

    Agatha didn’t step away.

    She stayed where she was, shoulder brushing Rio’s, the space between them small enough now that it no longer registered as distance. Rio hadn’t moved either.

    Her attention remained on the pasture, eyes tracking something beyond the immediate foreground. Not searching. Not analyzing the way she sometimes did when she was working.

    Just looking.

    Taking it in.

    Agatha followed her line of sight again, more deliberately this time. The land stretched out in front of them, the lower slope giving way to that familiar bend toward the creek. The grass there grew thicker, richer from the water that held longer in the soil. Beyond that, the land rose again toward the eastern fence line, where the posts leaned just slightly under years of weather and use.

    Agatha knew every inch of it.

    Knew where the ground softened after rain.

    Knew which patches would hold green longest into the dry months. Knew which sections she’d left alone, not out of neglect, but because something about them had told her they didn’t need fixing. Her gaze settled there.

    The eastern stretch.

    The part of the land she had never quite folded fully into the working rhythm of the ranch.

    It wasn’t unused.

    It just… wasn’t necessary.

    Which, for Agatha, had always been reason enough to leave it as it was. She shifted her weight slightly, boots pressing deeper into the dirt. Rio spoke first, voice low.

    “You didn’t clear that section.”

    It wasn’t a question.

    Agatha shook her head once.

    “No.”

    Rio’s eyes remained on it.

    “Why?”

    Agatha exhaled slowly through her nose.

    There were a dozen practical answers she could’ve given. Didn’t need it.

    Soil wasn’t right.

    Better left for runoff.

    All true.

    None of them the full reason.

    She tilted her chin slightly toward the slope.

    “Didn’t have to.”

    Rio considered that.

    Her posture didn’t change much, but Agatha could feel the shift in her attention, the way she was turning the answer over without pushing for more.

    That was something Agatha had come to understand about her. Rio didn’t pry.

    She listened.

    And then she waited to see if there was more.

    Agatha’s gaze stayed fixed on the eastern fence.

    The posts there were older than the rest. She’d replaced most of the western and southern lines over the years, reinforcing where the weather hit hardest, where the cattle leaned most.

    But that stretch—

    That stretch had been allowed to remain.

    Not because she’d forgotten.

    Because she hadn’t.

    She knew exactly what condition it was in.

    Exactly how long it would last before it needed attention. She just hadn’t touched it.

    Agatha rubbed her thumb slowly along the seam of her jeans. A small, unconscious motion.

    The land sat quiet under the sun.

    Waiting.

    Not in any active way.

    Just existing.

    Agatha had always understood that about it.

    The ranch didn’t demand more than what was needed.

    It didn’t ask questions.

    It didn’t require explanation.

    It simply responded to what you chose to do with it.

    Or what you chose not to.

    Rio shifted slightly beside her.

    Not away.

    Just enough that her shoulder pressed a little more firmly against Agatha’s. Grounding.

    Present.

    Agatha felt that too.

    And something in her settled.

    Not like a decision being made.

    More like a recognition.

    Of something that had already taken shape without her needing to name it. She lifted her hand again.

    This time it didn’t hesitate.

    It came to rest lightly at Rio’s waist, fingers curling just slightly against the fabric of her shirt.

    Not drawing her closer.

    Just anchoring.

    Rio didn’t look down.

    Didn’t acknowledge the touch directly.

    But she leaned into it again, that same small, instinctive adjustment that had become so natural between them.

    Agatha’s gaze stayed on the eastern fence.

    Her voice, when she spoke, was low.

    Even.

    “That patch by the eastern line—”

    She paused.

    Not because she didn’t know what to say.

    Because she wanted to say it right.

    Not dressed up.

    Not heavier than it needed to be.

    Just clear.

    Rio’s attention shifted fully to her now.

    Agatha felt it without turning.

    She let the silence stretch just long enough to make sure the words would land the way she intended.

    Then she continued.

    “It’s good ground.”

    A beat.

    “Doesn’t flood easy.”

    Her thumb brushed once against Rio’s side.

    Slow.

    Absent.

    “You could set up there.”

    She turned her head then.

    Met Rio’s eyes.

    There was no strain in her expression.

    No tension pulling at the edges of her mouth.

    Just the same steadiness she brought to everything else. “Work from there,” she added.

    The words came out simple.

    Practical.

    Like she was talking about where to place a trough.

    Or which pasture to rotate next.

    But the weight of it sat underneath.

    Clear.

    Offered.

    She didn’t say stay.

    Didn’t say I want you here.

    Didn’t say any of the things that might have made it feel like a request. Because it wasn’t.

    It was an opening.

    Space made where there hadn’t been space before.

    Given.

    Not demanded.

    Agatha held her gaze.

    Didn’t rush to fill the silence after.

    Didn’t explain further.

    She didn’t need to.

    The land stretched out behind her words.

    Visible.

    Real.

    Everything she was offering sat right there in plain sight. And for the first time in a long while, Agatha didn’t feel the need to take it back.

    The words settled into the space between them.

    Not heavy.

    Not sharp.

    Just there.

    The kind of thing that didn’t echo, didn’t demand response, didn’t push for acknowledgment the moment it was spoken. It simply took its place, as real and visible as the land stretching out behind it.

    Agatha didn’t move her hand from Rio’s waist.

    She kept it there, fingers curved lightly against the fabric of her shirt, thumb resting just above the seam where it met her jeans. The contact was steady, unhurried, as if it had always belonged there.

    She was still looking at Agatha, something quieter in her expression now, something that wasn’t surprise, and wasn’t uncertainty.

    Understanding, maybe.

    Or recognition.

    The wind shifted again, brushing past them, catching the edge of Rio’s shirt and lifting it slightly against Agatha’s hand.

    Agatha felt the movement.

    Felt the warmth beneath it.

    Her gaze stayed on Rio’s face.

    There was no urge to explain further.

    No instinct to soften what she’d said or take it back and reshape it into something easier to dismiss.

    She had offered it.

    That was enough.

    Rio’s eyes flicked once toward the eastern stretch of land, as if mapping the distance between what had just been said and what it meant in practice. “You mean that,” she said.

    Agatha’s hand tightened slightly at her waist.

    “Yeah.”

    Then they came back to Agatha.

    She took a small step forward.

    It wasn’t sudden.

    It wasn’t hesitant.

    Just a quiet closing of space that had already begun to disappear long before this moment.

    Agatha didn’t retreat.

    Didn’t brace.

    Her body adjusted to meet the movement without thinking, weight shifting, stance settling, the hand at Rio’s waist tightening just enough to acknowledge the change.

    Rio stopped close.

    Close enough that the space between them was no longer space at all, just the shared warmth of two bodies standing within the same breath. She lifted her hand.

    Slowly.

    Agatha watched it come.

    Didn’t interrupt.

    Rio’s fingers brushed lightly along the edge of Agatha’s sleeve first, then slid upward, settling against her shoulder.

    Not gripping.

    Just resting.

    The touch was deliberate in its softness.

    The same way everything between them had become.

    Agatha’s breath shifted.

    Barely.

    The pasture stretched wide around them, unchanged.

    Cattle grazing.

    Grass moving.

    The trough behind them catching the light.

    Nothing in the world outside this moment altered.

    And yet—

    Something inside it had.

    Rio leaned in.

    Agatha met her halfway.

    Not out of urgency.

    Out of familiarity.

    Their mouths found each other without searching.

    No hesitation.

    No question.

    The kiss wasn’t new.

    That was the difference.

    It didn’t arrive like something discovered.

    It didn’t carry the sharp edge of first contact, or the careful testing of boundaries that had once existed between them.

    It was known.

    Recognized.

    Their lips pressed together in a slow, steady contact that held without needing to deepen immediately.

    Agatha felt the shape of it.

    The ease.

    The absence of uncertainty.

    The wind moved again.

    Soft.

    Persistent.

    It carried the scent of the pasture through the space around them, threading through the moment without disrupting it.

    Agatha tilted her head slightly.

    The kiss deepened, not in urgency, but in presence.

    A slow parting.

    A quiet inhale shared between them.

    Rio’s breath warm against her mouth.

    There was no rush.

    No need to take more than what was being offered.

    Agatha leaned into it.

    Not pressing.

    Just answering.

    Her other hand came up without thought, settling at the side of Rio’s neck, fingers brushing lightly against the skin there, feeling the warmth, the steady pulse beneath.

    The sound small.

    Close.

    The kind of sound that didn’t ask for anything, didn’t signal anything beyond the fact that she was there, fully inside the moment with her. The kiss held.

    Shifted.

    Eased.

    Not escalating.

    Not pulling back.

    Just existing in that middle space where neither of them needed to prove anything.

    Where nothing had to be decided.

    Because it already had been.

    When they finally parted, it wasn’t abrupt.

    It happened in increments.

    A breath.

    A fraction of distance.

    Their foreheads brushed lightly together before settling there for a brief second.

    Agatha closed her eyes.

    Not to escape the moment.

    To feel it more clearly.

    Rio’s hand remained at her shoulder.

    Agatha’s at her waist.

    The contact didn’t break.

    Didn’t need to.

    She opened her eyes again.

    Rio was still there.

    Still close.

    Still looking at her with that same steady, grounded attention. Agatha didn’t smile.

    She didn’t need to.

    There was something quieter in her expression instead.

    Something settled.

    Something sure.

    Her thumb moved once more against Rio’s side.

    Then stilled.

    The kiss hadn’t changed anything.

    Not in the way moments sometimes did, marking a before and after. It had confirmed something that had already taken root.

    Made visible what had already been true.

    Agatha let out a slow breath.

    The air between them shifted with it.

    And for a second longer, they stayed exactly where they were— Close.

    Steady.

    Certain.

    Before the moment moved on.

    For a moment longer, they stayed where they were.

    Not because the moment demanded it.

    Because neither of them felt the need to break it.

    The wind passed over them again, softer now, carrying heat instead of the last traces of morning cool. The pasture had shifted fully into day, the light no longer tentative, no longer stretching in slow increments, but settled and sure across the land.

    Agatha exhaled.

    The breath moved through her chest, steady and unforced, and something in her followed it, something that had been holding without her realizing it. She eased back just enough to look at Rio properly.

    Not searching.

    Not checking.

    Just looking.

    Rio met her gaze easily.

    There was no question there.

    No expectation.

    Just the same quiet presence she had always brought into every space she stepped into, uncomplicated, grounded, entirely herself. Agatha’s hand slid once more along Rio’s waist, then fell away. Not as a retreat.

    Just a shift.

    A continuation.

    She turned.

    Not abruptly.

    Just pivoted slightly on her heel and stepped away from the trough, angling toward the open stretch of pasture that led down toward the creek. She didn’t say anything.

    Didn’t need to.

    Behind her, she heard the movement.

    Boots in grass.

    The sound familiar enough now that it didn’t pull her attention back immediately. It settled instead into her awareness like something expected. Rio followed.

    They fell into step without needing to coordinate it.

    Agatha took the slightly uneven ground without looking, her body adjusting automatically to the dips and rises in the land. Rio matched her pace after a step, then another, her stride aligning just enough to make the rhythm between them feel natural.

    Wider here.

    Less interrupted.

    Just taking in the land.

    The way the sunlight settled across it.

    The slow movement of the herd farther up the slope.

    The line of the fence cutting clean and deliberate across the horizon. She had walked this path more times than she could count. Alone.

    Always alone.

    It had never felt empty.

    That wasn’t the word for it.

    It had felt complete.

    Self-contained.

    A life that required no addition to function.

    And yet—

    Now there was something else layered into it.

    Not disruption.

    Not intrusion.

    Just presence.

    She felt it beside her.

    The shift of Rio’s weight as she adjusted her footing.

    She let the awareness sit where it was.

    Unforced.

    Rio’s attention was on the land.

    Not in the focused, analytical way she had when she worked. Just taking it in.

    Her eyes moved across the pasture, following the contours of it, tracing the way the grass changed density, the way the wind moved differently across the lower ground.

    Not enough to break the rhythm.

    Just enough that their strides aligned more closely.

    Rio adjusted without comment.

    They continued like that.

    Side by side.

    No destination named.

    No urgency behind their steps.

    The creek came into view gradually, the shimmer of water catching the light between the grasses that grew thicker along its edge. The ground softened there, the soil darker, richer where it held moisture longer. Agatha stepped over a narrow rut where the water had cut through during the last heavy rain.

    Rio followed.

    Their shoulders brushed once.

    Neither of them moved away.

    Agatha reached out without thinking.

    Her hand found Rio’s.

    Not tentative.

    Not searching.

    Just there.

    Fingers closing.

    Fitting.

    The contact was simple.

    Natural.

    Like it had always belonged in this part of the day.

    Rio’s hand shifted in hers, thumb settling lightly against the side of Agatha’s fingers.

    No words.

    None needed.

    They kept walking.

    The land stretched out ahead of them, unchanged in its shape, but altered in its meaning.

    Agatha could feel it.

    Not as something dramatic.

    Not as a realization that struck all at once.

    But as a slow, steady understanding that had been building over time and had finally reached a point where it no longer needed to be questioned. She wasn’t walking this land alone anymore.

    It felt like something that had already been true long before she named it. The fence line came up on their right again as they angled slightly toward the house.

    Not enough to draw attention.

    Just enough to register.

    The path they followed wasn’t marked.

    It didn’t need to be.

    Agatha knew it.

    Knew where it curved.

    Knew where the ground rose just enough to give a clear view of the house ahead.

    It came into sight slowly.

    First the roofline.

    Then the porch.

    Then the full shape of it, settled into the land the way it had been for years. Home.

    The word came without resistance.

    Without hesitation.

    Agatha didn’t stop.

    She didn’t speed up either.

    She just kept walking.

    Hand in hand.

    The space between where they were and where they were going closing steadily with each step.

    And somewhere in that quiet movement, the day shifted again— Not into something new.

    But into something that had been waiting, patiently, for them to arrive at it together.

    The house came into focus slowly.

    Not because it was far.

    Because the moment stretched.

    Agatha saw it the way she always had, the angle of the roof catching light, the porch sitting low and wide at the front, the screen door slightly off- center where it had been repaired more than once over the years. The same structure. The same lines. The same place she had returned to every day, for years, with no one waiting inside it.

    Now—

    Now it didn’t look different.

    But it didn’t feel the same.

    Their hands remained linked as they crossed the last stretch of pasture. The grass thinned near the yard, worn down by repeated steps, by cattle passing too close, by years of use that had turned certain paths into instinct rather than choice. Agatha stepped onto the packed dirt without breaking stride.

    Rio followed.

    The shift from field to yard was subtle, but Agatha felt it anyway, the ground firmer, the space more defined, the quiet of the pasture settling behind them as the house took its place ahead.

    She didn’t let go.

    Didn’t even think about it.

    Her fingers remained threaded with Rio’s, the contact no longer something she had to be aware of to maintain.

    It was simply there.

    The porch boards creaked as Agatha stepped up.

    The sound was familiar, the exact pitch of it unchanged despite the years. She had always known which boards would give slightly under weight, which ones held firm, which ones would catch at the edge of a boot if you weren’t paying attention.

    She knew where to place her feet.

    And now—

    So did Rio.

    Agatha noticed it without looking.

    The way Rio adjusted her step just enough to avoid the slight dip near the corner post. The way her weight settled into the boards without testing them first.

    Not new.

    Learned.

    Agatha’s chest tightened slightly at that.

    Not discomfort.

    Recognition.

    She stopped near the railing.

    Not at the door.

    Not inside.

    At the edge.

    The place she had stood a hundred times before, looking out over the land like it was something she needed to measure, to understand, to keep in check.

    She turned slightly.

    Leaning her hip against the post, one shoulder resting lightly against the wood as she faced the pasture again.

    Rio came up beside her.

    Close.

    Not crowding.

    Just there.

    Their hands hadn’t separated.

    Rio’s fingers shifted slightly in hers, adjusting the grip into something more comfortable for stillness rather than movement. The change was small, almost imperceptible.

    Agatha felt it anyway.

    The day had deepened.

    The light had softened from the hard, direct brightness of morning into something warmer, heavier. Shadows stretched longer across the pasture, catching at the dips in the land, settling into the spaces between fence posts and low brush.

    The herd had moved again.

    Now closer to the far edge, where the grass held green a little longer. Agatha watched them.

    Not counting.

    Not checking.

    Just watching.

    Beside her, Rio did the same.

    No notebook.

    No tools.

    No analysis.

    Just presence.

    The wind shifted, slower now, carrying heat and the faint scent of dust rising from the yard. It brushed past them, lifted the edge of Rio’s shirt again, stirred the loose strands of hair at her neck.

    She let the moment settle.

    The porch.

    The land.

    The quiet.

    It was all the same.

    And yet—

    It wasn’t.

    She could feel it in the way her body didn’t brace.

    In the way her shoulders remained loose instead of tightening against some anticipated shift that never came.

    In the way the silence didn’t press in on her chest like something that needed to be filled or escaped.

    It held.

    That was the difference.

    It held.

    Rio’s thumb moved lightly against the side of Agatha’s hand. Once.

    Then still.

    Agatha glanced down.

    Their hands.

    Interlocked.

    Easy.

    Unforced.

    She let her fingers tighten slightly in response.

    Not enough to interrupt.

    Just enough to answer.

    Then she looked back out across the pasture.

    The sky stretched wide above it. The clouds at the horizon had thickened just slightly, hinting at the promise of weather that would come later, not now.

    Not today.

    Today held steady.

    Agatha shifted her weight against the post, the wood warm at her back. She had stood here before.

    So many times.

    With coffee in her hand.

    With nothing in her hand.

    With thoughts she didn’t want to follow and others she couldn’t quite ignore.

    She had stood here and measured her life in work done and work still waiting.

    In what the land needed from her.

    In what she could give it.

    Always alone.

    Not lonely.

    That wasn’t the word.

    Just singular.

    Contained.

    Everything she needed to account for within the boundaries of herself and the land she worked.

    She had believed, for a long time, that was enough.

    That it was the only way it could be.

    That anything else would disrupt the balance she had built. She looked at the pasture again.

    Rio stood beside her.

    Close enough that their shoulders touched lightly with each shift of breath. Her gaze remained on the land, her posture relaxed, her presence steady in a way that didn’t demand acknowledgment to exist.

    Agatha studied her.

    Not with surprise.

    Not with disbelief.

    With something quieter.

    Something that had taken root over time and now sat fully formed in her chest.

    Recognition.

    She didn’t say it.

    Didn’t name it.

    Didn’t turn it into something that needed to be spoken aloud to become real. She didn’t need to.

    Her hand was already in Rio’s.

    Her body already angled toward her without thinking.

    The space between them already gone.

    The wind moved again.

    The boards creaked softly under shifting weight.

    The cattle in the distance lowered their heads to the grass again, unbothered by anything beyond their immediate world.

    Agatha’s grip on Rio’s hand adjusted slightly, her thumb brushing once over the back of it before settling again.

    A small movement.

    A quiet one.

    But deliberate.

    Rio responded in kind, her fingers tightening just enough to meet it. They stood like that.

    Not speaking.

    Not moving.

    Not needing to.

    The house behind them.

    The land in front of them.

    And for the first time, not in a fleeting way, not in a moment that might pass, but in something that held—

    Agatha didn’t feel like she was standing between what had been and what might come.

    She felt like she was already in it.

    Already there.

    Not waiting.

    Not bracing.

    Just standing.

    Together.

    And the quiet that stretched out around them didn’t feel like something she had to carry on her own anymore.

    Chapter End Notes And that’s the end of this story.

    I’ve loved writing them like this, slow, grounded, a little stubborn, and very much in love without always saying it out loud. This felt like the right place to leave them: not at a peak, not in the middle of something breaking or beginning, but in something that’s already steady. That said… I know myself 😅 I have a habit of coming back to stories like this in little one-shots, so there’s a very real chance we’ll see them again, just pieces of life on the ranch, moments that happen after this.

    But for now, this is where they stay.
